
BOY of seven came up from the yard into the open
kitchen door and called for his mother. As he lis-
tened, he studied the shadow of himself which the
evening sun stretched in amusing distortion across the
kitchen floor and on into the sitting-room of the log house.
Whatever his intention may have been when he called, it
passed out of mind while he waited. The silence indoors so-
licited adventure.
Cautiously on bare tiptoes he went through the house and
peeped out at the front door. Grandfather sat in his accus-
tomed place on the little roofless porch, smoking his corn-
cob pipe. His sightless eyes, shadowed by heavy iron-gray
brows, stared off into the quiet toward something that was
very far away and yet not hard to see.
Giles tiptoed back into the sitting-room, pushed a chair
quietly over the rag carpet until it stood under the clock-
shelf, and then with strained effort at noiselessness climbed
onto the chair. His eyes came just below the great new
book on the end of the short shelf. He had been cautioned
never to look at it except when somebody took it down and
showed it to him. But nobody wanted to show it to him
enough. Everybody thought he cared only to look at the
pictures, when in truth he wanted to try reading it. So
early had his mother helped him to read that he could not
now remember when it was. He only knew that he was
wearying of The Great Wonders of the World. He had
read about giraffes and the wild dogs of Australia until they no
longer induced the slightest imaginative response. He wanted
to find something new that would claim him body and soul.He tugged the book over to the edge of the shelf right
above his nose. Would he be able to hold it when it left
the shelf? Or would he lose his balance on the chair and
create a disturbance so that his grandfather would call out,
"What's going on in there, anyhow?" The total distance
from his head down past his knees and the seat of the chair
to the floor was frightening.
But the book was not so heavy as he had thought it was
going to be. With suppressed grunts he steadied it down to
the chair, then slid with it to the floor, and lugged it to an
open space in the middle of the room where the sun from
the kitchen made a bright strip across the carpet. There
he spread open the wabbly bulk of pages, and then stretched
out on his belly and elbows to read.
At the top of the first page it said Stories of the Bible,
just as it did in huge gold letters and curlycues on the cover.
The picture opposite the title-page was brilliantly colored.
Pictures, after all, were interesting, especially colored ones.
He thought he would leaf through and find more. There
were dazzling kings and queens and warriors and shepherd
boys and big sturdy-looking angels and soft, fluffy little
ones. When he had studiously regarded all he could find of
them in the immense volume, he turned back to the begin-
ning.
In the quiet, so enthralling that he heard not so much as
the lop-sided ticking of the clock on the shelf, he read.
What was this the very first words? He felt the sides of
his head creeping. There was a time, so it said, when there
was no world for anybody to live in. There were no hills,
there was no sea, there were no rivers or creeks, no men,
no women, no boys and girls, no horses, no cows, no chick-
ens, no guineas, no birds of any kind, no trees for birds to
sit in! For there was no ground for anything living or dead
to rest on, no food for anybody to eat even if there had been
anybody to eat it. Everywhere there was just emptiness-darkness and lightnings and terrifying uproar without end.
He scarcely dared breathe, but he read on. These things
were true because the earth and the heavens were then
without form and void-whatever that meant. But the
meaning was not hard to guess; for on one big page was a
picture of it all in clear black and white. Inky clouds were
rising up out of nowhere, mixing themselves inside out as
the zigzag lightning struck through them, and then bulging
slowly away into ghastly nothingness.
Such empty times had been. More appalling to him still
as he read on, he learned that when the trumpet of the Lord
should sound and when might it not?-such empty times
would come again. The cows in the fresh green fields, the
apple trees in blossom, the Kentucky cardinals singing to
split their throats in the top of every tree-all these along
with boys and their fathers and mothers would be swept
away in a great fire consumed in an inkling, until only the
blackness, and the lightnings, and the angry seethings re-
mained.
The awfulness of being nothing--not so much as a breath
of air-overwhelmed him with cold terror. A-tremble he
closed the book, hugged it against his body, carried it to the
chair, climbed up, and pushed it with a criminal's nicety
into the exact place on the clock-shelf where he had first
touched it.
Who could dare to be alone in a big quiet house already
full of shadows when such terrible things were going to
happen? He whisked out the door to find his grandfather.
But his grandfather evidently had taken his long staff of a
cane and felt his way off with it on one of his solitary eve-
ning strolls.
Mother! Where was his mother? It was his mother
that he really wanted to be with anyhow if things were going
to happen. She had said that she might pick a few black-
berries. But just where? How far away was she? Whatif the rumblings and the end of everything should come
before he found her?
Father! He did not often turn to his father for comfort,
but in such a crisis he had better try to find him. Some-
times his father was grave and severe-looking, as if he had
missed something which he had expected to find. But just
now, anybody was better than being alone.
In the oncoming twilight he scudded down the path along
the ridge toward where his father should be. In the west
the lightning quivered ceaselessly at the top of a great soft-
looking cloud which towered aloft where the sun had gone
down. It was like the picture in the book-yes, almost ex-
actly like it. He wanted to shriek in terror, but there was
no one to hear him. As soon as this the final uproar was
coming. He could hear it swirling round his ears as he ran.
Just as he was beneath the spreading sugar maple that
overhung the path, an early screech owl set up a tremulous,
unearthly wail. Giles tried to run faster, but he only kicked
into two lithe young rabbits that had crouched very low in
the path until he was upon them, and then had bounded
away with petrifying suddenness.
His mother! He saw her coming up the steep bluff at the
lower end of the ridge. "Mother!" he shrieked frantically.
"Mother! Mother!"
He seized her round the skirts and buried his face as he
burst into a great sobbing.
"Why, my darling!" she implored. "What on earth has
happened to you?"
She dropped on the high upper edge of the path and
sought to comfort him. His grief was boundless, but its
origin was not to be discussed. So despite her efforts, he
only sobbed on, trembling with fright.
"What's wrong up there?" his father called somewhat
unconcernedly from the little pot-shop in the hollow below
them. He was firing the kiln of ware which he had set theday before. The mounting flame was beginning to show at
the tops of the flue-holes round the lower edge of the crown
of the kiln. He crowded some more long dry white-oak
wood into the east door, and then stepped out from under
the kiln shed and studied the points of flame that appeared
in the flue-holes. In his son's persistent sobbing he de-
tected a strain that was not ordinary pumped-up blubber-
ing. He turned, looked up in that direction for a moment,
and then climbed the steep path to see what had happened.
"What's all the trouble?" he demanded good-naturedly,
and tickled Giles in the ribs as he stooped over him.
But Giles only shrank from his touch and sobbed on as
if the end of everything had already come.
"Why, this will never do," his father declared. "A great
big seven-year-old boy crying like a baby." To this he
added other appeals, but Giles was not in a state of mind
to be quickly consoled. At last when his father could see
no gleam of logic in the situation, he grew impatient.
"Now see here!" he exclaimed with sharpness. "Either
there's something the matter with you, or there isn't. If
there is, you can tell us. If there's not, you can stop crying."
The austerity of voice could not be misinterpreted. Giles
knew that his father was given to direct action. In the
presence of an immediate and definite physical danger, he
felt the dangers of a disordered universe vanishing ever so
little. He stopped his sobbing, but he kept his face hidden.
"That's better!" his father assured him. "Now suppose
we have a look."
Giles could not at first reveal his face. He was afraid it
would disclose something which he very much wanted to
hide. But his father became severe again, and he timidly
lifted his head, and then sat up.
He was slenderish in body like both his father and
mother, but his face was full-like his mother's his fore-
head was round and high-like his father's-and there wasa prodigal downward tug of sadness at one corner of his
generous mouth.
"Why, you're all right," his father said assuringly, "or
you will be as soon as you smile a bit."
His father smiled a smile that was almost a laugh. Giles
caught a trifle of its infection. The faintest smile started
across his face, but died at the sad corner of his mouth.
His father sat down beside him and his mother on the
edge of the guttered path. For a time all three were mo-
tionless. Then his father pulled a great red bandanna hand-
kerchief from round his suspender-strap, and mopped away
the sweat induced by the hot kiln doors. This done, he sat
with the handkerchief hanging loosely in his left hand over
his knee, and with the thumb and forefinger of his right
smoothed out his blond mustache.
"Guess that rain's going north," he said, addressing his
wife. "I don't believe I'll have to cover the kiln, after all."
So the thunderings would come no nearer. They would
shudder along the north for a time and then fade away in
the northeast. Still Giles could not put them out of mind
utterly while the bulging, smooth-looking thunder-heads
were a-quiver with lightnings.
"The fire's getting up through the flue-holes," his father
said. "It's getting white. It'll be coming out the cat-holes
before long."
Giles's mind was pulled away from the book. But he was
overwrought and sensitive, and the firelight that for the
moment cleared his fears away, brought them all back again.
For when he looked at the flaring brightness of the kiln and
then glanced away, the trees overhanging the hollow loomed
massive and black and ominous above the pot-shop as if
they might reach to the top of the sky where there was
nothing at all but chaos. Was it possible that his father
and mother had not read in the big book about the empti-
ness of long ago? And did they not know that all the ter-rible things that had happened in the past might happen
again any time? At any hour they could happen again if
somebody big enough should say so.
But his father and mother only sat in the cool of the
evening that was filled with all sorts of voices and talked
about stoneware and at what time in the morning the kiln
would be ready to burn off and how much the ware would
sell for if it should be a good kiln and they sold it all.
Whether they were at ease because they were ignorant of
the things that could happen to them, or for some other
reason, it comforted him to see them sitting there in the
presence of the eternal, absorbed completely in things that
were only incidental.
After a time his father stood up. "You had better be
getting on back up the ridge. The copperheads are out in
full force on these warm nights, and there's not much light
left to see by."
No, Giles did not want to step on a copperhead. Frieda
Burrell stepped on one one night when she was visiting her
grandmother, and her ankle where the snake sank his fangs
in swelled up as big as three ankles. Then she tried to
walk on it too soon, and "complications set in," and they
carried Frieda off in a little white coffin ordered from Cen-
terville.
By the time they had walked alertly to the house, watch-
ing especially for anything sinuous across the path, Giles's
mind was somewhat freed of the effects of the reading. But
when his mother lighted the lamp and walked to the door
to ask his grandfather if he did not wish to come inside, he
saw the book there on the clock-shelf and wanted to run
away from it. But it was dark outside now, and he had
to stay there in the room with it.
His mother led his grandfather in and helped him to settle
in a rocking-chair, and then sat down herself to read by the
table. Then despite the presence of the book, Giles felt apleasant wave of security sweeping through him. There
they were, in their combined power able to meet anything.
Outside the walls of the room, anything might happen, of
course. But inside, with a light, and two grown people,
everything was clearly outlined and safe against surprise.
If only he did not have to go upstairs to bed in the dark.
Grandfather was blind, though, and in addition he had ad-
mitted on sundry occasions that his legs were not so young
as they once were. It was reasonable that Grandpoppy
should sleep downstairs. Where else, then, was he himself
to sleep, but upstairs in the dark?
Right while he thought about the matter he heard his
mother's voice. "You must go to bed, Giles. We're all
going, so I can have some sleep before I get up at midnight."
Giles sat frozen on his chair.
She surmised his fear. "I believe I had better go up and
get a clean sheet or two out of the chest," she remarked
casually, "before I forget it."
She put her book aside, and took up the lamp. He
bounded away to the whitewashed stairs ahead of her, fear-
less when he knew that she was coming just behind.
But when she went back downstairs, and left him alone
in the darkness, he saw again the great book and its terrible
promises. He tried not to think about it; he tried not to
see it. But after he had shut his eyes tight and stuffed the
edges of the sheet into his ears, there it was on the clock-
shelf with the curlycues in gold on the brown cover, and
inside was the assurance of an awful confusion in which
there would be no mother, no father, no grandfather, no
self-just nothing.
The wasps that made their way beneath the edges of the
roof and built nests on the under side of the lap-shingles
above him in his cavernous room moved restlessly in the
warm attic air after they had been disturbed by the lamp-
light, and exuded a pungent odor. But for once he was al-most oblivious of them and of the fact that they sometimes
inadvertently dropped to his bed and crawled angrily about,
brandishing their stings. The pale brightness at the single
little window high in the gable where the clear summer night
looked in was there in its place, too. But to-night he made
no effort to see that it was lighter out of doors than it was
in his room. The universe was to be dissolved again, and
he sobbed quietly lest such a thing might happen before he
had a chance to grow up.W
CHAPTER II
HEN Giles awoke the next morning it was light-
as light as it ever became up in his room and he
thought he heard his grandfather and his father
talking unconcernedly somewhere downstairs. He thought
he heard his mother, too, in the kitchen getting breakfast
as usual. Outside the high little window in the gable a cat-
bird sang. Not far from the house a rooster flapped his
wings, crowed lustily, and called his hens as if he had really
found something. Out toward the stable the guineas cried
their raucous "buckwheat," and off toward Mr. Burrell's
house-it must have been he could distinguish the voice
of a bull that roared and trumpeted in pagan lust. The
universe was still undissolved.
He got up and dressed himself-which consisted of but-
toning his knee-breeches to his waist-and went downstairs.
Breakfast was just ready, and his father was impatient to
eat and be off to the pot-shop before the whole day should
be wasted. The brightness of a new day pushed into the
house from every side.
Why had he ever been afraid? That was what he wanted
to know. When he looked about in the light of the morn-
ing, he was filled with a rushing belief in the permanence of
everything. In the distance somebody rattled along the
flintstone road in a heavy farm wagon and sang "Marching
Through Georgia" at the top of his voice-just as usual.
Off to the northwest the Randolphs' peacocks stirred the
morning air with their high-pitched ca-a-ouck. Bobwhites
called cheerfully in the thickets, crows scolded and threat-
ened and warned along the edge of the woods, and Ken-tucky cardinals, brown thrashers, and catbirds made the
orchard and garden echo with music. Off to the south and
east, coal was beginning to roar and bump over the screens
at the mines. Faint and far away came the sound, too, of
the low water of the river sliding and rippling over Shar-
rock's milldam.
He was filled with a great readiness to participate in some-
thing. He overheard his grandfather saying that he must
have a stroll before the sun grew hot, and forthwith volun-
teered to take the other end of the long cane and act as
guide. They went far, yet when they returned it was not
quite eight o'clock. For all practical purposes of adventure,
the day had scarcely begun.
The pot-shop! Yes, he would go down and see what in-
teresting thing his father might be doing. Slowly he walked
out through the orchard, watching catbirds and cardinals
and speculating on the time yet required by his favorite
apples for partial ripening. But as soon as he was in the
open field, he was moved by a wild impulse to make haste.
He ran. Then as he felt himself descending, and the fresh
air in his face, he spread his arms into wings and sailed off
down the ridge.
Suddenly he pulled himself up to a full stop. It was
where the screech owl had wailed in the sugar tree the
night before. There was nothing of terror in the tree's out-
lines now, and he knew that the owl which had cried out
so tremulously was an insignificant little runt of a bird
that no one need be afraid of. The big ones hooted.
He might catch
sun was bright.
But no, he saw
He thought he would look for the owl.
it. His father caught one once when the
It might be on one of the long branches.
nothing except an empty bird's nest. He circled the body
of the tree and looked up. Close against a stump of a limb
in a shadowy recess, he saw a feathery-looking little lump
which he imagined he saw move. He made a noise andthe lump turned its unfocused gaze in his direction. He
gathered stones, backed off up the hillside above the tree
and began to rain them into the branches. Soon the owl
cleared out with silent uncertain flops of his wings, and
made off toward the edge of the woods below, where he
crashed into the top of a hickory sapling.
Giles rushed in pursuit. Sure enough, he might catch a
bird that couldn't see in the daytime any better than that.
But when he reached the edge of the woods he could see
nothing but trees, and he could hear nothing but quiet. All
the birds seemed to be out in the fields and thickets.
He climbed over the fence out of the bright, comprehen-
sible fields into the mysterious woods. Then he stood,
mouth open, and listened. His ears brought him nothing.
But suddenly his eyes did. Over there in the end of an old
pile of rails was the head of a great snake. He did not
know that the head of any kind of a snake could be so
large as that. Fearful, he glanced away and then back. It
was still there, just as when he first saw it. He whirled
and rushed for the fence.
Out in the open field he felt less fearful. Yet he ran on
down the ridge, his easy speed multiplying the terrors of
the woods. He burst into the shop out of breath, but as
soon as he could command speech he told his father that
he had just run away from a big copperhead.
His father was skeptical. But since there were plenty
of copperheads about, and since there was always a state
of war against them, he dropped his work at the wheel,
slipped out of his bed-ticking apron, and went to see.
Giles led him to the exact spot.
"Now where?" his father asked.
"There!"
"Where?"
"Why, right there in the end of the rail pile. Don't you
see his big head?"His father looked intently.
"Don't you see his big red eyes?"
Then his father saw.
A splotch or two of light on the bulging, knotty end of
a rail completed the definite outline of an enormous ser-
pent's head and neck.
"That a copperhead?" his father asked. "Do you call
that a copperhead?" Then he laughed.
"Oh, what a snake! Why, if a snake had a head as big
as that, his body would be as long as the pot-shop."
He was annoyed the least bit by the interruption of his
work, but he was pleased to discover that there was some-
thing of the contour of a snake's head in what Giles had
seen. He tickled him robustly in the ribs. "Why, he'd be
bigger than any South American boa-constrictor in The
Great Wonders of the World."
Giles smiled a broad smile which acknowledged that the
joke was on him, and subsided into literal-mindedness.
He went back to the pot-shop with his father, who strode
along with firm, hurried steps. Perhaps his father would
let him sit on the clay-crusher. He had always wanted to
go round and round. He could hold the long gad and give
old Fan, the blind claygrinder, a sharp cut with it if in her
blindness she decided that no one was watching and de-
sisted from her labors.
His father was doubtful, but not unwilling. He stopped
old Fan, helped Giles to a place on one of the big flat sand-
stones put on the framework of the crusher to give it weight,
and gave full instructions about using the gad.
"Now don't you move. If you fall down in there between
the framework of the crusher and the stout braces that hold
the center-post in place, you'll be torn limb from limb."
Giles had heard that before. That was the reason his
father had given for denying all previous requests to ridethe crusher. The caution was an old one, and therefore
not to be taken too seriously.
His father went back into the wheel-room, and old Fan
went on with her claygrinding. Giles hoped she soon would
be tempted to stop. He wanted to see what he could do
with the gad. But she went right on, putting her feet down
with amazing accuracy in the deep, gray tracks which she
had shaped in her tens of thousands of other trips round
the circle.
Giles became interested in a chipmunk that hopped into
the wide doorway. If he should leap from his place on the
big sandstone just when the high wheels of the crusher came
past where the chipmunk sat restfully, he might catch it.
Could he clear the framework of the crusher and the circu-
lar trough of clay?
While he considered the matter, old Fan slowed down.
She stopped for a moment, her head inquiringly high in the
air, and then moved on until she had completed another cir-
cuit. This time she stopped with an even greater effort at
circumspection. She directed her empty eyes toward the
door in the wheel-room, and with one ear forward listened
intently. When she heard no one coming, she made her-
self at ease.
Giles did not want to bother with the touch his father had
told him to give her. It would be more fun to make her
jump.
Very quietly he stood up and braced his bare feet on the
rough surface of the stone. Wouldn't he give her such a cut
with the gad as she had never had in her life? He could
just reach her over the middle wheel. Back of his shoulder
he brought the gad cautiously and then took a terrific swing
at the top of her hips.
She responded with a wild leap. It jerked him from his
unstable position and sent him hurtling backward and across
the rear wheel and clay carrier right into the circular track.In a flash she would be upon him. In a flash he prayed
that a kind heaven would restore her sight before she
blindly planted one of her well-shod feet on his head.
He flopped closer against the circular trough of clay.
Just as he did so a hoof with gray mud on it whiffed the side
of his face. Before she could come round again he made
a wild, scrambling leap and got outside her path next the
board wall of the clay-pen. She plunged and galloped and
stumbled by over the rough path a half-dozen times, and
then, having no intimation that the gad was to descend upon
her again, slowed down to a brisk walk. Giles fell in behind
the sweep to which she was hitched and followed her round
to the door that led into the wheel-room.
"I believe I'll go home now," he said as casually as pos-
sible to his father.
"All right, if you like," his father answered. "But how
did you get that mud on your cheek? Here, wipe it off with
this gunny-sacking before you start home."
When he mounted the ridge, the imminence of a cosmic
upheaval that would end all was entirely out of his mind.
He was concentrating in an effort to forget the whiff of
old Fan's hoof past his ear.
He walked in at the kitchen door. His mother was flying
about in unusual activity.
"The preacher is here," she announced, "and is going to
stay for dinner. And Grandpoppy went over to talk to Mr.
Burrell awhile and hasn't come back. So you must go in
and talk with Brother Leighton."
Old Fan's hoof in turn became remote in his mind. He
smoothed his hair a little, opened the blue-painted door into
the front room according to instructions, said how-do-you-do
to Mr. Leighton, and sat on the edge of the rocking chair.
The minister was pleased to see him. Always the saving
of men and women was uppermost in his mind, yet he con-
fessed to Giles that talking about purely matter-of-fact af-fairs was sometimes restful. How old was Giles, for in-
stance? And did he spend most of his time down at the
pot-shop with his father? And had he yet gone to school?
Giles explained that he had not gone to school, but that
he meant to start in September.
Mr. Leighton, in an effort to make pleasant talk, ob-
served that a boy soon learned to read when he started to
school.
"I can read now," Giles informed him, sitting up a little
straighter in the rocking chair.
"Oh, can you?" Brother Leighton answered. "That is
fine. How did you learn?"
"I don't remember," Giles replied, honestly.
The minister looked him over. Without doubt he was
lying. Either he could not read, or he could remember
how he had learned.
Mr. Leighton arose from where he had been sitting, and
hummed a little tune as he looked at some of the books on
the table, the mantel, the clock-shelf.
"Ah, here is a beautiful book," he remarked, taking down
the Stories of the Bible. "Won't you read a little from this
book for me?"
"No-o-o!" Giles answered, almost tearful with the old
terror.
The minister smiled wisely to himself. Then he hummed
on, and began to glance through the volume.
"There are some beautiful stories in this book."
"I don't like them," Giles protested in anguish.
The minister thought he was frightened only at the pros-
pect of being put to the test.
"Why, yes," he said comfortingly. "Here is the story of
Pharaoh's cows. Pharaoh dreamed one night that he saw
seven sleek fat cows come up out of the river and eat grass
in the meadow. After they had been there awhile, he saw
seven poor, starved, bony cows come up out of the sameriver and eat the fat cows up. And after they had eaten
them, they themselves were still just as poor and bony as
they were before. That was quite a dream, wasn't it?"
Giles made no reply. He seemed busy measuring some-
thing on the arm of the rocking chair with his thumb and
forefinger.
Brother Leighton began another story-about Jehu, the
fast driver. But he saw Giles's grandfather feeling his way
up to the door, and hurried to meet him.
"Why, how do you do, Mr. Boyd?" he asked cheerfully,
but in stentorian voice. "It's Brother Leighton."
"Oh, how do do," Mr. Boyd replied.
He stood for a moment, gaunt and solitary in his blind-
ness. Then he chuckled and said: "You're just in time.
Henry Burrell was in need of a preacher when I left. His
sled had lost its sole."
They both laughed heartily at this audacious joke, and
the minister assured Mr. Boyd that he had grown up in the
country and was as good at sled soles as any other kind-
maybe better!
Giles thought he understood the implications of their hi-
larity. Still he meant to think about it. But his father
came, dinner was announced, and they seated themselves in
the expansive kitchen at a table with a fresh white table-
cloth on it. The preacher asked a blessing in a voice which
Giles thought ought to be heard in heaven without difficulty.
The dinner was exceptional in abundance and in length.
The others were content to sit at the table and talk, but
Giles could see no reason for sitting there after the purpose
of the gathering had been carried through. His mother saw
him slowly sliding down from his chair, and when she did
not protest, he hastened the process and scurried out of the
house.
Within three minutes he bounded in at the door."All of Mr. Burrell's cows are out in our garden eating
everything up seven of 'em."
His father rushed away to save the growing cabbage and
sweet corn. In a few minutes he came back, relieved but
exasperated.
"There was not a cow in sight," he declared, "and not a
track where one had been."
Giles sat bewildered and fearful.
"What you deserve," his father said, "is a good tanning."
"No, no, Brother Dabney," the minister protested.
"Nothing so extreme as that. No, no."
He too seemed full of bewilderment.
"Here," he said, suddenly seeing the way, "just punish
the tongue that told the fib. Come here, my boy."
For the moment Giles thought his destiny safer in the
hands of the preacher, and marched round the table to him.
"We must punish that tongue so it won't do it again," he
explained as if he were preparing the communion table at
the church.
He shook out a little heap of pepper on the point of a
knife. "To make you remember not to do it again," he
said. "Stick out your tongue."
But when he thought of the biblical origin of Giles's re-
port, he allowed some of the pepper to slip off the knife.
Giles sneezed and coughed and shed a few tears, and at
last vanished while the adult world still exchanged wisdoms
at the table.
In a few minutes he bounded into the kitchen again.
"Seven other cows got into the garden and ate up the
first ones. That's why you couldn't find 'em. Seven big
bulls, these last ones."
Upon completing his brief second narrative, he marched
round the table to Brother Leighton and thrust out his
tongue to have it punished.
Brother Leighton went through the motions of adminis-tering the pepper, but there was little of it on the knife.
Giles bounded away without so much as sneezing.
Their amusement was too great to be concealed. "Well,
well!" Brother Leighton said. "Well, well! That boy pos-
sesses imagination, I do declare. He does, for a fact."
But he did not tell them how Giles's story of the seven
cows and bulls had originated. He only said, when he at
last thought he had better go on to his next call, "Now I
believe I must be going. So if you'll hunt up the boy, we'll
have a word of prayer together."
While Giles looked through the back of his chair as he
kneeled in the corner and wondered how they could put so
many colored flowers on a strip of wall paper and have them
all exactly alike, he felt that the minister was not such a
bad sort. It was soothing to hear those big unintelligible
words rolling so easily off a practiced tongue. But when the
resonant voice petitioned more specifically that all might
come to the Lord-"even the least among us"-before the
heavens and the earth should be dissolved in the great con-
suming fire, Giles felt the old fear sweep over him, and
wished that Mr. Leighton had never entered the house.
The great destruction about which he himself had surrep-
titiously read was, then, on its way! The preacher, who
knew so many things, openly admitted that it was. He had
prayed about getting ready for it. No doubt Grandfather
and Mother and Father knew about it, too, but for some
lofty adult reason remained silent.
That night was as full of terror for him as the night be-
fore had been. He tried to shut everything out by covering
up his head with the sheet. But it was hotter than it had
been the night before, and he soon had to push the sheet
down and risk seeing whatever the night held in conceal-
ment. He dreamed. And in his dreams he cried out when
he saw everything fading away into nothingness. He saw
the pot-shop swallowed up, and the sugar tree with the littleowl in it, and the seven lean cows and the seven fat bulls.
Jehu went riding in a chariot drawn by four horses across
the fields past the locust trees toward Mr. Burrell's house,
but Jehu, too, was at last overtaken by the flames and swal-
lowed up in a consuming nothingness.
Nor was he helped the next afternoon when black clouds
with silver edges rose in front of the sun, and hurried to-
ward him with flashes and thunderings. The winds came,
too, and mixed the clouds up with one another as badly as
they were in the book. After the sun had slipped down out
of sight through some cracks in the clouds, the lightning
played along the horizon everywhere. Tremblingly he went
to bed again in the gaunt room upstairs and cried out very
softly in his tears that some one lift a staying hand and pre-
vent the awfulness from destroying everything while the
world was dark. Then he slept.
The next day was bright. He overheard his father say
that they were sure to have fair weather now for several
days. He must try to be unafraid. Yes, he must try.
Still, there was the great destruction. The book and the
preacher both said that it was only a matter of time.
August succeeded July and helped him. August was al-
ways drowsy. Lazy day followed lazy day. The catbirds
and cardinals hopped leisurely about in currant bushes and
berry thickets looking at things without saying much. The
chickens did little but wallow in pleasant dust-holes and
lose their feathers. The cows, stuffed with grass, lay with
eyes half closed beneath the locust trees and chewed their
cuds in contentment. Bobwhites sat panting on shaded rail
fences, and stood up only when they had to answer the call
of some more nervous cousin on a neighboring fence. It
was a month to calm one's spirit. Who knew? Perhaps
things would not pass away until after he had had a chance
to start to school in September.CHAPTER III
NE morning, when a pleasant and invigorating cool-
ness in the air had edged away the listlessness of
August, Mrs. Dabney said to Giles, "I think we
had better walk to the store and get your books to-day.
This is only Thursday, but next Monday you'll have to
start."
Giles was wild with excitement. "Why didn't you tell
me sooner, Mother? Why didn't you tell me last night?
Didn't you know then that you meant to go to-day? Didn't
you?"
"Yes, I knew," she replied comfortingly. "But you are
such a nervous boy, and I wanted you to sleep."
He shouted, he leaped, he threw apples against the end
of the stable, he chased the chickens until they flew in wild
excitement over the fences and into the lower branches of
the apple trees. For a year the excuse had been that the
school was too far away for such a small boy. But now
he was going.
When he had expressed joy in every way he could think
of, he pulled up at the kitchen door. His mother had
watched with consternation yet with sympathy.
"Aren't you afraid you'll tire yourself out so you can't
walk the two miles to the Company Store and back?"
"No!" he answered. "Why, I could walk a hundred
miles-just as easy as anything."
He took a second look at his mother as she stood framed
in the kitchen door. Her dark hair was neatly combed
back, her eyebrows were smooth and heavy, her cheeks
were round enough for one who was slenderish, and theywere pink. She was beautiful. His mother was beautiful.
"Mother!" he said impulsively.
"What, dear?"
"Kiss me."
He ran up the steps and offered himself to be kissed.
"Oh, you blarney!" his mother said, playfully affectionate
for a moment, and gave him a big hug and a kiss. "You'll
soon be forgetting me, now that you're going to school."
"No-o-o-o!" he shouted, his face darkening with temper
at the thought.
"Well, you must get ready to go to the store, anyhow."
Getting ready meant that he must wash his face and comb
his hair. He was already clothed, was he not?
They tramped two miles across the hills to a little store
that was in the farther edge of the school district. It would
have been pleasanter to walk the two miles and a little more
to Culbertson-in the opposite direction for then there
would have been the pleasure of going to town. But local
self-government in education prevailed in the county, and
the teacher in School District Number 4 had his own ideas
about using books that soft town folks could understand
well enough to make anything out of.
An old man with spectacles well down on his nose, and
with a habit of twisting the end of his turnip-top of a beard,
listened thoughtfully to Mrs. Dabney's request for books.
She was not like most of the women who came to his store.
In fact, he wanted to prolong the preliminary conversation
so that he might look at her. But when she seemed ready
to examine the books without further protocol, he said,
"Yes, ma'm," with a chivalrous bow that he had had no
occasion to employ for years. He almost blushed with a
sense of well-being as he turned to the shelves set aside for
school-books between the canned corn and the bed-ticking
and muslin."Do you want a Primer or a First Reader, bub?" he
asked Giles, but with an eye on Mrs. Dabney.
"Oh, he can read well enough already," his mother an-
swered. "Let me see a Second Reader."
Giles reached eagerly for the book.
"There, can you read that?" the old man asked dubiously.
"Let's hear ye."
Giles read easily.
"Oh, I can read something lots harder than that," he said,
with a sudden burst of confidence.
The old man passed him a Third Reader. He read in
that with little more faltering.
"I could read in a Fourth Reader," he declared. But his
mother was in doubt, and the old man, wishing to be agree-
able, said it had been his experience that it was better not
to strain their young minds "onnecessarily."
So Giles went back home with a Third Reader, a begin-
ner's arithmetic, a spelling-book, a slate with red binding
round the edge of the frame to keep it from rattling on the
top of the desk, and a ruled copy-book.
How he managed to put in the time from Thursday to
Monday was something that only his mother could remem-
ber. But at last Monday came. He was up an hour before
breakfast, reading for the hundredth time in his Third
Reader. Since Thursday he had read it through-every-
thing except the definitions.
At eight o'clock he ran all the way to the pot-shop and
burst in upon his father to tell him that he was at last on
his way to school.
"Better go round the road the first time," his father ad-
monished. "If you try to cross the field, old Genevieve's
bull may get after you. You don't want to lose any of your
new books."
Yes, it was better to go by the road all the way.
Without waiting for further admonition, he marched off.The earth would now have to pass away quickly, if it
passed before he had a chance to see what school was like.
He carried his books under one arm and swung his dinner
bucket with the other. Soon he was down the road to the
edge of the woods. How far back to the pot-shop it looked
when he turned his head for a last glimpse!
The woods! The woods seemed to care little about what
went on out in the open. The woods constituted a world
apart. Flickers hammered with a noisy clatter in the tall
steeples of the white-oaks above the road. Gray squirrels,
already at work on the new crop of hickory nuts, moved
affluently, with many flourishes of their spreading tails, along
the fence at the side of the level stretch ahead. A big
groundhog, almost big enough to be afraid of, stood up as
straight as a yellowish gray stump and watched his approach
for a time, then scuttled into a hole beneath a rock over-
grown with raspberry briars. Crows scolded and darted
down with menacing "K'ya-a-Ks" at a great white owl that
sat high on a steeple and blinked in the bright sunshine.
The numerous small birds were not noisy, but they were
active. Between the house and the pot-shop Giles had
learned the habits of the chewinks, and now felt much at
home when he came upon two of them rustling about in the
briars and leaves by the roadside.
He thought he had better run awhile. Maybe he had
been in the woods longer than he thought. He did not
wish to arrive late on the first day.
Down the road he went, running and walking by turns.
At last he came out upon the main highway and followed
it to the right for a quarter of a mile, where the long white
schoolhouse stood in an open space just above on the low
slope of the hillside. Already the school-yard was alive with
boys who were shouting and organizing games, and girls
who skipped, and ran, and whispered together. Some of
them stopped whatever they were doing and took a lookat him, but went on then with their sport as if he were of
no special importance.
It seemed a mile across the school-yard. But at last he
came to the doorstep. Sturdy ragweeds had grown up
everywhere round about during the summer and had not
been cut. He mounted the steps, followed a big girl in at
the door, and put his dinner bucket on the shelf where he
saw her put hers. Then he stood and looked about inquir-
ingly over the great, empty room until the teacher, a tall,
pleasant young man, suggested that a certain seat ought to
be about his size. Giles slid in, and then crowded his books
and his red-edged slate into the desk.
How big the room was! It was almost as big as the
church at Culbertson, he thought. And how ugly! The
walls were nice and smooth, yet nobody had papered them.
The desks were smooth, too, but nobody had cleaned the
spilled ink from the tops of them.
But here he was! When he was beginning to feel a
little less self-conscious, and wondered if he might not ven-
ture outdoors, the teacher took a hand-bell from inside his
long desk, seized it by the clapper to prevent premature
warning, walked deliberately to the door, looked at his
watch, waited a minute or two, and then rang the school
into session.
They poured in, noisy, clamorous, and resistant. The
teacher put the bell away and then stood by his desk and
counted.
"Sixty-two," he said. "Very good for the first morning.
Now if you'd just try to come every day."
They ranged in size from six-year-olds to young ladies
and gentlemen of fifteen and sixteen. Across the aisle from
Giles on the left, sat two of the young gentlemen in the "big
row"; across the wide center aisle at his right-over on the
girls' side-in seats the size of his own, sat two young
persons of his own age, looking lost in so great a gather-ing, and self-consciously smoothing out their nicely ironed
dresses. The rest of the long room was only a great smudge
of faces of every size.
Giles wondered how the school would ever get started,
out of all this blur. But the teacher was at ease. He had
taught before.
He began with some fundamental observations on eti-
quette. When anyone wished to go to the water bucket, he
was not to ask, after he had raised his hand, "Can I have
a drink of water?" but, "May I get a drink of water?"
When anyone wished to leave the room, he was not to ask,
"Can I go out?" but was simply to raise the hand with only
one finger open. Then he sketched the program of the
school, and immediately proceeded to carry it into execution.
"Will all the beginners come forward?" he requested.
With many misgivings, a dozen slid hesitantly from their
seats and marched up front.
The teacher looked in the direction of Giles, who was
conspicuous because he had no seatmate. "Aren't you a
beginner?" he asked in a kindly voice.
"No, I'm in the Third Reader," Giles answered with
nervous promptitude.
The entire school looked up to see where this clear re-
sponse had come from, and then the entire school laughed.
This was the first incident they had had a chance to laugh
at, and the outburst was unrestrained. The teacher had to
laugh a little, too.
"Very well, then," he said, assuming that the boy was
frightened at the prospect of a recitation, "you may recite
later."
Giles felt very red in the face for a moment and sat try-
ing to look nowhere. But when the room began to buzz
again with miscellaneous activity, he very unobtrusively
got out his Third Reader and read the first lesson through
once more.When the class was called he slipped out of his seat and
went to the front of the room with eight or ten boys and
girls nearly twice his size. Again everybody laughed. Since
the larger members of the class crowded together compactly,
Giles had to take his place at one end of the line, and it hap-
pened to be the upper end. Once again there was uproar.
But this time the teacher did not laugh quite so freely. He
saw that Giles carried a book which looked like the others.
"Very well, then," he said, not quite knowing whether
his sympathies were with the boy or the school, "since you
are at the head of the class, suppose you begin."
The entire school, eager for another opportunity to let go,
sat expectant.
In a clear, slightly nervous voice, Giles read the first para-
graph. Everyone was much astonished. He went on into
the next before the teacher thought to remind him that he
had read his share.
The school murmured into activity again, but listened to
hear what the teacher had to say.
"Very good! Very good! But when did you learn to
read?" he asked Giles, hoping to justify in some way his
own first skepticism.
"I don't know," Giles replied.
The school laughed louder and longer than ever. Here
was an amusing specimen.
At recess the big girls looked at him with sneaking admi-
ration. He was not sturdy, and he was very young; yet he
seemed to know what he was about when the test came.
They wanted to pinch him or tousle his hair. The big boys
talked him over less sympathetically, and arrived at the con-
clusion that he could not read at all; that he had committed
the first lesson to memory and could say it quite as well with
the book upside down-or with no book at all. Somebody
in his inclusive experience had heard of a case like that.
The gang from No. 5 Company Row said that whatever thetrue explanation of the mystery might be, they meant to
have it before dark. They would follow the smart kid home
and get the information in such manner as necessity should
dictate.
Giles was too much occupied trying to fall in with cus-
tomary practices to be aware of all the gossip about him.
At noon he watched the others, and when they rushed for
their dinner buckets, he went to the shelf and got his and
ate his dinner as he saw the rest doing. The yolk of his
hard-boiled egg was so crumbly and dry that it almost
choked him, and he had to go to the water bucket for a tin
cup of water to wash it down with.
When he came back to his seat the piece of pie that he
had put out on the upturned lid of his dinner bucket was
gone. He looked questioningly about, and two or three of
the boys snickered. Oh, well, he could get along without
the pie, whatever had become of it. The schoolhouse was
musty and he wanted to get out into the sunshine.
He did not belong in any of the categories. The boys
and girls of his own age were afraid of his attainments, and
the ones that were older were not anxious to be humiliated
by admitting that a kid no larger than this belonged in their
class. He watched the others play, walked in the direction
of any group that he hoped would absorb him within itself,
and when none did he wandered down toward the dusty
road.
On the edge of sod where the constant caving down of the
embankment left a declivity a foot high, a little girl perhaps
a year older than he was, sat and played with some clean,
sandy earth.
"Hello!" she said. "See what I'm doing?"
Giles noticed that she pronounced the g's on the ends of
her words, as he had been taught at home to do.
"Yes," he replied. There seemed to be nothing else to
say."Why don't you make something, too?"
There was no reason in the world why he should not.
When he was seated with the pile of sand between them,
she asked pleasantly: "What's your name?"
"Giles Dabney."
"I knew it was. I asked just for fun. I live with my
grandmother and she knows your grandfather. He's the
blind man, ain't he?"
"Yes," Giles answered.
"Can't he see anything at all?"
"No. But he gets along pretty well. He goes almost any
place he wants to."
"Wonder how it would feel to have no eyes," she said;
and she turned hers toward him and closed them and opened
them very slowly to find out.
They were very blue. Her cheeks were not round, but
they were pink-as pink as could be. Her dress was pink
and blue, like the wall paper which Giles had studied while
Brother Leighton prayed.
"What's your name?" Giles asked as chivalrously as he
knew how.
"Hope."
"Hope! Why, that's not a name. Is that the only name
you've got?"
"No, I've got another name, same as everybody else."
"What's it?"
"Fealty."
"Fealty! Why, that's not a name neither. Never heard
of such a name as that: Hope Fealty."
"Well, it's the only one I've got, and it don't sound strange
to me."
They dug in the clean yellow bank between them.
"What does your pa do?" she asked at last.
"Turns ware in the summer and digs coal in the winter.
What does yours do?""He beats the drum."
"But what does he work at?"
"He beats the drum a good deal. He don't work very
much, 'cause he's not well. That's why I live at my grand-
mother's. It's healthier there. I'm not so very well myself."
While Giles glanced at her and thought that she was the
picture of wellness, a stout man in black clothes came whir-
ring by in a red-wheeled buggy. He smiled and bowed.
"How do you do, Father," Hope called in response.
"Father!" Giles exclaimed. "He's not your father, is he?
He don't look sick."
"No, the priest, you stupid. Don't you ever go to
church?"
"We call our preacher Brother," Giles explained-
"Brother Leighton."
"Brother? I wonder what the difference is."
"I don't know. Ours wears whiskers, too."
The frightening book on the clock-shelf at home was far
away just now; yet Giles remembered. In an effort to put
it out of mind, he patted a mound of sandy earth, and then
reshaped it, and then reshaped it again. Then he heard the
school bell, and they both stood up and dusted their clothes
and ran to the schoolhouse, although neither one of them
was ready to go inside out of the sun.
The afternoon, with the sunshine pouring in at the shade-
less west windows, passed in a warm drowse with only a
shrieking fifteen-minute recess to break its continuity. At
four o'clock-at five minutes in advance, to be exact-they
poured forth into the weedy school-yard, rattling and toss-
ing their empty dinner buckets, and racing as fast as legs
would carry them in an effort to put the day behind their
backs.
Many were going down the road, and Giles fell in with
them. He succeeded in making himself an inconspicuouspart of the crowd until he was ready to turn off into the
quiet road that led through Genevieve's woods.
A half-dozen boys turned off, too, a little behind him.
As soon as they were out of hearing of the two dozen who
kept on down the highway, they began to call to him: "Hey,
there, we've got somethin' to say to you."
Giles looked back, but he did not stop. He knew with-
out further telling that they were unfriendly.
They came on. Half the time they shouted derisively
after him; half the time they made confidential talk among
themselves.
Slowly they gained on him. They could walk faster than
he was walking, and he would not run. Something inside
himself told him not to do that.
As they came into the quiet shadows of the great woods,
one of them said in a loud voice: "Say, I'll tell you what.
We'll tell old Genevieve that we saw him stealin' her apples,
and she'll sick that dog on him or maybe chop him up
with her hatchet."
They all shouted in approval of this plan.
"Smart little boy!" another called mockingly, so that Giles
could not fail to hear. "Plays with little girls!"
"Oh, I'll tell you what le's do," still another proposed.
They stopped and inclined their heads as if to hear. Then
they raised a great laugh and finished off with skyrockets
of yells that echoed among the trees.
Maybe they did not mean to catch up with him at all.
Giles began to wonder. He also began to walk a little faster.
He looked straight ahead, despite everything they shouted.
But they increased their speed with his. Finally one of
them called: "Hey, there, you kid. Stop your runnin' away
from us, or we'll quarter you and hang you up on one of
these trees for the crows."
He was beside himself with fright at their threats, but hewould not admit it by stopping. On they came until they
were up with him.
"Say, who learned you to read?" one of them asked.
"My mother," Giles replied, without looking back. He
could see the boy out of the corner of his eye.
"No, your mother didn't do anything of the kind," the
same boy shouted. "You have to go to school to learn to
read."
A smaller boy right at his heels said jeeringly: "His
mother! I'll bet his mother can't read, herself."
Giles whirled round in fury. "You lie! She can! You're
a lot of pigs-following a boy home!"
The boy who had made the remark stood face to face with
him, and Giles in his wrath gave him a shove. He was not
much larger than Giles, but he was older.
Quickly the boy caught his balance and delivered a sturdy
sock to Giles's nose.
The blood spurted over his lips, his chin, his clothes.
More from fright than from the hurt of the encounter, he
whirled crying, and started up the road. But he would not
run.
"We'll show you how to read before you're old enough!"
they called after him. "If you know what's good for you,
you'll bring a First Reader to-morrow morning-or a
Primer."
Giles sobbed quietly and tried to wipe the blood from his
clothes as he walked. He picked up three or four stones,
too, and put them in his dinner bucket. If the gang should
catch up with him again but he knew they would not. He
was coming out of the woods, and they never ventured into
the open where they might encounter adult wrath so far
away from their base of operations.A
CHAPTER IV
T THE edge of the woods he stopped by a pool of
water in the little run below the road and washed
the blood from his face and clothes. With the evi-
dences of the encounter gone, he marched on a bit more
bravely.
The pigs! That's what they were. They didn't want any-
body to do anything they couldn't do. He'd show them.
He would he would he would-well, just what would he
do? It would be terrible, anyhow, whatever it was. He
picked up a smooth chuck that had been hauled down from
the smash-pile at the kiln along with the broken stoneware
used in repairing the road, and sent it with all his power
into the rail fence. It did not make as much noise as he
had hoped, but it made a little. Wouldn't he do something
to them!
He walked on, full of terrible plans. But soon his face
felt less flushed, and the trees on the hillsides above him
began to remind him of their presence once more, and the
pot-shop loomed large in the narrow space between the
trees. He must forget the gang and stop and report to his
father what school was like. His father, yes. He knew
how his father would act and what he would say: he would
look hungrily toward something that had got very far away
from him, and he would say with grim cheerfulness: "I hope
you learned a lot. That's what school is for."
When Giles pushed the dragging front door of the shop
open, a wall of blue smoke pressed itself quickly against him
and filled his eyes. His father had finished early and built
a dry-fire.He closed the door and then ran up the steep bank toward
the ridge path. When he was out of breath, he climbed the
ridge deliberately. There was his own house, there were
his own apple trees, his own bright, clean fields over which
he had roamed in undisputed possession. He shouted in
courage. This was where he belonged.
What all could have happened since morning? And what
could they have done through this interminable period with-
out having him there to help? In the orchard the hens
looked up at him casually as if their world had gone along
well enough without him. He noticed something strange:
one hen had the manner of one of the big girls in school-
squatty and heavy on her feet, but with a small narrow
head which was much more active than her body. And how
quiet the world was!
Mother was still there, anyhow. He heard her going
from the cave to the kitchen. It filled him with a great
new assurance to find his mother still so busily engaged in
projects designed for the good of all.
But his mother had news to report. Grandfather had
had a new experience. He was sick. For just plain sick-
ness he thought old Dr. Seabright at Cloverdale was the
only member of the profession to be trusted fully. Giles's
father had gone on the long journey to the doctor's house.
Giles found the situation useful. When he went into the
front room his grandfather did not talk as if he were sick
enough to die. Still he was sick enough to claim all atten-
tion except for a few casual questions about purely matter-
of-fact things in school. Nothing was asked about what he
saw or did in Genevieve's big woods on the way home.
Eventually, too, his father returned, and his report had to
be heard. Dr. Seabright might reasonably be expected the
next afternoon. Dr. Seabright made it a practice never to
excite patients by coming too promptly. "If they are that
near gone," he said in defense of his method, "why get myteam all in a sweat trying to reach the bedside before disso-
lution comes?" But always he arrived, sooner or later.
With objective life Giles occupied himself till bedtime.
He meant to go right off to sleep and get ready for the next
day. But when he closed his eyes he saw the gang jeering
at him in the woods, and turning back down the road with
many grimaces and threatening gestures. His fear of them
was unlike his fear of the terrible disorder in the heavens.
He felt as if he ought to be able to cope with the gang, and
was enraged when he knew that he was not.
He went to school the next morning with wary glances in
every direction. But nowhere either on the way or at the
schoolhouse-did he see one member of the gang. He had
not yet learned that the attendance of its members at the
fountain of knowledge was but occasional. At the end of
the day he felt comforted by the mob of noisy schoolmates
who shouted their way down the dusty road. When he
turned off into his own quiet track of a way and looked
back, he saw the mob stretching out and breaking up into
smaller groups in the dusty sunshine. Scarcely one of these
boys and girls had said anything to him-except Hope
Fealty-yet they looked friendly there in the distance. He
wished some of them were going his way.
On he moved toward the dark wall of green. Why did
old Genevieve want to keep such a big woods as this, any-
how? Little trees were matter-of-fact and close to one.
But these towering white-oaks and poplars and shellbark
hickories stood with their heads so high that they seemed to
have been there always, and never once felt the presence
of a boy on his way home from his second day in school.
He came into the woods. The warm quiet of early eve-
ning filled him with awe. Was it possible that the gang
should lurk here? In this world of mighty trunks, and leafy
arches, and subdued bird voices, annoyance by anything
human seemed out of place almost out of question. Hewalked along, his eyes alert, his ears intent, his mouth
slightly open, his dinner bucket making the least bit of
sound as it brushed the leg of his jeans breeches. He was
not frightened, but he was full of the potentiality of fright.
He rounded a sharp turn in the road. There! What was
that? Something between the wagon tracks yonder ahead.
Did it possess life? It seemed to, yes.
Cautiously he came up, his hands trembling, his heart
thumping. Yes, it did possess life-very much life. It was
a snake a good-sized, businesslike snake with dull brown
spots over its body. Did it mean to get out of the road?
Evidently not, for when it saw him drawing near, it merely
contracted its body into shorter, kinky curves and lay tight
against the ground. A small boy was scarcely important
enough to be an annoyance.
Giles stood and looked until he felt his heart pounding
right up in his mouth. Then he backed away, put his din-
ner bucket down and looked for as heavy a stone as he could
swing.
"I'll make him get out of my way," he said in a desperate
effort to buoy himself up. "I'll show him how to take up
the whole middle of the road."
With the heavy stone balanced on his shoulder he moved
as near as he thought would be safe in bare feet. Then he
gave the stone a mighty heave and ran without looking to
see where it came down.
Back by his dinner bucket he turned to see if the snake
were at his heels. It was not. It was still there in the mid-
dle of the road-writhing and flopping. By fortune the
stone had come down exactly on his head as he lay on the
hard clay of the roadbed.
Giles saw in a flash what had happened. He was filled
with hysterical courage.
"Oh, maybe I won't put an end to him!" he exclaimed,
suddenly moved by an ambition to do more than make thesnake get out of the way. "I'll k-k-kill him," he said to
the woods at large in high-pitched, nervous words. "That's
what I'll do."
He heaved other stones.
"Look at that one! Maybe I didn't drop that one on top
of him."
Right behind him somebody asked: "What are you doing
there, my boy?"
He turned and saw a sleek team of roan horses, and a
red-wheeled buckboard with a tall man sitting in it. Giles
had been so engrossed with the snake that he had not heard
the light walk of the roans, or the soft roll of the well-oiled
wheels.
"I've killed him!" Giles shouted, in the confidence brought
by the undoubted presence of such heavy support. "I've
killed him!"
The iron-gray man climbed from the buckboard, told the
roans to stay where they were, and came forward to inves-
tigate.
"Say, you did kill him. That rock landed right on his
head. Did you throw it after he was done for?"
"No, it was the first one-the very first one. Didn't I
kill him, though?"
"I say you did," the man assured him once more. "He's
dead enough already everything but his tail-no doubt
about it. His tail will quiver for a long time yet."
He looked at Giles and noted his slenderness.
"Do you know what kind of a snake it is, boy?"
Now that the snake was good and dead, Giles hoped it
might be the most venomous reptile that ever crawled. The
least deadly species to be considered was a copperhead.
"Is it a copperhead?" he asked in great eagerness.
"That's just what it is. And he's not a small one, either.
I've seen bigger ones. But don't worry; he's big enough.If he had got hold of one of your toes, you would have
needed a doctor in a hurry, believe me."
Giles felt prouder still. He walked up closer to the snake
and looked at it. But even a safely dead snake was not
pleasant to gaze upon, and he shivered.
"Guess I'll just toss him over out of the road," the old
man said.
Giles watched him intently while he found the dead limb
of a tree along the road, broke a smaller branch or two from
it, and then dexterously slipped one end of it under the
snake's body. With a flip of a swing he sent the snake into
the air below the road. It lodged on the low-hanging
branches of a beech tree, but slowly slid off and dropped to
the ground.
"Good thing he didn't hang there," the old man observed.
"He would have started the fall rains a month ahead of
time. At least that's what the old women say."
Giles glanced up at the man's face and saw a quizzical
smile playing over it.
"But you mustn't believe all you hear."
He was tall-as tall as Giles's father at least. And when
he took off his soft black hat Giles noticed that his hair,
which stood up in a stiff short pompadour, was pretty gray.
Yet he did not act old.
He turned toward the team and then toward Giles. "Hop
in and I'll take you as far as I'm going."
Giles hustled to the buckboard and climbed in. The old
man took his seat beside him and unwound the lines from
round the buggy whip. Giles thought he had never been
in a vehicle so springy. And when the roans moved off, he
knew for a certainty that he had never traveled so swiftly.
What was his bare foot touching down there? He peeped
to see. It was a little leather case of some kind with a
handle. He took a sidelong glance at the face close above
him."Are you a doctor?" he asked.
"Supposed to be."
The man clucked pleasantly to his horses as they came
to a steep bank in the road.
"Have to go up and make a call on old man Boyd. Had
to go clear over by Bristle Ridge to see another man, and
thought I'd save a little time by coming up this road."
"He's my grandfather," Giles said, modestly.
"Who? Old man Boyd?"
"Yes."
"Well, then, maybe you'll let down the bars for me-up
there above the pot-shop."
"There's another pair farther up on the ridge," Giles ex-
plained, "but they're easy ones. And sometimes they're
down."
"So your name's Dabney, is it?" the doctor asked. "Your
father's name is Luke, and your other grandfather was
Theron-Theron Dabney. Isn't that right?"
"Yes," Giles answered. "But I never saw my other
grandfather."
"I suppose not. I suppose not. But he was a mighty
fine old man. They don't make better ones. If he had had
the chance some fellows have-"
He stopped short. "Look up here. Do you know you
look like him? He was slender and wiry and had a head
that looked as if it had brains in it.
"But you've got your Grandpop Boyd's big nose and
mouth. And I'll bet you've got his big feet, too."
He glanced down at Giles's toughened feet and laughed
reminiscently.
The bars were easy to manage and the roans took the
path of a road along the ridge without so much as stopping
to catch their wind.
At the house the doctor proceeded to business at once.
But when he had made up the medicine in two or threeglasses, and had turned the spoon upside down across the
top of one of them, he was ready to chat.
"Did the boy tell you he killed a copperhead?" he asked
of the family at large. "Got in a lucky shot early in the
battle."
Giles stood at modest attention while Dr. Seabright told
with no minimizing of the size of anything just how he was
driving through the woods and what he had come upon.
"He'll get along-that boy. You won't have to worry
about him at least not yet."
He put his hand on Giles's head and pulled his hair until
he produced a facial response. "You know I'm a phrenolo-
gist."
With long, slender fingers he felt for the "bumps." "Ob-
servant; everything interesting; imaginative; lots of nerv-
ous energy; may get somewhere if he keeps going; but
he'll raise hell if once he ever jumps the track."
Giles was startled at the old man's language. He did not
know that doctors talked like that right in people's houses.
But his thought was taken away from language when the
old doctor gave his head another friendly but stout rough-
ing, gathered up his hat and medicine case and said he
would be back day after to-morrow.
Giles felt that he was getting the world a little under foot
again. Hadn't he killed the most dangerous kind of snake
that inhabited the hills? Hadn't Dr. Seabright testified to
the fact?
He wanted to tell somebody. He wanted to tell some-
body at school. In the morning there was no opportunity.
Nobody gave him a chance. But in the afternoon when the
teacher saw him standing more or less alone, he stopped to
talk. He wondered if Giles ever met old Genevieve and her
dog in the big woods. Giles had not. He had, however,
encountered a copperhead on the way home and had
smashed its head with a rock.The teacher could not believe that such a small boy was
able to kill such a dangerous snake. Yet there was the epi-
sode of the Third Reader. So without commentary he re-
marked to one of the older boys who happened along, "Giles
says he killed a copperhead on the way home yesterday."
Now he would be elevated in the esteem of his fellows.
Had he not done something that required courage? But
the next morning he discovered the eagerness of the world
to disbelieve.
"Bet he never killed a snake of any kind," the boys said,
spitting contempt in graceful arcs. "It was a rotten limb,
that's what it was or maybe a harmless little water snake."
That evening when Giles turned off the main road, the
gang followed him. As soon as they were somewhat out of
hearing of the crowd, somebody called in raucous voice:
"Say! What about this snake story? You told the teacher
you killed a copperhead."
"I did."
"No you didn't either."
The boy's voice was filled with pumped-up hotness.
"We've had about enough of your lyin'!"
"I'm not lying."
"Oh, listen to that, will you! He says 'lying.' He tries
to pronounce like the teacher."
They all raised a disrespectful wail.
"Well, where is it, then?" one of them asked, going back
to the snake.
Giles saw the possibility of a peace that would include
the honors of war.
"It's up here along the road."
"Can you find it?"
"I believe I can."
"Believe! Listen to that, will you? He b'lieves he can
find it. Well, I guess you'd better find it. If you don't
we'll lick you within an inch of your life."They walked on and on into the woods, Giles confident,
the members of the gang, a few paces behind, full of spe-
cially devised oaths and menacing remarks.
At last they came to a half-dozen stones in the middle of
the road. Giles turned to the left and walked a few paces
down the slope to the edge of the spreading beech tree. He
stopped suddenly.
"Here he is, if you want to see."
"Where is he?" And they went gingerly down to where
Giles stood.
Yes, there he was. After two days he was not a very
presentable snake, but nobody could deny his existence.
Giles felt triumphant. Now what could they say? But
they were not without resources of their own.
"You never killed that snake," the spokesman, a boy of
eleven or twelve, declared in his most unfriendly voice.
"'Course he didn't," the others shouted noisily in concur-
rence.
"'Course I did," Giles retorted.
"Nobody saw you."
"Yes, somebody did see me. Old Doc Seabright from
Cloverdale saw me. He drove along just as I was finishing."
They felt defeated and belligerent.
"I'll bet old Doc killed it himself," one of them said in
desperation.
"Yes," they jeered, "of course old Doc killed it. Then
you pretend that you done it. You're afraid of snakes,
yourself."
"I'm not, either."
"You're not? Did you say you're not? Then I'll tell
you what we'll do. We'll just tie this one round your neck
for a necktie. You like snakes, do you?"
"Grab him and hold him," somebody else suggested, and
that was done. Then they had to decide which one of them
should handle the snake. While they were making thisdecision, Dr. Seabright drove along. He took in the situa-
tion before the gang saw him. Deliberately he climbed out
of his buckboard with his long, sharp buggy whip in hand
and walked down. In consternation, they stood like statues.
"Do you know what you are?" he asked as though he ex-
pected to hang them to a tree before they got away. "I'll
tell you what you are. You're a lot of dirty cowards. Why
don't you pick on somebody your own size? You ought to
be sent to the State Farm till you're twenty-one, and for a
half-cent I'd send you."
He took out a little notebook. "What's your names?
And don't you dare to lie to me, or I'll take the hide off
your back with this whip."
With wooden tongues they gave him their names.
"Now if I ever hear of you following this boy up this
road again, I'll have you sent up. And I may do it any-
how. Now clear out of here."
Speechless, they hurried off down the road through the
woods. But out in the sunlight they lost some of their fear.
"He couldn't send us up, could he?" they began to ask
themselves. But they could not answer such a question
courageously until they were nearer home, and Giles and
Dr. Seabright were far up the road.
The old doctor laughed. "They are cowards. People
that run in packs always are. That's the reason they go
that way. Not one of those kids would have touched that
snake with a ten-foot pole. They'll probably get over what
I said to them and follow you again. But they're cowards
just the same. If you'd try coming over through Gene-
vieve's pasture where the bull is, they'd never dare follow
you, they'd be that frightened of the bull."
Giles himself did not like the thought of the bull. "But
mightn't he get me?"
"No, he's got something else to think about besides horn-ing you into the air. I don't think he's cross, anyway. Keep
a little close to the fence till you find out."
At the house the doctor was more communicative than
usual. He said he got hot enough down in the woods to
thaw out.
Mrs. Burrell had dropped in to help around a bit, and she
busied herself in the sick-room while Dr. Seabright looked
after his patient.
"Have you met the new doctor that has come to Culbert-
son since Dr. Sutton died?" she asked as soon as there was
opportunity.
"No, I haven't."
His reply was crisp. The new doctor was the only com-
petitor who had affected his practice.
"He's a very nice man," she went on to say; "a graduate
of a medical school-has a diploma and all."
"How do you know? Have you seen his diploma your-
self?"
"Why, yes! It's framed and hangs in his office."
"How big is it?"
"Well, about that long, and perhaps that high."
"God, then he's no doctor at all. Why, mine's as big as
a table cloth!"
Thus he disposed of Mrs. Burrell and the new doctor. As
for Nathan, he thought another visit would not hurt any-
thing. "I'm over this way anyhow, and I might as well
stop in. Have a case of typhoid over on Bristle Ridge."
His face brightened up with an ironic smile. "Old Ezra
Presnell's girl fell out of a cherry tree a year ago in June
and fractured her wrist. I needed money just then and I
knew he had it. So I charged him fifty dollars. He said
that was five or ten times more than anybody else would
have charged, and wouldn't have paid me, I guess, if I
hadn't threatened to use the law on him.
"I knew that was the last time I'd ever darken his door-way; so I wrote him a receipt saying that the fifty dollars
settled our account in full from the birth of Christ to the
death of the devil. Well, he contracted typhoid about three
weeks ago, and I'll be damned if he didn't send for me. I'll
be going to see him for some time yet, and I can look in on
you without hurting anything."
Every day on the way home from school, then, Giles
watched for the roans and the red-wheeled buckboard. He
was unfortunate for a time; Dr. Seabright came too early.
Then one afternoon as he marched freely up the road-the
gang had not appeared anywhere that day-he looked back
and saw the buckboard coming speedily.
When Giles climbed in, he noticed a box tied on behind
the seat with a piece of heavy twine.
"What's in the box?" he asked, trying to modulate his
voice so that he seemed not to be inquisitive.
"Oh! That? Why, that's a pup. A good-for-nothing
lout over on Bristle Ridge wanted a half-dozen aching teeth
pulled, and after I'd yanked them out, he said he had no
money. Offered me a three-month-old pup, and as I knew
a person across the country who needed a dog, I took him
up."
Giles twisted round with his knees on the seat when the
horses slowed down a little, and looked into the crate. A
serious but pleasant young face was upturned to the light.
"What kind is he?"
"Bull terrier. Good blood in him-better blood than in
the man I got him from. You see, I know that pup's father.
I saw a bull make at him once and he just set his teeth in
that bull's nose same as a piece of steak. The bull threw
his head in the air and bellowed and roared like a lion, but
the dog just held on and took the free ride. No ladylike
gentleness in the way he took hold."
Dr. Seabright was full of stories about dogs. He con-
fessed that he thought dogs were more to be trusted thanhuman beings. But that, of course, was only one man's
opinion.
When they arrived at the house Giles was still turned
with his knees on the seat of the buckboard intently watch-
ing the young face in the crate.
"Sorry to disturb you," the doctor said, "but I have to.”
He lifted the box from its place, set it on the ground, put
one foot on top of it, seized a slat with one hand, and ripped
it off. Then he ripped off another.
The pup scrambled out, happy for liberty, happy for the
beneficent-looking world that he saw everywhere around.
"Why, you've let him out!" Giles cried in amazement.
"Yes, a dog requires free range. You must never keep
him cooped up."
"But-but he's not mine, is he?"
"Why, you're the person I had in mind. But of course if
you don't want him, I'll try to dispose of him in some other
way."
Giles went to sleep that night without thought of the
thunderings and lightnings of a disordered universe, with-
out visions of the gang from No. 5 Company Row. He
must be up early to feed his dog before he yelped and
howled.I
CHAPTER V
N THE swiftness of new days, school became the merest
incidental. Giles tried to hurry the school hours by
sitting in his rigid, artificially shaped seat and reading
his Third Reader through once more, solving problems in
the Primary Arithmetic ten lessons ahead, and mastering
such long words as "eleemosynary" and "hypochondriac" in
the adult, final pages of the spelling-book. The only in-
genuity that school exacted was in outwitting the gang from
No. 5 Company Row.
One afternoon while the big girls droned through a lesson
in geography on Central America, Giles decided upon the
experiment of going through the bull pasture. Accordingly
when next the gang appeared at school in field force, and
the educational day was done at four o'clock, he slipped
through the jostling crowd at the door, climbed the high
rail fence above the school grounds, and started off with a
show of courage through Genevieve's field.
Almost at the top of the hill he turned to look back. The
gang, as well as the other pupils who went eastward, were
well down the road, blustering, skylarking, chasing one an-
other.
How high up he was! Yonder past No. 5 Company Row
was a stretch of the river. In the other direction, up the
slender valley above the schoolhouse, he could tell wherever
there was a vehicle even if it were not itself in sight, by the
great cloud of dust that it left in its wake. And just there
across the field was a giant woman in grayish-black dress
and black sunbonnet, zealously repairing a wire fence.
Funny thing! Building fences was a man's job.He turned to go. The spotted bull and his cows were
coming up from the other side of the ridge not far from
where he had meant to go down. At once the cows saw him,
brought their ears to attention, stopped, and then gazed at
him with great undiscerning eyes. After they had watched
for a moment, they moved forward a step or two and
stopped again. At first the bull gave little heed; but when
he saw that the cows were interested he became interested
himself. Leisurely he walked forward and looked intently
at this small object in a checked blue waist and jeans
breeches. His gaze was full of confidence.
Giles thought he had better try moving. The bull watched
for a moment, contemptuously tossed his head, gave a sub-
dued snort, and moved on in his original direction.
On that evening, on every evening now, Giles and his dog
were in the fields until dark. The dog was trying to live up
to his name of Rowdy. It was difficult. When he raced
through the ragweeds and running-briars after his master,
