his hind legs sometimes operated so much faster than his
front ones, that the failure to coördinate sent him flounder-
ing in an awkward heap. There was little in him as yet
that suggested deftness. But he was young, and on every
hand the world contained promise, and he was as ready as
his master to help the promises fulfill themselves. In some
manner the two got over the ground. And at the close of
each day Giles led Rowdy into the front room and let his
grandfather feel him to "see" whether or not he was main-
taining normal growth.
One evening when Dr. Seabright came from the house,
he deliberately untied the hitch-strap from the hitching rack,
unsnapped the other end from the bridle of one of the
roans, folded the strap together and invited Rowdy to take
hold. Rowdy was ready to take hold of anything. With
the strap firmly in his mouth, he growled playfully and
crouched with his front legs and chin close to the ground.The doctor shook the strap and pulled. Rowdy held on.
Encouragingly the doctor called to him to stick to it, and
then lifted harder on the strap. Rowdy had no thought of
letting go.
Luke came up from the pot-shop. He watched the con-
test with grim amusement.
"You'll have some tooth marks on your strap, won't
you?"
"Oh, probably not very big ones."
Nevertheless Dr. Seabright coaxed the strap away from
Rowdy, gave it a glance of inspection, and tossed it into
the front of the buckboard. He then climbed in himself,
but seemed to be in no hurry to leave.
"How's your patient?" Luke asked, glad of the right kind
of opportunity.
"Oh, so-so. Just so-so."
He viewed patients with sympathy but without sentimen-
talism.
"You see the old man's along in years. And what's more,
he's done the work of three ordinary men. It's hard to
imagine that he has anything left to build on."
He saw Luke shrink a little, and thought he had better
amplify what he had said.
"I'm not like old Doc Henshaw over at Bluefield, who
rushes in, feels a man's pulse, asks with an expression of
anxiety if he is prepared to meet his God, and then leaves
him nine different kinds of pills. But the old man is sicker
than he will admit. He's a brave old cuss. But his diges-
tion is sluggish, his pulse is slow and feeble, and he coughs
more or less as if he couldn't get down to the bottom of his
lungs. He'd have a better chance if he could see. When a
man's sick, and blind to boot, it's not easy to stiffen him up."
Giles was busy with his own tests of Rowdy's ability, and
did not hear what was said. He was not thinking of ail-ments. His only concern was that Dr. Seabright should
keep on coming.
The doctor was in his mind when he went to bed that
night. The doctor was still in his mind the next morning-
until he arrived at school and was confronted by the gang.
"Just you wait! Maybe we ain't a-goin' to git you, hey!"
All day he wondered if the bull, then, was to be no pro-
tection, after all. Now that they had seen him go through
the field unharmed, they would not be afraid to venture
across themselves.
Yet when school was out and he climbed the hill, they
did not follow him. He hurried along, met the cows and
the bull again, and was filled with comfort when the bull
gave him only the slightest glance.
As he went triumphantly down the hill, though, he saw a
half-dozen boys running along the lower end of the road
that led toward the woods. So that was the game! They
would not risk the bull, but they would hurry round by the
road and be ready for him when he came up out of the field.
He dodged over to the fence and the brush that overgrew
it, and ran. The lid flew off his dinner bucket, and it was
not quickly put back on; but he hurried. Soon he was down
the hill. In no time he was across the narrow valley. In
a minute or two he was at the top of the little ridge by the
road. Without getting over he leaned through the fence and
looked. They were coming, but somewhat breathlessly.
He ran along the fence, inside the field, until he was down
over the little ridge out of sight. Then he slipped through
the fence, took the sharp curve in the road, and sped on and
on over the straight level stretch past the twin poplars, past
the leaning oak with the cave-like hollow at its base. When
he came to the turn where he had killed the copperhead, he
took a final glance back. Nobody was in sight.
Whew! he was out of breath. But he had beaten them.He would now not have to think about them again until
to-morrow.
They were not at school the next day-nor the next, nor
the next. But when they did appear again, they were men-
acing in their looks. At recess, their leader, a boy with eye-
balls too big for their lids, said to him without the slightest
reference to any effort that anybody had made to be the
first at the woods, "Oh, boy, if old Genevieve ever sees you
a-goin' through her field! That dog will make mincemeat
of you. Took all of Johnny Kiener's pants, purt-nigh, and
a good big bite of him besides. A thousand dollars wouldn't
take me across that field. Wait till you get a good look at
old Genevieve-or the dog either."
Giles made no reply. But he thought he would risk the
field. Dog in general was not so frightening when so much
of the world was now pleasantly eclipsed by Dog in particu-
lar. When he went to his seat at bell-time and glanced lan-
guidly through his Third Reader, he saw Rowdy with his
front legs and chin down close on the page, his eyes beg-
ging. When he leafed through the part of the spelling-
book designed to test older pupils and read "Mastiff, a large
dog," he saw instead a little dog standing in pensive mood,
the tip of his nose twitching, his breath in suspense, his eyes
looking across the hills.
That evening when he came down to the road just as Dr.
Seabright drove along, Dr. Seabright asked: "Don't they
follow you any more?"
"No, they're afraid to come across the bull pasture."
"What I told you! Get the average human away from
the road he has learned not to be afraid of, or away from
the pack he runs with, and he's the biggest coward in the
animal kingdom."
It made no difference to him whether Giles understood
his philosophy or not. It was pleasant to get it out of one's
system after so much of the day spent in the buckboard.He flicked the roans with the silken cracker of his buggy-
whip. Giles was jerked tight against the back-rest of the
seat and held there. That was something like! Let things
rush by like this forever and ever.
"But they'll be after you," Dr. Seabright went on.
"They're bigger than you are, and there are more of them.
You'll have to spend a long time getting ready. Rastle with
any boy who wants to, over there at the schoolhouse, even
if he puts you down every time; rastle with your dog; chop
wood-but in God's name don't cut a foot off-and develop
a lot of muscle and knowledge. Then some day when you
catch one of them out by himself, lick him within an inch
of his life, and he'll become your friend. But don't try it
too soon."
Giles liked the doctor better than ever. He had seen
"wrestle" in the spelling-book, and his mother had told him
it was the correct word to use, but Doc had not hesitated to
say "rastle," the same as everybody else. Giles would be
prepared to pronounce it either way. Doc proposed a defi-
nite program, too. What he outlined was ten times more
interesting than drooling along in things which he could do
without half trying.
That evening he easily teased Rowdy into a wrestling
match. Rowdy was not very big, and he was still awkward,
but he handled his front legs as if a thousand generations of
his ancestors had done nothing but flop antagonists over in
order to have a better grip on their throats. Rowdy offered
resistance; and Giles could put him down.
The world began to sing a great new song. The song
hummed itself in Giles's ears when he got up in the morn-
ing. It followed him down the ridge and through the woods
when he went to school. It was with him while he endured
the dread monotone of the long, bare schoolhouse. It was
with him when he crossed the bull pasture on the way home.
"You can grow, you can grow," it sang. "What if you can-not do everything you want to do just now? Some day,
some day." And especially when he mounted the long ridge
below his own house was the song singing. He must say
hello to his mother and get Rowdy, and race over the hills
and hear this promise of strength. Next summer he would
be eight years old, and the next summer after that he would
be nine.
He stood on the high point overlooking Mr. Burrell's
woods and shouted, "Ho-o-o! Ho-o-o-o!" with a sense of
complete adequacy. And nothing negative answered him
back.
The sun began to shorten its journey after school was dis-
missed at four o'clock. The woods began to feel cooler, too,
and the occasional resistant green leaf that clung to a peach
tree looked out of place. It was necessary to hasten more
than ever if one were to have time to race over the fields
and enjoy the song that sang itself.
One evening he hurried exuberantly through the orchard.
He thought he had never made better time through the
woods and up the ridge. He saw Dr. Seabright's team.
That was good; that was better than having the doctor come
too early so much of the time. He rushed into the kitchen.
His mother was not there. Dr. Seabright must be giving
her directions for some new medicine.
He pushed open the blue batten door into the front room,
but stopped in astonishment. Round his grandfather's bed
stood eight or ten people. Some of them glanced toward
him, but no one spoke. He had never felt a silence like this
one; only the uneven tock-tick of the clock on the shelf
just above his head. Then he thought he heard his grand-
father say something.
He tiptoed over, and slipped into the group where there
was a little space. Somebody put a friendly arm round his
shoulders. His grandfather lay quietly in his bed with one
hand out on the cover. His face seemed all forehead andbrows and nose and ears against the background of white
pillow.
The fingers of the hand on the cover twitched, his lips
moved feebly, and then in a voice not quite out of hearing
he called without moving his mouth much: "Just look at
them! That woods is full of them. They're moving out
into the field to form. Watch! It's going to be bayonets!
...
...
Sudie-I say we must save the young one No,
Pewee-that's as good a roof-as you'll find in any mine
...
...
Val-oh-h, they
Luke-nothing wrong with him. My eyes-oh,
they're all right, but it's dark in here

..
And now-
His voice faded out of hearing, his throat rattled. The
arm round Giles's shoulder turned him away and led him
into the kitchen. It was a firm, masculine arm.
"No need to see more of the implacable enemy than's
necessary," Dr. Seabright said, directing Giles's steps on
through the kitchen and out of doors. "Better see what's
become of your dog."
Giles did not understand fully what Doc said. Yet there
was no doubt in his mind: he knew that his grandfather
must be dying. Everything on the earth seemed suddenly to
shrink away from him. In a fearful detachment he walked
toward the stable, Rowdy obliviously racing ahead. He
tried to look away and see something, but things had lost
their rounded liveliness, their nebulous edges of light, and
lay silent and remote and finished in the literalness of eve-
ning.
He picked up a large pebble and hurled it into the lower
orchard. It struck a chicken coop with a faint, impotent
rap. He turned and walked to the hitching-rack and looked
at Doc's horses. But the horses stood glum in the cool air
and offered no interest.
Aimlessly he wandered back toward the house. Mrs.Burrell bustled up from over in her direction. Just as she
entered the side yard, Dr. Seabright came out at the kitchen
door.
"How-? Has-?" she began.
"Just now," Dr. Seabright answered, and she did not
need to complete her awkward beginnings.
The doctor walked out and dropped his solid-looking little
medicine case in the front of the buckboard with a careless
swing, and then went back into the house.
In the presence of the august visitor just now come, Giles
felt utterly inconsequential. His strongest allies moved
about as if he did not exist.
He pushed the kitchen door open very slightly and
squeezed in. One of his mother's sisters whom he had seen
only a few times came out of the little bedroom off the
kitchen, wiping her red, wet eyes with an embroidered hand-
kerchief.
"Where's Mother, Aunt Leonore?" he asked, in subdued
gentility.
"In there," she answered, her voice trembling.
He opened the door and stepped into the little room. His
mother sat on the edge of the bed and wept as if her heart
were breaking. She reached out and pulled him up beside
her, but could not restrain her sobbing enough to say a
comforting word. He was much more overwhelmed than
he had been at his grandfather's bedside. He had never
seen his mother in tears. Always she had been serene and
rosy and hopeful.
•
"Did you know..." she began between sobs
Before she could gain enough self-command to complete
the question, he answered with a dry, unyielding tongue,
"Yes."
She hugged him close and kissed him, and her tears ran
down across his face wherever her eyelashes touched.
"He can never speak to us again."She broke down in fresh sobbing.
Giles wanted to be helpful. It would not do to have his
mother go on like this.
"Won't he ever, Mother?" he asked consolingly.
She dabbed her eyes with her handkerchief; kissed him a
dozen times.
"Won't he?" He tried to make his voice like his moth-
er's when she comforted him.
"Some say yes, sweetheart, and some say no."
He made a greater effort: "But somebody could tell us if
we asked."
She kissed him again and again, but said nothing.
He tried to express his wisdom and his confidence. "I'll
bet Doc knows."
She did not answer even this suggestion except by draw-
ing him closer and kissing him again.
"He was so good," she said at last, and broke into sob-
bing once more.
"Always thinking about some of the rest of us and never
about himself. And he had to work so hard-all his life
digging coal-and then be blind when he might have rested."
Giles had always believed that his grandfather was a
great man, but now he felt that he must have been ten times
as great as he had ever thought.
"I'm going to be a coal digger myself like Grandpoppy,"
he announced in a voice designed to be heartening.
In fright she held him out at arm's length.
"Oh, my precious, no, no! You must never think of that.
Why, that would be the most terrible thing our boy could
do. You must never-why-why, if you were to do that,
you would have to stay here all your life."
She wept all the more unrestrainedly, called him her only
darling, and told him that staying there was the last thing
imaginable that his grandfather would have wished him to
do. Somewhere there was a greater and more beautifulworld than one could ever hope to find in Wiggam's Glory.
"We wanted to get out and find it ourselves, your father
and me, but we couldn't. That's why your father loses
himself in a brown study so much, and is so-so blue. We
couldn't. And you must."
It was all mystifying enough. And there was such a
sickening swirl in the house that Giles could not think things
out. Women neighbors some of whom he had never seen
before came in every few minutes and wept with his
mother, and men neighbors were busy helping his father
with what they called the arrangements.
Late at night when he was too baffled and too drowsy to
keep going longer, Mrs. Burrell took him across the field
and out the road to her house.
She led him to a great plastered and papered room up-
stairs where windows reached down almost to the floor,
turned back the covers on a bed just half as wide as the big
one he slept in at home, and told him that this was where
he was to spend two or three nights.
It was all as upsetting as the seethings and roarings re-
counted in the big book on the clock-shelf. And Mrs. Bur-
rell's spool bed felt very soft and unsteady compared with
his own firm straw-tick at home. So the best he could do
was to cry without making any noise and wonder if any-
thing else would happen before he got up in the morning.I
CHAPTER VI
N THE battle between Giles's anguish and his pushing
young spirit, his young spirit soon came off victorious.
Within an hour or two after he had returned from
Green Valley where he saw his grandfather buried with ap-
propriate Grand Army ceremonies beside Grandmother Su-
die, he slipped away from the house and raced with Rowdy
across the field toward Mr. Burrell's woods. A rabbit scut-
tled from the running-briars, and Giles shouted. It was, to
be sure, not a resounding shout of victory. There was still
a hardness in the world. But before the end of the week
most of this was gone; and soon winter came and dispelled
the little that remained.
There was something businesslike about winter which
simplified life. In winter one moved directly and without
hesitation. In winter not even going to bed and getting up
were unnecessarily prolonged. Fine snow sifted through
the lap-shingle roof and settled like sand on the bed covers.
The procedure was to slip under the covers, make a warm
place, and go to sleep without trying to peer into the future
of the cosmos; and in the morning leap quickly from bed,
slip into a part of one's simple outfit of day clothing, and
hustle down to the roaring fireplace to put on stockings and
high-topped boots.
Winter brought other simplifications, too. After the last
day of school before Christmas when the teacher customa-
rily gave every pupil present a bag of candy-the size of the
bag was usually the measure of his pedagogical fitness-
the gang from No. 5 Company Row virtually ceased to avail
themselves of the opportunities provided by the state forintellectual enlargement. There were rabbits to be trapped,
there were frozen apples to be found in orchards not too
near people's houses, there were stray chickens to be caught
in the snow out of sight of farmers' hen-houses. Such major
concerns put school out of mind except on occasional un-
promising days. Then by the first of February, boots and
shoes in the Row were beginning to give up their integrity,
and this fact constituted an unanswerable argument for the
complete abandonment of all systematic educational ac-
tivities.
As a result, Giles found that he could use for original ex-
plorations all the energies he had been obliged to employ in
field strategy. When the bull and his cows had broken a
path through the snow drifts just over the ridge in the hill
pasture, Giles could go over the hill to school and marvel at
what might be taking place at the tremendous brick house
on the flattened-out ridge. Old Genevieve had a mighty
reputation. No less a person than Dr. Seabright had said
that she feared neither God, man, nor devil. Giles hoped
he might have a nearer view of her.
Her house cried aloud with mystery. It was a great brick
rectangle fronting the east, with a wooden L running back
from the south end westward. Across the entire front, and
three-fourths the way back along the L, stretched a wide
porch painted white. On all sides the grounds were spa-
cious; and where the long front walk came down to the lane
stood two untrimmed evergreen trees. Off to the northwest
a convenient distance beyond the house was a white barn
around which Giles saw the cattle that belonged in the east
pastures. Beyond it some distance was the great bank-barn
where she kept farm implements, and stores of grain, the
bull and other cattle from the west pastures, and the half-
dozen draft horses that were used wherever there was need.
With one corner stretching down into the hollow and theedge of the woods, there was a deer park fenced with pickets
eight or nine feet high.
A woman looking after all that! What kind must she be?
If he should meet her in the field-her field-some day, or
in the quiet of the woods-her woods, too-just how scared
would he be?
School one morning helped him toward an answer. The
weather, the teacher reported, was the coldest of the season.
The depleted ranks of learners were gathered close about
the stove. While the teacher was explaining a difficult word
in the poem, "The lilacs are in blossom, the cherry-flowers
are white," somebody rapped at the door. Everybody
jumped, and then everybody rustled in excitement at the
pleasant thought of having the routine of the day broken.
The teacher himself was a little excited, for a knock at the
door usually meant an irate parent, and on occasion meant
a fight.
With interest expressed in his face, he walked to the door
and swung it open.
"Why," he said in a sudden great effort to be at his pleas-
antest, "how do you do, Miss-Crosby. Won't you step in
out of the cold?"
"Yes, I will."
Giles saw a strapping woman in gray-black dress, gray-
black shawl, and black fascinator, step in at the door. Her
cheeks were red, her nose was redder than her cheeks, and
her dark eyes shot glances in every direction as if the owner
of them were some magnificent sorceress. She hesitated be-
fore she came nearer the stove and the pupils.
"Is there is there something I can do for you?" the
teacher asked.
"Yes, there is. That's what I'm here for." Her voice
was electric with rage. "You can stop your damned schol-
ars from coming up my lane and throwing rocks against thefence to plague my dog. You ought to be able to manage
your own school."
"Why-why, of course. Of course, I shall be glad to-
to do whatever may be necessary."
"Well, you'd better."
The teacher's alacrity was great. "I only wish you had
let me know sooner." He was wondering just what he could
do if she were to attempt administering punishment to him
as the official representative of the pupils.
"I shall be only too happy to coöperate. And if at any
time you will be good enough to let me have the name of
any specific boy, I shall see that he is duly punished."
"All right," she said, in a voice high-pitched and com-
manding, "you can begin, then, this morning. I can't tell
you his name, but-" She cast her eye over the school.
"But there he is. He's the one that did it last night. That
little red-headed devil over there with his back to the
stove."
Without daring to move more than their eyes, the school
looked toward Sandy Tharp. Sandy flushed, glanced help-
lessly at the teacher, and then fixed his gaze on some spot
on the top of a desk a few feet ahead of him.
"Very well, I shall attend to the matter at once, Miss
Crosby," the teacher assured her.
"Well, yes, if you don't want some of them torn limb
from limb. He'll do it, if they plague him with rocks till
he jumps the fence. And I'll be God-damned if I'll be re-
sponsible for what he does."
Before the quick shock of her animated speech had lost
its force upon the school, she whirled and was out of the
room.
When it was evident that she was safely gone, some of
the big boys near the door snickered maturely to one an-
other. Her dress was short-almost up to her shoe tops-for purposes of unencumbered movement over the farm, and
the boys saw her gray stockings when she whirled to go.
Giles had never seen a woman who exhibited such an
abundance of malevolent energy. He thought the devil him-
self-or the devil's wife-must look like that.
When school was out he went discreetly down the road
with the handful who still came from the No. 5 Row. He
was a bit sorry when he had to turn into his own less trav-
eled way, although not one member of the gang was there
to threaten him.
At the supper table, in the presence of two strong and
sympathetic allies, he reported Genevieve's visit with fidel-
ity and dramatic effect.
When all three had subsided into composure after his
recital, he asked: "How does old Genevieve happen to be
there? That's what I'd like to know."
"Lots of people would like to know that,” his father
answered.
"Who's her father and mother, or didn't she ever have
any?"
"That's what everybody knows less about than anything
else. When old Curtis Burlingame wanted to sell out and
go west, she came along and handed him over a bag of
twenty-dollar gold pieces and hundred dollar bills, and took
the farm."
"Everything that's over there?"
"No, not quite. She has added some land and a barn or
two."
"Gee, what a lot of money! A bag full!"
"She's made it all back, and then some. Lends money
now to anybody if she likes them."
Giles was less impressed by the magnificence of her hold-
ings than by the power and mystery of her person. She
just walked into the schoolroom as if she were more than
equal to all the people there. She could have chased themout into the cold, the teacher included, if she had so willed.
His persistent inquiries throughout the evening set his
father and mother to talking after he had gone to bed.
"Pewee Dunkle says he heard," Luke explained while the
full grate of coals made the room cheerful, "that Genevieve
got tangled up with some man down in Virginia and he
gave her enough money to buy a farm with if she'd clear
out and let him stay."
"Well, Pewee would be sure to hear the worst side of it,
wouldn't he? What I heard from Mrs. Burrell was that
Genevieve was once a very beautiful girl-a perfect belle,
Mrs. Burrell said and belonged to a wealthy family, and
when the man she was about to marry was thrown from a
horse and killed, she had to get away from it all, and came
up here. And now she's working herself to death, trying
to forget it. That's much more likely than Pewee's story."
Luke found himself enjoying the gossip. "Of course," he
said, "both of them may be wrong. That bow-legged drum-
mer that comes to Culbertson from Centerville says he
heard that she's a man-hater. And you know she won't let
any of her hired men sleep in her house."
"No, I don't know any such thing," Val protested, catch-
ing him up with his own logic. "All I know is that that's
what they say. And they may be wrong!"
"Well, let it go at that. But this bow-legged drummer
says he heard she made her money wherever she used to
live by getting into correspondence with men through a mat-
rimonial bureau, having them come all prepared to marry
her, and then murdering them and keeping their money.
He wouldn't be surprised if she still does the same thing
over on the hill. That red-mouthed dog never lets anybody
come inside the grounds, and the drummer says that that's
to keep anybody from getting into the big garden behind
the house and finding out where she has planted her lovers
after she had made away with them."Through the cracks in the floor that served also as ceil-
ing, Giles could occasionally hear the name Genevieve. His
imagination was active, and for a long time this name was
more potent than sleep. Nor would Genevieve stay away
when he slept. In a dream he saw her rolling along the
road like a great bear, and when she came up to him, she
lifted her hatchet as if to cleave him in twain.
He watched for her the next morning in the woods. But
he saw only a few winter birds, a hungry-looking hawk, and
thousands of rabbit tracks in the white snow. She came no
more to the schoolhouse, and Giles eventually became en-
grossed once again in preparations for combat-in turning
an adversary over when the adversary happened to be on
top, in making a quick movement that would convert a dog-
fall into an undisputed victory. One day a young Irishman
brought a set of boxing gloves which he personally had con-
structed, and after the big boys had boxed until noses were
bloody and everybody was in a mood for a free-for-all fight,
they settled everything amicably by inviting the small boys
to put on the gloves and hammer each other. Giles for the
second time in his life got a bloody nose, but this time it
was, according to the big boys who watched, in the course
of a most creditable encounter. Giles wished the winter
term were not coming to an end.
His regret was all the more profound when the summer
term opened on the third Monday in April and a new
teacher stood by the desk to greet the pupils. She was
middle-aged, Giles thought-she was twenty-eight-and she
announced when she called the school to order that she
hoped to employ new principles in carrying on her educa-
tional duties. For one thing, there were to be no expres-
sions of the combative spirit. There was to be no fight-
ing; there was to be no wrestling, since wrestling served
only to induce fights. She herself meant to apply this prin-
ciple to her own conduct. There would be no corporal pun-ishment. It was a relic of barbarism. She meant to appeal
to their better natures, and she knew they would respond
joyously. If a boy did not she assumed that the girls
would make no trouble-she would try to impress upon his
mind the enormity of his trespass simply by keeping him for
an hour of quiet study after school was over for everybody
else. That was the new spirit in education.
For some days the principle was applied with success.
The big boys never attended the summer term, and such
small ones as came were for two weeks caught up by the
new order as if they were on a crusade. Then one day at
noon some of them went over by the creek behind a screen
of willows and sycamores to explore a newly washed-out
swimming hole, and there they wrestled and banged each
other to the content of their strong hearts. When they
heard the faint, far-off tinkle of the teacher's bell, and
raced across the open field to avoid being too late, the
combative spirit still lingered, and Slim Dunkle, thus far
Pewee's youngest, said to Giles banteringly, "That last time
was a dog-fall. You never put me down."
"Did too," Giles responded in sweaty exuberance.
They sparred as they ran, paused long enough to seize
each other at arm's hold, and then hurried on.
They did not suppose the teacher would observe any such
minor expression of the older spirit. But when the work
of the day was over and the desks had been put in order
preliminary to dismissal according to the new method of
character development, the teacher stood at her place and
announced: "Giles and Charles will please remain in their
seats."
Giles and Slim looked across at each other in amazement.
There was no opportunity for friendly consultation. They
could only smile a feeble, despairing smile in admission of
the fact that she was slyer than they had thought.
She stood pleasantly-too pleasantly-at the door untilall the other pupils were far enough away not to run back
for anything forgotten.
"Now," she announced, "we shall be unmolested." She
smiled to them and walked cheerfully back to her desk.
Slim wished she would not be so good-humored. He
wanted something to get mad about, to have a grievance for.
Her happy manner did not comport with his notion of ad-
ministering punishment at all.
And now she was going to be pleasant about something
else, too. "I have a whole blackboard full of problems to
copy for C Arithmetic, and when I have finished doing that,
I have a most interesting book to read. Perhaps you will
wish to prepare all of your lessons for to-morrow. Or, if
you think that would leave you nothing to do then, why,
here is a book for each of you to read. We can all read
together."
To Slim she carried a boy's Life of George Washington;
to Giles an illustrated Story of Daniel Boone.
Giles never knew before that a schoolroom could be so
quiet. In truth, a sudden quiet seemed to have overtaken
the entire world. Apparently the last team had passed along
the road, the last friendly rooster had crowed in the school-
yard, the last cow had bawled. At the moment, he would
have welcomed the angriest bellowing of Genevieve's east
bull. Instead, there was only the buzzing of a big green
fly, accompanied by the scratch-scratch of the teacher's
chalk as she covered the blackboard.
Some tenseness of the atmosphere made a book unin-
viting. Giles preferred to look. Through the window across
the row of big seats he could see many things. He was not
tall enough to see along the road, which ran level, but he
could see the hillside above it, and a ridge on Genevieve's
west farm, and still farther westward a diminutive man car-
rying something on his shoulder as he followed a hill path.
And beyond the farthest hill he could see clouds. Theystood up in soft-looking heaps, like straw piles, but silvery
at the top and dark at the bottom. Some of them must have
rain in them. If his father were here, he could tell whether
the rain would go north. Probably it would. It was north
of the sun and the sun was now in the west. Still he wished
his father were present to tell him for a certainty. He did
not like soft, bulging clouds that were half black.
Miss Brubaker finished her copying. She looked at her
watch. The lesson in character-education must go on, evi-
dently.
She brushed the white dust from her hands and the front
of her dress, pushed her chair over by the east window near
her desk, rested her feet on the end of the recitation bench,
and read.
For a time she cast an occasional glance over her shoulder
to see that the subjects of the new education were still there.
But the book grew in interest. She quit looking.
Giles and Slim tried to communicate by making high and
wide and silent motions with the mouth. When they wear-
ied of this, Giles decided that, after all, he had better look
into his Daniel Boone. But the window was more interest-
ing. At a more convenient season he would read about this
man who wore a bearskin cap.
What he most wanted was to get into the open air. If he
should tiptoe to the shelves, grab his bucket and cap and
rush away, could she catch him? He thought not, in that
dress.
But he would have to come back to school and face her.
What turn might her character-education be expected to
take then? It might be a turn for the worse. He watched
the cloud. How slowly it changed its outlines. Yet when
he shut one eye and measured its height along the sash in
the window, how far it had come! It seemed to be coming
faster, too. It stood right up beside the sun but did not
cover it, except one edge.He looked listlessly at some of the pictures in the Daniel
Boone, but they were no more interesting than the print.
He closed the book and looked at the cloud again. What
had happened to it? It stood high in the heavens. The
point of it would soon be overhead.
He tried to communicate with Slim. While he did so,
there was the faintest rumble of thunder back over the hills.
He held his breath and listened. Had Slim heard? Slim
went in another direction, but he had almost as far to go.
There! There it was again! No doubt could remain in
his mind now. The big world outside, in which he had been
absorbed only a few minutes before, had suddenly become
the thing from which he most wanted to escape.
Slim looked in his direction anxiously. Giles pointed to-
ward the window and the cloud. Slim nodded in affirma-
tion. The sun had slowly disappeared behind the southern
edge of the wall of black.
There was a heavier rumble.
What were they to do? The teacher was lost in her ro-
mantic book. She knew not whether it was daylight or
lamplight.
The front of the big cloud had now become many little
ones. They were near, and they were hurrying.
There was a thin flash against the black, and then a
heavier rumble still.
"Teacher! Miss Brubaker!" Giles called, correcting
himself. "It's going to storm. I've got to go home."
She closed her book over her thumb, whirled round, and
glanced into the darkened west.
"Why, so it is."
She looked at her watch on the top of the desk.
"Oh, goodness, I didn't mean to keep you this long. But
I got interested in what I was reading. I must go, too."
Giles was not interested in her going. She had only a
quarter of a mile to go, and he had a full mile and a half.He was out of the schoolhouse in a breath. The clouds
were racing toward him, soft-looking white tumbling ahead
of the leaden black.
Up the hill he raced, his ear alert for thunder. When he
was on the high ridge, the clouds seemed to be almost within
reach above his head. The white, foggy advance guard only
accentuated the blackness of the entire western horizon. He
must get through the woods. Maybe he might get as far as
the pot-shop.
Down the hill he ran, terror swirling in pursuit. He never
stopped for a full breath until he was across the bottom and
up by the road. The thunder was coming in sweeping rolls.
Occasionally there was a deafening crash that almost stopped
his heart.
He thought he felt a raindrop on his cheek. He thought
he felt another. He heard some drops on the quiet, lux-
uriant leaves of May. But try as he might, he could go no
faster.
The drops began to patter on the leaves steadily. Then
he heard the wind in the trees over toward Genevieve's house
on the ridge, and then right over his head and all round
him.
The rain burst upon the woods with a roar that obscured
the stirrings of the wind. In a moment he would be sopping
wet if he could not escape the deluge that poured down
through the trees.
The big leaning oak with the cave-like hollow at its base!
Yes!
He darted in, and was at once as if he were in a house-
except for the floor, which consisted of dry, rotten wood
softer than a carpet.
But after the briefest moment of thought about his com-
fortable dryness, the storm claimed his attention again. The
water trickled, then poured, off the under side of the tree at
the top edge of the hollow right beyond his nose, but didnot come inside. Still it might, for the roar of the rain in-
creased.
Suddenly from nowhere there was a blinding light in front
of his eyes and a crash that deafened him. In the darkness
brought by the low-hanging cloud, the flash shone almost as
if it had come in the night. Before he could take a breath,
splinters and bark and miscellaneous fragments were tum-
bling down from the steeple of a white-oak that stood just
below the road. Then he thought he heard some one calling.
His father, maybe! Yes, wouldn't his father wonder why
he was so late, and start after him?
He listened, intent. Before he was deafened by another
near clap, he heard the voice again.
But that could not be his father. It was a shriek, a
screech.
And then he saw.
"Damn you!" Genevieve screamed.
Her face was bursting with rage, her heavy hair was loose
over her neck, in one hand she carried her wet sunbonnet,
and in the other her hatchet. The dog kept his head close
to her wet skirts in an effort to shield himself from the
storm.
Another flash of fire darted down almost at her nose.
"Damn you!" she shrieked at the top of her voice, and
waved her arms wildly aloft. "Slaughtering a woman's
timber!"
A bolt crashed into the oak steeple just above them on
the hill, where the buzzards and the owls were accustomed
to sit, and there was a shower of bark, splinters, and fragile
dead limbs.
She lost herself in a spasm of shrieks and curses.
Giles thought that once she looked directly at him. He
expected her to cleave him with her hatchet for being in
her woods. But the storm in its slow progress shot down abolt quite over the hill in the edge of the woods, and she
pushed on, screaming her defiance at heaven.
Giles wanted to get away. He was ready to take the storm
in preference to Genevieve. But he was afraid she might
look back and see him. Terror-stricken, he peeped past the
edge of his retreat and watched her as she made her fiery
march homeward.
What did a little water matter? Giles ventured out of
his haven and stepped down over the oozy softness of dead
leaves into the road. He felt the mud squeeze up between
his toes, and wished it were not necessary to leave such dis-
tinct tracks.
At the edge of the woods he met his father.
"Your mother was getting worried," his father said, glad
enough to subordinate his own great anxiety. "So I thought
I'd better come along and see why you were not home ahead
of the storm."
"She kept us in," Giles explained, pleased to be frank with
somebody about something. "Slim and me rastled, and she
saw us."CHAPTER VII
W
ASN'T there a way of going and coming without
passing through Genevieve's woods? In such a
state of inquiry he started to school the next
morning. He went undisturbed down the ridge, down the
wagon track of a road. Couldn't he escape Genevieve if he
should encounter her openly? He could run pretty fast.
He was almost eight years old. He might even escape the
dog. He could climb a tree and the dog could not. Rather
bravely he began to pick out trees that he could scale under
pressure. That maple had a convenient crook in its body,
and that beech had limbs all the way to the ground-almost.
He seemed destined, anyhow, to pass through the great shad-
owland this morning without incident. He would have done
so had not a big bushy-tailed fox squirrel crashed out of
nowhere into some leaves by the roadside and caused his
hair to stand on end in his plaid cap.
His sense of adequacy, however, could not withstand the
stuffy, dull atmosphere of school. With his mind a pris-
oner, the many possibilities of meeting Genevieve alone in
the woods began to beat in upon his contemplation. Espe-
cially in the dead, smelly hour following the afternoon recess,
did he feel uncertain. He might escape her and still not
escape the dog. He might not be able to reach a tree soon
enough.
When he left the schoolhouse he felt that he might never
see it again. When he reached the edge of the woods his heart
was thumping beyond control. In dread he peered along
the road under the arch of dark, silent green. It would be
just like her to be over there now, looking at what the stormhad done. And she would not be in pleasant temper. No,
he must not venture.
Yet what else was there to do? Cautiously, he walked on a
little. Beyond the first ravine that ran down to the road on
his right, he stopped and considered the path which followed
the hogback of a ridge above the ravine. Where might this
path come out? Would it be better to try it or the road?
It seemed to go in the general direction of home, yet it
seemed also to cut across the corner of the woods. It might
come out in a field where the way would be open.
He turned into the path. He followed it up over the
gravelly steps made by protruding beech roots along the
steep bank. And when he was short of breath, he stopped
by a lofty white-oak in an open space and turned to look.
Back across the ravine and the corner of the woods beyond,
he could see his road all its length down to the main thor-
oughfare, and the railroad at the farther edge of the valley.
Beyond the railroad, three box-car loads of Italians were
working like an army of dark ants on the hillside and at
piles of bright timbers where the switch to a new mine was
to cut through the edge of the hill and across a wide, marshy
hollow. He could see everything. He was almost on top
of some of the trees that stood in the deep ravine below him.
And he had not met her!
He must be going. He turned and pushed on along the
path, careful not to bang his dinner bucket against anything
and make an unseemly noise. Soon he saw the blue light
of the sky straight ahead through the trees. Then he came
out into a pasture. As he had divined, the path turned
somewhat to the left and homeward. He walked along in
freedom, the shadow of the woods quite below him, and in
the other three directions, open field.
The path rose slowly toward a mound-like hilltop. At the
foot of this round hill the path branched. On the left it
bore abruptly off and seemed to go back into the woods; onthe right, it went more nearly straight ahead past the hill.
He kept to the right.
Soon he saw that he must have taken the wrong turn.
The path swung off sharply to the right, and then a distance
ahead brought up at somebody's back gate where a white
house stood by the north-and-south road.
He did not like the thought of going back and following
the path into the woods. It would be safer, he believed,
to go on to the house and ask how to get home.
As he came up to the orchard he was suddenly seized with
the fear that a dog might bowse out upon him. But before
any appeared, a slender, cheerful voice under a spreading
apple tree not yet entirely through blooming called, "Hello
there!"
"Hello-o-o!" he replied, prolonging his greeting in amaze-
ment at seeing anyone he knew.
So this was where Hope lived! His mind found the fact
hard to accept. He had not been thinking of her.
"I didn't know you lived here."
"Didn't you?"
"No."
"Well, you see I do."
She looked at him with friendliness in her bright blue
eyes. She was more slender than he was, and no taller; but
in her voice there was something that might be years older.
He struggled to think of something to say on short notice.
"You haven't been at school for a long time."
"No, I haven't been very well."
Her grandmother happened by. "I thought I heard Hope
talking to herself," she said to Giles, agreeably. She seemed
relieved when she found him there, but wondered where he
had come from.
"Aren't you lost?"
"Yes," Giles answered honestly. Then he offered the ex-
planation: "I followed the path, and it brought me here.""Why, bless my soul, you took the wrong turn out by the
little hill. If you go to the left, the path takes you right
along the upper edge of the woods and then out onto the
ridge that goes clear over to where you and the Burrells
live. You can see your house 'way across, just as soon as
you get round the little hill."
She looked at his slender body. "But ain't you hungry,
child?"
He hesitated. She beamed upon him with grandmotherly
beneficence.
"'Course you are. Boys are always hungry."
She moved as if to turn away. "Now you just wait a
minute. Your ma won't mind. I know her a little bit-
and I knowed your Grandpa Boyd from the time he was
knee-high to a toad-just as high as I was! You can eat a
piece of nice warm raisin pie while Hope drinks her glass
of milk."
Before Giles could say anything, she was off to the
kitchen.
"How do you go to school when you go?" he asked Hope.
"Down that road there till I come to the valley road."
"Why don't you take this path?"
"I don't have anybody to go with when I go that way.
I'd have to go through the corner of the woods, too."
"I'm going by the path every day after this. I like it
better than the road."
"I'd not mind going with you," she said, "but-but-"
"But what?"
"Oh, I don't know as I'd better."
"Why?"
She did not answer.
"But why?" Giles insisted.
"Oh, because."
"Because what?"
"Well, if you have to know, it's because you're outside thepale. Father Connolly says the faithful are just as well off
if they don't spend all their time with somebody outside the
pale."
"Outside the pale-what does that mean? I never heard
of that."
"It means that you're a heretic."
"What's that? I never heard of that, either."
But since Mrs. Fealty came out at the moment with a tall
glass of milk and a great quarter of raisin pie, the question
of faiths was left unclarified.
Mrs. Fealty asked Giles about school, and pottery, and
whether his mother had many little chickens, and how many
more she expected to have the hens hatch out. Before he
went his way he remembered to thank her for the pie. And
he told Hope that she must come to school again.
"Yes, when she's a little stronger she'll come," her grand-
mother assured him. "Just as soon as she gets a little
stronger."
Giles looked back once as he rounded the hilltop. But
he was thinking chiefly about home. He wondered how late
he was this time.
His own house! There it was, a half-mile away, a smudge
of a little log house, seen from this distance, with a bright,
whitewashed fence under the cherry trees.
He ran, he shouted, he threw his dinner bucket high in the
air and did not mind if he failed to catch it when it came
down. He would never have to follow the road through the
murky woods again unless he liked.
At home he made use of artistic selection in telling his
story. He had followed the path just to see if he could find
a new way home. It was a little farther, to be sure, but it
was a pleasant way. And he had taken the wrong turn, and
Mrs. Fealty had given him the biggest piece of raisin pie he
had ever seen, with thick raisins, too, and that was about all
there was to tell. He scarcely wanted any supper at all,though he guessed he might as well sit down at the table,
since his mother had his place ready.
When there was opportunity, his father asked: "How's
the little girl?"
So they knew about her. "Why, she'll have to wait till
she's stronger before she can go to school again."
He attached no special significance to his words, but he
saw his father glance toward his mother. "They're all gone
now except the little girl and Denis. And my guess is that
she won't last the summer through."
What was his father saying? "Wouldn't last the summer
through?" That meant she was going to die? Giles's hot
imagination leaped headlong from rapture over his way of
circumventing Genevieve to despair at the thought of Hope's
dying. Why hadn't he heard them say something before?
They seemed to know a lot of things which they never told
him. Well, so far as that was concerned, he knew some
things that he never told them. But that consolation did
not reduce the magnitude of the essential fact. He knew
that old people died, old people like his grandfather. That
was to be expected; that, from such talk as he gathered, was
what many of them lived for. But must little girls die?
And-it was too terrible to contemplate-if little girls, then
must little boys also?
Now, in truth, he did not want any supper. He pretended
to eat, but he could not. He crumbed his bread by his plate,
and that was proof positive that hunger did not gnaw tem-
pestuously.
His mother noticed. "I guess you did eat too much pie."
How little these elders all about him thought of the real
problems of life. Pie! Pie, nothing. Here was death con-
fronting him.
When he went to bed the prospect was still there directly
in front of his eyes. Hope! She was not yet grown up.
She was a girl, too, and could never do all the things hisgrandfather had done, even if she were big. And now she
was going to die without ever being anything more than a
little girl. In the darkness and the gauntness of his big
room he heard the Methodist revival choir singing tremu-
lously:
"Should the death angel knock at thy chamber
In the still watch of the night."
Not even the brightness of the next morning could dissi-
pate this mood; nor that of the next morning; nor the next.
He dared not ask anyone about Hope, yet he strained his
ear for news about her. He heard and saw the word "con-
sumption" everywhere.
Life was becoming sweeps of experience rather than stac-
cato successions of moods that had no ability to persist.
With only occasional intervals of freedom, he was under the
spell of this one all summer. His mother asked him from
time to time if he were not well. The teacher said he was
addicted to brown studies. And Mr. Burrell chaffingly ex-
pressed the opinion that he was afflicted with softness and
needed some hickory oil. He himself tried to apply curative
measures. With Rowdy for company, he sought to run
away from the smothering power. Down the long hill he
would race with such a rush of air round his ears that he
was sure the terror would be swirled out of his head. But
when he slowed up at the bottom of the hill beneath the
red-oaks, the terror was still with him.
One day when he and Rowdy were on one of their ram-
bles, Rowdy was bitten by a copperhead. The snake, as a
matter of fact, struck at Giles at one of his bare legs as
he ran wildly by-but missed him. Giles did not see the
snake, but Rowdy did-while it was extended full length on
the ground after it had reached so desperately. When
Rowdy stopped to investigate, the copperhead drew itself
back very modestly in a knot-like heap. Rowdy was ayoung, believing dog, and thrust his nose up to it inquir-
ingly; and it sank its fangs into his jaw.
He was at once a sick dog. He crept into the tiny white-
washed dog-house and refused to eat or to be comforted.
His neck puffed up to the size of two necks at least, and his
eyes grew tight and strained. Giles's father one night ad-
ministered the last known curative, and said that if he
should not be dead of the snake's poison the next morning,
they had better put a bullet through his head and relieve
him of his misery.
Giles wondered if it would do any good to pray-or to
have somebody else pray. His father spoke only of specific
material means, and Rowdy clearly enough seemed to be
beyond the reach of any of these. Might not one in such
an extremity appeal to somebody greater even than his
father or mother? He did not want to lose his dog. So
when he went upstairs to bed that night, he stood half-
disrobed in the hot room and promised up into the great
darkness which enveloped him that if Rowdy should not die,
he himself would-well, he could think of no special sin to
give up, but he would be the happiest boy that ever lived.
The next morning Rowdy's neck hung down flabbily like
that of one of Genevieve's cows. He was not so sick. He
wiggled his tail a little, and he drank some milk when Giles
placed it in front of him. Eventually he was a well dog
again. But when Giles was free of the fear for Rowdy, he
reviewed the incident fully and wondered what might have
happened if the snake had been successful in fixing itself
upon one of his bare legs. Would he have been as resistant,
as tough, as Rowdy? If he had not been, where might he
have ended?
Other fears preyed upon his receptive mind. He heard
his father reading in the newspaper to his mother one eve-
ning about the cholera in Russia. Thousands were dying.
The bodies of children were loaded into carts and hauledaway like so much garbage. The disease was sweeping
westward and would be at the seaports, and maybe in
America, before frost could come and arrest it.
He had amused himself much by reading in the news-
paper. Now he was afraid to open its pages, lest his eyes see
something about cholera. Yet each week he sat just inside
the front door when his father sat outside and read aloud
to his mother, and listened, his pulses quick and weak for
the report of progress the disease was making across Europe.
One evening his father read on the Home Page about
keeping one's health in the summer months. There had
been a great increase in deaths from an important disease
called appendicitis. It was better not to swallow cherry
pits and similar hard substances, and it was well not to put
off consulting a physician when the disease appeared. It
began with a pain in the side, usually, and unless it was
dealt with at once, by a surgeon—and sometimes even then
-it was fatal. It attacked people of all ages. His mother
clipped the article out and put it on the clock-shelf along
with other bits of valuable knowledge.
One day after he had sat for a time in the sweet-apple
tree, he felt very uncomfortable. When he investigated he
found that he had a pain in his side.
Now what should he do? Should he tell his mother at
once and have her send for Doc Seabright? But what if
Doc should cut him open and he should die anyway? If he
could put in the time, perhaps it would be better to wait till
the next day.
After breakfast the next morning while he felt a little ade-
quate and believing, he reached up on the clock-shelf for
the article. His heart pounded while he read, but he fin-
ished with a free breath of relief. His pain was on the
wrong side.
Here was a happy advantage. He felt so out from undera great burden that he smiled at the absurdity of his fear.
Then one day toward autumn, he heard his father read that
the cholera probably would not reach America after all.
Maybe he had been a fool. Maybe he had. He took to
reading the Centerville Gazette again. But one day as he
leafed through it he read in great black letters reaching a
third of the way across the page:
STOP!
CONSUMPTION CAN BE CURED
BUT YOU MUST BEGIN
IN TIME
HAVE YOU ANY OF THESE EARLY SYMPTOMS?
He read fearfully on: "Are you out of breath after you
run up a long flight of stairs? Do you ever have a hacking
cough? Do you ever break out in a cold sweat?"
And then after a long list of such questions, there was the
final one, "Have you any of these first symptoms?"
Giles put himself through an examination to see whether
he had or not. And he found that he had not only one, but
all of them every symptom in the list.
It couldn't be true. At times he felt an irresistible health.
Some of the symptoms were without significance. Yet al-
ways there was Hope. News about Hope filtered through
to him, and about Hope there could be no illusion. He over-
heard his father say to his mother that "lingering" consump-
tion was just as bad in the end as any other kind. Nobody
could brush aside the reality of her condition. Nobody tried.
One Saturday when the sugar maples were splotched with
scarlet, his mother said that she understood Hope Fealty
was not so well, and asked him if he did not wish to go along
to see her.
Giles could think of no good excuse for not going. Yet
he feared a painful ordeal."We must take her something," his mother said. And she
gathered a huge bouquet of chrysanthemums.
They started; and after Giles had chased Rowdy back
home, he carried the flowers. But when they came round
through the orchard in order to go in at Mrs. Fealty's front
door, he whispered, "Mother! You give them to her!"
His mother glanced at him and at once felt his suffering.
"All right, then. I'll give them to her."
He was faint with foreboding, yet he was not prepared
for what he was to encounter. Far off there in a chair of
pillows in front of the fireplace, he heard the frailest shadow
of a voice: "Yes, Grandmother, that helps. I feel better
now."
Grandmother Fealty took the flowers and thanked Val for
them-"I'll have to call you Val as long as I live, for that's
what I heard them call you when you was little, though I
didn't know you by sight much." Then she invited them
both to sit down on the other side of the fireplace.
"Here's Mrs. Dabney come to see you," she said cheerily
to Hope; "and Giles."
Hope turned her head ever so slightly, and Giles dared to
look toward her.
"Hello-Giles," she said, just above a whisper.
He wanted to say "Hello," but his mouth was dry, and
his tongue was stiff like a stick. So he only gazed at the
great blue eyes, the shrunken temples and luxuriant hair,
the bird-claws of little blue-white hands that lay on the
comfort spread over her lap. The comfort was a creamy
white, Giles noticed, and knotted with bright red yarn.
His mother and Mrs. Fealty talked about many matters,
and a widowed aunt of Hope's-an aunt by marriage-came
in from the kitchen and contributed a share.
How could they feel free to talk about chrysanthemums,
and fall-roses, and winter apples, and marriages, and the
price of materials for dresses, in the presence of such a dev-astating reality? Giles looked away through the front win-
dow, his eyes full of tears that he did not wish to acknowl-
edge, and heard the conversation as if it were in some other
world.
At last when his mother thought they had better be going,
he ventured to look again toward the fireplace. On one end
of the mantel stood a little crucifix, and above it, framed in
black and gold, was the Sacred Heart. These only added to
his suffocation.
"They are going," Mrs. Fealty announced to the little
figure limp against the pillows.
Hope opened her eyes and with an effort said, "Good-by-
Giles."
"Good-by," he said, and wanted to run.
He did, as soon as he was outside the door. While his
mother and Mrs. Fealty had a final whispering word on the
front porch, he rushed along the path past the big apple
tree and on out into the field, crying with choking sobs.
Lest his mother overtake him, he ran on and on, his sobs
hindering his breath, his tears blinding his eyes.
Up on the ridge by the hilltop, he turned and looked back.
His mother was coming with reassuring serenity along the
path, and he dried his tears and waited.
After a trip to church at Culbertson on Sunday, he went
to school as usual. But when the pupils from the East Road
reported that Hope Fealty had died that morning, and then
the lesson in the Fourth Reader turned out to be a poem
about school:
"I've wandered to the village, Tom, I've sat beneath the tree,
Upon the schoolhouse playground, that sheltered you and me;
But none were left to greet me, Tom; and few were left to know
Who played with us upon the green, some forty years ago "
he buried his face in his arms on the desk and cried, and
told the teacher that he was sick and must go home, andwent home through the woods, and ate his dinner with his
father and mother, and sauntered over and watched Mr.
Burrell all afternoon and helped him while he made cider
out of little red winter apples that were too knotty to bother
with in any other way.G
CHAPTER VIII
ILES wanted to put all thought of Hope out of
mind. He had dwelt on her departure until his
fearful and weary head longed for something else
to meditate upon. But for days he was obliged to listen to
neighborhood accounts of the funeral. Mrs. Burrell dropped
in to say that the little white casket covered with flowers was
the loveliest thing she had ever laid eyes on. She hoped it
would soon be the style to bury old people as well as chil-
dren in white caskets, for she'd be needing one pretty soon
now, she supposed, and that was the kind she'd like to be
put away in. Mrs. Fred Coombs left her seven children and
her sickly mother and came across the hills one afternoon
long enough to say that the grave over by the sugar maples
and evergreens was just too beautiful for words. She'd like
nothing better than to be buried in a nice quiet place like
that herself, and just sleep till she was ready to get up.
Pewee Dunkle had to come over and give his version, too.
He chatted with Luke and Val and confirmed the women's
strange notions of loveliness and beauty. He decided, he
told them, to go to the funeral with Sam Swisher. "Sam said
he'd see that this big new priest didn't eat me alive. And,
believe me, I needed protection. That old boy certainly did
give hell to the heretics-to everybody who had not accepted
the true faith as the little girl had. But say, the choir sung
the sweetest music I ever hope to hear. That's all I ask
for when I die-just voices a-singin' like that. But like as
not I'll end up some day in a fall of slate, and there won't
be time enough to listen to a damned note. The roof over
the entry in the new mine where I'm workin' now seemsawful dangerous. They say not, but that's the way it looks
to me from where I roost."
Giles was not displeased to hear that Hope's little march
had ended so gloriously. What for him was not to be under-
stood was why all the neighbors were looking for this or that
for their own funerals. He did not wish to take up the ques-
tion of his funeral at all. Let Hope sleep on, her white
slender hands folded, through unending years on the sunny
hillside. He wanted to live; and he wanted assurance that
he was to have the privilege. Something within him recoiled
at the slenderest thought of any kind of cessation.
Somewhat to his amazement, he found himself day after
day without symptoms. Health could not forever be sup-
pressed. The external world once again took on interest.
Snow came early and had to be talked about. Runners on
sleds had to be made smooth. Boys and girls in school were
beginning to treat him a little more considerately, and they
had to receive more of his thought. Some of them, too, after
a year and a half, were standing forth as unconfused indi-
viduals who possessed special abilities worthy of admiration.
There was Cross-eye Timson, for instance. He was not,
in fact, cross-eyed; he could only seem to be. When the
teacher walked to the rear of the room to help some big boy
or big girl with a problem in arithmetic, Cross-eye turned
slowly in his seat, his eyes in the general direction of the
teacher, but in appearance utterly unfocused. When an
image of the teacher intent over a desk drifted in at the
corner of one obscure-looking eye and made action seem
somewhat safe, Cross-eye swiftly put on a vaudeville act
that set the school in an uproar.
When the teacher straightened up to see what had caused
the disturbance, he saw many devilish young faces, but not
Cross-eye's. Cross-eye was deeply engrossed in his big
geography, and after the outburst, looked round in disgust
to see what everyone could be laughing at.Just as the teacher bent down to his work again, Cross-
eye ruffled his own hair savagely, as if he were trying to
frighten something out of it. If this wild gesture did not
come within the range of the teacher's vision, Cross-eye
would then rise in the aisle with one arm outstretched up-
ward and the points of his fingers close together as if he
were catching something that had flown from his hair.
Sweepingly he brought the something to his mouth and
cracked its little bones between his teeth until all his near
neighbors heard, and responded with an outburst. This
time when the teacher looked up, Cross-eye was laughing
at somebody with all the rest of the front half of the school.
While the teacher sharply requested silence, Cross-eye sat
with his feet carelessly in the aisle. As soon as he saw with
his neutral-looking eye that the teacher was once more
engaged, he lifted his hand casually and gave the under side
of his nose a little bump. His tongue popped out so far
that the point of it touched his nose. Just as casually he
gave his right ear a quick pull, and his tongue flashed to
that corner of his mouth; then to the left; and then when
he gave his Adam's apple a deft jerk, the tongue disappeared
like a rabbit in a warren. When, on occasion, the teacher
suspected him and brought him up to the green-topped desk
for examination, Cross-eye asked with such tremulous inno-
cence, "Why, what have I done?" that the pupils themselves
almost believed their eyes had been deceiving them.
Giles could not help admiring a boy of such distinguished
gifts. And Cross-eye encouraged this admiration. He was
ever looking for disciples who would try less successfully to
outwit the teacher. As for Giles, Cross-eye promised to
teach him tricks that would make reading lessons and
geography look silly.
Nor could Giles help admiring Sockey McPherson. Sockey
was two years older than he was, and commanded wide re-
spect for his much worldly knowledge. Giles had long notedthat others profited by Sockey's wisdom, but he little
dreamed that he was so soon to profit by it himself.
There was a girl in school, a blonde with fetching eyes,
quick speech, and lithe body, whom the big boys always
hung round with. Sometimes two or three minutes after the
teacher had called the school to order, she and some boy of
sixteen or seventeen would come in from just outside the
door and the boy's face would still be flushed at something
she had said to him.
Toward the end of the winter she stopped coming to
school. Not long after, when eight or ten boys were warm-
ing themselves in the late February sun on the coal house
roof, and were discussing things in general, Giles decided to
act like some of the bigger boys and ask a question or two.
In a flash of confidence when no one else was speaking he
made inquiry: "Say, will somebody tell me why Cora Barger
don't come to school any more?"
There was an outburst of shouts and laughter and whis-
tlings. It died down. Then it started all over again.
"Say, ain't that good? Ha, ha, ha, ha! 'Why don't she
come?' The best thing in this school for a year. Ha, ha,
ha, ha!"
Everybody seemed to know except Giles. If some of the
smaller boys did not, they made a bold pretense, which
served as well.
Giles felt red about the ears as long as they were on the
roof. When they saw the teacher at the door with the bell,
and climbed down, it was Sockey who befriended him.
"Wait," Sockey whispered simply.
They stayed behind the coal house while the others
went on.
"Didn't you hear about Cora?"
Sockey's blue eyes were sincere and generous, and Giles
was intent like a puppy about to learn a new trick.
"No-o-o!""Why, she's goin' to have a baby. Understand?"
"'Course I understand-now." Giles had watched cows.
Immediately, without further ceremonial, he and Sockey
were friends.
Sockey had noticed that the gang followed Giles. So one
day he spoke of that fact.
"Yes, but some day I'll lick one of them," Giles declared.
"See if I don't."
"But you must get good and ready first," Sockey coun-
seled. "If you don't lick him the first time, they'll be
harder on you than ever. Begin on Chuck Bowles."
"But how'll I know when I'm good and ready?"
That was a question. But Sockey was qualified to an-
swer it. He himself could put Chuck down without half
trying-every time. So the goal for Giles to work toward
was putting Sockey down.
"If you can put me down two times out of three, then you
might begin on him."
He taught Giles how to duck, how to smack hard on the
end of the nose, and how to step in close and poke a hard
fist right up inside the other fellow's arms and sock him
on the chin. "It makes their teeth crack when you sock
'em under the chin, and they see stars. I saw 'em once.
Bowser Malloy, that big boy up at the priest's house, was
teachin' me."
What a transformation school had undergone! It was
now a place of serious activity and manly aspiration. And
then the winter term had to draw to a close and leave Giles
with only his bare hilltops and the March wind for com-
pany. How could he ever manage to put in the time until
the summer term opened on the third Monday in April?
In search of something yet undiscovered, he ran through
the muddy fields until his mother declared he did nothing
except carry in mud for her to sweep up. In these raw days
with so little of sustenance in them, he watched the methodsof a great red-tailed hawk and tried to make himself believe
the hawk was an eagle. He rolled marbles along the stripes
in the front-room carpet, and wished he had somebody to
play with. He lay on the bed in the little room off the
kitchen and chewed dried apples and read his next term's
lessons-somewhat carelessly and listened to the wind.
Sometimes the wind excited him. It shouted to him to
leap up and march forth and do great things. But at that
time of the year there was nothing to do. It was neither
winter nor spring.
Sometimes the wind made him melancholy. He did not
like to hear it howl and cry and whistle so round the corner
of the house. It was then that he could not help thinking
of Hope. The wind was whistling through the maple trees
and evergreens-the same wind. She was a little girl, no
bigger than he was now. Yet she had died. The wind made
him remember all that.
One day after he had looked in vain along the hollow
fence rails of the stake-and-rider fence for the first blue-
bird, he came to the house and sat down in front of the fire.
He felt tired-almost dizzy. He had never felt like that
before, he thought. He coughed a little huskily; according
to his mother, he "barked." He coughed up something, too.
What could be the matter with him?
He went out into the fields again and let the raw wind
sweep across his face as he ran down the hill. But he could
not shake off the feeling that something was wrong.
That night his sleep was troubled. In his dreams he saw
things which he did not like to see. And he coughed. In
the morning when he awoke, his hoarseness frightened him.
He dressed and went downstairs wearily and confessed to
his mother that he was sick.
His mother had visions of two little twins she had tried
to save when they coughed; but Giles thought only of Hope.
She was the only person he had ever seen sick with a cough.At noon Giles's father came up from the pot-shop, looked
him over, and decided to go for a doctor.
"I'll just run down and get the new doctor at Culbertson.
They say he is very good."
"No!" Giles shrieked at the top of his voice. "No!
Don't go for him! I'll die if you get him. His diploma's
no good. Doc Seabright says so. I've got to have Doc
Seabright."
"But, sweetheart," his mother begged consolingly, "Dr.
Seabright lives six miles from here, and the roads are rough
and muddy both. It will take him a half-day to come over
here and back."
"I don't care! I don't care if it takes a year!" he shouted
nervously. "I've got to have him. I'll die if I don't! And
maybe I will anyhow!"
He burst out sobbing, and buried his face in the comfort
thrown over the back of the rocking chair where he sat.
His father and mother walked in silence out to the kitchen
stove for consultation. They were both a little grave. Luke
fussed thoughtfully with a stove-lid while he tried to con-
centrate his wisdom, but Val stood thoughtfully serene.
"Maybe we'd better have him, Val," Luke proposed, all
the while tampering with the stove-hook. "If it should turn
out to be anything serious, why, it would be easier not to
have to change and we'd feel better."
Val had arrived at the same conclusion. So Luke swal-
lowed a little food and a cup of coffee and set out on his
six-mile tramp. Within an hour he reappeared at the house
with the doctor. He had met him on the ridge road just
over west. Luke had expressed delight at the good turn of
fortune, but Dr. Seabright took the chance meeting as only
a part of the day's work.
"Raw wind," he said, when he took Luke into the buck-
board. "But it doesn't keep the babies from coming. I've
looked out for two since four o'clock this morning."Dr. Seabright walked into the house as if he were quite
at home there. He threw off his mud-bespattered, fur-lined
overcoat, put his square little medicine-case up on the end
of the mantel and turned with his back to the fire to watch
Giles. His gray hair, straight and stubborn, came well up
toward the whitewashed joists and ceiling.
"If you don't mind," he said to Val and Luke, as if the
patient were not important, "I'd like to have a bite to eat.
Thus far to-day I've had to get along on one cup of coffee."
Luke and Val hurried to the kitchen to rebuild the fire
and set things going.
The doctor stood with his back to the fireplace for a time,
walked over by the window to look at a flowering plant of
bleeding-heart on the three-cornered stand, and then directed
attention to his patient.
He took a casual, general glance, picked up a wrist and
felt the pulse, placed a long hand on the forehead, asked
Giles to stick out his tongue-"clear out"-and then walked
back to his place in front of the fire. After a moment he
turned and began opening his medicine-case on a little stand
by the jamb.
"Am I going to die?" Giles asked in a dry, faint voice.
"Are you going to die?" Dr. Seabright repeated without
so much as turning round. "Why, of course you're going
to die. Everybody's going to die."
"But I mean-I mean, right away?"
The doctor turned and saw how frightened he was. He
dug him in the ribs a little with his thumb and said, "No, I
don't see any prospect of an early death."
Giles smiled the feeblest little smile. "Then it ain't con-
sumption?"
"Consumption! God Almighty, boy, it's measles. As
soon as I get you to bed with a little of this medicine in
you and a nice hot iron or a jug of hot water at your feet,you'll break out clear to the end of your little toe in just
one big measle.
"You'll cough 'em up, too. Your throat is full of them,
just itching to show themselves. They'd have been out all
over you a day or two ago if you had stayed in here by the
fire where it's warm."
Giles felt limp with a sudden great comfort. But he
wanted ultimate assurance.
"Don't measles ever kill anybody?"
"Well, if none of my patients had anything any worse, I'd
be ready to take a day or two off-maybe a week and go
duck-shooting."
Giles's comfort increased. He smiled with a renewed
hopefulness into the glowing fireplace.
"Are you afraid of dying?" the old doctor asked him very
gently.
A cloud covered Giles's face. "Yes," he answered, scarcely
above a whisper.
Dr. Seabright turned with a long slender bottle of some-
thing in one hand.
"No need to be," he said, still speaking with great gen-
tleness. "None whatever. You'll probably live to be eighty.
One of your grandads was killed in an accident when he was
an old man. Heaven knows how much longer he might have
lived. And your other one-see how old he lived to be. If
he hadn't had quite so many bumps, he would have been
good for another twenty years. You're a long-lived family
on both sides."
He caught Giles's eye and smiled into it, a direct, wise
smile.
"How old are you, about nine?"
"Going on nine. My birthday's in July."
"Well, my boy, if I should come back here seventy years
from now, I'd expect to find you around somewhere. And
barring accidents, I probably would."Giles did not mind when his mother came and told Dr.
Seabright that if he would now come out, they would be
able to give him enough to stay his stomach till he got home.
It was pleasant just to sit there alone with the firelight in
one's face, and hear the friendly voices in the expansive
kitchen, and catch the clear notes of the Plymouth Rock
roosters crowing and cackling in the windy March sunshine,
and contemplate the fact that one was going to live to be
a very old man, like Dr. Seabright.S
CHAPTER IX
UMMER was one confident exploration. And when
summer was over, there were still things to be dis-
covered and deductions to be made. Was not that
what a world was for-to be seen and heard and tasted and
smelled and felt and thought about?
Giles knew the crow of all their nearest neighbors' roost-
ers, and the bellow of every bull in the Wiggam's Glory
area. He knew where the cows usually went to calve, where
a fox was likely to be found, where melons grew best, where
services ripened earliest, where persimmons hung on latest.
He knew when birds left in the autumn, when they came in
the spring, the different kinds in the orchard and locust
thickets, and the color of the eggs in their nests. When a
train roared down the valley he knew unmistakably whether
it was a long freight or a passenger; and if a passenger train
chanced to be drawn by an engine with a freight whistle,
the fact called for family report.
He knew every tree along the way home-the big hickory
with the long strips of shellbark, the oak with the round top,
the six oaks with steeples of dead limbs, the two butternut
trees in the edge of the woods, the maple that got red on
just one branch in early September, the gum tree with the
red berries which yellowish, waxy-looking birds seemed to
like, the persimmon tree, the beeches with smooth bodies,
and the beeches with little limbs all the way from the
ground to the top. One day he saw a huge blacksnake
making his way cautiously from limb to limb up the side
of the spreading beech that stood just below the hogback
in the woods. Snakes made him shivery, but he was notmuch afraid of this harmless kind-harmless to him, he
knew-and he watched it fascinated until it had oozed its
way up from limb to limb along the body of the tree to a
hole twenty feet above the ground. It went in, but Giles
did not see it come out, and never discovered its mission
there.
Often he stood on the wooded hogback and looked over
the lower tree-tops down the narrow valley to where his
little road came at right angles into the main thoroughfare.
When he heard a train rumbling or whistling in the distance
he waited to see it swing round the curve beyond the road
at the base of the high hill. The railroad company was still
at work on the new switch over there. All along the hillside
the three box-car loads of Italians still worked like an army
of dark ants.
His favorite lookout was in an open spot under the big
white-oak with the coon scratches on the bark. There in
the cleanness and the silence he could stand as long as he
liked and look at the little world yonder on the hillside.
The ant-like men tore roughshod into anything that stood
in their way. They scampered about and tugged at heavy
