rails. They spiked these down with a great confusion of
clink-clink noises that spread along the entire switch when
the heavy sledge and the head of the spike established con-
tact with the edge of the rail. Over the newly laid rails they
pushed hand-trucks with cross-ties upon them and made way
for still other rails that were waiting to be put down. Above
the men the lately denuded hillside stood yellow and hope-
less in the September sun.
One afternoon when he stopped at his lookout, he detected
unusual activity at the switch. The dark figures were dash-
ing about with more than ordinary lack of regularity, and
some of them were singling themselves out entirely from
the anthill.
A gray squirrel high in the oak above him dropped theshells of acorns on his cap. He watched the squirrel until
it happened to see him and vanished behind the body of the
tree. He followed it round in a circle, but always it was
out of sight.
When he looked again toward the switch he saw some of
the Italians hurrying across the meadow between the rail-
road and the dusty road that led up past the schoolhouse
beyond the ridge. What was there for them to do in the
meadow? Yet there they were, stretched out in a line and
running as if they wanted to get somewhere.
A minute or two later he saw one of them coming over
the edge of the hillside that flanked his own little road on
his right. Soon another man appeared behind the first;
then another; then another; and then two or three more
until there was a thin line of them along the hillside.
A shout on the hillside opposite these men attracted his
attention. There he saw another line of men running. Soon
he could hear them calling to one another back along the
line and then across the narrow valley. It was a language
that Giles did not understand. Nevertheless, he was sure
from what he heard that the men were much interested in
whatever they were undertaking.
They shouted more demonstratively. Then the men on
the hillside at his right began to swing down toward the
hollow at the edge of the woods. Then the ones on the
other hillside began to swing down. At that moment he saw
something that he had not before noticed a lone man run-
ning quietly, and as if hoping to keep out of view, just in-
side the field along the narrow road.
Were they trying to catch him? Evidently not. They
were already more or less ahead of him, and they could have
run down the hill sooner if they had tried. But just as he
came to the lower end of the ravine, and behind the tree-
tops from Giles, the men became frantic in their shouts to
one another and swung swiftly into the corner of the woodsthemselves. They brought him to bay on a level spot in the
ravine not more than two or three hundred feet below Giles,
just where an opening in the top of a dogwood beneath his
nose enabled him to see.
Men kept running up until fifteen or twenty were crowded
into the circle round the man. Their talk was bantering;
then it was excited; then it was frivolous; then it was des-
perate. Giles wanted to run; yet he could not resist the
temptation to see. His lips were dry and his heart pounded
as if it were in his throat.
The men drew in closer. By stepping forward a pace or
two they could have touched the bronzed young man whom
they surrounded. Some of them shouted menacingly at him
and flourished their fists in wild gestures. Cheerfully brave,
he talked back to them, evidently trying to meet their
chorus of accusations.
One man in the circle lost his self-control. He leaned
forward a step and shouted until his face was red with effort
and rage. The bronzed man in the circle leaned forward
and shouted back.
The older man made a quick gesture as if to strike. In
defense the man in the circle drew up his fists. As he did
so, some one exactly behind him struck him between the
shoulders. He whirled round to face this new assailant.
Quick as a flash the enraged man who had stood in front of
him likewise dealt him a graceful blow in the back. This
time he threw his hands wildly above his head and then
wilted down in a heap.
They stood back a little, and in silence watched him. The
man who had dealt him the last blow picked up some dry
leaves and wiped off his long knife.
Somebody said something. Somebody answered. Some-
body else added a word. Then they all laughed.
But in a moment they were serious again. The man with
the knife spoke in a commanding voice and pointed to agreat pile of leaves which the streamlet in flood time had
pushed into a ditch-like excavation against the yellow bank
where somebody had prospected for coal. Hurriedly and
silently all set to work, some stripping the clothes from the
man's body while it sagged whitish and helpless like a dead
pig at butchering time, and the others clearing the leaves
away and enlarging the trench a little with sticks and a
knife or two.
Giles suddenly became aware of himself. He had seen a
murder. Now the ones who had committed it were going
to hide what they had done.
He must get away. He must run. He must reach home
and tell somebody what a dreadful thing had happened in
the woods.
He turned to step round the big oak into the path. A
young Italian in besmudged blue overalls clambered up
from the road on the other side of the hogback.
Giles stopped as if he had been turned to stone, and the
young Italian seemed almost as much amazed.
They stared at each other for a moment, then the young
Italian asked:
"You see something, boy?"
Giles swallowed, tried to speak but could not, and made
a frightened little gesture in the affirmative.
"Wait!" the young Italian commanded.
He stepped over to where Giles had stood and peered
down through the top of the dogwood. Then he came
back, greatly excited.
"You only one know," he said. "Nobody else. All right.
Dandy. You never tell. Anybody ever find out, we come
back and-like that."
He grasped Giles's head lightly and made a flourishing
gesture as if to cut his throat.
"Now! Understand?"י
There could be no doubt in Giles's mind or his face."Then go!"
He pointed up along the path, and Giles moved as swiftly
as he could without breaking into a panicky run. He did
not know whether the man was watching him or not.
He pushed on, a straight, slender little figure in a blue
waist and extra long knee-breeches of jeans, his dinner
bucket held out from his side so that it would not brush
against him and make a noise.
His fright could scarcely have been greater, yet for the
moment he enjoyed a strange feeling of safety. He would
now be able to get home. For if the young Italian had
meant to kill him merely for having seen, he would have
done so on the spot.
At the edge of the woods when he climbed the fence, he
dared to glance back, but he could not see as far down the
path as the white-oak. He jumped down, and was soon out
in the sunlit field. He seemed to be propelled without legs.
He was just wafted along-far too slowly, to be sure-and
did not feel the clay path under his feet.
Up toward the hilltop he ventured again to look back.
Nobody was in sight.
He drew some deep breaths. He could not help doing so.
Yet even these were insufficient for his lungs' needs. But
he became aware of his legs once more, and tried to walk
along somewhat less desperately. His heart still pounded
and his dry throat choked him.
At the foot of the little hill he followed the path that led
sharply down toward the edge of the woods and then out
into the hillside meadow directly homeward.
As he came round the hilltop into the meadow he saw
Genevieve and her dog coming along the path some distance
ahead. Old Genevieve! And yet to-day he was not fright-
ened at the sight of her. Relatively, she seemed a near
member of his race. She would not assail him there in the
sunlight, he knew, nor let her dog do so.Like a great black bear she came rolling along, the dog
with his head at her heels. Nearer and nearer and nearer
she came.
He had better try to say, "How do you do?" pleasantly
to her before she had time to speak to him. But he could
not get ready. The terror of the woods would not let go of
him. It would not even leave his face. When he looked
into her dark eyes in the depths of her black sunbonnet, just
as she came up, she looked back almost pityingly, as a very
experienced and powerful cat might look at a baby mouse
too small to bother with. She attributed his state of alarm
to the reputation of her dog.
"He won't hurt you," she said in a strong resonant voice
which Giles thought sounded the least bit sympathetic. "Не
won't hurt you if you don't bother him first."
The dog scarcely lifted his head as he passed.
What fortune to encounter her in any such fashion as
this! Maybe she would not now think of him as belonging
with the boys from No. 5 Company Row who threw rocks
at her paling fence and her dog.
But before he could enjoy this assumption a killing
thought flashed into his mind. Genevieve was on her way
down through the woods-her woods. She would come upon
the Italians before they got away. It would be just like her
dog to hear something or smell something and start bark-
ing. Then the Italians would see that she saw them.
And they would believe that he had met her and told her
what was going on in her woods. And the young Italian in
the blue overalls would come and kill him as he had said
he would do.
Must she go right down that way? Maybe she would go
out in the other direction to see Mrs. Fealty about some-
thing. As she rounded the hilltop he hurried cautiously back
to watch her. But, no, she took the path down toward the
corner of the woods.He must cry out to her and ask her to wait. He must
think of something to tell her that would occupy time. He
must get her to talking about the boys who bothered her and
her dog. He must ask her about her deer, and where she
got them, and if she would sell him one. "Wait!" he tried
to shriek.
But his voice was dead and did not respond to his will at
all. There she went, rolling leisurely along with her hatchet
in her hand. Strange that he had not noticed the hatchet
when she passed.
He stood for a moment, then turned and ran. He must
get as far away as possible. He must get home.
His own house and fields-how friendly they looked this
afternoon. Always they were there, clean and bright and
waiting to receive him. He must straighten himself up and
try to look as usual if he were to say nothing about what
he had seen. He must not do anything that would move his
father or mother to ask questions.
The half-mile seemed a hundred miles at least. There
was time to think of everything he might do to prevent sus-
picion. He would beg his mother to let him go to Mr. Bur-
rell's for something; or he would rush right off with Rowdy
and scare a rabbit out of the running-briars; or he would
chase the chickens and make them fly until he fixed his
mother's attention on that; or he would ask to go out along
the Wiggam's Glory road but would not go far.
Or would it be better-he stopped at the thought of such
a thing. Would it be better, despite the promise he had
made the young Italian, to confide in his mother or even his
father?
Suppose he should tell his mother. Maybe the young
Italian meant men when he said "anybody." Maybe. But
now let's see. If he told his mother, she would tell his
father. That was to be expected. They told each other
everything. True, his father might not believe the story.He might think it a wild concoction of the imagination like
that story of Burrell's cows devised from the preacher's bib-
lical story. Yet he might, on the other hand, set off at once
to look into the matter. His father had a way of going to
the bottom of everything. Had he not made a thorough in-
vestigation of the story of the cows?
And what if his father should discover the facts? What
if he and Mr. Burrell and Dr. Seabright and the teacher
should send the murderer to the penitentiary? What if his
father should try to show these Italians that they must not
threaten boys?
On so many points the long distance home did not enable
him to satisfy himself. The woods could not be wiped away.
Always there would be trees from behind which the young
Italian could step. He must not do anything to stir up the
young Italian.
But, of course, he might already be stirred up. Did Gene-
vieve and her dog discover anything when they went down
the path? If only one could know that!CHAPTER X
W
HEN HE came up near the house, he saw his
mother going in at the kitchen door. He wanted
to run to her and sob out the entire story. But
he wanted also to live, and with the will to live he kept faith.
He called Rowdy and raced off down through the field for
a pre-supper excursion. He meant to run clear to the red-
oaks in the hollow, but before he had gone far, he came to
a full stop. A question stopped him. He must have stood
there longer than he knew, for when he became aware of
himself again, Rowdy was in the edge of the woods in pur-
suit of a rabbit, and he had to think a second to remember
where he was. He was standing there like a terrified statue.
Later, back at the house, his mother said cheerfully, “I
saw you standing down there in a brown study, just like the
rest of the Dabneys."
In reply, Giles made a great effort to smile.
At supper, he was content to listen. His father thought
of closing up the pot-shop early that season. There was
little sale for ware, and if he got into his diggers, he could
make some extra money in the mine. But after his father
had thought this, and viewed the matter from all sides to
make sure, he was silent.
Giles hoped a question might send him off on another dis-
cussion of his own, and keep him away from the subject of
school and the journey home through the corner of the
woods.
"Is the coal bank really as dangerous as Pewee lets on?"
His father pulled at the point of his neat blond mustache
and smiled. "Pewee is afraid of everything on the face ofthe earth-pretty nearly. Just the same, you can't quite
laugh in his face, for lots of the things he has been afraid
of have happened."
The meat for supper was ham, big thick slices of it, fried
red. It was Giles's favorite dish. Yet this evening he could
not eat any; it made him think of something. He cut his
piece up and then cut it up again, and at last forced one
bloody-looking bite into his mouth. But it would not go
down without the aid of some dry bread and baked potato.
He succeeded in carrying the evening through. But when
darkness had settled everywhere upon the earth and he went
upstairs to bed and closed his eyes, he could not help seeing
everything he had seen that day. He squeezed his eyelids
tight and tried to shut the woods out of view, but there
everything was the men running along the hillsides, the
argument in the ravine, the graceful, vigorous stab in the
back, the young Italian in overalls taking him deftly by the
head and showing him what would happen if he told any-
body about what he had seen.
In the middle of the night he dreamed that the Italian
had come. He himself was walking along in the woods
looking at the browning slit the lightning had made down
the side of the tree below the road, and the young Italian
in overalls stepped out of the big hollow oak above the road
and was just reaching for him for his head-with a supple
left hand. Giles tried to run, but his legs would not go.
They were just like dead sticks of wood-and the Italian
was reaching for him.
"Mother!" he shrieked wildly. "Mother! Father!"
Both his mother and father came rushing upstairs to see
what had happened to him.
He was too nervous to talk. "Oh! Oh!" he sobbed,
trembling from head to foot, and buried his face in his
pillow.
His mother brought the light. Then she talked comfort-ingly to him, and tried to get him to say what it was that
had frightened him.
But he only shook his head in agony and said, "I thought
I saw something."
When he was somewhat quieted his father and mother
thought they had better go back to bed.
"Leave the light, Mother," he begged. "Turn it down
a little, but let it burn so I can see. I can't go to sleep if
everything is so black."
His mother humored him. The lamp might just as well
stand on the chest under the high little window. If he got
tired of it, he could blow it out.
In the somewhat smoky light that brightened up the low
log walls of the room and made visible the sweet-potatoes
and seed-beans spread out in the far corner, he went to sleep
and slept until his mother called him for breakfast.
Immediately his fearful mind was alive. How could he
ever go to school? What if Genevieve and her dog had seen
something, and then the Italians had seen Genevieve? What
if they had? Had they threatened to kill her, too, and then
pledged her to secrecy?
Maybe she had promised. Yet even if she had, that
would not prevent them from coming back to kill him.
They would believe that he had told her, no matter what
she might have said to them.
He must not go to school!
Breakfast tasted little better than supper. And soon after
he had eaten, he announced to his mother that he was sick-
too sick, in fact, to leave home for school.
She examined his tongue and timed his pulse. Although
she discovered no serious irregularity of any kind, she told
him he might stay at home. She remembered his bad
dreams.
He would be consistent; he would remain mopy through-
out the morning. With careful simulation of illness hewalked out toward the stable and corncrib. But before he
arrived he noticed two roosters crowing at each other, and
he quickly forgot his illness, shelled some corn and baited
them into a fight. He watched them until their heads began
to get bloody; then he chased them apart. Then he walked
across the open field to a heavily laden hickory tree. The
nuts were ready, and he clubbed them.
In the earlier afternoon he raced about with little show
of a troubled body or mind. But as evening drew nearer
and the problem of the next day presented itself, he drooped,
and looked pale and woebegone.
Despite the fact that he slept, he was sick again the next
morning until it was too late to go to school. Then he made
himself useful by helping his mother and by going to the
pot-shop and doing his father the service of goading old Fan
into making her final autumn rounds with whirlwind alac-
rity. After that he raced over his own fields with Rowdy
and discovered once again how superior his fields were.
Toward evening he showed signs of wilting again. Mrs.
Burrell happened over, and at once pronounced the malady
fall malaria. She said he would feel bad every day in the
afternoon-it was a part of the disease. And he wouldn't
be a blessed mite better until he took quinine for a week or
two. She had seen dozens of cases just like his.
The next afternoon, true to her prediction, the symptoms
recurred. In the evening at a kitchen council, Luke and Val
decided they had better go to the expense of having Dr. Sea-
bright come over on the morrow.
Dr. Seabright came. He made detailed inquiry about new
chestnuts, overripe melons, and many other matters but re-
motely connected with the case. For safety he left a little
quinine and some red, pleasant-looking medicine in a bottle
which he labeled Elixir of Pepsin. "Your stomach is not
very ready for food, and I want to find out what the trou-
ble is."Giles took the medicine dutifully for two days. If he was
sick enough to take medicine, why, it ought to follow that
he was sick enough to stay at home from school.
On the third day, Dr. Seabright came again, according to
promise. He was more or less puzzled. He could not find
anything seriously wrong with his patient. But it was better
to take no chances. There were numerous cases of typhoid
fever in the region. He thought he had better increase the
doses of quinine.
By the next day Giles's imagination had been quickened
to abnormal activity. His head was roaring with racing
images. He rolled and tossed and dreamed, and lay in hot
fright at the certain return of the Italian in overalls. His
mother noticed his great restlessness and hurried Luke off
to bring Dr. Seabright back a day early.
Dr. Seabright was more puzzled than ever. "I can't find
a thing wrong with him," he told Luke, "except that he is
very nervous. That doesn't tell me much, because he is
high strung naturally. You've not frightened him in any
way, have you?"
No, they had not. He did cry out in his dreams one
night. He had a bad nightmare, they guessed.
Dr. Seabright caught up this new clew. He suggested
that he have opportunity to be with the boy alone. He was
thinking of his conversation with Giles when the measles
were on the way.
With a serenely encouraging countenance he went back
to his patient.
"I'm wondering if it isn't time to change your medicine,"
he began. "Do you like this so well that you couldn't give
it up?"
Giles smiled lamely. "It's not very good."
"Maybe I can find something just as useful that would
please the tongue better."Giles felt tremendously honored thus to be taken into the
confidence of the medical profession.
"Maybe I could give you something, for instance, that
would make you sleep better. You don't sleep so very well,
do you?"
"No," Giles answered, just above a breath of a whisper.
The old doctor stood over him and looked sympathetically
into his face. He discerned fear.
"You needn't be afraid when you sleep. Remember what
I told you? You're going to live to be an old man-bar-
ring accidents older than I am now, a whole lot."
Giles smiled as he clutched at this inadequate assurance.
"How old are you, Doc?"
"Sixty-two."
What an immeasurable stretch of years! Giles's face held
its serenity for a moment as he contemplated all that might
be done in such a lifetime.
"And I expect to be around for at least twenty more," Dr.
Seabright added. "At least I hope to. I'd rather practice
medicine than play a harp."
He was not certain that Giles caught the full force of this
last reference. "So there's nothing for you to worry about,"
he said, swinging back to the essential matter. "The chances
are you won't die till you're ready to."
"But an accident ..."
Giles was unable to finish the sentence.
"Can't count on their coming at all, sir. Most of the ones
we expect never turn up. You just go ahead as if they
didn't exist and usually they don't."
Giles seemed interested.
"Take a doctor," Dr. Seabright went on, eager to follow
a promising lead; "I might accidentally pick up disease.
Some of my patients have got plenty of things wrong with
them. Or suppose I'd nick my finger some day when I'm
cutting into somebody: I might develop an infection thatwould put an end to me in no time. But I go ahead just the
same. Maybe I'll never meet the accidents."
Dr. Seabright thought these instances sounded a bit pro-
fessional; and he noticed that they made little impression
on Giles.
He decided to try something else. He would strike out.
"See here, boy," he said as if he expected an answer. "I
believe something has frightened you half out of your skin.
Has old Genevieve been after you with her hatchet?"
He laughed invitingly and kept an eye open for the effect
of the question.
"No."
"And that gang from No. 5 Company Row-they haven't
done anything to you, have they?"
"No. They're not coming to school yet this fall."
He brightened up a bit.
pretty soon."
"I'm going to lick one of them
"That's the stuff! Now you're talking! Good! Go after
him. But you want to be fairly sure you can do it when
you begin."
"I know. I'm going to be. Sockey McPherson is train-
ing me."
"He is? Fine! But is he pretty good himself?"
"'Course he's good. He can lick any of them, big ones
and all. And he says I'll soon be ready."
Giles felt cheered at the thought of somebody or some-
thing that he might overcome. And Dr. Seabright felt
encouraged.
Out in the kitchen he told Luke and Val that the boy
showed signs of recovery.
"He'll probably be out in a day or two. But he's high-
strung and fearful. So I'm coming back day after to-
morrow. I've got to go over beyond the railroad to see
old man Highshoe, and I might just as well stop here for
a minute."That evening Giles seemed much better. His father and
mother talked to each other with cheer again, and his
father enlarged upon the improved prospects of a boy who
could get away somewhere and learn something. Giles
listened to this discussion with such interest that his father
ventured to ask him if he did not think he ought to go to
school the next morning. At this suggestion he became
limp again.
And when he went to bed he rolled and tossed and
dreamed frightening dreams. He thought he met old Gene-
vieve on his way to school, and she smiled at him out of
her black sunbonnet and said, "You saw something, didn't
you?" He tried to run away from her without answering,
and in the effort he awakened himself so that he could not
remember whether he had to answer her or not. But he
lay a long time and wondered what he could say in reply
if ever he should meet her and she should ask him the
question.
Dr. Seabright looked in on them according to promise.
"How's this?" he asked Giles. "I expected to find you in
school."
Giles seemed to regret being the cause of disappointment;
but he said nothing. So Dr. Seabright talked to Val for a
time about things in general.
There was much typhoid fever, he told her. And people
ate too much unripe or overripe fruit and made other trou-
bles less serious than typhoid, yet serious enough. "Most
people," he observed, "eat too much of everything. Of
course some of these poor little devils down in the Company
Rows aren't troubled in that way, but most people are.
That's all that's been wrong with old man Highshoe. He
stuffs himself with heavy food until the machinery stalls,
and then he sends for me."
"Well, at least he has plenty of money to pay the doctorwith," Val remarked slyly, knowing Dr. Seabright's basis
for making charges.
"Yes, and every time he turns around he makes some
more. He wouldn't let them go through the corner of his
place with that switch until they'd paid him more than I
make in a year. It wasn't worth much-the part the Com-
pany wanted-but they had to have it."
"Are they ever going to be through with that switch?"
Val asked. "Seems as if they've been at it a long time."
"They're through now. They finished day before yester-
day, the old man told me; and yesterday all the Italians got
into their box-cars and went back to Pittsburgh."
He redirected his attention to Giles. He felt his pulse,
took his temperature, looked at his tongue and the whites
of his eyes. "Aren't you really any better, boy?"
Giles lay in a pleasant fever of tranquillity. "Yes, I'm
better."
He was startled to find how easy it was to say it.
"Hadn't you better get out of this and get back to school
pretty soon?"
"Yes, I guess so."
"You guess so, eh. But just when? It's better to set a
date. This is Friday; so how would Monday do?"
Giles agreed that Monday would be a good day. He was
certain he would be well by that time.
And he was.
He went along the path through the cool meadow on the
western hillside, and round the little hilltop. But instead
of following the path down through the woods, he kept out
in the fields, where there was sunshine, until he came to the
main road. Here he fell in with all the noisy boys and girls
who were on the way to school themselves.
The box-cars over by the switch were gone. During the
day he looked a dozen times from the school grounds tomake sure. Yes, they were gone; and they did not come
back.
When school was out he felt emboldened. He meant to
go home right through the woods. That was what he meant
to do.
As he passed the ravine he cast an eye up that way and
saw the little level spot; but he could not see the yellow
bank of the streamlet. He did not climb the hogback and
follow the path. He pushed straight along the road. Nor
did he look too much to one side or the other, although he
did cast occasional quick glances back over his shoulder.
When he saw the tree where he had sheltered himself from
the storm and had watched Genevieve go by, all the blood
in his body, all the nerves, all the receptive energy of every
sort seemed concentrated in his ears and face. He was
pleased to see no Italian in overalls there.
Until he came to the edge of the woods he remained sen-
sitive to breaking twigs and fluttering leaves. But when he
could see the sky ahead, and the open fields and the pot-
shop, he felt a great reassuring calm creeping over him. He
was once more back in sight of hills that nourished him with
their cleanness and brightness, and shouted to him to be as
unafraid as they were.PART TWOT
CHAPTER I
HE WINTER passed, a long smudge of trips from
seat to blackboard, and back again; of grittings of
muddy boots and rundown button shoes on unswept
floor; of distinct, unclean odors of individual slates and in-
dividual small human bodies. Yet there were many bright
flecks of reality in the smudge. On the way down through
the woods Giles followed the narrow stream and skated—
without skates—wherever the ice was smooth. On the hill
above the schoolhouse, when he arrived there, he rode down
with early schoolmates on a wide smooth board that would
carry a dozen boys and girls swiftly over the snow, over the
ice, and somewhat less swiftly-over the mud of early
spring. After the opening of the summer term there was
always something pulsing with natural life, something in the
current of adult affairs, to set him off into the freedom of
dreaming when he went home in the evening. Once he
found there a benign-looking old man busily soldering up
all the cracks and holes in the kitchen tinware. This old
man told Giles's mother that if everybody was going to have
to eat out of tin cans, it was just as well to solder up the
holes as soon as they came and save a little or maybe a
great deal of money. Giles watched him while he care-
fully heated the soldering iron, applied a little acid to the
spot to be mended, whisked the iron past his cheek to make
sure that it was hot enough but not too hot, and then with
the end distributed the solder in smooth silvery pools. He
let Giles try it once, and Giles immediately decided on the
career of a solderer.
A magician in the guise of a tinker! He left Giles asmall piece of solder and a tiny bottle of acid, and showed
him how to use the kitchen poker as an iron. For two days
Giles soldered everything that Mr. Works had left un-
soldered everything including the end of the tiny spout on
the coal-oil can. He had then to turn elsewhere if he were
to practice his craft further.
Mrs. Burrell! Perhaps she had not accepted the services
of Mr. Works. He must go and see. And after he had
teased long enough, his mother reluctantly consented. But
when he arrived he found another visitor there in whose
presence he shrank from stating his errand. The evening
seemed organized without a part for him. So he just sat
and listened.
The visitor was Mr. Burrell's brother-an older brother
-who according to his own testimony had seen everything
that was worth seeing and a little besides. He had not been
back in his home country now for thirty years. In that time
he had accumulated a vast store of adventures and he had
practiced daily in recounting them. At the moment Giles
arrived, he was discoursing on the wonders of Illinois. The
black soil of the state would grow anything, and it would
never wear out. One man tried to dig a well, and he couldn't
get to the bottom of the black loam, to say nothing of find-
ing water. He himself had now lived in the state for three
years-the longest he had ever lived anywhere since the
War. That fact in itself was enough to prove the state's
greatness.
He noticed that a boy had arrived, and at once swung
the current of the discourse over within his reach. He
talked about the Illinois rabbits.
"Lots of 'em, I expect," said Mr. Burrell, who was just
beginning to feel how prosy his own long life had been.
"Lots of 'em?" his brother echoed. "Why, you can live
on rabbit meat whether you have anything else or not. In
clear weather you don't see so many, for they push out intothe prairie. But when the first snow comes, by cracky, if
there was a hollow log as long as from here to Culbertson,
it would be crammed full of rabbits from one end to the
other the next morning."
Giles enjoyed hearing a man who saw the outlines of his
subject with such poetic vision. He listened on and on until
it was far past time to go home, and then when he did start
he never mentioned solder.
At home he explained how his plans had been thwarted,
and his mother was not displeased; she did not wish to have
a small boy bothering Mrs. Burrell about her kitchenware.
But his accounts of the distinguished visitor who had pre-
vented him from soliciting business were so numerous and
so picturesque that two or three nights later the entire Dab-
ney family went over to Mr. Burrell's to spend the evening.
The brother had to make some inquiries in order to es-
tablish contact with his audience. He learned that Val's
father and Luke's had participated in the Civil War from
one end to the other and had come back whole and in great
honor. He therefore turned to his own wartime experiences
for their edification.
Giles sat in the corner of the slowly darkening little porch
and saw countless thousands of men in blue and even more
thousands of those in gray sweep by to meet each other in
mighty contest. And then after all these came other thou-
sands and thousands. Always Mr. Burrell's brother was in
the thick of everything that took place.
"At Shiloh," he said, when he had warmed to his subject
until the least important movement was executed on the
double quick, "I had a short stocky little fellow named
Curtis on my left. As we advanced on the first day of the
battle, damn me if we didn't have the luck to come right
into an uprooted tree. We took shelter behind the roots
and earth-perfect breastworks and peppered away. We
thought we had won the battle, but the damned Johnniesjust kept on coming till it looked like it was going to be
settled with bayonets. We loaded and fired as fast as we
could, but they kept on coming-closer and closer. I hap-
pened to look round and our whole line had fallen back, and
was still falling back. Me and Curtis had been trying to
stave them off by ourselves.
"He saw, too. 'We've got to run for it,' he yelled; and
we started. Thousands of Minié balls were flying through
the air, but damn me if they were traveling much faster than
the two of us. I'm just six feet, and I always thought I was
pretty swift-footed, but I couldn't gain a half-inch on Curtis.
"A bullet flicked off my cap, but never touched a hair of
my head. On we raced! Another crashed into the stock of
my musket and split half of it away. It just missed my
hand. On we raced! Another wasted itself on the shoulder
plate of Curtis's Springfield as he carried it free in his right
hand. On we raced! Another buried itself in my knap-
sack on my back. But I was still alive, and the knapsack
had saved me from dying from a wound on the wrong side
of my person. On we raced!
"Just as we were coming up within two hundred yards or
so of our lines, the command was given to commence firing.
We supposed they would never withhold fire for two men,
and dropped to our knees and started crawling, looking
right into the muzzles of the muskets of our own men. The
muskets roared and the bullets flew over our heads, but high
enough not to touch us.
"Forward, and re-form on those two real fighters out
there!' the colonel commanded. And when the line came
up to us, I'll be damned in hell if there wasn't the hole for
the two of us, right where we belonged in our company.
That time when we advanced there was no stopping us."
Before there was any opportunity for Giles's father or
mother to say, "I think we had better be going now; it must
be getting late," Mr. Burrell's brother had fought throughmost of the battles of the western campaigns and finished
off with a dramatic account of the way Hooker's men clam-
bered over the rocks of the side of Lookout Mountain, out-
fought the Confederate forces he did not explain that they
were small-and planted the Stars and Stripes on the moun-
tain's highest cliff.
Giles could not sleep for two hours or more after he went
to bed. He saw myriads of soldiers sweeping by to the
strains of martial music; he heard the roar of battle, the
cries of the dying; he saw the brave vanquished yielding
the field; he saw the brave victorious press on and on-al-
ways in blue uniform, the uniform of his grandfathers.
Perhaps the time had come when he should lick one of
the gang. As a matter of fact, he had been ready to try
now for a month or two, but always when he put Sockey
down in their practice encounters Sockey would protest,
"Well, I don't know about that, whether you put me down
fair or not. I guess it was a dog-fall. I don't believe I'd
start a fight just yet."
But the next morning after he had listened to Mr. Bur-
rell's brother he was sure he was ready. He marched at
double-quick down the long ridge to the pot-shop. When
he entered, the tempo of his life was suddenly lowered, for
his father was engrossed with a mill of clay and was not
talkative.
Giles watched for a time and then said with a rising in-
flection of inquiry: "I believe I'll take Rowdy and go down
to Genevieve's big woods."
His father carried in a block of the smooth, soft clay from
the millshed and then, noting that Giles still stood waiting
for a response, said:
"All right. But be sure you're back in time."
As Giles marched gloriously down along the track of a
road, the magnificent army in blue accompanying him in the
clouds, his face tingled with more and more warmth at thethought of personal combat. He entered the woods. He
passed the spot where he had killed the snake. He passed-
with but slightly less resolution-the spot where he had
found shelter from the wild storm. And then still with a
bit of resolution he came down to the dip in the road below
where the young Italian had told him what might happen
in the future if
...
He stopped shouting to Rowdy, and walked briskly. He
could hear his own bare feet rasping against the gravelly
clay of the road. Perhaps he might as well whistle a tune.
Yes; he whistled. And soon he was away from the ravine
and out of the woods in the forenoon sun.
Just where was he going, anyhow? He scarcely knew.
But it felt easy to keep on going; he must be going in the
right direction. He wished he might stumble upon an iso-
lated member of the gang from No. 5 Company Row. They
roamed at large on Saturdays and Sundays, and on occasion
they apparently did individual errands.
He marched down the way whistling and singing and
shouting, "Marching Through Georgia" and "Yes, We'll
Rally Round the Flag." But nowhere did he see a boy of
his own size.
At the main road he stopped and looked about. If he
should turn to the left and go far enough, he would come
out down at No. 5 Company Row. He thought he might as
well go down in that direction a distance.
Whistling, he marched on.
"What you whistlin' about?" somebody called contemptu-
ously from the shade of a little sycamore tree above the
road.
Giles drew up with a jerk. "Why," he said, trying to re-
cover himself, "I was just whistling."
"Whistling' did you say? You want to pronounce like
the teacher, don't you?""Why, no, I guess not." Giles thought he pronounced
just as his mother did.
It was Sib Pendleton. Sib was not much larger than he
was, but he was a year or two older; and he was hardened
in the ways of the industrial world.
Giles whistled for Rowdy, who had trotted on ahead. Sib
got up and walked in superior fashion down to where Giles
stood.
"Say, where did you find that hip pocket?" he asked air-
ily. "It's too big for your pants."
He walked round Giles and scrutinized him. "I'll bet
your mother made them pants for you out of your old man's
worn-out ones. Let's see!"
He stepped up close in an attitude of inspection. Like a
flash Giles administered one of Sockey's "close-ups to the
chin." Sib let out a cowardly yell, and staggering, tried to
recover himself.
But Giles gave him no chance. He clutched him in a
neck and knee hold like a cat, brought him to the ground,
flopped his own knees tight over Sib's waist and thighs and
pummeled him in the nose, the eyes, the neck, the jaws.
"Oh! Oh!" Sib wailed frantically. "For Christ
A'mighty! Oh! Oh! What have I done? You're killin'
me. Honest to God, you're killin' me."
Sib was lost unless he had the gang to back him up; and
the gang-the rest of them were over the hill. Giles was
accustomed to direct himself and to act alone. So the cir-
cumstances were not unusual for him.
He cast a quick glance up and down the road. When he
saw nobody, he sailed into Sib with a new volley.
"You'll-laugh at me-will you because I can read?
And you'll put a snake-round my neck, will you?"
"No, we never!" Sib protested helplessly.
"Well, you would have if old Doc hadn't come along-
if you hadn't been afraid to pick it up. I'll make you eata snake, that's what I'll do. I'll make you bite the end of
his tail off-while he's still alive."
"Oh," Sib wailed, all the less heroically, in a great blub-
bering. "Stop! Don't! Don't! What you want to kill me
for?"
By the unmartial tone of voice Giles knew that the sur-
render was unconditional.
"Had enough?" he asked derisively. "Say 'Enough,' or
I'll never stop."
"Yes, enough!"
Giles sat back on Sib's belly, his knees tight against the
ground on each side of the prostrate form. Sib felt that it
was better to offer no resistance at all, and let his hands rest
lax against his ribs.
"Promise me something!" Giles commanded.
"What is it?"
"Promise me whether you know what it is or not." Giles
made a menacing gesture with his right fist.
"Yes, I promise. But what is it?"
"That you never tell the rest of the gang I licked you.
If you tell, I'll catch you out some day and not leave an
inch of hide on your face. I'll lick you within an inch of
your life."
Sib renewed the promise, and Giles let him up. While he
looked for his cap he sniffled and hobbled like the merest
kid. When he found it he started down the road.
"Remember what you promised!" Giles called after him.
While he watched, a wave of fellow feeling swept over
him. They were out there by themselves. This was not
the way to part.
But his sense of revenge, of superiority over an antagonist
a little bigger and older than he was, of justice done in an
otherwise unjust world, had the better of him. Without a
mollifying call of any kind, he turned and marched back up
the road.He might have sung again, but he did not need such sup-
port now. Within him welled a new assurance that in itself
was enough. His face and temples felt round and full size,
and he plumped his bare feet down so hard in the patches
of deep dust in the road that they shot sprays of dust out
on all sides as if he were splashing in water. The world
about him recorded itself with pleasant sharpness.
He cut through the path across the corner at the forks of
the road and saved himself ten steps. Early summer weeds
had almost concealed the path, and he glanced down to
make certain that no copperhead lurked in the cover.
He saw something down there just ahead of his feet. It
was not a snake, though. It was bright. Somebody had
lost something a knife-a large one. He picked it up. He
opened it. It was a frog-sticker! And it looked sharp. Не
tried it on the end of his finger. It was like a razor.
Only one boy in his entire acquaintance possessed such a
treasure. And this was not that knife. It did not belong
to anybody he knew. It was his. It had slipped through a
hole in somebody's pocket, and he had found it, and that
made it his.
He tried cutting a slender elm shoot, and the blade of the
knife slid through the green wood as if it were not there.
He picked up a stray piece of pine grocery box and tried
that. The long shavings rolled back over his hand as if he
were using Mr. Burrell's sharpest drawing-knife.
What could he not do with this! It was the kind men
fought with, too. If anybody should now bother him, he
would have a knife. With such a knife in his pocket he
need not be afraid of anybody-of the biggest boy in the
gang.
He hurried a little in the woods; he wanted to tell his
father that he had licked Sib Pendleton, and he wanted to
show him what he had found. But as he approached the
pot-shop, two doubts arose in his mind: Would it be politicto report to his father that he had gone deliberately in search
of the enemy, had found him, and had vanquished him?
And, especially under the circumstances, would it be wise to
show his father such a stiletto-like knife? If he told him
one thing, he had better not tell him the other. But which
should he report?
Inasmuch as his face, when he looked at himself in the
clear pool of water below the pot-shop, revealed no special
marks of battle, and his hair was no more tousled than usual,
he decided to say nothing about the fight-unless questioned.
And when he took a last admiring glance at the knife just
before he stepped into the wheel-room where his father was
at work, he thought he had better say nothing about that
either for the present.
So when his father said, "You were gone quite a spell,"
he only replied with convincing enthusiasm, "Yes, I went all
the way to the forks of the road and back."
In this manner the discussion of the trip came to an end.
Giles mounted the ridge martially; but reflectively, con-
tentedly also. The glorious army still accompanied him,
but in the shouts from the clouds there was less and less
lust for blood and more and more generosity toward an-
tagonists who had gone down-even if somewhat ignomini-
ously in the heat of the struggle.CHAPTER II
ILES sat in church the next morning and reviewed
all the details of his encounter with Sib. Since it
G
was early summer, and therefore not the season
for revival meetings, there were no terrifying predictions
from the pulpit to distract his attention from the matter in
mind.
The Methodist church in which he sat had once been a
neat rectangular building with frosted windows to prevent
rude heretical peeping-where men and women of hardy,
dignified lives came quietly on Sunday without so much as a
bell to call them, acknowledged their many sins, prayed for
forgiveness, and enjoyed the freedom of a somewhat re-
pentant spirit. But a new civilization encompassed the re-
gion, and more obvious means had to be devised for at-
tracting an unthoughtful populace to the means of grace.
A square belfry that did not harmonize with the architec-
ture of the rest of the church-in fact, with any architec-
ture whatsoever was planted on the roof over the door,
and a raucous bell was installed to clang forth the new
spirit. It was thought better, too, to institute and maintain
modern devices for bringing sinners to repentance, once the
bell had lured them inside the church. So succeeding min-
isters and they succeeded one another steadily-placed
more and more emphasis on the protracted meetings of the
winter season. The preacher annually organized a large
choir, and both he and the choir usually spent so much en-
ergy in these meetings, that by the coming of spring the
choir had faded away almost to invisibility, and the preacher
had become temperate even to dullness.So Giles sat undisturbed while the sweet odor of the
opening locust blossoms drifted in from the declining old
trees round the church, and the faint drone of the busy
honey-bees put to sleep many of the less active-minded
members of the little congregation. In fact, Giles himself
felt the least bit drowsy when the preacher prayed finally
and announced the doxology with which the Methodists al-
ways closed their meetings.
In the afternoon he kept to his own hills in quiet Sunday
fashion. He saw boys and girls and men and women from
the different Rows gallivanting along roads and paths in
quest of anything that might furnish a little diversion. He
watched them, and tried to imagine where they were going
and what they were saying. He viewed everything in the
orchard; he leafed through the most inviting books in the
house and read a little here and there where it looked easy;
he incited Rowdy to activity, since Rowdy was no more re-
stricted on one day than another. Why couldn't they let
him go himself and find something diverting along with the
other boys-and become grown-up and unafraid in manner,
as they were? He was ready now, especially since yes-
terday.
About three o'clock he wandered down toward the maple
tree by the path to the pot-shop to see if there might not be
something of animal life concealed in its shadows. As he
walked along, lazy in the warm sun, he heard something like
the throb and rattle of bass and snare drums, and occasion-
ally, the piercing note of a fife. It was substantial earthly
music. It came not subtly from the clouds. But where
was it?
This was something like! He straightened up. He
marched back and forth along the ridge, trying to keep step
with the swift music of the drums, until he was ready to
march off, Sunday or no Sunday, and launch some great
military enterprise. When he was weary, he went up to thehouse and overheard his father say that Denis Fealty must
be home for good, since he had brought his drums. He
would die of consumption, as the rest of the family had
done.
Giles was so much alive inside that not even the reference
to consumption and death subdued him. Some kind of re-
lease in physical or mental activity he must have. Since
there was nothing to be instituted for that day, his mind
ran ahead to the next day. Always he was specially full
of ideas to be carried into execution when Monday morning
came.
Yes, when he should go to school, what echoes would he
hear of his battle with Sib on Saturday? Would Sib keep
his word, or would he whisper to some other member of the
gang? And what would the effect be if he did whisper?
Would the entire gang be down in the woods waiting for
him when the warm day was over? If they should-yes, if
they should, couldn't he couldn't he just use his big new
knife? Would they dare touch him, if he drew that on
them? Of course they would not. He was almost ready
to believe that the young Italian in overalls, should he some
day appear, would stay his hand if he saw such a means of
defense drawn to oppose him.
The next morning he felt even more secure. He whistled
his way down the ridge; he whistled his way through Gene-
vieve's big woods. But as he approached the forks of the
road he was a trifle disturbed. He saw a boy standing at
the corner as if he were waiting for some one. Who was
this boy, and for whom could he be waiting, there by him-
self?
The leisurely tempo of week-end existence quickly
mounted to that of full approach to battle. Curiosity, sus-
picion, eagerness, lifted his heart-beats high.
The boy was swinging about and throwing gravels as if
he were utterly content with the circumstances of life. Itlooked like Bugs Henderson, who had banged him on the
nose with such effort on the first day of his school career.
Ah, now he knew! The others of the gang were in am-
bush, waiting to wreak vengeance for what he had done on
Saturday.
He turned round so that his body would be between his
hands and the forks of the road, swiftly opened his new
knife, and slipped the long, sharp blade up his sleeve as he
had seen the big boys do when they were recounting deeds
of combat. Then he marched on with as much show of
courage as he could command.
He watched for heads or backs in the growth of weeds
and grass. He saw none. But as for the boy himself, yes,
it was Bugs.
"Hello, Giles!" he called cheerily long before Giles felt
close enough to offer salutation.
"Hello!"
There was the slightest bit of query in Giles's voice.
Nevertheless, he stopped, put his dinner bucket down as if
he were forcibly adjusting the lid of it, let the knife slip
down out of his sleeve, closed it, and put it in his pocket.
Then he went on to where Bugs waited.
"Say," Bugs began, "do you know what I've waited for?"
Giles saw that his attitude was not only friendly but re-
spectful. "No. What?"
They moved along the smooth, dusty road.
"Why, let's us be buddies."
Giles was so startled by the suddenness of the proposal
that he did not speak for a second.
"What you say?" Bugs urged.
"All right!"
They made the appropriate signs, and were buddies.
For a moment they walked in silence. Bugs was aggres-
sive and hard, and his dull gray eyes looked hungrily toward
anything he thought worth possessing. Giles was lithe andimpressionable, and had learned so well to get along without
material comforts that he thought about other things with
freedom.
"Say," Bugs began, after their bare feet had for a short
interval made squishy little sounds in the deep dust, "I've
got a crackerjack to tell you right away. What have you
got to tell me?"
Giles was flushed with the new honor, and hesitated not
to confide first of all that he had found the sharpest frog-
sticker that had ever been made.
"Where is it? Let me see."
Giles exhibited it, and Bugs agreed that it was a marvel.
Better still, he declared that he knew no one who had lost
such a knife. Undisputedly, it now belonged to its pos-
sessor.
"Now what's yours?" Giles asked.
"Oh, boy! Mine'll make your eyes hurt just to hear
about it. Oh, boy!"
"Well, what is it, then?"
"What is it! Why, the girls are a-goin' in a-swimmin' at
noon-to-day."
"Sure?"
"'Course I'm sure. I heard Gustie and two or three of
'em fixin' it all up. They're a-goin' over by the crick to eat
their dinners in the grove, but they mean to go in before
they eat, so as they won't get caught."
Giles's mind ran ahead to the possibilities. "S'pose we
can slip up on them?"
"Yeah-h! But we ought to have another buddy. I
wanted to get Sib, but he wouldn't come to school to-day.
What about Sockey? You like him, don't you?"
No one could have suited Giles better.
As soon as Sockey appeared, the three conferred without
exciting comment, and agreed upon a course of action.
"Yes, that's the thing: go up the road and then overalong that rail fence. There'll be trees and bushes to hide
us all the way."
And at noon, along the rail fence they went as soon as
the dozen girls who customarily brought dinners had hur-
ried off a little too obviously to play picnic.
Cautiously the boys crept and dodged along the edge of
the meadow. In their stooped position, the magnitude of the
enterprise began to affect their breathing and their pulse.
Their faces felt red and heavy.
"Gosh!" Sockey whispered. "Say, I've got to yell or
somethin' purty soon or I'll bust."
But the enterprise must not now be abandoned. They
crept on.
"Listen!" Bugs commanded.
They held their breath for a moment and heard some of
the girls over by the creek laughing and squealing. Then
they crept on and on. The early June sun and the excite-
ment of secrecy covered their faces with beads of sweat.
"Listen!" somebody commanded again.
This time they heard not only laughter, but the splash of
bodies in the water.
"They're in! By jiminy, they are!" Giles whispered as
if they were not ten feet away.
Bugs felt superior in the latitude of speech allowed by
No. 5 Company Row. "Damned if they ain't! We'll just
snake out there all of a sudden and git every piece of clothes
they've got."
They moved on again. Eventually they came to the
screen of small trees just back from the giant sycamore that
spread over the swimming-hole. The fence and its over-
growth of weeds and briars and poison ivy made standing
up quite safe.
The suspense was broken by a frightened field mouse
frantically trying to flee in a straight line through the thickgrass at their feet, and the flash of a rustling blacksnake
close upon him like a streak of flowing ebony.
The boys jumped.
"Criminee!" Sockey exclaimed in a hoarse whisper.
"S'pose many of them things stay around here?"
But the snake distracted them only for the moment.
Swiftly they moved along in the shelter of the fence row
until they were within fifty yards of the swimming-hole and
could peep through to a clear view.
"Look, will you?" Bugs whispered. "Look at that. Just
look at that! Oh, boy, won't I tell Sib somethin' to-night
that'll make him wish he'd a-come to school?"
Only part of the girls had had the courage to disrobe en-
tirely. These three or four bravest ones were plunging
about in the water as if they enjoyed the experience. The
others had kept their dresses on, but had rolled them up
snugly round their waists in order to wade in water that
deep.
Bugs did most of the talking. "Look at Blanche tryin'
to stand up there like a boy! She thinks she's somebody,
don't she? And look at Cora! Look at them legs, will you?
They ain't any thicker than pipe-stems. And see Lydia
Pendleton comin' out on the bank. La, la! She thinks
she's quite a lady. Watch her strut!"
"We'd better hurry," Sockey suggested, "or they'll all be
out."
"Yes, let's."
"What'll we do with the clothes after we get 'em?"
"Throw 'em up on the limbs of the sycamore so they'll
have to climb for 'em."
Giles, who was as much interested in having had the ex-
perience as he was in having it, ventured to raise a ques-
tion: "What if they tell the teacher?"
"Yes," Bugs admitted, knowing that this was something
to be thought about; "he'd lick hell out of us for watchin''em, and then do it all over again for throwin' the clothes
away."
"Say, I'll tell you," Sockey proposed, unwilling to leave
the scene without some demonstration; "let's just jump out
and yell-jump out and yell like Indians, and say we mean
to snatch their clothes, but give 'em time to grab every-
thing and run. Then they won't dare to tell the teacher, for
they've no business a-bein' in our hole."
This proposal appealed to reason. Giles and Bugs im-
mediately accepted it.
"Now when I say three," Sockey said by way of instruc-
tion, "we'll all yell, and jump over the fence, and make be-
lieve we're just ready to grab the clothes. But we'll yell
long enough to let 'em get a start on us. Make 'em think
the whole school is here."
At the signal they let out a rebel yell, thrust their heads
above the fence, and shouted to all the other fellows to
come on. "Come on! Hurry! They're in!" they shouted,
looking back into the meadow and clambering onto the
fence and down into the grove.
The girls shrieked; they rushed for their clothing; they
rushed toward the open field in the opposite direction from
the boys; they tried to dress themselves somewhat without
stopping. Cora Dever and some of the other small girls
cried in fright and shame. Lydia Pendleton and two or
three others who had attained to ten or eleven years ran
and screamed, but laughed also. And Gustie Malloy did
not run at all. She deliberately picked up her dress, threw
it over her head, made a defiant bodily gesture toward the
three, and told them that if they dared to come near her
she would cave in their thick skulls with the first rock she
could lay hands on.
"You ornery, low-down snot-noses!" she shouted to them.
"For five cents we'd strip every stitch of clothes off all three
of you and burn 'em up."Lydia!" she called, meaning the girls in general. "Any
of you kids got some matches?"
The other girls had slipped into their clothes, and the
bravest were venturing back to get their dinner buckets and
to hear.
"You dirty little sneaks!" Gustie went on, keeping the
boys occupied until the girls were back. Then she said to
the girls: "Come on, let's git 'em. S'pose all of us can't
handle three little squirts like them?"
Gustie moved toward the boys and the other girls moved
with her. The boys, fearing the disadvantage of being
greatly in the minority, retreated to the top of the fence.
Gustie shrieked in triumph: "Cowardy-calf! Cowardy-
calf!"
The boys were humiliated. And as they moved on away
with school ahead they became fearful.
"We never had a mind to take their clothes, did we?"
Bugs observed by way of fortifying himself a little against
his conscience.
"'Course we didn't." Sockey felt better after he had
spoken three words emphatically. "Didn't we say we
didn't mean to touch a stitch of their things-only pretend?"
"Sure, that's what we said."
Giles's alert mind was beginning to make trouble for him.
"But what if they tell the teacher anyhow-tell him that
we watched 'em?"
"He'd take our hides off, that's what," Bugs answered.
"He's just a-dyin' to have a crack at me, anyhow."
They came out into the road and looked toward the school-
house. Everything seemed ominously inactive. Without a
word of suggestion from themselves they started to run.
They were filled with the added fear that the bell had rung.
When they were nearer, and their jaws were drooping
with this new fright, they were overjoyed to see the edge of
the crowd of boys beyond the building where a game ofstraight-base was at an exciting stage. Since they were now
saved from this immediate extremity, they thought it would
be better to saunter up and be assimilated in the crowd be-
fore the teacher came to the door to watch for a minute or
two before he rang the bell.
They pattered along leisurely in the dust for a time, then
Sockey spoke:
"Ain't you got somethin' on Gustie, so's she'd be afraid
to tell?"
Bugs went carefully over Gustie's record for the week
preceding. Anything more ancient was too remote to be
serviceable.
No, he could think of nothing. Her record in general was
bad enough, but if they were to use anything in a trade it
had to stand high in relief among her ordinary daily mis-
demeanors.
But Bugs thought of something else. "She likes you,
Giles. I've heard her say so. Why don't you shine up to
her? Maybe she'll tell you whether they're a-goin' to re-
port us."
"She's older than I am," Giles observed, as if that fact
would be an argument against the proposal.
"That's just why she won't be so hard on you, maybe."
They decided to sit down on the bank at the edge of the
school grounds and lounge casually, as if they had been
there, almost out of sight, for the entire noon hour.
"Better see her before the bell rings," Bugs suggested.
The girls soon came across the level stretch of bottom
land. Their shouting and laughter were too hilarious to
be soothing.
When they came up, Bugs said to Giles: "Ask her-now.
Don't wait till she's had a chance to say somethin' to the
teacher."
Giles obeyed.
"Gustie!""What do you want?" she asked in a voice as metallic
as she could make it. But she dropped out of the crowd and
walked half-way to meet him.
"You don't mean to tell the teacher, do you?"
His voice was full of penitence.
"'Course I don't mean to tell the teacher. I'm just a-goin'
to report to the school directors, that's all I'm meanin' to
do. They may send you to the State Farm, for all I know."
Pleased with this sudden devastating delivery, she whirled
and ran on after the rest of the girls.
Giles walked back to the bank and reported.
"Criminee!" Bugs said, shuddering. "That Bell kid over
at Bluefield that got sent up had to roll out every day and
be ready for breakfast at six o'clock, and then work till
dark. You have to march lock-step, too."
It was too frightful to contemplate.
Sockey wanted to be encouraging. "Oh, I don't believe
they'd send us up just for lookin' at 'em."
This heartened Bugs. "I don't believe she'll ever report
us. If she begins that, maybe I won't have something to
report to somebody."
But all afternoon the three were a trifle restive. At recess
when Giles chanced to pass the little girls of the group, they
looked shamefacedly away. When he passed Lydia in the
doorway she gave him a push and said, "You think you're
smart, don't you?" And Gustie, boisterous and loud-spoken
and large-eyed, said to him as they straggled in at bell-time,
"I'd hate to be in your place."
Giles went home that night with something else on his
mind that he dared not tell his father and mother. He
hardly believed anybody would have to go to the State
Farm, but his conscience troubled him all evening in an in-
clusive, general way.
The next morning the sun was bright, and the sky was
blue, and the flickers hammered high on the steeples of thewhite-oaks, and the road through the woods seemed short.
So it was appropriate to the occasion that he should fall in
with the crowd from No. 5 Company Row when he reached
the forks of the road, and that Gustie should say to him
secretly but quite cheerfully: "I'm not a-goin' to say any-
thing to the school directors; but don't you tell Bugs and
Sockey."T
CHAPTER III
THE NEXT Saturday morning Giles hoed the patch
of early potatoes by the orchard. The evening be-
fore, his father had brought the new brown clay-
grinder up from the pot-shop, hitched her to the shovel-
plow, and with Giles riding her to keep her from stepping
on the potatoes, had plowed every row. But the plow left
weeds untouched between hills. So Giles was now laying
low these pushing young weeds and smoothing up the
ground in general according to specifications, so that each
potato-hill would hold rain when the rain came.
The number of rows yet above him did not diminish rap-
idly. In fact, the tempo of the entire world seemed low
this morning. The sun shone through a slight haze; the
cardinals were nowhere to be heard; the bee-birds were
content to sit in the top of the sweet-apple tree and swoop
deliberately from time to time upon some bee that came too
near to be resisted; and the two roosters had declared a
truce and were amicably following him along the potato-
rows devouring luscious fish-worms and grub-worms.
He devised all sorts of means of making the unhoed rows
above him transform themselves swiftly into hoed rows
below him. Yet after he thought he had hoed a week at
least, two rows yet remained above him to be done. While
he looked at them and tried to decide whether it would be
easier to take the two together or hoe one at a time, the
inactivity of the world about him became oppressive. If he
could only close his eyes and then open them again and find
that that five-foot strip of earth had been swallowed up like
a bullet in quicksand, he would feel that there was still aremnant of justice in the scheme of things. He guessed,
after trying two or three hills "two rows at a time," he had
better settle down to customary procedure and be content
to take one.
Somebody called to him from over the orchard fence:
"What you doin'?"
So there was some other human being abroad, after all.
"Hello!" Giles called back, at once full of a new eager-
ness. "Where you going?"
"No place. Just come up."
"Then come over."
But Bugs was already half-way over the paling fence
when Giles invited him.
"Have to hoe all that's down below you, yet?"
"No, you always hoe up hill. Can't you see I've hoed
up to here? Just two rows left. I can do them in no time."
"Looks easy. Got another hoe?"
Giles was overjoyed at the thought of assistance. "Here,
you take this one; there's another one in the shed."
He was back in a breath.
"Criminee!" Bugs exclaimed. "I cut two hills of 'em
clean off, tryin' to slither the weeds."
Giles looked in consternation. A hill of potatoes meant
enough for supper in three or four weeks.
"Maybe you'd better let me do it, after all," he suggested.
"It won't take long."
"Guess so. Don't believe I was ever cut out to hoe
'taters."
Giles attacked the row with wild energy. The weeds fell.
The dirt flew. The edge of the hoe rang clear whenever it
struck a flintstone. Bugs talked, and chased the roosters
until they flew with noisy cacklings over the fence out of the
patch; and threw clods at the bee-birds; and followed other
easy diversions until Giles was through."Now," said Giles when he had finished, "it's done. Let's
think up something."
They raced over the fields with Rowdy, killed a black-
snake, climbed a tree and looked at a turtle-dove's nest of
sticks, went in swimming in Giles's private hole down by
the edge of the woods, climbed the rafters of the stable
sloth-wise and looked into the nest of young barnswallows,
slipped up on a bobwhite that called cheerily from a shady
rail along the raspberry patch, watched a Plymouth Rock
hen lay an egg, and made a journey to the pot-shop so that
Bugs might consider the mysteries of pottery. He was more
interested in chasing the old claygrinder round her path and
swiping her with the long gad.
He stayed for dinner. He felt quite justified when Giles's
mother told him that he had better not start home until he
saw whether that thunderstorm was coming over or going
north. He sat down at the table with his cap on, but when
he saw that he constituted the entire minority, he slipped it
off and pushed it under his thigh on the chair. He liked
the dinner, but he thought Giles's father seemed a funny
fellow with little to say, and he regretted the delays in-
volved in having things passed when everyone had good
arms with which to reach to any part of the table.
By the time they had eaten, the thunderstorm was com-
ing over. Luke hurried back to the pot-shop in order to
carry in some green ware that was drying in the sun. When
the storm was almost ready to break, Giles and Bugs sat in
the front room and watched the outlines of everything
darken swiftly.
A flash of lightning, followed instantly by a crash of
thunder, made Giles shrink.
"Ho! You act as if you was afraid of it."
Giles did not wish to seem a coward, yet he wanted to be
honest.
"Oh-""Oh, what?" Bugs wanted to know. "It won't hurt you."
"It might. It might strike you."
"Naw!"
Bugs spat from where he sat clear to the edge of the
hearth partly covered by the summer fireboard.
"It wouldn't hurt you, anyhow, even if it did strike. It
struck our house once-ripped off a board right in the cor-
ner where the old man sleeps, and purt-nigh skeered him out
of his hide. But it didn't hurt him."
Just the same, Giles felt better when the thunder was
through with its banging about, and the black cloud had
moved to the eastward on its way.
While the rain was finishing off, Giles made Bugs ac-
quainted with the interior of The Great Wonders of the
World. In a burst of courage, he went so far as to get down
the Stories of the Bible, and tell him briefly how everything
was created out of seethings and thunderings and groanings.
He felt relieved when he had for the first time communi-
cated-though very imperfectly-something of the source
of one of his great fears.
"Don't believe it," Bugs replied. He spoke with finality,
and spat again to the distant hearth. "Whoever could be-
lieve the likes of that?"
Giles wanted to speak of other matters that sometimes
trembled through his mind-matters about which he could
never speak openly to his father or his mother. But when
he considered telling them to Bugs, the pale edges of mys-
tery faded away until there was nothing at all to lay hold
of for discussion. It seemed that some things were impor-
tant only to oneself. He was disturbed, too, lest Bugs's
marksmanship fail him and he hit the fireboard which
his mother had carefully papered to match the walls.
The hot afternoon sun burst forth upon the dripping hills,
and Giles proposed that they look about. He wanted Bugs
to be permanently impressed. They baited the two roostersinto a half-hearted battle, watched a hive of bees, full of
threats to swarm, creep out in a dark wave over the front
of the hive, and chased a groundhog that had come forth
into the sunshine down in the meadow.
When some more soft, wet-looking clouds began to push
up toward the afternoon sun, and thunder rumbled dis-
tantly, Bugs thought he had better be going. "The old
woman may tan my hide as it is, for stayin' away all day
after she'd cooked beans."
Giles walked with him down to the maple tree on the
ridge.
"Come over and see me," Bugs proposed.
"Well."
"How about next Saturday? Have to hoe?"
"No, I don't have to hoe again for two weeks. Well,
maybe. I'll ask."
"Ask? You don't have to ask, do you?"
"Sometimes."
"That's funny. I never."
Giles did not wish to seem too dependent. Still he did
not wish to commit himself to an arrangement that would
have to be canceled. "I'll let you know Monday."
That evening when it was dark enough to have a lamp
lighted, he lay on the floor while his father and mother
talked and read together, and tried to frame a question
that would present his case favorably. In a lull when
everything in the house was quiet except the clock, which
ticked on with lopsided regularity, whatever else was hap-
pening, Giles said without turning his face toward the table
and the circle of light upon it: "Bugs wants me to come
and spend a day with him."
Still there was silence.
"Can I go?"
He could hear only the heavy then the light "tock-tick"of the clock. Where was he? They could not have failed
to hear, could they?
At last he heard his father's voice, more remote in its
quality than usual: "Do you think you ought to?"
Giles felt that this inquiry was a reflection on his years.
"Why, I'm big enough. I'll be ten years old next month."
There was another long silence. Then his mother spoke.
Her voice for the moment seemed almost as far away as his
father's. "You might go this once, if you promise not to
ask again-very soon."
"Why not again?" Giles sat up in nervous annoyance.
"Why not again, Mother?"
Luke and Val glanced at each other.
Always he had felt that his father and mother dwelt in a
world which he could little penetrate, and that they some-
times were in a conspiracy to keep him out of it. Now he
had caught them carrying on some silent communication of
their own right before his eyes.
"Why not?" he asked impatiently. "That's what I'd
like to know."
"All right, then," his father answered, "climb up here
on this chair where I can see you, and don't get too excited,
and I'll tell you."
Giles got up from the floor and slid onto the third chair
by the table. The council became formal, impressive, far-
reaching.
His father spoke first: "We just don't want you to spend
too much time over there."
His voice was sympathetic but grim, as if he were con-
sidering a matter of life and death. Giles was impressed by
the solemnity of the moment, but he did not yield his point.
"Bugs is all right," he argued, thinking of Bugs's table
manners and front-room etiquette.
"I didn't say that he wasn't," his father replied. "Most
boys are. But-""But what? But what?" Giles's impatience swept aside
the initial solemnity.
"But they never had a chance, that's all."
The clock tock-ticked on and on and on. Giles did not
quite see the full logic of his father's argument, but he re-
mained silent.
"You get to running with boys over there and you'll
never be good for anything else. They've nothing to do.
They just hang around as they can till they're old enough
to dig coal. They never have a chance to see that there's
anything else for a person to do."
It became more painful for Luke to talk than for Giles
to listen. Yet he wanted to make himself understood.
"There's something better for you to do than you'll find
around here, and a better place to live. We want you to
find it. We wanted to ourselves, but we didn't learn how-
in time. It takes a little money, too."
"Didn't you ever have any money?" Giles asked in hon-
est innocence.
"Yes, boy; once. Once I had about enough. Just once.
But I had to spend it. Then it was too late to make any
more at least for myself."
He sat in the yellow light, a body from which all soul
had suddenly stolen away. He heard Winnie shrieking at
the top of her voice about more money. Was it possible
that he had ever had a wife like that? He saw himself
making fruit jars out of the wrong kind of clay; he felt
the frost that caught the peach crop the next year when he
had plenty of ware for sale; he saw his last chance slipping
away when the tin can age crept in upon him and stole his
market.
He gave a breath of a little "Huh!" and something of the
spirit of a man stole into his face again.
"That's why we're saving up money for you. That's why
I work in the mine all winter, when there are plenty ofthings I'd rather do. That's why your mother cooks and
manages, and saves butter and eggs and buys cheap brown
sugar instead of white, and gives you one spoonful instead
of two, and maybe doesn't take any at all herself, and
patches your clothes, and makes you pants out of what's
left of my old ones, and dries apples and peaches and corn,
and makes peach butter, and shells beans and puts them
away for winter, and cans blackberries and everything else
she can find, and-"
He had almost lost sight of the immediate question in
Giles's mind. But he came back to it.
-"And people who spend much time in Company Rows
and such places, seem to lose interest in anything that's
better. That's why we don't want you to get to running
off just to be with Bugs and Sib and Cross-eye and the rest
of the boys over there. It's not their fault, you understand.
They can't help it. But they grow up living from hand to
mouth, and don't know how to think of anything that's any
better. Somebody buys up the coal who don't care for any-
thing else, and first thing you know the hills and the houses
and the boys and the girls all look equally God-forsaken and
good-for-nothing."
He looked to see that Giles was taking it all in.
"Do you remember how it looked when we went to Green
Valley the last time? Gullies and gob piles and sulphur
water and smoke and dirty houses and hills covered with
sedge grass? Well, they're all right over here at No. 5 Com-
pany Row now. That broomsedge begins to grow wherever
there's nothing to keep it out. I dig up a bunch of it out
here in our own field every few days. It squeezes in and
frowsies up everything, same's the coal banks. If it ever
gets a fair start on our place, and things run down at the
heel, and you forget that you can go where there's some-
thing better than this around here, then you're here till your
dying day, same as a lot of other people."How completely his son comprehended, Luke did not
know; he only knew that he seemed to comprehend.
"So we're saving up money for you. Some of these times
when you're old enough, you'll want to plan some kind of
schooling that's better than anything we can stir up around
here, so you can make something of yourself. There are
some things-the best things, maybe that you can't do
without knowledge. You've got to know. The big bluff
may work in some things, but it won't if you want to do
something better than the other fellow has done. In two or
three years more we'll have a thousand dollars saved up.
That ought to give you quite a start."
A thousand dollars! Who ever heard of such an amount
of money? And right here at home, his father and mother
would soon have that much. Who would ever think that
people who lived in a log house with a lean-to kitchen,
would be the possessors of such sums of money as that?
Why, with that amount, they could build a new house for
themselves and paint it white and plaster it-maybe two
houses, if they wanted them.
And it was to be his! Before the great owl stopped hoot-
ing in the locust tree below the orchard that night, Giles
turned all these matters over and over again in his restless
young head. While he watched the streak of moonlight
from the little window in the gable creep slowly along the
foot-board of his bed, he reached a dozen conclusions
about what he could do with such untold wealth as a thou-
sand dollars. He slept for a time before morning, but
early he was awake, thinking again of what he was one day
to possess. The family went to church; and while the
preacher tried vainly to make clear the intricacies of sancti-
fication, Giles went over it all many times again. What
could one not do with such a fabulous sum?Ο
CHAPTER IV
N SATURDAY Giles went to No. 5 Company Row
to spend the day with Bugs. At school the day
before, Bugs had promised the excitement of steal-
ing rides on bank cars, of having the girls show themselves
to the boys over behind the switch, and of baiting five
hounds into a single fight.
