Here was a new world, to be sure. It was near at hand;
it was definite. Yet as Giles went down the dusty road, the
immediate, which he had thought would be so fascinating,
had to battle in his mind with the remote-the magnificent
adventure which he was destined to take as soon as he was
qualified for the journey. This far-off magnificence began
to color everything, to give richness of tone to what before
had seemed incapable of any sympathetic response. He did
not feel toward Bugs as he had felt the week before. He
liked him, yes. He liked him better than ever. But he him-
self felt at least two or three years older than when he was
hoeing potatoes on the preceding Saturday.
He went with Bugs and Cross-eye over to the mine open-
ing, and they stole a ride on two or three bank cars. The
experience seemed tame and unworthy. The girls had oc-
cupations of their own and would not so much as look at
the boys. And the dog-fight was a languid affair. The dogs
treated it as if it were a frame-up.
The day lagged before dinner time. But eventually
dinner time came. Bugs's mother thrust her head out at
the front door and yelled: "If you kids want anything to
eat, you'd better come on in. But you ain't a-goin' to git
much, I'm a-tellin' you that before you come."They went in.
In the front room, which was full of the odors of the
kitchen, stood a giant oak bed with a worn, frowsy com-
fort spread over its sagging body. A stand with one leg
badly fractured stood by the bare hot south window, and
supported a Christmas present of a globe lamp in colors.
But the globe was gone, and three brass wire supports stood
out emptily from the ornate burner. By the front window,
which buzzed with flies, stood a battered sewing-machine.
In front of the fireplace on the faded green and red boughten
carpet was a wide radiating circle of coal grime where
Bugs's father put the tub of water when he cleaned up after
his day's work. Two or three chairs, unmatched and hurt-
looking, stood about in the room.
"Come on out," Mrs. Henderson commanded brusquely.
"This is Saturday and I had to cook up some things for
Sunday; so I didn't have time to bother cookin' anything
for to-day."
They went into the kitchen. The walls consisted of out-
side boards only, plus the stringers to which they were
nailed. Everywhere these stringers were used as a make-
shift pantry and a catch-all. The rain had beaten in and
discolored the wood almost beyond recognition.
A table with a hacked oilcloth for a covering stood
against the wall.
"Set down and eat," Mrs. Henderson commanded. "I'm
too busy to stop."
The boys sat down at the table. There were three or
four thick slices of bread, still warm from the oven, some
odorous butter, a dish of kidney beans cooked with slices of
pork, and two pieces of dried-apple pie. They said little
while they ate, for Mrs. Henderson's injured spirit hovered
over them as she scrubbed the kitchen floor, the back steps,
the flat stone at the foot of them, and then rinsed out the
scrub-bucket and hung it up with a bang.Bugs was humiliated. He tried to eat deliberately as he
saw Giles doing. He wished his mother had not made a
scene when he had special company. He must think up a
project that would make Giles forget the circumstances of
the dinner.
"Say," he said, with renewed enthusiasm when they were
again out of the house, "I know what we'll do. We'll go
down the main track to the siding and climb into a flat-car,
and when the local freight comes by to take the string of
cars down the track and push 'em up the long switch to the
tipple, we'll have a ride."
Giles had never been in a flat-car. Nor had he ever be-
fore felt the bigness of one. They came alongside this string
of them, and viewed them with the intention of mounting.
The stirrup of a step was just level with their chins on this
first one. Upon its red side it read, "Hocking Valley."
"Some of 'em's got lower steps," Bugs explained. "That
one don't belong on this road."
They walked back to the end car and went round to the
other side. The step was close to the steep bank, and they
tugged until they were safely on. Then they sat on the
edge of the car bed and rested with their feet hanging down
inside. The inside was worn and splintered with the impact
of many lumps of coal, and it was unkempt-looking, as if
it were not attached to the red-painted, white-lettered ex-
terior at all.
Before they had sat long they heard the freight-roaring
down the valley toward them from the southward. When
it finally appeared some distance away, Bugs counseled:
"We'd better slide down inside and stay there till the engine
hitches on and starts, or somebody will see us and put us
off. Uncle Joe brakes on this train, and knows I like to
ride; and he's afraid I'll lose a leg and they'll say he let
me."
The roar of the approaching train slowly increased untilthe engine crashed by so close that Giles thought it must
be coming right into the car. Bugs was not dismayed. He
kept out of sight close against the car's side, but unconcern-
edly spat in graceful curves at a tiny remnant of coal in the
middle of the battered floor.
Some time after the rattle of the rear end of the train had
died away, they heard a bump down the track round the
curve, then running along from car to car back to where
they sat.
"They've hooked on to us," Bugs observed.
Their own car received the waning impact and jumped
backward the least bit.
"Oh, that's nothin'," Bugs assured Giles with a friendly
show of wisdom. "Just wait till they start."
Almost before he could say it, couplings far down the
way, then nearer and nearer, rattled and banged right up to
their own car, and they were jerked so quickly that they
both rolled over on the floor. There was nothing to hold
to on the inside of a flat-car.
"Here we go!" Bugs shouted. "Now we can stand up.
They won't stop the engine just to put us off."
The wheels ground on the rails, the unfreed brakes shud-
dered, and the speed increased until to Giles it began to
seem a little like a real train ride.
"I've never been on anything but a passenger," he said.
"What's it like? I've never been on anything but a
freight-'cept to look in."
Their car began to slow down. They could see a brake-
man waiting to throw the switch that would send them back
across the lowland and up the hollow past the tipple so that
the cars could be let down as needed without the aid of a
locomotive. He might put them off. They had better
crouch down again.
They felt the heavy, smooth roll of the wheels as theengine began to push. The brakeman climbed aboard and
sat on the edge of the forward end of their car.
Giles was fearful. He could give no satisfactory reason
for being there. And now they were right in the front end
of the train, too.
The brakeman looked at them but said nothing.
"Hello, Harry," Bugs called before the train became too
noisy. He wanted to seem acquainted. "Where's Uncle
Joe?"
"Layin' off for a couple of days."
The conversation progressed no farther.
As the train increased in speed Bugs grew more confi-
dent. "Let's go up there to the end where he is and watch
ahead. It's fun to be on the head car."
It was no easy task to cover the distance. The floor of
the car looked smooth enough, but the track was wavy and
the car wabbled from side to side like an elephant walking.
But when they arrived, they were fascinated at the sight
of the track and the adjoining landscape rushing toward
them and under their feet.
They crossed a little wooden bridge with a whoo-o-o-up
of sound. The car jerked and oscillated as if it might leave
the track.
"You kids would be in a hell of a fix," the brakeman said
half-seriously, "if this front car'd jump the track and the
whole damned string of empties, and the engine to boot, pile
up on top of us."
Bugs was skeptical. "It never has."
"Just the same, it might. You see that, don't you?"
The speed became tremendous. The brakeman noticed
that the boys were impressed.
"He's got lots of steam to-day."
They were coming to the hollow below the mine. The
grade ahead was noticeable, but the engine had a long run-ning start, and as they entered the hollow the speed dimin-
ished but slowly.
There was the tipple up ahead! How it was running to
meet them! They were almost up to it! There, they were
past it! Far up at the head of the hollow they could see
the yellow earth of the hillside where the switch came
abruptly to an end.
Gradually their speed diminished, yet right along they
swept.
The brakeman looked ahead and then looked back. "He's
goin' to send us too far."
He stood up and signaled. But the train rolled on. He
signaled again, this time a little more persistently. Still the
cars rolled on with only the slightest diminution of speed.
He leaned far out and signaled desperately. But the string
of empties was long, and the engineer was still on the wrong
side of the curve. He leaped to the other side. The fire-
man was not at his place in the cab.
There came the yellow hillside, right at them!
The engineer at last shut off the steam, but it was too
late.
"We're goin' to hit the hill. Get to the other end of the
car!"
They all plunged in that direction, but before they could
arrive, the car struck the bank, hurtled them to the front
again, and then when the pressure of the train jammed the
car high up the bankside, they were brought back by gravity
in the other direction once more.
The brakeman had been in such jams before, and man-
aged to keep his feet at least his head. But the boys
were helplessly banged against the car-end.
Then just as they were straightening up, the engineer
gave the train a jerk to correct what he had done, and they
were catapulted again half the length of the car.
When Bugs got up, blood was creeping down from abovehis left eye. Giles was not shedding blood, but he had a
paralyzed knee.
"Here, I'll help you down," the brakeman said to them,
not certain whether he ought to be severe or sympathetic.
He steadied them to the ground. "Now you'd better run
along before you bump into a bigger smash-up."
But Giles could not run along. He could not so much
as hobble. His knee would not work.
He tried vainly to restore it to life. While he was thus
occupied, Bugs leaned over the ditch that ran along the
tracks and bathed his cut eyebrow. For a long time both
were silent. Then Bugs said hopefully: "Mine ain't so bad.
The hole ain't nearly as big as I thought it was. It'll stop
bleedin' after 'while."
Giles, too, was soon able to report progress. His knee
once more began to have feeling in it. He stood up and
very gently tried it. Whoopee! It would walk.
Bugs watched cheerfully as he bathed his eye. "Will
she go?"
"Beginning to."
Bugs walked over from the pool to where Giles was going
round and round on a grassy spot.
"Gosh, didn't you think you was a goner?" he asked. "I
did."
Already he was beginning to view their ride as history.
"Maybe it didn't jerk us forty-nine ways before you could
say scat."
Giles now wanted to feel heroic himself. "I'm about all
right. It was just the funny-bone in my knee. It wasn't
much."
He walked about experimentally in a larger circle. "Yes,
I'm all right."
As they walked across the low ridge which separated
them from No. 5 Company Row, they talked courageously
-about all that had taken place. Hadn't they had a ride?Wouldn't they have something to tell about at school?
Giles emphasized his limp, now that he did not need to, and
Bugs felt at his eye, now that it had stopped bleeding.
As soon as they were back among the grimy dwellings
at the Row, Giles thought he had better be going home. He
went by the Fealty road, and then took the path round the
hilltop.
When he came within sight of his own hills, he was a-trem-
ble with joy. They were high in the sun, and clean of under-
brush and weeds. The cows ate leisurely in the afternoon
shade of the locust trees, and the whitewashed kitchen and
paling fence beyond the corner of the orchard assured him
of something in human beings that would not down.
His father and mother, instead of building a new house
for themselves, meant to have a thousand dollars for him
some day. What if he and Bugs hadn't jumped soon
enough when the flat-car hit the hill? That frightening
question was in his mind when he came to the edge of his
own fields.
His father was down toward the hollow below the locust
trees with a big "nigger-hoe," leveling some young iron-
weeds and some broomsedge. Giles felt like talking.
"What you doing?" he asked as though he could not see.
"Oh, just having a little Saturday afternoon holiday."
His father mopped the sweat from his blond face, and
fanned himself with his broad-brimmed straw hat.
"It rests one just to get out into the fields with nothing
to do but whack a weed once in a while. See that clean
strip? I've done all that from the fence up to here since
dinner time."
Giles surveyed the clean pasture and the occasional wilt-
ing weeds.
"I believe I could do that."
"You have to hit 'em harder than you do the soft pig-
weeds in the garden.""Well, can't I?"
"I don't know. Try if you want to."
Rowdy came up from the thicket, happy to see his master
after this Saturday spent irregularly away from home.
Giles took the big hoe and made a pass at a blackberry
briar. Then he made another and the hoe caught it, but
did not cut it through.
"I'll file it for you," his father said, "if you want to try
it when school is out. Maybe you can keep the broom-
sedge down."
"All right."
Giles went on to the house. His mother was glad enough
to see him back, yet her inquiry about the day was very
casual. Had he had an interesting time? And what did
he do?
Yes, he had had an interesting time. But he had done
nothing in particular, he guessed.
He found it pleasant just to talk with his mother about
things in general, and walk through the house and see how
thoughtfully the little in there was cared for, and go on
out front and watch the catbirds in the spreading rose bush,
and listen to the squeaky chirps of the young barn swallows
that circled low over the meadow.
He went outside the yard and stood perfectly still. An
old Plymouth Rock rooster walked up toward him, craning
his neck and turning his head first to one side and then to
the other, in silent, friendly inquiry. A farm wagon bumped
and rattled over the flintstone road across on the ridge by
Wiggam's Glory where the white Joan of Arc erected by
Wiggam looked hopefully from the blackberry briars. A
solitary bobwhite called gayly between long lapses of quiet.
Here he was, only a mile and a half from No. 5 Com-
pany Row; yet there was nothing down there that was here.
Two distinct worlds. He liked Bugs. In truth, he liked
him much better than ever before. Yet the world in whichhe stood was one which the destinies in their inscrutable
irony had already decreed that Bugs should never have the
gladness of knowing. He could see vaguely, yet definitely
enough, the difference between a world in which it is futile
to dream because the dreams are early suffocated, and the
world in which dreams may thrive.
"Ho-o-o-oh!" he called at the top of his voice, and listened
to the sound in the hollow below him. Then he walked
back into the house.T
CHAPTER V
HE "NIGGER-HOE," Giles found, was a well-
adapted instrument of destruction for ten-year-old
arms. If an ironweed was still young and there-
fore tender, he could take a slashing swing at it with the
great hoe and clip it from the earth with little or no sensa-
tion of impact. If a bunch of broomsedge were not too old
and firmly rooted, he could take a mightier swing and lift
the bunch free of the ground so that it would not grow again
after he had shaken the earth from its roots and turned
them up to the sun. If a small hickory bush, even, were
not too sturdy and he minded not too much the jolting of
his arms, he could level it with persistent hacking. Or if
he chanced upon some field snake that disputed his right
to the domain, he could turn the hoe over in his hands and
bring the heavy head of it down on the less resistant head
of the snake, and settle the question without great ado.
There were other uses as well. When he came upon a
specially interesting flintstone-not true flint, but chert-
and wanted to break the snail-like fossils from it, he could
use the heavy head of the hoe on it as he had used it on
the snake. With his hoe he cleaned out springs, too, and
watched the clear, cool water push from the slaty hillside
and bear away all the muddy, slushy water he had stirred
up. He cut off the legs of big crawfishes, and noticed that
they seemed to mind little the loss of a leg or two. They
could still back up.
Early every day he was out with the hoe. First he marked
off a path of destruction. Then keeping an eye on two ob-
jects ahead, as his father had shown him how to do, heslashed down every weed antipathetic to pasture. After he
had cleared a land or two of reasonable width from one side
of the field to the other, and had heard the nine o'clock
train laboring up the river valley a mile and a half away,
he went to the house and ate a thick slice of bread covered
with butter and a sprinkling of sugar. When he came back
from this respite, he worked again; but not so zealously.
He heard young hawks crying in the edge of the woods, and
when the mother of them had darted among the trees with a
tasty mouse or frog that she had pounced upon, he followed
her to discover, if possible, exactly where the nest was.
Such an expedition had to be carried on with circumspec-
tion. It therefore consumed much of the block of time still
left before dinner. Or he inadvertently hacked into a yel-
low-jackets' nest, and after he had dropped the hoe and run
for his life and then had reapproached cautiously and re-
trieved the hoe, he sometimes beguiled Rowdy into the nest
in order that he might have to run madly, too, and maybe
roll on the ground to dislodge the swiftest of his adversaries
from his short hair. Giles felt better after Rowdy had
shared the experience. Rowdy trotted back smilingly, as
though he thought it as much of a joke as anybody; but
while he was trying to escape the stingings, he was serious
enough.
Giles went to the field again after dinner. But the broom-
sedge and the ironweeds suffered little from his afternoon
sessions. The sun was warm. It was better to hunt birds'
nests and discover how many kinds of birds were through
for the summer, and how many were bringing up a second
family. He watched for the earliest blackberries, also; and
then he had to help pick them when the crop was at its best.
One afternoon while he and his mother picked the large
berries down in the shade of the woods, he came upon an
enormous blacksnake that was inordinately thick and yel-
low-looking in the middle of its body. Giles went after hishoe; and since the snake seemed lazy, it was not difficult to
kill. Giles decided upon a post-mortem examination, and
found half of the family of baby guineas that had vanished
from the landscape that morning.
But these utilitarian and anatomical matters were gross
and inferior. His best afternoon employment was lying on
the clean grass below the locust trees while the cows lay in
the shade and chewed their cuds and from time to time
groaned in lazy contentment. He looked straight up into
the sky and watched fluffy, harmless white clouds that
floated gently eastward. He watched them, and forgot that
there was any earth.
He took many rides on the clouds. Usually he had to
stand up. He could lean a little against a white, soft-look-
ing mountain that must be ever so much fluffier than the
softest feather-tick. Or he could stand out confidently on a
solid black layer on one side of the main part of the cloud.
Then one day he rode sitting down. A great oozy cloud,
half white, half gray, came along with a big easy chair in
the front of it. He could sit on that and let his feet hang
over. That made riding on a bank-car or a flat-car seem
too jouncing and unpoetic to be thought of. He passed a
hawk that had circled so high it was out of sight from the
earth, yet there it was, just below where his feet were hang-
ing. There was the railroad way down there, like a smooth,
winding path. And the river-he could see it long before
he came to it. It looked like one of the pictures in his geog-
raphy. He was passing over towns, too, but he was too
high up to see down the chimneys, or to tell whether the
men were bald-headed or not. He just floated on-silently,
breathlessly, on and on and on.
But how would he get down? Or, so far as that was
concerned, how did he get up? Oh, why bother one's brain
about matters so irrelevant?
The summer drifted along like the clouds. Before half ofit was over, Giles was already counting the months of school
he would have to endure before he could file up the big hoe
for another season of slaughter.
But one day he was whisked out of his dreamland. A
girl cousin of his, two years older, came from town to spend
some days at his house. She was the only girl Giles had
ever acted as host to for more than one day at a time. All
his resourcefulness was required. She liked to go bare-
footed, and she liked to swing in the rope swing down below
the orchard under the walnut tree, and she liked to have
Giles swing her, instead of swinging herself. He was gener-
ous. Yet sometimes he would lie down on the grass just
above the swing and try to watch the clouds. But Clarissa
was not interested in clouds. She would sit and turn round
and round by pushing on the ground with one foot, until the
two ropes were twisted into one strand from the limb to
which the swing was attached, almost down to her head.
Then she would swing enough to get a good start, so that
she would unwind in a sweeping, dizzy whirl at the same
time she was swinging. On the way back toward where
Giles lay on the grass, she would smile and purr a little, and
let her pink young legs, yet scarcely sun-burnt at all, swish
so close to his face that they obstructed any distant view.
It was Clarissa's way of bringing him back from the clouds.
Mature human logic, too, broke into his summer of dream-
ing. One Sunday afternoon in August while the Culbertson
baseball team played the team from the mines over at Shan-
nondale, a thunder cloud pushed up to the zenith from the
north, and when nobody thought it was near enough to do
so much as disturb the game, sent a startling bolt of light-
ning down and killed two of the players-the catcher and
the man at bat.
The new United Brethren minister declared that he had
never come upon a more perfect instance of the righteous
expression of God's wrath. He had not gone even to thetrouble of sending His cloud all the way over, but just far
enough up in the heavens to enable the lightning to reach
the players. What a terrible thing that they should be
snatched from the world unsaved!
But God, he admonished, usually had a deeper second
purpose in all his acts. If He had wished, He could have
struck down every player in the field-and every spectator.
But he was a generous God, and brought about this visita-
tion of death to one representative of each team so that all
the others might have warning. For those who were spared,
the day of salvation was still at hand.
In a burst of interdenominational amity he proposed that
his congregation and the Methodists unite in a great special
day of union services the next Sunday and give every sinner
an opportunity to repent and come forward while there was
yet time. They would meet in the Methodist church in the
morning, and in his own church in the evening.
Every non-church-member was made to feel that this
was probably his last chance to put himself right. Giles
felt the heavy gloom that settled over the entire community,
and his young spirit quailed under it. The seethings and
thunderings and roarings of the big book on the clock-shelf
became terrifying once more. Every day he tried to put
them aside, to laugh them off, as Bugs had done; but they
were too oppressive to be thrown off with a gesture. His
imagination was too alert and too insistent. And to make
matters worse, the two ministers prepared lists of those
upon whom they should call personally and press this emer-
gency invitation to be saved, and included Giles in their lists.
There must be something hidden in the life of a bright ten-
year-old boy who seemed so utterly free of guile. It would
be better for him to come out with an open confession of
sin and a solemn declaration for God.
Not only one of them, but both of them came out from
Culbertson and sought to impress upon him and his parentsthe precariousness of his situation. There had been so much
sin in the world recently that they confidently expected a
great visitation of wrath now almost any time.
Giles slept fearfully every night. There were rainstorms.
And with the first faintest rumblings of thunder in the west,
he was wide awake and terrified. By the time the storm
was completely up, he was clammy with a cold sweat. With
every flash of lightning that brightened up the part of his
room nearest the little window in the gable, his heart was
ready to stop beating. As the body of the storm moved off
eastward, he experienced a certain small relief. But always
he was afraid that another would come before day. So
overwrought was he by the middle of the night, so fearful
that he might hear other shudderings in the west, that the
coming of the first light of morning usually found him only
half asleep and still limp with fear.
By the end of the week he was broken with anguish and
sleeplessness. Try as he might, he could not think of any
special sins he had committed. Yet some terrible doom
hung above his head. Since there was no fear on account
of anything specific that he himself had done, his feeling of
fear floated about freely until it found something to which
it might attach itself. There, for instance, was the murder
he had seen. What if he should follow the injunction of the
ministers and lay bare his soul? That would mean that he
would have to tell about the Italians, would it not if he
told everything. What would his father and mother, who
seemed more or less numb about something as it was, think
of him if he admitted that he had kept something from
them? And might not the Italian in overalls hear about
the confession, and come back? Italians, he had always
heard, had special ways of finding out what they wished to
know.
By Sunday morning, when Wiggam's Glory was visited by
another terrific storm, his heart beat so faintly that hedoubted whether he could live to the end of the first service.
On the road to Culbertson, his father and mother talked
cheerfully or at least unconcernedly-about all sorts of
trivial everyday matters and scarcely noticed that he moped
fearfully with a feeling of faintness round his heart.
But when they arrived, he found the town full of a new
excitement. In the course of the early morning storm, the
lightning had struck the belfry of the Methodist church,
toppled the framework to the ground, and left the bell,
naked and broken, standing aloft in the humid sunshine.
The unrepentant sinners who had come to see which ones
of their number were to be overtaken by the spirit of re-
pentance, wanted at once to know what the United Brethren
preacher would say to that. But he found nothing in the
circumstances to tax logic in the slightest. The lightning
had to be released, he said; it had to strike somewhere.
And it was but one more proof of God's goodness to His own
children that He had it strike while no one was in the church.
Nevertheless, the number of those frightened into the
fold was not so great as it might have been. Ever so
slightly the tension was lessened. And Giles, though he was
aware that lightning would still dart down from the clouds,
was filled with a timorous cheerfulness when he saw, as he
thought, that the Lord of heaven and earth did not single
people out for destruction on the basis of whether or not
they belonged to church.
Summer passed. Autumn came. Winter came. All win-
ter he dreamed of what he would do the next summer. If
thunderstorms had not been associated in his mind with
summer, there would have been nothing to mar the dream's
perfection. As it turned out, there was little, anyhow. For
when the next summer came, it was dry. Luke Dabney
lamented the great need of water, but Giles himself was
content. With a blue sky over his head, there were few
days to be lost from the fields; and with less moisture inthe earth, the ironweeds came up less numerously and grew
less luxuriantly. The birds that nested on the ground fared
better, too; and the young rabbits survived infancy in larger
numbers, and were everywhere waiting to be chased.
In fact, with time on his hands, his inquisitive mind led
him to cast about for pursuits yet undiscovered. One day
he announced to his father that he believed he would gather
up all the stones that covered the ground in the big pasture
where he had reduced the weeds to subjection.
"Go ahead," his father replied, without irony. "But it'll
be a job."
He began the next morning. At the lower side of the
field he took a strip across, as he had done with the weeds.
By noon he had several piles. But in the process of making
them, he noticed how many of the stones were flat sand-
stones. Some of these were circular, and he rolled them
down through the field below the fence like small chariot
wheels, and sent birds and rabbits scurrying off in conster-
nation. Others that were flat were not round. They were
rectangular or hexagonal-regular in shape as if they be-
longed somewhere. They invited handling. They ought to
be used. He had better make something out of them. He
would build something a wall, maybe; a fence. No, he
would build just what? A chimney? Or why not build
a house? He would build something, he was sure of that.
He would build
...
The tons of irregular flintstones were forgotten. He
deigned to pick up nothing that was not flat, that was not
somewhat regular in outline. Everything that suited him
he began to carry down to a level grassy spot thirty feet in
diameter just above the walnut tree by the fence. He
needed a level spot if he meant to build. Anyhow, it was
easier to carry the stones down hill.
After a day or two he had to try building a little, just to
see how it would go. But he did not know what he wastrying to build, and when he resumed gathering, the stones
which he rolled down the hill demolished his random be-
ginnings. He thought it would be more fun to build at some
later time.
A procession of listless August days rolled slowly along
under the blue rainless sky. Why was it necessary to have
such a month as August in the calendar? Nobody seemed
to enjoy it. Rowdy was content each morning with a half-
hour's pursuit of some rabbit that he did not hope to catch,
and then panted in the shade for the rest of the day. Stout
Plymouth Rock hens gathered grasshoppers and pecked ap-
ples until nine or ten in the morning, and then made them-
selves comfortable in some sheltered corner where there was
dust. If they ventured forth in the hot afternoon, they hesi-
tated, stuck the feathers up bristle-like on their heads and
necks, drooped to the ground, and stretched out legs and
wings in an enforced relaxation from which they did not
try to escape until the heat became unendurable. Robins
made their way to tree cover near springs and bathed fre-
quently in the cool waters, only to wilt like the hens when-
ever they ventured into the open. And Giles, his mind free
of thunderstorms, spent long hours under the locust trees in
the parching fields, where his eye could follow hawks that
sailed upward and upward, and study the dry-weather
clouds that floated over, and dream of all that he might see
if only he could go far enough-as he meant to do some day.
Occasionally he went listlessly out into the field and rolled
a few stones down the hill. Some day-next year, maybe
he would build.CHAPTER VI
ATE THE next winter one day when the March wind
gusted with bumps and wailings round the corners
I
of the house, Giles lay in the kitchen bedroom and
wondered what he could do till summer came. His eye fell
upon the shelves of his grandmother's books above the foot
of the bed. He had never explored them with any thor-
oughness. On a few occasions when he had looked into
them, he had found nothing that invited immediate attention.
The print in most of them was small and crowded. He
thought they would be hard to read. He liked open pages
with only "Yes" or "No" or "You don't say!" on some of
the lines. One of the volumes, moreover, was about the
Bible-so it said on the backstrip-and he had feared to
open it, lest he come upon some new terror.
But to-day, with the accumulations of impatience imposed
by March weather, he felt courageous and able. He not
only took down a book, but he took down the very one that
discussed the Bible. It purported, he found upon looking
at the title-page, to explain away all the difficulties of both
the Old and the New Testaments. Giles leafed through a
few pages and glanced at the diagrams and pictures. One
picture was of a great tower of stone. As soon as he saw
it he had a vague recollection of having seen it before. It
was called Babel, and the designers, he learned from the
text, originally contemplated building round and round and
up and up till the top of the tower reached heaven, so that
they could go there of their own energy should other means
of ascent be denied them.
He believed he could build one like it. For a time hisbelief was not very definite or positive, and he simply lay
on the bed and dreamed. Then he studied the authentic
sketch more critically. Men in vast numbers were mount-
ing the great spiral staircase built as a part of the outside
wall, and carrying huge stones to the men who were build-
ing at the top. The circumference of everything taken to-
gether became much smaller as the tower rose toward the
clouds. It was easy to see why they built it like that. But
how would they be able to tell whether the tower might not
come to a point before heaven was reached? That, how-
ever, was only an incidental and irrelevant question; he had
no thought of building his all the way up.
He leaped off the bed, climbed back and put the book on
the shelf, then found his cap and hurried to the field and
commenced rolling stones down toward the walnut tree.
But the ground was soft, and the stones stopped before they
arrived, and he had to follow them and roll them the second
time-or maybe the third. The wind was cold and raw, too,
and the trees everywhere, especially the locust trees, looked
black in their nakedness, and the sun, though it shone
brightly enough, lacked its customary power. It was not
the season for enthusiasms.
He would build the tower, yes. He would build it when
summer came. That was when he had really meant to do
it anyhow. In the less auspicious interval, he would have
to be content with contemplation.
Early spring brought its own preoccupations. Leaves and
cornstalks and sticks had to be raked up in order to dis-
pose of the deadness and miscellaneousness of winter. They
had to be burned, too, and this set one's eyes smarting with
blue smoke-the blue smoke that smelled of promise, of a
fresh new world, of universal growth. Then came the time
to plant, and the time for his father to put aside his diggers
and return to the pot-shop. Then one day when the plant-
ings were everywhere springing up into the sun with resist-less exuberance, summer came. It had been spring in the
morning, but before noon it was summer. There could be
no mistaking what had taken place. Giles saw every grow-
ing thing move into a season of enlargement and multiplica-
tion. It was time to build.
Down in the field he surveyed the stones lying half em-
bedded in the luxuriant June grass. He selected an even
spot six or seven feet in diameter and began to collect stones
and pile them round it. He was bubbling with snatches of
songs which he did not know in their entirety.
Then he became thoughtful and silently intent upon the
smooth circle of green grass. He looked up toward the
long outstretched branch of the walnut tree directly above
his head and estimated the distance. He would not build
to heaven, but he would build till he could stand on the
tower and climb onto the limb of the tree.
To build! It was a sacred ceremony! He picked up a
large flat stone and placed it where one side of his circular
tower was to come. He placed another; then another; and
then others until he was half-way round the circle.
He stepped back and viewed what he had done. The
sight of the even curve intoxicated him. He sang, he
shouted, and he began swiftly to place other flat stones of
the same size. He needed big ones for the bottom, he could
see that.
He remembered one of just the right size out by the
skeleton of an oak stump in the pasture. With buoyant
swing he marched forth to get it. He picked it up with one
sweeping movement and let it rest on his shoulder while
he looked to see whether any lizards of special coloring had
housed themselves under it. On the cool spot that the stone
had covered lay a young copperhead flat in a coil. There
had been just room enough to enable him to squeeze in. He
was little more than a foot long, yet he lay there with as
much confidence in his abilities as if he had been a yardlong and as thick as a man's wrist or forearm. He lifted his
head the least bit and let it rest on the flint which had held
the flat stone up from the earth and made the house under-
neath possible. Giles raised the stone high above his shoul-
der and brought it down with a terrific smash on top of the
snake. The sandstone bounded off the flint, and he saw the
quivering snake with his head virtually severed from his
body.
"That's how much you're good for!" he cried trium-
phantly, but with a little of the hysteria of release in his
voice. He looked to see that there was no other snake near
to get hold of his bare toes, and then picked the flat stone
up and slammed it down once more on the snake and left it
there.
He found another stone, cautiously took hold of the edge
of it with the tips of his fingers, and tilted it back from its
place. There was nothing under it except a cricket. This
stone he carried to the walnut tree and put in place. The
circle was completed. It was almost as true as if somebody
had drawn it with a compass.
If just doing that was so exciting, how would it feel to
build some of the wall? How would the wall look? He must
build a little on one side and find out. There were plenty
of stones at hand for that. And he scarcely stopped to take
a breath until he had the wall a foot high.
Whew! How his back ached! He had done enough for
one day, maybe. So he was glad when his mother came
down into the upper edge of the pasture and asked him if
he knew he had forgotten his dinner.
The next day he built again, though not so much. Then
his enthusiasm waned for a time. But before a week had
passed he was back again, and the tower climbed a little
each day. He had to consult the book to see how to make
the wall slope in enough to leave room for the stairway
outside without having it slope in so much that it would
bring the tower to a point too soon. His stairway, despiteall his study and all his pains in building, was not very
perfect. But at least he could stand on it and put the stones
in position as the wall mounted.
One day in July the cone-like tower came to a point. He
thought he must climb onto the top of it where he had put
the last stone with some effort, and see whether he could
reach the limb of the walnut tree. He balanced himself
cautiously and then straightened up. He could just
reach it.
"Hurray!" he shouted. But before he had a firm hold
on the limb with both hands, the stone on which he balanced
tilted ever so slightly, and he went plunging with a thud to
the grass below.
He got up, ashamed of himself for having fallen. He
made a contemptuous sputtering noise with his lips and in
other ways tried to treat the matter casually. Yet despite
his efforts, his left wrist remained numb and useless. Then
it began to pain him. It would be just as well, he thought,
not to try the limb again until the pain abated. In an ef-
fort to forget, he walked round the tower a dozen times and
tried to admire it. But all the time he suffered. When he
tried to bend his wrist the pain was sharp. And soon the
wrist looked almost large enough for two.
It would be humiliating to go to the house and let his
mother see how awkward he had been. He would just stay
down there where he was. Yes, he would just stay down
there. Yet as the pain grew in intensity, he could think of
nothing he wanted so much to do as go to the house and
tell his mother.
At once his mother was disturbed. "It's broken, darling,
as sure as I'm alive," she said with less composure than
was habitual.
Down along the ridge she hurried without a minute's
delay and called Luke. When he came up to see what had
happened he saw that there was nothing to do but go for
Dr. Seabright.Dr. Seabright was at home; the summer diseases had not
yet developed to full effectiveness. He put his well-groomed
team to his top-buggy and came at once to see his patient.
"So you've taken a tumble from the heights, eh?" he
asked when he walked into the room.
Giles tried to smile, but his face did not change very much.
"Pretty sore?" Dr. Seabright queried professionally. He
seemed to be in little more of a hurry than on other oсса-
sions. He looked at the wrist scrutinizingly for a time
without touching it. Then he grasped the forearm and the
hand firmly and tried the wrist ever so slightly. He tried
it again, a little rigidly. Then he went over the swollen
part with great thoroughness.
At last he said: "No fracture. No fracture; but a mighty
bad sprain. You'll need to carry your arm in a sling for a
time."
With professional disregard for surroundings, he turned
to the doing of all that the case required. When he was
through, and Giles sat against a pillow in the big rocking
chair with his wrist in a sling made of one of his father's
enormous handkerchiefs, he said half to Giles and half to
Luke and Val, "I guess I'll go down and see that tower."
Luke went with him.
"Say!" he observed when the tower confronted them.
"No wonder he got a wrench tumbling from the top of that.
It must be eight feet high."
He walked slowly round the tower, sizing it up, noting
every detail. His blue eyes were a-twinkle. "I was trying
to figure out how he got those top stones up there. Looks
like the Tower of Babel, almost, doesn't it?"
Luke, too, was interested. But he contented himself with
a grim smile of pleasure and an occasional "Heh!" of ap-
proval.
"Quite a boy!" Dr. Seabright observed as they started
back. And Luke smiled a concurring smile.W
CHAPTER VII
HEN THE old doctor stopped early one morn-
ing several days later, he seemed to be in a hurry.
He took a look at the wrist, was content, and at
once started out through the low front doorway. Then he
turned to explain. "I've got to go to Centerville. Old man
Skinner, this side of town, sends for me once in a while-
has ever since he moved from these parts. I tell him he
just wants somebody to gas with, but he says I'm his doc-
tor, and I have to go. So I guess I'll just go on into town,
stay over night and pay my taxes and get a few office sup-
plies, and drive home to-morrow."
He walked back into the house and stood above Giles,
straight and iron-gray. "It's a long drive, and I was just
wondering how you'd like to go with me. You're good for
nothing here where you are."
Giles almost forgot his wrist-till he encountered it when
he tried to hustle into his Sunday clothes. And he did for-
get completely the prospect of imprisoning his feet in shoes
for two entire consecutive days. Within a half-hour of the
time Dr. Seabright drove up, he and Giles drove away, their
faces toward the city.
There were long lapses when nothing broke the silence
save the click of the horses' neatly shod feet against flint-
stones, or the jouncing of the well-oiled wheels when the
road was rough. Giles did not mind, for he was soon look-
ing upon landscape he had never seen. Before many miles
the hills flattened out the least bit, and the fields were pros-
perous-looking with harvests of hay and yellow shocks of
wheat.Old man Skinner and Dr. Seabright had much to talk
about at the dinner table. So Giles was in the hands of
Mrs. Skinner, a stout, cheerful old lady who wore a bright,
clean apron and crooked spectacles, and knew exactly what
twelve-year-old boys liked to eat, and how much. In truth,
it was the boast of the Skinners that everything they pos-
sessed, from the house cat and her kittens to the brindle
bull in the barnyard, was well fed.
The magnificence of everything filled Giles with wonder.
He had looked at Genevieve's spacious grounds from afar,
and her great house, but here he was, walking about and
seeing everything at close range as if it belonged to him.
He admired the white-painted fence out front, criss-crossed
with diamonds for a quarter of a mile along the road; he
walked on the soft, shaven turf and watched the deer nibble
grass and low-hanging leaves in the park; he studied the
peacocks while they strutted in glory along the driveways;
and he tried to count the white and iridescent pigeons that
sat high on the roof of the great barn in the shade of the
square towers.
But all this magnificence was only an incidental. The
city was what he constantly thought of. So when at last he
saw the two old men going to the barn to hitch up the doc-
tor's team, he was ready for the remaining miles of the
journey.
They drove away amid flourishing good-bys and cordial
commands to come again. Nor did the volubility which Mr.
Skinner had awakened in Dr. Seabright decline at once.
Giles heard how old man Skinner had come to have so much
of the world's fatness; how one family always had certain
kinds of ailments, and another seemed to cultivate some
other kind; how some paid him every time he made a call,
and others never so much as spoke of paying him; how
Giles's own grandfathers were "the salt of the earth"-and
his father and mother, too. "Your father and mother gotbanged harder than some of the rest of us, and it left them
a little numb. But they've got the sublime vitality."
At the foot of one long hill they saw a crowd up ahead
of them in the road. A farm wagon and two or three other
vehicles were more or less surrounded by people who moved
about excitedly. When the doctor's team came up he put
the lines in Giles's well hand and climbed out to see what
had happened.
A woman on the way to a family supper and reunion had
thrown herself back too heartily on the board of a rear
seat in the farm wagon when somebody said something
amusing, and she had gone in a wild flip-flop over the end-
gate and had landed in the road on the side of her stout
face. Her face remained distorted and she was unable to
speak an intelligible word. Some of the women cried; some
of them crossed themselves and prayed.
Dr. Seabright pushed into the crowd. He thought he
saw that the woman suffered only from a dislocated jaw.
"Stand aside!" he commanded authoritatively.
Some of the people knew at once who he was, and they
and the others immediately gave way. There was an ex-
pression of pleasant interest on his swarthy face when he
gave the woman's chin the right kind of quick twist and the
jaw was in place again.
"Thank the Lord!" the women cried. "Praise God for
that!"
"Oh, no, don't you do any such thing," Dr. Seabright
said, his eyes twinkling. "The Lord would have let her go
around here all day with her jaw out of place. If you've
got to praise somebody, praise me. But if you'll just let
my team get by so I won't have to wait, you needn't bother
with that, even.”
After he and Giles had edged their team by, he laughed
and smiled over the incident, and then for a time was silent.The horses, warm in the late afternoon sun, came leisurely
to the top of the hill.
Giles started as if he would leap from the buggy. Ahead,
in the valley and stretching up on the hillsides, lay Center-
ville.
Didn't Doc see? But Doc was musing on something not
at the moment before his eyes.
At last he looked up. "Oh, here we are," he said gently.
For Giles it was the city of his dreams. Brick and stone
buildings lined the streets up the hillside-buildings five or
six stories high. They were all that he required to imagine
that he was seeing Rome or Paris or London or New York
-the reputable cities in his geography.
Dr. Seabright noticed his fascination. "Quite a sight," he
said, "especially the first time. I remember my first one.
A good while ago now; but just the same, I still like to see
one once in a while. There's something romantic in the
idea-human beings gathering together in big huddles just
as though they could fight something off better when there
are lots of them in the same place. They can't. They have
to have doctors the same as anywhere else. But they feel
braver when they see so many others doing what they are
doing themselves."
Giles found the outskirts of Centerville disappointing.
They were very much like No. 5 Company Row. But as
Dr. Seabright's team carried them to the heart of the city,
the poverty and dirt gave way to prosperity and lavishness.
"I want a front room," Dr. Seabright told the man at
the desk in the Jefferson Hotel. "A big room. I don't al-
ways sleep, and I don't want to wake the boy if I get up
to sit awhile and look at something."
The clerk glanced at this towering, straight old man and
the country boy in unused Sunday clothes who stood beside
him."I think we can fix you up, Doctor," he said, as he whirled
the register round and put a room number on it.
Giles was overwhelmed when he walked into the room.
The carpet was soft and velvety, and his feet went down
into it almost as if it were turf. The great beds were pol-
ished and thick and expensive-looking. And the windows,
each much larger than a door and hung with gold-edged
draperies, looked out upon a park shaded by round, even
trees and adorned with a tall white monument. The soldier
on top looked small, but the monument as a whole suggested
pictures of Gettysburg and Lookout Mountain.
They ate supper in a large dining-room filled with snowy
white tables and black waiters. Their waiter called Dr.
Seabright "Doctah" and Giles "Captain" and trusted that
they found everything to their liking.
After supper they sat in the front of the hotel and en-
joyed their sense of well-being. Occasionally when Dr.
Seabright stood up to chat with some one he knew, Giles
watched some men who talked about the money they were
making and spat tobacco juice at tall brass spittoons on the
marble floor. They did not always take accurate aim.
When they went up to go to bed and it was early for
Dr. Seabright-he said to Giles: "Now I'm not always a
good sleeper. I've got out of the habit. So if you hear me
up in the night, or see me by the window, don't think there's
a burglar in the house."
But Dr. Seabright was the first to go to sleep. Giles
heard him snoring. He heard the courthouse clock strike,
too. It struck ten. After a time he heard it strike eleven.
He did not feel sleepy at all.
He reviewed the day; he planned the morrow; he won-
dered how things were at Wiggam's Glory. He tried his
left side; then his right; then his back. The clock struck
twelve.
Midnight! He had never in his life been up at midnight-except to be frightened by a storm, and then he never
knew for a certainty that it was not earlier. Of course he
was not up now, but he was awake and he knew what time
it was.
He moved a little in his bed to see if Doc stirred. He
did not; he slept peacefully. Giles then slipped out of bed
and went cautiously across the room to the open windows.
The air was cooler over there, and he thought he would sit
down in a deep, soft-armed chair and look out. He saw
men-and occasionally women-moving along the streets as
if it were the middle of the day instead of the middle of the
night.
Now he would have another chapter to recite when he
got back home. He would begin his narrative with an ac-
count of the Skinners, and then go to the fat woman with
the dislocated jaw, and finish off with how he sat by the
window after midnight and watched the men and women in
the streets.
The clock struck an isolated clang of a one. One o'clock!
He was up at one o'clock. The city seemed never to go to
sleep.
The clock struck two.
Dr. Seabright was so little accustomed to a night without
interruption that he made the most of it. At four o'clock
when the robins were setting up a loud chorus in the trees
across the street, he was awake for a time. He did not wish
to disturb Giles, and after he had very quietly thought over
the patients he must see as soon as he was back, and how
many of them might reasonably be expected to pay, he
turned over and went to sleep again and did not wake up till
the clock was striking seven.
He listened. He heard no boy. He sat up and looked
across to the great bed against the other wall. There was
no boy in it."Well," he said half aloud. "He must have got tired of
waiting and gone out to see the town."
He stood up, glanced into the long mirror on the closet
door and thought what a figure a thin old man in a night-
shirt made, and walked over to look out at the window.
Giles was crumpled down in the deep, soft chair, as sound
asleep as if it were the middle of the night.
"I'm getting up," Dr. Seabright announced in his gentlest
voice; and after he had repeated the announcement a time
or two, Giles was awake.
"What you doing over here?" Doc asked, his rested face
alight with good nature. "I didn't know but you'd run
away."
Giles was the least bit sheepish when he stood up, smil-
ing. "I got up at midnight because I couldn't sleep."
"God Almighty, boy, you oughtn't to have anything on
your mind yet to keep you awake of nights."
"Oh, I just wanted to see."
"Oh, that was it, was it?" Doc asked as though it might
have been something else. "Well, we'll get into our clothes
and have some breakfast and then we'll see a little more be-
fore we start back."
They saw the glass factory and the flour mills, the big
stores, the steamboats; the monkeys, the eagle, and the bear
in the park. Best of all, they climbed to the top of the
courthouse tower and viewed the entire city and the country
round about. "Pretty stiff climb for my old legs," Doc said,
"but I still like to come up."
It was like looking from a cloud. But what a muddle the
city was in, with everything running crisscross, and engines
pushing trains in and out, and the tops of houses so much
alike that you couldn't tell where one ended and another
began. But beyond the city there were well-marked fields,
quiet winding roads, and countless other evidences of a
life that was deliberately ordered.On the way home, when they were within a few miles of
Wiggam's Glory, an unassuming cloud obscured the sun.
Giles was a trifle weary and therefore not on his guard
against fears. He did not want any thunderstorm to come
along now and smudge the clear outlines of his pilgrimage.
They seemed to be traveling very fast, yet the cloud was
catching up with them. Above them there were rumblings
and well-rounded roars that were full of threat. To the
southward there was rain, but they themselves had only a
few splatters of big drops.
Suddenly from the head of the cloud the lightning shot
down in a quivering zigzag. Giles's mouth became dry with
fear.
Doc said nothing. He seemed to be far away. And the
team, quickened by the fresh air, needed no encouragement
from the whip.
Other bolts descended.
"What if I should never get home?" Giles asked himself.
"And so much to remember."
Nearer and nearer came the bolts behind them. Dr. Sea-
bright noticed this. "We're right under the edge of that
cloud," he observed without any show of being disturbed.
"I hope we don't have to get out and put the side curtains
on."
The red spokes of the buggy spun on over the road.
There was a murmuring roar in the cloud overhead.
A blinding blue flash darted down beside them in the edge
of the field where some locust trees stood, and a snapping,
piercing crash leaped back toward the cloud and spread in
a reverberating roar to every part of the heavens.
The horse at the left of the tongue plunged sidewise at
the flash, but at once caught itself and went on in its regu-
lar cadence.
"It struck!" said Giles, with a wooden tongue.
"It did for a fact," Dr. Seabright replied as if he werediscussing a game of billiards. "It was pretty close, too."
Giles thought it was too close. His heart was fluttering
to regain regularity.
But merrily the red spokes of the buggy spun on.
"Look at that!" Dr. Seabright suddenly exclaimed.
"Look at that, will you? You won't see that twice in a
lifetime."
The sun was shining down behind the cloud, flooding the
hills everywhere to the westward with brightness and filling
the hollows with blue mist.
Just as Giles looked, another bolt leaped down-into the
top of a locust tree a hundred yards ahead, and left a strip
of white inner bark and yellow wood from the highest limb
to the ground.
"Say," Doc said, with a quizzical smile over his face,
"this is exciting!"
That was the way Giles wanted to feel about it, too. But
he could not quite.
A fluid-looking flame leaped down farther eastward in
another locust thicket. "See, it's getting ahead of us now,"
Dr. Seabright observed cheerfully. "And I guess we shan't
have any rain either. All the fireworks without having to
put the side curtains on!"
Giles felt himself speedily restored. The red spokes spun
on, and he was glad. He must have his supper and go to
bed and make up for lost sleep. He meant to build a city
down in the field.T
CHAPTER VIII
HE CITY had taken hold of him with a strong
hand. That night upstairs he had to think over
once again all that he had seen. When he had con-
sidered every detail and was ready to turn on his pillow
and enjoy perfect content, he heard his father and mother
talking downstairs. Since they occasionally referred to
him, he was not unwilling to listen to what they said that
came intact up through the open stairway or through the
wide cracks of the upstairs floor. They talked freely, en-
thusiastically, about a visitor who had spent two hours with
them while he and Dr. Seabright were in Centerville.
According to this visitor, a new era was at hand for Wig-
gam's Glory. Old Bogart had sold the Sparkling Diamond
Coal Company, and the new owners were going to share
some of their prosperity with such of the miners as had been
foresighted enough to save some of their earnings. The new
Company hoped that it was not quite so devoid of human
considerations as the old Company had been. But that was
not for the visitor to say. He could say, however, with
perfectly good grace that the Company expected to lose no
money in this innovation. It was reasonable to assume that
men who had their earnings invested in the mine would
work with greater enthusiasm, would be less likely to make
trouble by calling for strikes, and in general would take
some of the property-holder's interest in making the enter-
prise profitable all round. He was ready to say in confi-
dence that the Company had made as much as fifteen,
twenty, and even thirty-five per cent on its investment.
So Coal, with all its dirt, was to bring them their fortune!If they put their entire seven hundred dollars in, and it
brought only the minimum dividends which the Company
had earned, they would have the thousand dollars which
their son would require even if they were unable to add
anything in other ways. They admitted that they could
endure the dirt and general frowsiness of a mining com-
munity, if Coal were to work such a miracle as that. In
truth, they were eager to brave the dirt; they were impa-
tient to submit themselves to every humiliation, if their son
could but have what they themselves learned not how to
achieve.
The next morning, Giles went early to the pasture and
with much singing and shouting and whistling wrecked the
Tower of Babel. His sprained wrist did not recover quickly.
He had to use one hand only in his wrecking, and for many
days thereafter. And when at last the wrist did seem per-
fectly well, if he used it in carrying stones, it protested with
a heavy ache. He planned his city-its streets, its parks, its
hotels, its courthouse, its high school, and its arched bridges.
But August brought lethargy, as usual; and September
brought school; and school, usually stupid enough, one day
before the winter was over brought him an experience more
disquieting than the marvels of the city.
It was in February. The weather was warm, the school
grounds were soft and muddy, and interest in organized
education was at its ebb. The teachers in the community,
in order to bolster up morale so that it would not break
utterly for a few weeks, had begun to have visiting days.
The Wiggam's Glory school had recently visited Gravelly
Run. Only eight or nine pupils made the trip for the suffi-
cient reason that only that number could marshal clothing
equal to such unwonted demands. Giles might have met the
requirements by wearing his Sunday suit. But when he
brought the question to the attention of his mother, shedeemed it too hazardous thus to wear Sunday clothes when
the entire surface of the earth was mud.
But the Wiggam's Glory pupils felt more at ease when
they were to act as host, and came in such clothes as they
customarily wore. The teacher brought the forenoon ses-
sion to an early close so that his pupils would have ample
time to eat their bread and butter and hard-boiled eggs and
dried-apple pie and have their seats and desks in order be-
fore the visitors should arrive. Then everybody except the
teacher got outside to watch for the visitors' approach.
While the biggest boys were engaged in hop-step-and-jump
and running-jump on the mushy hillside, they saw the ad-
vance guard coming across the level creek bottom below
the swimming hole. Instantly play became noisier. Boys
shouted to each other in overpitched voices; the girls flut-
tered about with much defiant squealing; and the teacher
swept the mud once again from the area just inside the
schoolhouse door.
The visitors came up-very slowly, it seemed to the home
pupils and immediately the boys redoubled their activities
and their shouts. Giles threw himself with abandon into
everything anybody proposed. He found himself display-
ing an unheard-of comradely feeling toward school-fellows
whom he did not like, and toward ragamuffin boys scarcely
half his own size. Some of the visitors immediately joined
in the wild sports. Some stood and watched. And three
or four, or four or five of the older ones went into the school-
house. After Giles had exhibited prowess to all those out-
side, he bethought himself of a drink of water, and went
in to the water bucket to get it.
As he came up the steps he was confronted by the most
beautiful creature he had ever seen. Perhaps she was a
year or two older than he was, she was the least bit slen-
der, her hair was brown and full of sunshine, her face was
full of color and free from the hardness of Gustie Malloy's,and she wore a dark red dress with bands of velvet yet a
little darker coming over her shoulders and down to a V in
front. She was standing in the doorway and looking across
the muddy valley to the railroad.
She could not help noting his amazement, but she did not
laugh at him. This fact made her all the more beautiful.
When he revealed his clear intention of entering the school-
house, she stood aside as if she had done it many times be-
fore, and let him. The look which she gave him was neither
unfriendly nor haughty nor defiant.
While he drank a tin-cup of water at the huge wooden
water bucket, he tried to keep his eyes on the beautiful ap-
parition. In the process he spilled some of the water down
his coat front, swiped it off with his sleeve, and hurried
out, although he did not know just what for.
At once he tore into his play with a frenzy unapproached
by that of Sockey or Cross-eye or Bugs. He became a
prodigy of strength, he was irresistible in what he proposed,
and he displayed a sudden "niceness" to all the home girls
that set them marveling.
For once he was glad to hear the bell ring. Now he
would be right in the room with this beautiful creature, and
in the official miscellaneousness of the session it would be
possible to cast an eye in her direction without attracting
undue notice.
The visitors contributed somewhat to the morale of the
home teacher. He was more awake. He moved about with
something of the expression of interest that he displayed on
the first day of a term. He wished to have his school make
a good showing. Therefore he sought to divine which of
his pupils had prepared their lessons. His exhibit must im-
press the visiting teacher as well as the visiting pupils. As
soon as the session had been called to order, Giles plunged
into his reading lesson, and for the first time in months
found study interesting. He hoped the teacher would callon him. The teacher saw his evident readiness, and se-
lected him to read the longest and most difficult paragraph.
Not even the teacher had ever before known with what
understanding and imagination Giles could read. The visit-
ing pupils, as well as the visiting teacher, listened with fas-
cination and respect that they made no effort to conceal,
and the home pupils were beside themselves with pride that
they should have such a marvel among them.
The first session flew by on rapturous wings. The after-
noon recess, although the teacher extended it from fifteen
minutes to twenty, seemed no recess at all. Then before
anyone could believe it, the entire afternoon had gone, and
the home pupils were going up and down the muddy road,
or across the fields above the road, and turning from time
to time to watch the procession of visitors following the path
across the creek bottom and up the hill on the other side.
Genevieve's east bull and his cows had been out in the
February sun, and were at the time not far above the school-
house on the hillside. Yet Giles took his solitary way up
over the hill. What did a bull or two count for, anyhow?
If Genevieve's attempted to bother him, why he would-he
would-well, the bull would be sorry. He picked up a
smooth stone as large as a turkey's egg, and marched hero-
ically up the hill. The bull never so much as looked in his
direction.
As he went down the hill toward his own road and the
naked woods, he shouted across the valley, and the shout
came back to him. With prolonged shoutings and whis-
tlings and efforts at song he crossed the road and entered
the path that followed the hogback. Was the young Ital-
ian in overalls going to return? Well, what if he did? He
stopped by the oak tree and looked down to where the
group had put an end to their compatriot. "Ibity-ibity-
ibity-ano!" he jeered in an effort to say something Italian.
He marched on into the field above the woods and outonto the long ridge beyond the round hilltop where he could
see his own log house with its washed-out white kitchen and
its high paling fence. How small everything looked!
When he arrived he burst into the kitchen in great ex-
citement and announced that he had decided to tap the
sugar maples down in the woods, carry the sugar water to
the house, and boil it down into maple syrup. His mother
was startled. She doubted whether anyone of his years
would be able to keep to such a comprehensive resolve, but
her doubts were not equal to her belief in encouraging pro-
ductive activity.
He was alive with all sorts of jerky twinges that made
him want to shout, to run, to struggle with somebody, to
make something yield. It was the sugar trees that had to
suffer. He held to his resolve and tapped them. He held
to it further and carried sugar water till dark every eve-
ning after school. He carried two buckets of it at a time,
and when he stopped by the walnut tree half-way up the
hill to give his arms a rest, he saw himself leading armies-
armies in blue and shouting commands which everybody
joyfully rushed to obey. He saw himself standing before
multitudes swaying them with his eloquent words. He saw
himself rushing into a burning building-no, nothing so com-
mon as that; it was a burning passenger train and hero-
ically saving lives that otherwise would have been lost in
an agony of fire and smoke. One of the persons he carried
out was the beautiful girl who had come to school. She
was pinned down under the wreckage, but he released her
before the fire could touch her, and carried her away in his
arms. She felt so silky and limp and trustful that he wanted
to carry her on and on forever.
But "Ho! Ho!" shouted from the hillside over the tree-
tops was the only command in his vocabulary. And his elo-
quence was limited to the same "Ho! Ho!" And when he
tried to express his great heroism in saving lives, he couldonly seize the two buckets of sugar water and rush up the
hill in the twilight till his heart was ready to stop beating
and his lungs were without breath.
How dare he think of those bleak days in March and
early April when there was no school and yet no summer?
As long as there was school there was always the remote
possibility of seeing her again. But when March came, and
the winds howled, and there was nothing to do till summer,
how could he ever hope to see her?
He wished he knew more about her, but he dared not
mention her name to profane ears. All that he learned by
listening round the edges at school was that she was visit-
ing somebody over at Gravelly Run, and that she was to be
there for a month, maybe. When the big girls discussed
her to see what flaw they could find, somebody said she had
come from England, and was just visiting at Gravelly Run
while her father found work.
March came, and Giles felt a great ache in his heart.
The winds were colder and noisier than ever. He could
only lie on the bed in the little room off the kitchen and
dream futile dreams.
England! Were there any books in the house that gave
information about England? It seemed improbable, yet his
grandmother had had the reputation of having read every-
thing.
He took down a book from the shelves above his head.
It had hard-looking pages of print, and he was moved to
put it back. The title, too, was hard: Ivanhoe. What did
that mean? Why hadn't he asked his mother? But at the
bottom of the title-page, it said something about England
and Scotland.
He read a little. It was very dull. He read on. It was still
dull. He turned over to page 50 and read again. Eventually
he came upon something about a beautiful young lady. Не
read on, despite small, pale print and yellowing pages."What are you doing?" his mother asked from the
kitchen.
"Reading," he answered; and she let him read-till sup-
per. He wanted no supper, but in order to conceal his great
interest, he went out and sat at the table. Then he read
again until his mother insisted that he put the book aside
and go to bed.
The next morning he was up early, and he read till break-
fast time. When he came to breakfast and sat before his
plate of eggs and potatoes, he saw only men in armor and
beautiful ladies in flowing garments sweeping before his
eyes. They were so substantial that he could scarcely see
through them to the plate at all. What was this he had
discovered? Were there things like that in books?
Day after day he read. When he had finished the thick
volume, he roamed the fields again with Rowdy. But
Rowdy was no longer a young dog and eager to roam, and
the fields looked unfinished and briary when compared with
smooth battlements, men in clanking armor, richly be-
decked horses, and young ladies with never a gross inclina-
tion in their minds or bodies. He saw right through the
hills and the briars and the locust trees and the coal mines
and gob piles and sulphur smoke to this other world that
stood forth magnificently in a mist of gold.
Were there other books on the rough, whitened shelves as
full of reality as this one? Why had he never asked his
mother more about them? She had read all of them-most
of them many times. But he had assumed that they were
uninteresting and therefore only for grown-up men and
women. No one could say that they were in a class with
the books required of boys and girls in school.
He was afraid to speak to his mother now lest she ask
some question that would uncover his motive in reading.
It would be better to choose for himself.
He found another that had a beautiful girl in it. It wascalled Henry Esmond, he observed; but he thought the
name inappropriate, since the person of importance in it
was named Beatrix. The book was harder reading than
Ivanhoe, yet the beautiful girl emerged no less distinctly.
As she stood on the stairway she was a perfect image of
the little English girl who stood in the doorway at school.
He did not trouble to read farther. The book had a right
to exist. He drove into another great thick volume named
David Copperfield. It was English, and therefore justified,
but there was not enough in it about beautiful girls; and
what there was was tearful and disheartening. He wanted to
see girls of buoyant health sweeping queenlike into drawing-
rooms filled with admiring older people all except himself
-or wandering meditatively, their faces fresh and sunlit,
through gardens of bending roses and white sundials and
murmuring fountains.
He might have dreamed on in books forever had he not
come upon one by a man named James Fenimore Cooper.
This man robbed him of rest even when he was asleep. Не
had to use some of the night time for shooting Indians off
white-oak limbs-like the ones in Genevieve's woods-that
arched over streams of blue water. This writer called for
action. It was time to quit moping around and get out and
do something that the beautiful girl would see or at least
hear about. This writer sent him off in a whirl of universal
sympathy for everybody except the Indians whom he had
to kill for the beautiful girl.
Occasionally his mother called upon him to do an errand
-perhaps to go as far as Culbertson and carry a basket of
eggs and bring back a quarter's worth of sugar and a pound
of coffee. He made silent protest against such interruptions.
But he did not let them interfere with his dreaming. On
the way home from Culbertson he walked the two miles and
better beside the beautiful girl. Nothing disturbed their
divine communion-nothing unless he discovered that hehad been dreaming at the grocery store and had forgotten a
part of what he went after. And not even such a discovery
interfered with dreams. In truth, it often prolonged them;
for he could have only that much more time with her when
he had got what he had forgotten and was again on his way
home. If he grew weary of walking beside her merely, he
undertook chivalrous enterprises to which she was witness.
Or he engaged in games in which she also engaged. The
school from Gravelly Run visited the Wiggam's Glory
school, and all the older pupils played a monster game of
needle's eye. When it was Giles's turn to be half the eye,
he waited while all the other girls passed through-Gustie
and Cora and Blanche and Lydia, and all the visiting girls-
until the one of his heart came round the circle. Then he
closed the eye and caught her, and kissed her with just the
right balance of dignity and fervor. Then she took his
place, and he took hers in the circle, and she waited while
all the boys that passed were praying to be caught; and then
when he came round, she very modestly closed the needle's
eye again and waited for her kiss.
Naturally he was annoyed when Rowdy met him out at
the road and affectionately demanded attention, or when his
mother asked him how much eggs were a dozen. Why could
they not let him alone? Since the time the world in its be-
ginnings leaped out of chaos, no boy had ever had just the
experience he was having now. They might have thought
they were having it. But it was only some pale ghost of
his. Dogs and the price of eggs! Yet the beautiful girl
was not frightened away even by such matter-of-fact odd-
ments of life as these. For she was the kind of beautiful
girl who hesitated not to enter the humblest log house when
the chivalrous young man she loved and the only one-
lived there.Ο
CHAPTER IX
NE EVENING while Giles sat on the edge of the
roofless front porch with his chin in his palms and
watched Beatrix descend the stairs in a towering
old castle in England, the road supervisor came to ask Luke
if he could take two days off to help with the spring work-
ing of the roads. Luke protested that every day was now
precious in the pot-shop. The supervisor saw Giles, and
generously offered to take him in his father's place. He
was big enough to keep the water jug full of fresh water,
and to see that the extra shovels and mattocks were not left
behind.
Was road-working a sufficiently chivalrous enterprise to
please the beautiful girl? As a matter of fact, Giles did not
take time to ask. It was an adventure; and in the invita-
tion to participate, though in a subordinate rôle, was a cer-
tain recognition of the fact that he was on the way to be-
coming a man.
Leaping out of bed before five o'clock in the dewy morn-
ing, eating a more substantial breakfast than usual, and then
trudging off a mile and a half to the point where work was
to begin, all seemed of a piece with the glorified world
which for two or three months had been opening about him.
Two full days could not fail to bring forth much. He would
see plenty of bobwhites, fox squirrels, groundhogs, and
pheasants in quiet places, and perhaps help kill several cop-
perheads when the plows and the grading-machine gouged
into briary, leafy spots under stretches of overhanging trees.
He would hear the mighty conversation of grown men, too.
He would pass houses where he had never been, and mightsee buoyant girls in bright pink and blue aprons coming
and going as they helped their mothers blithely.
He was in a state of mind, then, to be interested when
the men on the road settled down almost at once to talking
about women. Women were not girls exactly; and such
women as they talked about seemed not to be especially
beautiful. Nevertheless, the conversation and he had
plenty of time to hear it was different enough from any-
thing he ever heard at home. The men's stories required
adequate acquaintance with biology, and a certain recogni-
tion of the most tempestuous desires of earth's children.
They progressed along the road until they were on the
high ridge beyond Genevieve's bulging barns and well-
kept house.
"There's a woman for you," somebody said, "if you're
out to find a real one!"
There were jocose shouts and ironic words of concurrence.
Then they fell to talking about her.
Where did she come from? Aye, why hadn't somebody
answered that question? And how did she happen to have
enough money just to walk right in and buy up the best
farm in the township, and then make it all over and add
two or three to it, until her hills were little short of a won-
derland? And how did she learn stock-breeding, and go
right on stocking her farms with the best cattle and horses
and sheep and pigs and deer in the region? It must take
money to do such things. And yet she still had enough so
that when the Company came along and told her that if she
didn't sell her coal at their price, they would take all the
coal around her and leave her high and dry, she looked
Bogart and Eli Raynor in the face and laughed a little
that's what Eli himself declared and said quite casually,
"Oh, go to hell with your coal mines!"
Her speech was not that of a lady. In this, all the men
