agreed. They were accustomed to the strong speech of un-shrinking men, but not to that of an unshrinking woman.
Lem Conaway testified that she had made him blush. He
took his cow over to breed her to one of Genevieve's prize
bulls, and unfortunately came upon Genevieve herself be-
fore he met one of the hired men. Lem spoke euphemisti-
cally to her about "the ox." "Oh," Genevieve said in sharp,
ironical voice, "you want the bull, do you? Well, there's
one in each barn."
Clearly there was something wrong with a woman who
would talk like that. They had a sneaking respect for her
ability to manage her own affairs, but when they added her
frankness about most matters to her absolute refusal to say
anything whatsoever about herself-the one thing every-
body in the region wanted to know about the result was
a definite count against her character.
"Amos Dusenberry says it's his honest opinion that she
practices magic or somethin'," Bill Kellifer observed after
he had turned the water jug up to his mouth and let the
water gurgle in until Giles thought he was going to drink
the last drop and thereby make him miss a part of the
conversation.
"I've heerd that, too," Royal Burgoon said. He stood in
the shade of a hickory sapling and fanned himself with his
broad-brimmed straw hat. "Don't know whether there is
such things or not; but there's somethin' God-awful queer
about her, I'm a-tellin' ye. You know she won't let any
of the hired men stay in the house at night-not a one of
'em. Just takes that damned lion of hers in with her, locks
the doors, and ain't seen ag'in till it's light the next day."
Old Joe Pendleton straightened up. It happened that he
fought under Meade in the Civil War, and let his beard
grow accordingly. He pulled at the tip of it for a moment
to let the others know that he contemplated speech, and
then said darkly: "Things goes on in that house. I'll not
say more'n that. But I'm a-sayin' that much. If you'dcreep up to that big porch some night where you could look
in and see, you'd mighty soon find out that what she's up
to ain't legal."
"I'd expect to see her cuttin' didos if I looked in," Bill
Kellifer said in answer, "dancin' around pickin' rabbits' feet
out of the air, or green persimmons out of the fire."
The road supervisor came up, listened a minute, and
then observed: "The trouble with all of your damned big
talk is that not a one of you could be dragged up to her
porch after night with a yoke of oxen. Now ain't that the
truth?"
They all laughed; they all admitted that it was. Bill
said he was not dying to see anything he'd have to try too
hard to forget. But just the same he thought it would be
fun to know.
Already, with the aid of James Fenimore Cooper, Giles
had formulated plans. He would get Sockey and they
would go over some night soon and seek to make the great
discovery. Just what such an adventure had to do with
the beautiful girl was a trifle vague; yet Giles felt that there
was some connection.
From that moment, all that the men said and did was
low in tone. So when they stopped the next day to have
their dinners in the shade of a neighborly farmer's orchard
and the farmer came out and told them that there was a
barrel of good hard cider over in the shed, and the super-
visor got so drunk that he declared the roads already too
smooth for safety, and sent everybody home without com-
pleting the day, Giles was ready to get back to his own hills
where it was easy to dream things out. Slipping up on
Genevieve at night would require circumspect planning.
He and Sockey fixed upon a night. When it came, it was
not just what they had expected, for a mist was floating
down. But this was not to deter them. The beautiful girl
would love most a man of iron will.Bravely they made their way down the ridge past the
pot-shop, on through the woods, and on up the hill toward
Genevieve's house. Giles bit his teeth together hard to
prevent them from chattering.
They stood for a moment and listened before they climbed
over into the lane that led up to the front gate with the pine
trees on each side. Neither one was anxious to get over at
all, but both felt that they must. Giles went first, then
Sockey, then Rowdy.
Again they stood and listened. The moon on the other
side of the thinning clouds kept the earth from utter dark-
ness. It was possible to discern the outline of things.
Rowdy stood at their feet, his front legs a-tremble, his
nose and sharp eyes and ears at work trying to divine the
nature of this unusual quest. In a very subdued voice he
whined inquiringly.
"Won't he bark?" Sockey asked. Sockey's voice was
shivery.
"I'll tell him not to," Giles replied in the same kind of
voice.
What were they shivering about, anyhow? The cold
was only the drizzling cold of May. And the drizzle had
stopped, and the moon was appearing now and then be-
tween the clouds, and the wind was coming up.
Sockey felt his foot begin to patter uncontrollably.
"Hadn't one of us better hold-Rowdy?" He shiv-
ered almost beyond speech.
Giles bit his teeth together till he thought his mouth was
steady, then said: "No. He'll stay where I tell him."
Rowdy was growing impatient. He walked inquiringly
up the lane, and Giles and Sockey followed. They were
not quite so fearful after they began to use their mus-
cles.
Rowdy stopped directly in front of the gate. The pinetrees loomed symmetrically in the darkness above the pale
white of the painted picket fence.
"Gee," Sockey whispered, still shivering, "they look-
like a-graveyard."
Giles stood silent for a moment. Sockey's fear made him
feel the least bit courageous, especially since Sockey was so
much older than he was. With as much composure as pos-
sible he said: "We'll tell Rowdy to stay here outside."
"Say, I'll tell you what'd be better. One of us'd better
stay here and hold him. I'll hold him while you creep up
and look, and then you can hold him while..."
He did not finish the sentence but began immediately:
"Yes, that's the way. We don't want to get a dog fight
started."
Giles felt more courageous still. "All right; you hold
him."
"Yes, I'll hold him, and then if anything happens, we'll
be ready."
Giles cautiously reached for the gate and tried it, to see
which way it opened. He held it while he stepped through,
and was careful not to let it slam. A pale rectangle of
light came through the upper part of the front door, but
the windows were dark. With great wariness he made his
way to the porch. When he arrived, he saw a slit of light
on the side-porch floor. One blind was not quite down.
He walked on the sod to the edge of the side porch, then
for a moment stood waiting. The porch floor came just to
his knees, and he knelt over on it to crawl to the window.
He pulled one knee forward slowly and then the other.
It was not so difficult as he had feared it would be. He
kept his head down as he came near the window so that no
one would see.
His cap came gently against the wall of the house. He
rested a moment and listened to his heart thumping away.The wind rattled the tin roof above him. He would wait
another breath before he peeped in.
Suddenly there was a scratch-scratch of a scuffing noise
on the porch floor round the corner next the front entrance!
The dog! Why had he not thought before that it might
be outside?
His heart gave a great flutter and then seemed to stop.
But something kept him alive.
The scratching started again and came with great noisi-
ness round the corner at him. There was no time to run.
He simply crouched like a sheep that awaits the fatal ax.
Then he saw almost against him the dim outlines of the
wood-basket that the wind had rolled from the front of the
porch.
He was faint with relief.
After an hour of waiting-he thought it was about an
hour-he dared to peep. Just as he was ready, the wind
caught the basket and rolled it back in the other direction.
This time he was not quite so much disturbed; yet he felt
more at ease when he heard the basket bump into one of
the pillars of the porch and then roll off on the sod.
Could he gaze upon a terrifying old woman engaged in
black magic? He steeled himself. While he waited this
time, he thought he heard some strange, almost melodious
sound inside the house.
He lifted his eyes to the slit of brightness at the bottom
of the window. In the full light of a hanging-lamp in the
room, he could see every detail of what was there.
The great dog lay at one side of the fireplace with his
nose extended on the edge of the warm hearth. Genevieve
herself sat in a wide rocking chair directly in front of the
fireplace, her skirts pulled up to her knees to give the
pleasant warmth a chance at her bedraggled stockings, and
in her hands an accordion on which she picked out in ama-
teur fashion the notes of "Carry Me Back to Old Virginny."Was this a preliminary to some more violent part of her
incantations? Less fearful now, Giles waited to see. But
he saw nothing new, and he heard only some other senti-
mental old melodies no more conjuring in tempo.
He crawled back to the edge of the porch and then scooted
down across the front yard to the gate where Sockey and
Rowdy awaited him.
"Did you see her?" Sockey whispered, almost too nerv-
ous for words.
"Yes, I should say I did. Now you go up and look while
I take my turn holding Rowdy."
"What did you see?" Sockey asked. "I want to know
that first."
"Oh, go up and see for yourself," Giles commanded, sud-
denly superior. "Go ahead."
"Oh, if you saw, that's enough," Sockey answered. "I
helped you to see her. I held the dog. You can tell me."
"All right, I'll tell you. But let's get away from here
first, so I won't have to whisper."
Down in the field, as they hurried along, Giles recounted
all that he had done, all that he had seen. He did it so
picturesquely that Sockey declared it was just as good as
if he had gone up and looked for himself.
Giles felt his superiority growing. Sockey had been afraid
and he had not. That was how much difference there was
between them. He had seen with his own eyes. Weren't
eyes made to look with? You could find out things if you
looked. He knew. But Sockey was afraid. And so were
the men who worked the roads. When they talked about
Genevieve they only guessed; they did not know. But he
did. And things did not look so fearful when one knew.
Black magic! Playing an accordion! After Giles went
to bed he had to think it all over again. Genevieve was
related to the beautiful girls and the beautiful women he
had read about in the little bedroom off the kitchen. Shehad known the outside romantic world, which was the only
real one. Here she was, despite her hatchet and her dog,
a kind of missent ambassador from the world about which
he dreamed. A strange ambassador, to be sure. Yet she
had authoritative credentials.CHAPTER X
M
IGHT there not be other ambassadors from the
romantic outside if one only looked for them or
waited a little for them? Perhaps; but they
were slow in revealing themselves. In their absence Giles
tried to fill up the time. He lived through long periods of
reading and cloudy dreaming that alternated with shorter
periods of great activity when the whole of life was feverish.
He mooned. That was a fruitful process, for in it he began
to relate all sorts of things that had seemed unrelated before.
Things hitched themselves together if only one could explore
carefully enough, far enough. That was it! He must go
on a great exploration. Others talked about getting out;
but he meant to do it. He would be getting ready whether
the ambassadors came with information or not.
He must grow up. He was impatient with himself. He
wanted to see just what kind of a figure he would present
when he got away. But whenever he carried on the slightest
self-examination he was disappointed. His feet, he found,
were larger than those of any boy of his acquaintance. He
must bring them into conformity. In his efforts to do so,
he wore shoes that were too small-wore them persistently
until the pain became unendurable. Then he bought shoes
that were large enough, and in defense pretended to take
pride in having larger feet than anybody else. Did not
Abraham Lincoln wear No. 13's? And were not George
Washington's even larger?
But he discovered other defects. One of his aunts came
for a day's visit and assured him that he was almost a man
now, but that his nose was big out of all proportion. Whenhe studied the mirror in an impartial new approach, he
found that his aunt spoke truthfully.
Now what could be done about that? His nose was not
only long but sturdy. He squeezed it up to make it thinner,
but it would not stay. It required relentless pressure. For
a time he spent periods of reading in the kitchen bedroom
with a clothes-pin clamped firmly over the end of his nose.
But this necessitated breathing through the mouth exclu-
sively, and that was an awkward process when one was in
a hurry to get over the pages to see whether Reginald mar-
ried Clementine or not. In the end he had to reconcile him-
self to the big nose, too.
Then he found that his ears were large and ungainly.
Why did they have to stick out from his head like that-a
pair of lop ears, almost. They ought not to be so difficult
to manage. He had heard women talk about straightening
babies' ears. Yet just how?
For days he tied a handkerchief tightly round them when
he read. And when he went to bed he tried to make him-
self sleep on his side, so that the weight of his head would
press firmly on one ear or the other. But he was a restless
sleeper-so restless, in fact, that he usually had kicked most
of the covers off his bed before morning. So it came to pass
that each ear after a fortnight of treatments was just as
large and healthy and firm in its high relief as it had been
before the pressure was applied.
He was in despair. Then one day when he read and
dreamed he overheard Mrs. Burrell say to his mother while
they sat together and darned socks, that women liked men
of large features.
Of course! Why not!
Immediately he began to cultivate pride in his large feet,
his large nose, his large ears. It was for the time a defen-
sive pride; for he boxed the ears of a smaller boy in school
who spoke in general terms of reproach about big feet.Yet it was unmistakably a pride. Who cared what these
little scrubs of kids said, so long as the beautiful girl adored
large features.
He must become wise and get the hang of things, and
keep up with his growing feet and nose and ears. Already
he knew more than anybody else about Genevieve. She
came from the true world of glories toward which he irre-
sistibly moved. Others from the same world might happen
along-others who were more communicative.
Some of them came, too, almost before he was prepared
to receive them. Mrs. Burrell's niece, for example, came
for a visit-for the summer, in truth and she brought the
breath of a fairer region.
Before she had long been established in her aunt's house,
a man from somewhere began to occupy time there also.
He was older than the niece, and she treated him as if he
were infinitely wiser. No doubt he was; his face indicated
that in general he was somewhat the worse for wear. But
his clothes were of stylish cut, and his attitude toward the
Burrells was one of great respect, even deference. He led
them to assume that he was a man of very positive moral
interests. In the evening when the niece in the fluffiest of
city clothes sat at the old square piano and played, her
suitor was invariably moved to lift up his voice in song.
The Burrells' library of music was restricted to Gospel
Hymns, Volumes 1 and 2; so he sang from these with gusto.
Once when he came to pay his regards, he had one arm
in a sling. It seems that he was stepping out of a cab be-
fore the hotel where he lived in Cincinnati, and slipped on
a banana peel. He threw his hand back to save himself
and broke his wrist on the pavement.
Cincinnati! Think of having been in a great place like
that! There was a picture of it, and the suspension bridge
across the Ohio River, in the big geography. Giles spenthours turning over in his mind all that Mr. Leacock had
seen in Cincinnati.
One day he chanced to speak about Mr. Leacock's acci-
dent when he was talking with Sockey.
"Oh-h-h!" Sockey wailed in derision. "Oh-h-h! Banana
rind! Oh, Christ A'mighty!
That dude!"
Then he went into detail. "Bucky Tharp pitched him
head first out of the Last Chance over at Breadless-that's
how he got his broken wrist. Bucky heaved him out of
the saloon and he rolled down the bank clean to the road.
My old man was over there and saw it."
Giles thought he had better investigate Mr. Leacock, as
well as Genevieve.
One day before Mr. Leacock's arm was out of the sling,
he came to Mr. Burrell's house with a piece of white court
plaster across his nose. Giles at once sought Sockey out for
the true explanation.
Sockey felt more nearly Giles's equal now, with this op-
portunity to pass along confidential information. He could
now make up for his somewhat uncourageous part in ex-
ploring the private life of Genevieve. Old Pete Henderson
had been up at Green Valley and Breadless the night before
and had seen a beer bottle intended for somebody else come
into oblique contact with Mr. Leacock's nose.
These misfortunes had befallen Mr. Leacock seven miles
away. And since Mrs. Burrell's niece was not in communi-
cation with anyone in Green Valley or at the Last Chance,
the news of them never came to her chaste ears. So far as
Wiggam's Glory was concerned, the only effect of the mis-
fortunes was to increase Mr. Leacock's vehemence in reli-
gious song. Should, then, the news ever find a way to Wig-
gam's Glory, the absurdity of anything so gross in one of
such exalted interests must be patent even to the most sus-
picious.
Giles liked music. And Mrs. Burrell's niece, just pastnineteen, was having the glorious experience of loving almost
everything male. She thought a boy of about fourteen was
just darling. So she invited Giles to come much when her
beau was absent, and played for him almost everything in
her repertoire. She encouraged him to come almost as much
when her beau was present. And since Mr. Leacock lived
only to do the will of his sweetheart, why, if she liked Giles,
then of course he did too. If he could, therefore, create a
favorable image of himself in Giles's mind, that would be
only one additional means of having his sweetheart catch
some slight breath of his own great unsullied devotion.
Two things Giles liked to have Mr. Leacock do. The
first was to stand beside the piano and sing, "Here Am I,
O Lord, Send Me." Not that Giles was convinced that Mr.
Leacock was an ideal ambassador for the King of kings.
But to his mind there was poetic reality in the picture: A
beautiful young lady of nineteen dressed in soft white, and
touching the yellowed keys of the dark old piano lightly;
and a well-groomed man of graceful build, with the echo of
much experience in his voice, standing at the corner of the
piano and singing with an ardor designed to bury the
coarse, ugly facts of life beyond exhumation. It was almost
as good, Giles thought, as if he had found it in a book.
Then he liked to have Mr. Leacock go hunting. Mr.
Burrell professed to have an unsteady digestion. Mr. Lea-
cock seized upon this misfortune as a reason for prolonging
his visits. He could go to the pleasant woods and kill
tender young squirrels from which broth could be made
that would tempt a stomach given to the extremes of lassi-
tude or rebellion. Frequently he invited Giles to accom-
pany him and direct him to the best woods. In fact, he
sometimes entrusted Giles with the business of shooting the
squirrels.
But Giles was most interested in the hours they whiled
away together under clean, shady maples at the woods'edges. The great world? Why, yes, that was just what
Mr. Leacock knew most about. He had lived for five years
in Cincinnati; he had visited the World's Fair in Chicago;
he had been three times in Philadelphia, twice in New York,
and once in Boston. He had seen the Bunker Hill Monu-
ment-had, in fact, climbed to the top of it. He had seen
Concord, known not only for its grapes, but for its battle.
He had seen the house where Longfellow the poet lived,
and he had walked through the grounds of Harvard Col-
lege, the oldest and most firmly established institution in the
country. If Giles had not possessed the information about
Mr. Leacock which Sockey had passed on to him, he might
well have believed that Mr. Leacock's wornness of spirit
had resulted solely from the extensiveness of his travels.
Anyhow, under the maple trees on the cool clean sod, he
forgot Mr. Leacock's grosser inclinations and saw him only
as a traveler in cities of golden domes and tall monuments
erected in honor of man's nobility of purpose.
One day another emissary from the great poetic world
came. He drove up in a covered wagon to sell Beaver Oil,
Celery Compound, and Lemon Extract. He happened
along just at dinner time, and he said he might as well pay
Giles's mother for dinner as anyone else.
Giles helped him put his angular horse in the guest stall
of the stable. Before they were back at the house he con-
fided to Giles that he had not always been a peddler of
patent medicines. He had once been in college for a year.
But his father died, he himself had gone home to look after
things, and-well, he had never been able to get away again
until he was too old. So there he was.
But he had continued his education. In many respects
he had found a covered wagon superior to college as an
abiding place for the intellect. Under the seat he carried
magazines, and in these, whenever he was free, he read
about whatever engaged men's thinking. When the roadwas too rough for reading, he interested himself in turning
over in his mind all the matters that had impressed him
most profoundly while he had read. Each month he bought
a half-dozen magazines. By the time he had read these and
had digested them somewhat, another month's output was
ready.
He showed Giles copies of Harper's Magazine, the At-
lantic Monthly, and others. Many had accumulated under
the seat. He disliked the thought of throwing them away;
in fact, would not do so. But soon he would be needing
space, and he begged Giles to accept an armful for later
reading.
This traveling specialist in affairs professed openly to be
also a specialist in minds. Before he left reluctantly in the
middle of the next afternoon, he told Giles that he had been
studying his father and mother. They were of the idealistic
type-he was sure of that; evidently artistic, also. He had
not come upon a man and wife of so much of the same
temper for many months. Their son, if he might be par-
doned for speaking of the matter, was very unusual. He
was a combination of the precocious and the ugly-duckling.
A most amazing combination. Some day he might reason-
ably be expected to do something unusual. In fact, he was
quite certain to, if he ever did anything at all.
Immediately Giles was filled with a new and for the
moment-resistless enthusiasm. There was nothing very
unusual for a boy to do in the Wiggam's Glory area, was
there? Well, he would find out. He wanted to do some-
thing grim, something blood-curdling. He began by pur-
chasing second-hand, and in great secrecy, a revolver of
small caliber but long barrel. Did he mean to kill some-
body? That was just yet too far ahead to be thought about
in detail. But he enjoyed the sharp ring of a revolver shot,
and the ability which he developed soon for taking off a
dime-somebody else's dime-from its edgewise position ona post or rail. When autumn came he made the rounds at
butchering time and shot fat pigs for the neighbors. Using
a big long rifle for killing a helpless pig! It was an ab-
surdity. It was too impersonal. He had a way of his own.
He threw down a little shelled corn by the fence and when
a fat pig had waddled up and eaten all the grains he could
find and looked up inquiringly for more, Giles plumped a
bullet at close range into the depression just above his small,
heavy eyes, and the pig was at the end of his journey.
Before summer his ferocity increased. After sheep-
shearing when farmers were beginning to kill muttons, he
aspired to kill one by cutting its neck off with one clear
swing of the ax, as he had once seen an expert butcher do.
All spring he practiced on chickens when his mother wished
to have them killed. One day he overheard Mr. Burrell say
that he meant to kill a mutton that afternoon, and saun-
tered over. He volunteered to do the killing himself in his
own way.
"Can you do it, boy?" Mr. Burrell asked.
"Give me your ax and I'll show you."
He decapitated the thin-necked, submissive ewe as skill-
fully as if he had been trained in the French Revolution.
If only the beautiful girl should happen along! Wouldn't
she shudder at his coolness? She would turn her lovely
face away and say, "Oh, how can you?" She would have
to admit that no boy of his age anywhere else did such stag-
gering things as he was able and ready to do.
That autumn another ambassador came or at least an
emissary. The new principal of the three-room school at
Culbertson proposed having a high school where all kinds
of romantic knowledge hitherto little known in the region
would be made somewhat available. Giles might go.
At once he began to see the Wiggam's Glory school ob-
jectively. It was a place he meant to leave. It was no
longer any essential part of himself. One morning whenhe walked with full confidence down to the main thorough-
fare, a half-dozen pupils from No. 5 Company Row came
along. Gustie Malloy was one of them. She was the oldest
of the four or five big girls now in school.
"Hello, Giles!" she said.
She had said that to him before, a thousand times at an
underestimate. But this morning there was something no-
ticeably pleasant in her voice. He took a second glance at
her. She was almost a pretty girl. If she had been a little
more careful about washing her neck and fastening her dress
together at the waist and keeping all the buttons on her
shoes, she would have been.
And Lydia Pendleton, who walked with Cora Dever just
behind Gustie, was pretty-undeniably. Strange how he
had never noticed that before. She had grown much since
the day when Giles had come upon her in her frightened
and innocent nudity at the swimming hole. Her flaxen hair
was lustrous in the sunlight, and her bright blue eyes and
the white and pink of her face gave her an appearance of
coming into bloom.
At noon while Giles leaned on the window sill after the
manner of all big boys in school and watched "the kids" at
play, Gustie came and put her elbows on the sill beside
him. And Lydia, divested of all false modesties by fourteen
years in No. 5 Company Row, took her place between them
and leaned on both.
Giles felt the warmth of her pliant self close against him,
and glanced over at the gracefully curved, plump forearm
that came just into view across the top of his shoulder. He
hoped she would not move.
"Hear you're thinking of going to the new school over at
Culbertson when it's finished," Gustie said, careful to put
all the g's on the ends of her words as if she were now
grown up and educated.
"Who told you that?" Giles asked.I've asked if they'd
But I hadn't told any-
"A little bird. Why, ain't it so?"
"Oh, I'm thinking about it a little.
let me come in case I wanted to.
body yet."
"You don't have to, around here. News just floats away
in every direction on the sulphur smoke. But just the same
we'd hate to see you go."
"Yes," Lydia said in concurrence, and she pressed herself
ever so gently against him. "Just when we're beginning to
have good times."
Gustie reflected with a little more detachment: "Well, I
don't know as I'd stay if I had to decide. There ain't much
in a little one-horse school like this for anybody as smart
as Giles is. Wish I was smart enough to go myself."
Giles felt his face coloring. This from Gustie who was
reputed to have the most inclusive vocabulary of cuss-words
in No. 5 Company Row! He had a great new respect for
her judgment and her aspiration.
"All the same," Lydia said, using an argument that has
stood the test of time, "I hope you stay here."
But when the new three-room schoolhouse at Culbertson
was at last painted and varnished and the new fresh-smell-
ing yellow desks were screwed to the floor, and it was an-
nounced that the building would be opened for use after
Christmas, Giles took leave of Wiggam's Glory and turned
his face toward Culbertson.
One entire room was devoted to the high school; and the
principal, who was also the superintendent, was to give all
his time to the teaching of high-school subjects. This great
educational innovation would enable those who finished the
course to secure a teacher's certificate with fewer hazards;
and, in addition, it might, if one unit of credit should be
regarded as just as good as another, enable graduates to
enter college. Many residents of the region thought it
criminal to maintain such an extravagance; and others weresure it was immoral to bring boys and girls of that age to-
gether in such a public enterprise. Yet there was the school,
and there was the teacher-a zealous, intelligent little man
of thirty-five who came with much news for Giles from the
world outside.
Still for a time Giles was not comfortable. He needed a
comradely word from Sockey or Cross-eye or Bugs or Lydia
or Gustie. The boys in the high school, and they numbered
twenty-one, saw in him only a lank country-jake. Did he
not walk more than two miles every day through snow or
mud in order to attend school? And did he not once come
with a stalk of timothy hay sticking up under the collar of
his coat? How the whole school snickered while he stood
at the blackboard in the front of the room, the timothy hay
fully visible on the back of his neck! Could anything be
more incongruous in the heart of a town of six hundred
souls where men earned their genteel bread by keeping feed
stores and meat shops and saloons, and working in livery
stables and saw mills? Although Giles went to classes, he
knew he was not in school. He was not a part of it; he was
only on the outside looking in.
His boisterous young spirit was pained more than he ever
admitted. Why did they not take him in? Did they not
know that he meant to get away and do as much as any of
them? He was eager to have them feel that he was like
them. They looked comfortable and companionable and
at ease among themselves. It sickened him to think that
he was missing so much when their circle was closed against
him. He kept all suggestion of his ostracism from his
father and mother. In the evening after he had done the
chores, he walked back to town in order to attend whatever
church socials or protracted meetings happened to be in
progress just as if he were one of the group now sought out
for the adornment of all sorts of occasions. But he wasnever closer than the outer edge of anything. To his school-
mates he was only a pleasant jake from the country.
Fortunately for appearances, the Methodist preacher at
the moment in charge of the church was not fiery in his
crusading. Under his ministry sinners came to repentance
with great deliberation. So what the meeting lacked in dis-
patch had to be made up for in extent. For more than six
weeks it provided Giles with regular evening diversion. He
brushed his clothes, combed his hair, cautiously slipped his
long seven-shooter into his inside coat-pocket, and strode
away in the darkness-soapy clean, buoyant, alone.
Outside the church door the town boys stood about and
made wise cracks at the expense of everybody who entered.
Giles always tried to stand among them for a time as if he
were one of them, in order to escape humiliation when he
stepped up to the door.
One night after he had waited among them for a time,
he moved forward to go in. A sleek young gentleman a
little older than he was seized him by the arm. "Don't go
in there, my man! What do you want to go in there for?"
Giles tried gently to free himself.
"No," the young buck commanded as if he were address-
ing a child. “No!"
Giles jerked free, and in the same movement let fly one
of Sockey's uppercuts to the chin. His adversary went
sprawling in the early March mud.
But he was up in a flash. "You God-damned hayseed!"
he shouted in the approved manner.
Giles stepped down and stood waiting for the onrush just
where the pale light shone through the frosted glass of the
long transom above the double doors. His adversary was
full of preliminary words: "Just you stand there, damn you,
where I can see you for five seconds, and somebody will
have to bring the undertaker."He backed off into an area of light made by a window
on a hitching rack. "Just stand there till I get this-"
He pecked and pounded as if he were forcibly loading a
six-shooter. "Keep him there, fellas. I've got a ca'tridge
stuck in this gun."
"Oh, is that the trouble?" Giles asked, a mood of great
unafraidness sweeping aside all his more customary subor-
dination. "If it's a gun you want, here, I've got a good
seven-shooter that I kill pigs with. The ca'tridges never
get stuck in it. If you want to shoot somebody, this will
do it for you."
The crowd could see the glitter of the bright weapon in
the light from the transom. His adversary could see, too,
and vanished in the darkness.
"Don't want it?" Giles called, in full possession of the
deprecatory manner himself. "All right, then, I guess I'll
go in to church."
If only the beautiful girl could have seen that! That was
out of a book-like Mr. Leacock's singing over at Mr.
Burrell's.
While the minister prayed drearily for the salvation of
a sinful people, Giles felt readier than ever for the great
poetry far from Wiggam's Glory. The school principal,
nifty in his tailor-made clothes and nose-glasses, sat near,
with his head respectfully but not too humbly bowed. Giles
wished that he might have the company of the principal
without the impenetrable company of the school. The prin-
cipal's little head was alive with much that was heartening.
If only the minister would bring his laborious prayer to
a close! He had come from the outside somewhere, but he
brought no news. And Giles just now was interested in
tidings.E
CHAPTER XI
ARLY ONE autumn another ambassador came.
This man was erect and tall, he wore on week-
days-a striking gray suit, his hair was abundant
and clean white, and his round beard was such as Stonewall
Jackson's might have been had he lived to be sixty or sixty-
five. Though he was nervous by temperament and emo-
tional interests, he was at ease in the presence of others, no
matter how important these others might seem to be.
Across his left cheek was the scar of a saber cut he had
received in an encounter with a distant Virginia cousin of
his when they chanced to come face to face one day at
Bloody Angle. He was a fighter when he was in the Union
Army, and he was a fighter now.
On the day when he assumed charge of the Methodist
church at Culbertson, he announced that he was there to
represent the King of kings, and that the devil would have
to leave town before spring. He believed in fighting fairly;
so he hereby served notice on all indifferent and ungodly
men and women, too-that he meant to bring them into
the heavenly fold. He did not mean to be in a splutter. He
meant to take abundant time for complete preparation. But
when the appointed day came, sinners would have to yield.
He was in the habit of accomplishing what he planned.
The prospect of something dramatic set the entire town
talking and speculating. Lazy sinners who made a practice
of writing back to the town that a preacher had come from
in order to get reports to his discredit which would justify
them in not going to church, hurried off letters to the
last parish of the Reverend Randolph Avery. But whenthe replies came, they afforded little comfort. He had a
good record. The only serious charge brought against him
was that he dressed well. Too much could not be made of
this, however, since there was full evidence that he always
paid for everything he wore.
Something electric quickened the street whenever this
graceful old newcomer in light gray clothes stepped from
the parsonage door and walked toward the post office. He
seemed to know where he was going. Proprietors who
waited in shop doors for women to come and buy pork or
coffee or thread or pie-pans found pleasure in speaking to
him. "Good morning, Reverend," they said with a gener-
ous wave of the hand. When he passed the Blue Front
saloon, or the Gilt Edge, or the Purple Moon, bar-flies who
sat on rickety chairs out in front and dreamed away the
pleasant October mornings lifted their pimply faces and
watched him as long as they were able to concentrate their
attention on a moving object.
One day when he passed the Blue Front, Cy Allerton
lounged in a decrepit armchair propped back against the
door post.
"Well, Parson," he said, "I hear you mean to lock all our
saloons up."
"Oh, no! No, not at all," Mr. Avery replied. "I never
thought of such a thing. But I mean to dry them up. Be-
fore I get through with this town there won't be enough men
with a thirst for liquor left in the place to pay the federal
tax on one saloon, to say nothing of three or four. If people
don't drink, why, then there won't be any saloons, will
there?"
Cy's mind was too habitually befuddled to be certain
whether this was good reasoning or not. But in deference
to a minister he admitted that something of the kind might
be true.
"And I expect you to be one of my first converts," Mr.Avery went on to say. "But whether you are or not, you'll
want to come round to the church and see others repent.
It will be a great sight. I hereby invite you."
The minister walked on down the street. Cy got his fat
self out of the chair and waddled into the saloon.
"What kind of a God-damned town is this a-gettin' to
be?" he demanded of the proprietor. "This new parson
says he aims to dry all the saloons up. He said nobody'd
be left who'd care for a drink by the time he was through
with the place. And damned if he didn't invite me around
to see how it's done. Maybe that ain't brass, huh? Better
give me a whisky 'fore I lose my taste for it."
Mr. Avery served similar notice on other sinners. When
they looked skeptical, he always added: "Well, just come
round and see for yourselves. We are known by our works.
And if I have no power of good works left in me, then it
will be your turn to rejoice."
Giles watched all the preparations for battle without
thinking of himself as a mark. He only looked with inter-
est toward witnessing a dramatic struggle.
Every Sunday Mr. Avery announced that the day of sal-
vation was coming. What he was doing now was only pre-
liminary to the great battle for souls which would begin on
the first day of the New Year. He asked everyone who
came to church to spread the news. He wanted the people
from near and far to come and see the wonder-works of
the Lord.
The women began saving their nickels. By Thanksgiv-
ing they were at work on new dresses designed to bear the
inspection of a house of worship. By Christmas all the
girls in high school had provided themselves with an extra
dress or two of becoming length so that they might stand
in the front row of the choir beside the pulpit. By New
Year's morning everybody for three or four miles roundabout was watching the weather to see whether it would be
mud or snow for feet that night.
Giles arrived twenty minutes or more before the hour set
for the service. He slipped into one of the two or three
places still unoccupied, and waited. He wished he was a
little farther away from the pulpit.
At first there was enough whispering and moving of feet
to make the atmosphere normal. But as the clock on the
side wall pushed its long hand toward the hour fixed upon,
and the people kept crowding in quietly, a hush of ex-
pectancy spread over the house. Just five minutes before
the hour, the Reverend Mr. Avery came in with a Bible
and a song-book under his arm. He walked with great im-
pressiveness to the pulpit, placed the books on the desk,
and then turned and kneeled at one of the high-backed red
plush chairs against the wall. Until he stood up, the silence
was absolute.
Now they were going to see the fighter in action.
He announced a hymn. The choir, long practiced and
eager, began with such abandon that the congregation
joined in almost as unrestrainedly. As soon as Mr. Avery
heard the singing of that song, he knew he was going to
have converts.
He prayed eloquently. He read a short scripture les-
son. He asked all to join in the singing of two other hymns.
While everyone sang, Mr. Avery stood rapt behind the
desk, his face uplifted. Then he was ready to preach.
He took as a text the words: "How shall we escape, if we
neglect so great salvation?"
Aye, that was a question for Culbertson to answer.
The residents of the region had never before heard the
possibilities of the future set forth with such awful power.
Mr. Avery did not shrink in his words to the people. He
depicted them as a community of men and women for whom
life consisted solely of eating and drinking and enjoying thelusts of the flesh. In fact, some of them had so degen-
erated that they no longer had a good healthy appetite even
for the gross things of the flesh. They had lost sight of
the ends of life, its rewards, its punishments.
Giles thought there was a grain of truth in what Mr.
Avery said, especially in what he said about losing sight of
the ends of life. Give it to them!
There was a better way, Mr. Avery went on to tell them,
the way of escape. If only they would wake up! If only
they would open their eyes and see! But what if they did
not? What if this better way were not chosen?
Here he was most at home. He pictured God as a merci-
ful God, yes. But they must never forget that he was a
vengeful God, also! Men and women who frittered away
their lives could not hope for anything except an eternal
punishment too frightful to contemplate. Yet he proceeded
to reveal the horrors, and to let the congregation contem-
plate them. They were more terrible than the seethings
and thunderings and roarings that Giles had once read about
in the big book on the clock-shelf. They were more ghastly
than anyone in Culbertson had ever thought the work of the
Lord could be. As he entered farther and farther into the
future, those who had thought he was a little too well
dressed, a little too bright, a little too intense to be dead in
earnest, lost their prejudice and all their sense of smallness.
He made the most of his opportunities. "See here!" he
cried and waved a page of the morning Centerville Gazette
high above his head. "Dare anyone say I do not speak the
truth?"
He then read about the sudden death of ten men in a
mine over in West Virginia. "They were snuffed out!
Snuffed out! And only a part of their number were saved
men. The others had neglected the great salvation."
He had other specific instances, rising in directness of
appeal to his best one, his last one. "As I came to thishouse of worship this evening I passed the Blue Front
saloon. They were just carrying out the body of old Cy
Allerton. He had dropped dead at the bar after a day of
drinking in celebration of the New Year. What an end!
He neglected the great salvation. He was the first person I
invited to this service when I came here last September.
He might have been here instead of where he was. But he
neglected the great salvation. Oh, lost! Lost! Lost!"
These last words he pronounced as if they rolled rever-
berating from the farthest corner of the skies.
He made a dramatic pause.
"Now we mean to have a great Decision Day. Better
not wait for that. Next month or even next week or to-
morrow-may be too late. We walk about confidently or
carelessly as if we were going to be here on the earth for-
ever, and to-morrow we are gone and forgotten as if we
had never lived at all. To-night we are gathered here, full
of health and strength, and we expect to keep on coming,
for another day, and another, and another. But as I stand
here in the awful presence of God, we shall not all be here
to-morrow night-or any other night. Somebody will be
sick, somebody will be called away to the deathbed of a
relative or friend, somebody himself caught up by death.
Never again as long as this earth revolves about the sun and
the stars pursue their way will this congregation be gathered
together again just as it is this evening. There will be modi-
fications; there will be subtractions. And oh, if you should
happen to be the one snatched away!
"So you had better not wait till Decision Day. But some
will wait. And if you are one of those tempted to do so,
prepare to come then and confess your sins on that day set
specially apart."
This was his plan. Every night there would be oppor-
tunity for those who decided not to risk waiting. And there
would be opportunity to-night.He announced the hymn of invitation. After the long
tenseness, the seats all over the long church squeaked as
the people stood up. Then the choir sang as if it had been
transported to another world:
"Should the death angel knock at thy chamber
In the still watch of the night
Say, are you ready? Oh, are you ready?"
A dozen disengaged themselves from the awed assem-
blage and went forward to the altar. Among them was
Pewee Dunkle.
Giles was somewhat awed himself, but he could not help
reflecting upon Pewee's fear of disasters in general. It
seemed logical that he should wish to make himself secure
against the disaster of all disasters.
Another who knelt and prayed in fright was Stub Ma-
lone, from over by the Gravelly Run schoolhouse. He was
almost as old as Giles, but until to-night he had never con-
sidered the possibility of a disordered universe, and the
thought surprised him and overwhelmed him. He had never
tried before to pray, and he was not certain what to pray
for now. But he knelt; and he buried his face in fear.
Two or three at the mourners' bench professed quick
conversion. After their shouts of victory there was a long
lull in which only the songs of the choir and the prayers at
the altar kept the congregation engaged. There were no
other conversions that night.
The minister was not dismayed. Here were nearly a
dozen to work on for the rest of the week-or longer, if
necessary. When he dismissed the meeting, he assured
these unconverted seekers that God would not hold them
guilty, should they now be snatched from the transitory
earth, since they had expressed a desire to be saved. But
they must seek on.For miles round about Culbertson the news spread that the
minister was in earnest. He was going to have everybody
make a definite and open decision, either for hell or for
heaven. Swiftly the entire community was caught up by a
great spirit of fear. The earth was unsteady under people's
feet; the heavens hung heavily just above their heads.
The world as they had been aware of it as a familiar thing,
now became alien and unreal. The most cheerful sounds
produced by winter carried overtones of doom. When
roosters sat in wagon-sheds in the scanty warmth of the
January sun and crowed, in defiance of the weather, as they
had done since Wiggam's Glory and Culbertson came into
existence, there was in their voices a wailing plaintiveness
that few had detected before. When cows bawled in neigh-
bors' farmyards, they sounded as if they proclaimed an im-
pending doom. Work at the mines was not steady, but
when coal did rumble and bound and rattle over the screens
at the tipples, it only reminded men of the threat which the
hill held over the head of everyone who dared to work be-
neath.
Coarse-fibered men who had never before spoken of the
church except with disrespect, now began to think of it as a
terrible but necessary haven to which they should flee. If
it could save them from the awfulness of eternal punishment,
then it was not something to be spoken of lightly.
By the end of the first week a new terror invaded the
common mind. Stub Malone, though only fourteen years
old, and quiet at that, could not get converted. He had
tried every night to pray for himself, and others had lent
their aid. Yet he could not come through to any releasing
sense of forgiveness, any joy over sins that had been swept
away.
Whisperings flashed about. The case was very mysteri-
ous. Many felt that it was the solemn duty of the entire
community to get Stub converted. Others were content tomake gossip about him. It was all so dark, so completely
out of reach of human understanding.
A local preacher who attended these meetings wondered
if Stub might not have committed the unpardonable sin.
At once that view prevailed. It was comforting to believe
that somebody else's sins had been blacker than one's own.
Stub himself eventually came into possession of this opinion
and at once wanted to know what the unpardonable sin was.
Nobody seemed to know-unless it was "the sin against the
Holy Ghost"-yet all agreed that Stub had doubtless com-
mitted it.
The thought of having done something that put him be-
yond the pale of the redeemable, regardless of what he
might try to do about it, drove Stub to distraction. He tried
all the harder to get converted and disprove the contention.
But the desperation of his effort only pushed him farther
and farther from any feeling of glorious peace.
He became a public figure. Everybody pointed him out
and said, "Wouldn't it be terrible to know that you were
bound for hell and that nothing could be done to save you?"
Stub thought so himself. He quit walking across the
hills to the Culbertson church. He was afraid to face his
more fortunate redeemable fellow beings. He walked about
in the fields and by-roads, stopped with his hands in his
pockets and gazed off dejectedly into the blank skies until
he felt the heat of hell upon him, and then hurried on, only
to stop again when he had to try to the uttermost to think
once more of some way of escape.
Soon he could no longer face even the blank skies. He
resolved to commit suicide. Why should he live on and on
in a hell on earth if the great and terrible hell were his ulti-
mate destination anyhow?
He picked out a place in the river where he would take
the plunge. But the weather suddenly turned bitter cold
and froze the river over. So Stub had to wait. He couldnot eat. His nights were but an endless series of wild
dreams. Bats snapped past his head. Snakes crawled over
his coverlet. Strange creatures, unlike any he had ever seen,
peered at him from around corners everywhere. His par-
ents, almost as much distressed as he was, and humiliated
at the thought of having a son who stood beyond forgive-
ness, decided to keep up appearances by sending for a doc-
tor-old Dr. Seabright. Dr. Seabright came, examined the
boy, and said that medicine would do him no good. He did
not mean, however, to dismiss the case on purely profes-
sional grounds. He undertook to conduct an inquiry into
Stub's private life.
Stub made a vow before the awful God who had spurned
him that he would tell Dr. Seabright everything bad that
he had ever done since the first day he could remember.
Dr. Seabright towered above him, perplexed but not utterly
serious. When Stub had completed his list, Dr. Seabright
smiled and said, "Why, God Almighty, boy, the reason you
can't get your sins forgiven is that you never committed
any. If I had your record, I'd regard myself as past the
Great Gatekeeper already. My only worry would be to
find people who ranked high enough to keep me company.
Don't worry any more about your sins unless you first com-
mit some."
The burden rolled from Stub's shoulders. Nevertheless,
the view prevailed at the church that he ought not to be ac-
cepted into full membership unless he gave more satisfac-
tory evidence of a new birth.
In the meantime the major activities went irresistibly on.
Every night there were new penitents; every night, new
converts. Giles was unable to say just how he felt about
the entire enterprise. There were so many ways of looking
at things.
One evening while the choir sang the hymn of invitation,
Old Shiner Humphrey, completely saturated with liquor,passed the church, heard the singing, thought he was in
heaven, and wanted to see more of it. He wandered in, his
nerves a-tingle until every object, every sound, every move-
ment, seemed momentous, and hazily took his place at the
end of a seat beside Giles.
At once a personal worker was at his side. In brotherly
fashion he put his arm about Shiner's shoulders and asked
tearfully: "Shiner, don't you know that Jesus died for
you?"
Shiner's spirit was already at the unsteady height where
he found it difficult to make close distinctions between the
figurative and the literal.
"Don't you, Shiner? Don't you? Die-e-ed for you! Oh,
don't you know?"
Shiner inclined his head and replied in a husky voice.
"How's that?" the personal worker asked.
Again Shiner replied, this time with a great effort to make
his thick voice understandable. And Giles heard what he
said.
Horrified, the worker turned and walked away. But
Giles had to smile-despite the solemnity of the occasion.
Yet sometimes Giles could not smile. The discrepancies
he encountered were not ridiculous. One night as he passed
the house at the edge of the town where Pewee Dunkle now
lived, he heard a rhythmic, intoning voice. He stopped in
the road and listened. It was Pewee, conducting family
worship as prescribed by Mr. Avery.
Giles wanted ever so little to smile at Pewee's pronuncia-
tion; but he could not. Then he decided that he did not
wish to. Pewee was reading: "Now there are di-v-er-sities-
Now there are diversities of gifts, but the same Spirit.
"And there are differences of differences of a-d-min-is-
tra-tions are differences of administrations, but the same
Lord.
"And there are di-di-ver-si-ties of op-op-e-r-a-tions-diversities of operations, but it is the same God which work-
eth all in all.
"But the man- But the man-man-i-f-e-s-t-a-t-i-o-n-
manifestation of the Spirit is given to every man to profit
with-to profit with-w-i-th-a-l-to profit withal."
What strange, compelling power could send Pewee with
countless haltings and blunderings through a chapter of
Corinthians like that?
Giles walked on in the winter quiet. Just where was he,
anyhow? He had thought he knew where he was going;
the way had been growing clearer. And now this dramatic
and overshadowing something was upon him.
A great owl set up a disquieting "Whoo, whoo! Whoo,
whoo! Whoo, whoo! Whoo, whoo-ah!" on the steeple of
a tall white-oak above the road. Giles slipped his long-
barreled revolver from his pocket and sent a bullet singing
off some dead limb in the top of the tree. It was pleasant
to hear something worldly. So he fired another shot in the
same direction. Then he pushed on homeward. He must
get to bed, and get up and do the chores, and go to school,
and come home and do the chores again, so that he would
be ready to walk back to town-in case his father and
mother did not go and see how many got converted, and
who they were.T
CHAPTER XII
HE HEAT of Mr. Avery's campaign became
greater every day. Men and women, boys and girls,
long starved for something more dramatic than their
drab everyday existence, yielded themselves with little re-
sistance to this thunderous drama of Mr. Avery's, which,
he never ceased to warn them, was likely enough to be their
last. Giles turned in every direction in the hope of seeing
out from under the pall of an impending doom, but every-
where he found himself confronted by some embodiment of
the revival spirit.
One day in high school it confronted him there. He had
eaten luncheon-it was no longer "dinner"-from his half-
gallon tin bucket, and was leaning on the window sill in the
large upstairs classroom enjoying the sunlight that was
bringing a late January thaw. Three girls who had hurried
back from luncheon at home walked up, arms around waists,
and waited until he noticed them.
"We have something to ask you, Giles," one of the girls
said most pleasantly.
Giles turned to face them. He was overcome. They had
never before called him Giles. They had never before
treated him with consideration, to say nothing of intimacy.
So abashed and so enraptured was he at the sight of the
friendly, radiant faces, that he could do no more than stand
for a moment and look-a great, lanky boy with large nose
and ears, a shock of blond hair and a pair of gray-blue
eyes, confronting three cheerful girls of his own age. Theda
Kistler, slender, bright-eyed, and with much dark hair to
set off her attractive clear face, dominated the group notonly because she was in the middle, but because she was a
ready spokesman.
"We want to ask you about Decision Day," she said.
"Nearly all of us that have not joined are going up that
night. We came back early to have a good chance to ask
you to go up with us. We are all going up together."
First one of the other girls added a few words, then the
other.
Giles stood silent, but his mind was experiencing a hurri-
cane. "All your problems solved," something whispered.
"You can unite with the church, put aside all this thumping
dread of a vague doom, and at the same time become one of
the school group whose activities you have watched with
hungry longing."
"You'd better," Theda urged. She saw that something
was rushing through his mind. "We'll all go up together."
Giles moved his lips, but they were dry. "I-I'll see," he
said awkwardly.
"Yes, you must," Theda urged in a voice that expressed
both zeal and witchery. "We're going to sit together. We
hope to have two or three seats full from high school."
They saved him further awkwardness by swinging pleas-
antly away. He leaned on the window sill again, his face
warm with surprise and a new pride. He thought for many
minutes. The girls were over there at the other end of the
room, and no other boys or girls had yet come back. It
would be easy and it would be pleasant just to go over and
tell them that he would go up with them.
But he waited; why, he did not quite know. Something
too far inside himself to be recognizable as a reason pro-
vided enough resistance to keep him where he was.
In the afternoon, he recited with alertness and a quicker
intelligence than was customary. Whenever he chanced to
cast his eyes toward the three girls, their faces were alive
with friendliness. He was feverish with a complicated newlonging that he made little effort to analyze. It was enough
just to experience it. To be for once one of the dominant
group! It would be worth passing through much in order to
have that feeling of rightness.
When he started home he was a trifle less feverish. And
when he was out in the country, away from Culbertson, he
felt the sudden familiar ease of comprehension which always
came to him when he was alone under the right circum-
stances. The arguments which the girls had presented now
seemed artificial and wholly inadequate. He could be rid
of the impending doom! But could he? And if he could,
would he be any happier with the feeling that he had sold
out to fear? And the girls weren't they also selling out to
the same fear? Or were they swayed by less important
impulse still? Something within whispered until it was al-
most an audible voice in the quiet of the road, "Wait!
Wait!" Where were these voices coming from? One whis-
pered how pleasant it would be; and now this one said no.
How many selves did he consist of, anyhow? The voice
that said wait was more authentic, however many there
might be.
But waiting could not free him from the rush of the re-
vival spirit. It pressed itself into every corner of the neigh-
borhood, into the private life of every individual. So great
was its force that it caught up one full-fledged Catholic and
whirled him to the mourners' bench, where he declared he
made a more inclusive enumeration of his sins than he had
ever made at confession. The Methodists rejoiced more
over this one convert from another fold than over any ten
that had come from no fold at all. They could not only
snatch souls from the devil, but take them away from com-
peting denominations.
The Catholics took much the same view of the matter.
It would have been bad enough to see Jake go straight to
hell without so much as extreme unction to comfort him orhis family, but it was a catastrophe that reflected upon the
entire Mother Church when a member in good standing
wandered into the Methodist meeting house and surren-
dered. A mission was the logical answer to a revival. So
there was soon an awakening in St. Bernard's that was di-
rectly attributable to the activities of the Reverend Mr.
Avery.
Other denominations were likewise spurred to new activ-
ity. The United Brethren proclaimed a salvation that was
just as good as that preached by the Methodists. In fact,
Mr. Avery said that was just the trouble. The United
Brethren were only freshwater Methodists. The Reverend
Mr. Morgan, of the United Brethren, retorted with religious
warmth that so far as Brother Avery's brand of Methodism
was concerned, it was only warmed-over Episcopalianism;
that if the Episcopal clergy in Maryland, Delaware, and
Virginia had not resigned their posts at the time of the
Revolutionary War and gone back to England, the Meth-
odists never would have had any congregations to start with.
Still he wished Brother Avery great fruitage; and Mr.
Avery, in turn, finished off by wishing well to Mr. Morgan
or to anyone else who battled for the militant faith.
The awakened community attracted another minister who
preached still another gospel. All the others, he declared,
Protestants and Catholics alike, preached only creeds. He
preached the uncontaminated, unadulterated gospel of the
New Testament, and that alone. It soon developed, though,
that he had a three-fold creed. He believed in hell's fire,
baptism by immersion, and the free and unlimited coinage
of silver. It was difficult for Mr. Avery to look upon him
cordially. Yet here he was, one more harvester in the
freshly stirred vineyard.
But Mr. Avery kept in the lead. He was resourceful. He
organized not only his reputation but his new converts. He
announced a ramifying system of house-to-house prayermeetings conducted in part by veterans, in part by the new
recruits. Anybody was welcome; anybody was eligible to
act as host or hostess. They were ready to save souls of
any kind, and they were ready to save them anywhere. But
it usually turned out that the meetings were held in the
houses of Methodists. It was their revival, was it not?
At any time they might descend upon a house for a half-
hour of singing and praying. Individual families must not
lose interest in the revival. The attendance at the night
meetings held in the church must not decline.
One afternoon when Decision Day was not far off, Giles
enjoyed the spiritual freedom of walking home from school
by himself and noting the arrival of some spring birds weeks
before their time. He met Mr. Burrell, who was hurrying
to town to get an emergency supply of sugar and coffee. A
dozen crusaders and the minister were coming for a half-
hour just at supper time.
"I understand," he told Giles, "that they are coming over
to your house right after supper, before they go to the
church."
So Mr. Avery and the praying crusaders were after him!
He had always looked with interest and with an element
of admiration upon the militant parson. Nor had he been
scornful of what the church members in general were at-
tempting to do. But now he began to experience a revulsion
of feeling. Why could they not let him alone? Were they
suddenly so much better than he was? Something sug-
gestive of the hothouse instead of the natural growth of the
out-of-doors, made him want to run away from the entire
proceeding.
He had read that afternoon a poem by Shelley which the
principal had dismissed as too difficult for high school, and
at the supper table he talked with more than ordinary free-
dom about this and many other matters. His mother was
interested, for she had once read the same poem. And hisfather made many inquiries about an experiment in physics
which he was carrying through with some apparatus which
the principal had contrived. Once, in a lull, his mother
asked casually if he meant to go to church. He glanced at
her and at his father to make sure that they were not in
some conspiracy against him. He lived with them. He
knew that in a last extremity he would turn to them before
anyone else in the world. Yet in everyday life where any
crisis short of the greatest was concerned, he never felt un-
restrained in their presence. Something in their numb in-
scrutability prevented the intimacies more characteristic of
the well-to-do. He often wished that his mother would
burst into tears and throw her arms round him as she had
done when his grandfather died, or that she would laugh
with him as if nothing held her back. He took a second
glance at them. No, he would not make any reference to
the prayer meeting.
While his mother washed the dishes and put them away,
he busied himself for a few brief minutes in the front room,
and then slipped out and hurried to the stable and climbed
into the haymow. From the depths of the aromatic hay he
could watch through a wide crack and see the approaches to
the house as long as there was light.
Before it was entirely dark he heard voices. Soon he
could discern the outlines of a dozen or more figures almost
alongside the corncrib. A small man stumbled over the
frozen ground.
"They will be surprised," some one said.
"Yes, and he will be the most surprised one of the lot,"
somebody else answered.
"We must deal patiently with him," another said. Giles
thought the last voice was Mr. Avery's.
So the plans were all made for closing in on him that very
night, were they?
He waited until he heard the cheerful, noisy salutationsat the front door of the house, and then, after the crusaders
had all gone inside and the door was closed again, the old
claygrinder's steady chewing of hay in the stall directly
under him. He slid down through the mow-hole to the floor
of the stable and then stepped outside. He could hear
nothing.
Cautiously he walked toward the house. Before he was
half-way there, he heard the group singing, "Pass Me Not,
O Gentle Saviour." He had been tricked, but since he had
not been cornered, he was able to smile a little and enjoy a
certain poetry in the song.
He stood in the side yard in the friendly darkness and
listened to all that went on inside the house. The lay cru-
saders sang, and several of them prayed. He heard Major
Holcomb-who conducted the meeting in order to conserve
Mr. Avery for the evening service at the church-call awk-
wardly, "Will Sister Mary Jane Thompson pray over the
river?" Soon the voice of the Mrs. Thompson who lived on
the other side of the river was lifted high in strident appeal.
Then Major Holcomb asked Mr. Avery to close with a brief
word of prayer.
Giles crept up to the window and peeped in. Men and
women had knelt by every chair, some by the jambs of the
fireplace, and some by the square stairway in the corner.
The minister himself knelt by the table in the middle of the
room and the shaded lamp threw a full circle of bright light
upon him.
Giles heard Mr. Avery give the Lord of Hosts a full ac-
count of a lusty young man named Dabney. According
to Mr. Avery this young man was not a bad sort. In many
ways he was an unusual person. But there was a great stub-
bornness in his spirit which ought to be broken. He needed
to have the experience of complete surrender. He ought to
confess his many sins-though they seemed not to be at all
grievous-and join the church as others were doing. Heimplored the Lord of Hosts to smite this young man with
such an awful conviction of his sins, that he would surren-
der himself along with his many high-school friends.
Brother Avery was not an uninteresting figure in the lamp-
light, his white-bearded, saber-marked face uplifted, his
heavy watch-charm dangling against his vest, his neat striped
trousers stretched tightly over his knee. But he filled Giles
with a numb trembling. There was to be no peace ahead
unless he surrendered in compliance with Mr. Avery's
method.
Just as the minister said "Amen," Giles dashed away. He
ran on and on as fast as his long legs would carry him until
he was far enough from the house not to be heard or over-
taken. Then he walked more leisurely. The edges of his
face felt shivery, but he laughed. He felt better. Then he
tried laughing again. Then he slipped his slender seven-
shooter from his inside coat pocket and fired two or three
shots at nothing in particular. He felt better still.
He was at the church a half-hour early. The sexton was
ringing the first bell. A half-dozen very devout believers
and a half-dozen specially hardened sinners were the only
ones who had arrived. Giles took his favorite place in one
of the short middle seats by the stove on the men's side,
and enjoyed the experience of watching the crowd flow into
the church. He watched the stove, too, on the women's side,
as it turned from grayish black to red, and then he studied
the line of red to see how far it would creep up the stove-
pipe. It went to the elbow, and then a distance along the
horizontal stretch of pipe toward the chimney in the side
wall. The sexton boasted that nobody was ever frozen out
of his church.
Down the middle aisle came a steady stream of men and
women, boys and girls. Ten minutes before time for the
last bell, when nearly all the seats were filled, everybody
was startled to see Miss Genevieve Crosby come in. Shemarched half way down the aisle and took a seat on the
women's side almost directly opposite Giles. There was a
prolonged buzz of whispering.
Mr. Avery, a few days before when the success of his
revival filled him with an abounding confidence, had dis-
patched a messenger to Genevieve to learn if they might
not come and have a prayer meeting at her house. The
messenger went his way dutifully. But when he arrived
and found Genevieve in the bank-barn bent over a tiny
lamb that had arrived ahead of season, and she straightened
up with power and asked him eloquently what he wished of
her, he was so overwhelmed that he only inquired the way
to Joe Smedley's house, and departed with the briefest of
compliments when she had given him unmistakable di-
rections.
No one had ever seen her in any kind of a church. And
now here she was. She had come of her own sturdy will.
Did she mean by that, that she was there to confess her
sins? And how many of them were there? And what were
their nature? All of these matters were of public interest.
Giles sat in the pleasant warmth and saw her sweeping
along in the wild storm while he crouched in the hollow of
the great oak above the road. She shook her fists at the
lightning, didn't she? Well, didn't she still look as if she
would shake her fist at anybody or anything? He saw her,
too, on the ridge by the round hilltop after he had watched
the Italians deal with their compatriot in the ravine. Sup-
pose she should confess her sins, and in the process make
public a few of the sins of other people. What if she should
say, "I've known about a murder for years a murder com-
mitted in my woods? And I know somebody else who
knows about it, and he has never spoken, either?" What
if she should say that? Well, what if she should? The
young Italian in overalls was beginning to seem much far-
ther away.The meeting itself was uninteresting. Mr. Avery evi-
dently had used up so much of his energy in chasing the
sinners in, that he now had little left when they sat fear-
fully before him. He contented himself by speaking further
about Decision Day. He was impatient, he said, to have
the day come. With this Giles agreed. The tenseness of
the atmosphere was rapidly becoming unbearable. If only
he could get away on an eminence where he could see
clearly! But he was in some kind of highly charged fog
which at the same time excited him and made it impossible
for him to see.
Whether others shared exactly the same degree of yearn-
ing for relief, they looked forward to Decision Day. House-
wives said, "Perhaps this will be enough flour to last till
after Decision Day." Schoolchildren said, "We won't have
to study much till Decision Day is over." Grocers said,
"We're out of eggs, but when Decision Day is over, we'll
have our regular supply of eggs and butter both." And the
proprietor of the Blue Front saloon said, "If ever they
get through with this God-damned Decision Day, some of
our regular customers may come back."
The weather did all it could for the great occasion. For
two days the February sun shone warmly, and the inevi-
table February rain did not follow. On the afternoon of
the day, when the sun reached the western horizon, there
were thin clouds veiling the brightness; but it would be
hours before the rain arrived.
Everyone knew, too, that the crowd would be too great
for the church. Mr. Avery was sure, also, that the perma-
nent mourners' bench, stretching the full length of the choir
platform and altar in front, and along the altar on one side,
would not be long enough. So he had a temporary one built.
just inside the altar rail for those who should come forward
first. All day the sexton kept a good fire burning so thatthe warmth would be distributed throughout the church be-
fore evening.
The oil lamps in the chandeliers were freshly filled, their
wicks were trimmed, and their chimneys were rubbed to a
bright, unwonted cleanness. The books of revival songs
were carefully placed in the seats extra copies of them-
so that anyone who chose to join in the singing could have
the authentic words before him. One pedal of the organ,
long in a half-sagging condition from overuse, was lifted to
a proper tightness by Lon Dusenberry, the town's handy
man, and stood ready for the firm foot of Mrs. Elsie
Wheeler, the regular evening organist.
Giles dreaded going. He wondered if his father and
mother meant to go. He thought he would not go if they
went. But they were sensitive. They knew that members
of the family were not the people that most boys wanted to
have looking on when an intimate crisis was to be met.
They chanced to say at supper that there would be plenty
of people without them. With this assurance, Giles felt the
pull of the drama too strong to be resisted. He went. He
went early and squeezed in with all the others who had
gone early, and took his accustomed place by the middle
aisle.
The crowd poured in. It filled the aisles more than half-
way to the altar. It spread into the space between the last
seat in Sinners' Row and the wall behind. It filled the
spaces round the two stoves. It piled itself up round the
door outside the church. Outside somebody hit upon the
idea, since the weather was almost that of summer, of rais-
ing the lower sash of a window and watching in. Immedi-
ately there was a scramble for every other window. A half-
hour before time for the meeting, Mr. Avery had as large
a congregation outside looking in, as he had inside.
He moved about quietly and with an expression of satis-
faction on his face that he made little effort to conceal. Fif-teen minutes before the hour announced, he stepped into
the pulpit, looked into the faces of those in the church and
those who peered in out of the darkness, and declared in
full voice that since everybody seemed to be there, he could
see no reason for waiting fifteen minutes longer.
They would sing the first hymn. It was going to be one
in which saints and sinners alike could share. For everyone
looked forward to something better than what he enjoyed
now. He stood for a moment like a statue, his white hair
and beard sharply in contrast with the black of his frock
coat. Then he announced: "The Child of a King."
What a hungry crowd they were! Ill-fed, many of them,
but whether ill-fed or prosperous, they revealed their dis-
appointment with life as they had thus far experienced it.
If Mr. Avery held out the hope of something better, they
were ready to hear about it. Out of respect for an ocса-
sion which promised a life that did not fail, every article of
respectable clothing that the community could muster was
there on the back of somebody.
Two or three seats forward and across the aisle stood
Pewee Dunkle and his wife. Pewee was radiant in song,
and Mrs. Dunkle, arrayed in the best hat and dress she
could bring together out of all the oddments in her house,
stood silent and smiling beside him like a sturdy Plymouth
Rock hen that has promise of something more than has been
customary.
When they sang the last stanza:
"A tent or a cottage, why should I care?
They're building a palace for me over there!"
her smile increased in fullness. After forty-five years with
nothing but dirt, she could not help reveling in contentment
at the thought of a place where everything would be clean.
No more sulphur smoke! No more Company houses! No
more black water on the front-room carpet! No moremuddy dog tracks all over the back steps and front porch!
Maybe it would be a great house with Brussels carpets every-
where, and papered walls with pictures on them, and maybe
a gas stove in the kitchen! By the time they had come to
the end of the stanza:
"Tho exiled from home, yet still I may sing:
All glory to God, I'm the child of a King,”
her lips moved ever so slightly with the words.
The preacher prayed, and it seemed easy for him. Then
he announced another song, "Let the Lower Lights Be
Burning." He was bringing them up one step in emotional
pitch. During the singing of this song Giles saw others
that interested him. Beyond the crowded aisle were two
long seats filled with high-school boys and girls. Theda
and her aids had made good their promise. There was
Genevieve again, too, just back of the stove on the women's
side. She seemed interested, even entranced, by the singing.
Her dark, sharp eyes looked far away, and they looked
somewhat pityingly. Over in the other direction just out-
side a window, he could discern the iron-gray head and
quizzical face of Dr. Seabright.
When the resounding chorus had died away after the last
stanza, the preacher immediately announced his text: "The
Son of Man is come to seek and to save that which is lost."
He repeated the words with emphasis: "The Son of Man is
come to seek and to save that which is lost."
Before he could utter the first word of his sermon, some
overwrought woman back in the body of the congregation
cried out: "Oh, God, save me! Save me, God!"
In a flash Mr. Avery thrust aside all thought of a sermon.
"The Lord is already here, waiting to do His work!" he
shouted. "I shall interpose no feeble message of mine. Let
the choir sing the song of invitation, and let the penitent
come forward."There was a shuffling, buzzing moment of excitement
while everybody found the page. Then the choir sang
"Softly and Tenderly Jesus is Calling." Mr. Avery stood
beside the desk, his outspread hand uplifted, his face so full
of light that the saber scar on his cheek stood out livid and
ugly, and begged and warned the sinners to come.
They came.
Many were so borne down that they dropped to their
knees almost before they reached the altar, and never got
inside the rail to Mr. Avery's specially constructed mourners'
bench at all.
"Come!" he cried.
The music throbbed to a new quickness with the minis-
ter's one magnetic word.
"Come! If you are numb and weary, if life is emptiness
and bitterness, come now and taste of the living waters
that satisfy."
The choir caught up:
"Turn to the Lord, and seek salvation,
Sound the praise of His dear name."
When they had swept through that rhythmic tune, they
swung into "On Jordan's Stormy Banks I Stand."
In the excitement, Giles lost all thought of his own situa-
tion. He was busy watching the men and women crowd
