forward to give Mr. Avery's kind of salvation a trial. How
desperate the world was for something satisfying! They
kept coming-out of the depths of the crowd inside the
church, through the doors, through the windows-until Mr.
Avery's emergency mourners' bench was crowded and over-
flowing.
"Come on, Giles," some one said at his elbow. He
jumped. Then he saw that the fifteen or twenty boys and
girls from the high school were filtering through the crowdin the aisle on the way forward. It was Theda who had
spoken.
The concentrated power of the meeting swept through
him like a shock. In an effort to keep himself in hand, he
inclined his head and tried to think. The boys and girls
pressed through the aisle until they stood round him in a
group.
They were not experienced, either as revivalists or as
philosophers. Their exhortations were restricted to a few
conventional sentences which they had heard others use.
"Now is the time, Giles." "Better not put it off any
longer, Giles." "The rest of us are going, Giles."
He was more completely overwhelmed by their entreaties
than he had thought he could be by anything. They re-
newed their pleading. They waited. It was not difficult
for them to see that he was deeply moved.
Some of the more impatient ones went on forward. When
they reached the altar there was a fresh burst of song from
the choir and a great shout of triumph from Mr. Avery.
Above the perpetual tumult he called something to the con-
gregation about "all these young lives dedicated to the
service of the Master."
"Hear that, Giles?" one of the boys who had remained
behind asked. "That's what we mean to do-dedicate our-
selves. You must, too."
"Come on, Giles," Theda pleaded coaxingly, and slipped
her arm through his and gave him a slight pull.
He yielded a little. Never in his life had he been im-
plored like that.
"Come on," she coaxed again, and swayed him toward
the aisle, this time a bit more positively. "Come on with
us."
He felt a rush of blood over his face. To be led forward,
when everybody else was going, by this glorified being who
tugged at his arm.
..."Come on, we must go, Giles," she implored yet more
persistently. "Now."
She thought he was coming.
"Come on!"
But suddenly he freed his arm from hers. "Not to-
night," he managed to say with awkward, heavy lips.
"Oh, but you mustn't put it off. That means that you
won't come at all."
"Well," was all he could say.
She could not believe that he would not yield, and stood
patiently beside him for a time. Then in the uproar, she
and two or three who had waited with her went forward.
Giles felt himself burning up. He thought he must look
as red as a beet to everyone who saw him. And he knew
that the eyes of the entire congregation had been turned in
his direction. But gradually the hot trembling left his legs
and arms, and he imagined that his face looked less red.
He peeped out from under his eyebrows to see what others
were going forward.
Genevieve! She was not in her place!
But in a twinkling he saw her in the side aisle where she
had gradually been pressed by the throng. He then ven-
tured to peep in the other direction. Dr. Seabright was not
in his place, either. But he had crowded up closer to the
window and was looking over toward Giles with gratified,
approving eyes.
A joyful scream burst from the vicinity of the altar. Mrs.
Fred Waters, like a benignant tigress, rose with uplifted
arms from her place, leaped up and down, shouted "Glory!
Glory!! Glory!!!" and whirled through the crowded aisles
catching at her friends and shaking them in her boundless
ecstasy.
A masculine shout went up. "Praise God! Praise God!
Glory to God! Hallelujah!"
It was Luther Jackson. He weighed more than two hun-dred pounds, and hard work kept his muscles firm. So
when he swept back through the middle aisle, shouting about
a glorious life, everybody tried to get out of his way. He
met Mrs. Waters, whom he had not liked because she had
let her chickens scratch his sweet-potato ridges. They gave
each other a quick, glowing glance of understanding, grasped
each other's arms and leaped and praised God till the heav-
ily taxed floor of the church shuddered.
Other converts began to leap up. Still the mourners'
benches were crowded. Still the workers bent over them
and encouraged them with exhortations, brief prayers, and
snatches of song caught up from whatever the choir hap-
pened to be singing:
"What can make me whole again?
Nothing but the blood of Jesus."
"I'm kneeling at the mercy seat,
I'm kneeling at the mercy seat,
I'm kneeling at the mercy seat,
And Jesus saves me now.
"I can, I will, I do believe,
I can, I will, I do believe,
I can, I will, I do believe,
That Jesus saves me now.”
Or the primitive beat of
,
"The great Physi-cian now is near
The sym-pathiz-ing Je-sus."
,
Above all the tumult was the clear, commanding voice of
Mr. Avery. Once he stopped the choir, shouted to the
over-wrought assemblage for silence, and said a brief word.
"Almost every moment some one is rising up from this
altar with such joy in his heart as he never experienced be-fore. But there must be others in this multitude who are
almost ready to come. Now we are going right on with our
prayers for those who are at the altar, but we are going to
have a new song by the choir, and while we sing, let others
who are sorely convicted of their sins come and join these
many who are already here."
The choir then sang "Almost Persuaded," and with such
effect that several others came. At the last words of the
last stanza, "Sad, sad, that bitter wail: 'Almost, but lost! "
Mr. Avery stopped the choir again and with terrifying so-
lemnity asked, "What if this should be the last time you
should ever hear those words 'Almost, but lost!' Lost!!
We are going to sing them now for the last time this winter
-and for some of you, for eternity."
Amid the hurrying of frightened sinners to the altar,
some of the high-school boys and girls rose from the mourn-
ers' bench with beatified faces. Their first thought was
Giles, their "unsaved" classmate. They rushed to him.
They were filled with the new convert's desire to pass on
experience to some one else. Fervently they entreated him.
But he did not yield. He had somehow got beyond their
reach. Something inside kept whispering, "I am more
precious than this! I am more precious than this!"
What it was that fortified him, he could not himself
quite feel sure. He only knew that he respected it utterly.
So he looked the boys and girls squarely in the face and
told them that it was useless to linger. And they knew that
he meant what he said.
On and on went the uproar. Men and women of every
temperament rose up and expressed their diverse degrees of
release. Some shouted-could do nothing, say nothing, ex-
cept "Glory! Glory! Glory!" Others with arms uplifted
moved about in semi-trance, saying little, but beaming with
joy. Others leaped up with new friendliness in their faces
and rushed through the crowd to find some neighbor andask forgiveness for some unneighborly offense. Still others,
too happy in their relief to find movement possible, sank
back in the arms of praying friends who kneeled beside
them, and looked away in rapture.
Three hours! Some of those who stood outside the win-
dows, chilled and thinking that there would now be nothing
new to witness, began to drift away, light their pipes, and
talk about how the clouds had cleared from the sky. Inside,
many of those who had been obliged to stand grew aware
of aching legs and shoulders and sought passage through
the crowd.
Giles was ready to go. When two or three who stood in
the aisle at the end of his seat turned to push their way to-
ward the door, he unobtrusively followed them.
Out in the fresh, cooling air, he did not escape the rhyth-
mic beat of the song, the rumble of the little organ, the
prayers and wailings of the penitent, the glad shouts of those
who professed conversion.
As he turned into the quiet, empty road to take his way
home, he walked in a great sense of light. He had not
surrendered his individual spirit.
Myriads of bright stars filled the heavens. There was
assurance in their coolness, their remoteness, their seem-
ing unconcern, As he walked peacefully along, his feet
crunching gravel or kicking into small flintstones, he tried
to visualize an entire night's ritual of the skies. He felt
the stars flashing something of themselves down, so that he
seemed a part of them, and they of him. He preferred this
direct communion to the interposition of any ambassador
who had come to save the people of the Wiggam's Glory
area.PART THREEG
CHAPTER I
ILES found himself more of an outlander than ever.
In school his reclaimed classmates were now Chris-
tians, and he was not. What, then, was more to
be expected than that they should clique together and for-
get him-save occasionally to mention him in pity. In the
church he was even more of an alien-a ramping young
buck with too much self-will for any good purpose. In the
Blue Front saloon, the only other social center of impor-
tance in Culbertson, he would have been welcome if he had
chosen to come; collarless men with one foot on the brass
rail confessed admiration for his ability with his fists. But
if he preferred going to the church and thereby inviting
trouble for himself, then they washed their hands clean of
the whole matter. Thus there came to be unanimity of
group opinion in Culbertson that he was a queer young
person, a person to be let alone.
Aye, but he was free also. He might go untrammeled
wherever he saw a path. But where? There were not many
possibilities in the Wiggam's Glory area. The only alterna-
tive to a revival was a drunken dance, and dances were more
or less in abeyance during the season of protracted meet-
ings; the promoters did not wish to antagonize the church.
He must hunt up another revival. And the one he learned
about was at Beechwood Chapel four miles to the north-
west. Now that he was no longer afraid of a revivalist's
power, he could march away into this unfamiliar community
just as if he were going to read a story. He could go into
the church without trepidation and enjoy the total proceed-
ing-the poetry of the singing, the drama of famishing hu-manity on its knees begging for a life less disappointing than
the one everybody seemed to be living.
He waited a few days. He wanted the meetings to pro-
gress beyond the warming-up stage. Then one evening at
early supper while his father bent his clear-featured, inquir-
ing face close over a dessert dish of dried peaches, and his
mother sat upright sipping coffee, looking away toward the
declining sun, and musing seriously but pleasantly, he an-
nounced his intention.
His father looked up. "Quite a walk, won't it be?" For
a moment his face lost its intentness on something remote.
"Eight or nine miles all told, after the round trip to school?”
That was nothing, Giles thought. And his mother shared
his view. It would be worth the walk, if one started early
enough, just to see the big clean farms and the magnificent
houses.
Luke on second thought admitted that he shouldn't mind
going himself. He went over there a few times a long while
ago, trying to sell fruit jars. He did not sell many, but he
saw smoother, cleaner fields than he had ever before seen
anywhere.
Quickly Giles made himself ready and was off. He must
make most of the trip before daylight and twilight vanished.
In ruddy exaltation he swung along the road. Who cared
what the people down at Culbertson thought of him?
He walked faster; he whistled courageously. Who could
know what might not be ahead when one set forth on a
journey definitely to discover something interesting? He
felt mature, resourceful, distinguished. High school! A lot
of nice little boys and girls! Who said they were so much?
If they were, why didn't they show it when they recited?
For half a cent he would chuck school and march off like
this to a place where people grow up enough to get the hang
of things. If he knew just where that was, why
There was St. Bernard's ahead. In the evening light thechurch loomed as large as a cathedral, and the gold cross
high at the top of the steeple stood ablaze in the last clear
beams of the sun. He regarded the fantastic configuration
of some of the white tombstones in the churchyard-angels
hovering with crowns clasped in their hands; rounded
cherubs with embryonic wings; penitent creatures in flow-
ing robes with arms upstretched to the Rock of Ages. On
the edge of the ridge there that ran back from the road was
where Hope was sleeping. She was still a little girl! As
he came alongside the low iron fence, the evening quiet be-
came greater. Yes, she was still a little girl. Never would
she be anything else. And here he was, about as big as he
would ever be, and going off at night to a place where he
had never been.
Ahead and to the right, one hill rose high above every
other-a mountain, almost. It was a strange hill. Just how
did it happen to be there? The shoulder of it on the south
and southwest, he could see, was wooded. But above the
woodland it was smooth like a meadow, and on the summit
one round tree stood solitary.
The other hills receded ever so gently right in the direc-
tion he was taking. In the luminous twilight of late Feb-
ruary, he could see houses-white painted ones, red brick
ones-wherever he looked. So many houses! And so dif-
ferent from the ones at Wiggam's Glory! No doubt they
were full of romance and mystery. Probably there was a
girl in almost every one of them. From such houses a girl
as beautiful as Beatrix might reasonably step forth at almost
any time. Possibly she might come to church. Yes, pos-
sibly. Of late so many of his notions of things had been
upset that at the moment he was not certain just what the
beautiful girl's status in religion would be.
The road followed the side of a ridge down into the long,
narrow valley. Ahead Giles saw the outlines of a pleasant
church, with steep roof and thin belfry, in a grove of beechtrees. In the semi-darkness, the palest of lights shone from
the windows, and on every side of the white building the
silver gray of beeches in early springtime rose in shadowy
multiplying lines until they merged with the gray blue of
the skies.
By the time he came to the church door, others were there
-dozens of them, scores of them. For a moment Giles
stood just inside the outer door and got his bearings, then
made his way to an unoccupied seat along the side aisle.
How elegant everything was! From end to end the aisles
were covered with thick soft carpet so that there was no
noisy shuffling of feet when people moved about. The seats
were not just stiff painted boards, but dark polished wood,
shaped to the human figure. Velvet coverings hung across
the preacher's desk, the tables used on communion day, and
the top of the large but pipeless organ. And instead of
mourners' benches constructed like the benches in a car-
penter's shop, there was a low, polished balustrade reaching
entirely round the pulpit and the slightly raised pavilion
set apart for the choir. Everybody reflected this elegance,
too-even the preacher. He walked easily and confidently
to the pulpit as if the earth were a perfectly safe place. In
no movement or attitude did he suggest the divine terror
which Mr. Avery exhibited in every act, every glance.
The members of the choir began to take their places. A
fashionable-looking woman not yet thirty came and took an
end chair in the front row of four. The minister moved
over and whispered to her as if she might be his wife, al-
though he was older by fifteen or twenty years. Another
woman, a little younger than the first, took the second chair
in the row. Soon a third came. She had the same dark
hair, she exhibited the same fitness of design in her clothes,
and carried herself with the same assurance of manner.
When nearly all the other chairs in the choir were taken, a
girl of fifteen or sixteen came and took the one place thatremained unoccupied in the front row. She belonged with
the other three. In her glance, still girlish enough, there
was something that said she was not afraid to be looked at.
They were the daughters of Wesley Lettinger. Wesley
was what was known in the region as a "paying Methodist."
His spiritual connection with the church was inconsequential.
It would be difficult to say whether in the entire stretch of
his rounded years he had ever confessed either faith or sin.
But he was the wealthiest man in the community-at least
he stood ready to argue that point and he found pleasure
in spending some of his surplus dollars on the church.
Always he sat just half-way between the door and the
pulpit along the center aisle, a sturdy figure of a graying
man with a flourishing spread of beard and a bald head over
which he vainly tried to comb the hair that grew nearer his
ears. His eyes were bright blue and awake, his full, ruddy
lips voluptuous but kindly. His wife, a dark woman of great
spirit from whom the girls had taken their physical traits,
was willing to sit nearer the altar, but Wesley laughingly
declared that he had to be where he could clear out if ever
the preacher called on him to pray.
He jollied all preachers. He told them that he kept a re-
serve supply of yellow-legged Plymouth Rock chickens for
ministerial purposes, and then gave support to his declara-
tion by having the preachers much at his house to enjoy his
hospitality. He took pride in showing them the fatness of
his fields, the bulging fullness of his barns. And he found
the greatest of pleasure in seeing them smile and hearing
them thank the Lord when they needed money for the
church and he gave them more than they asked for.
But his four girls-they were his pride. They were
sturdy of spirit, and he encouraged them to be sturdier
still. He talked much about the need of boys in his family.
He liked to ask his girls why some of them were not boys
so that he could have more help on the farms without wait-ing for sons-in-law. Yet, as a matter of fact, he would not
have had his family otherwise, even if the matter had been
left wholly to him. He liked womankind in general. He
and his wife were pointed to by everybody in the region as
a model of marital happiness. And they were. Yet Wesley
did not let thought of feminine charm rest exclusively in his
wife. He liked to philander in warm innocence with his
wife's best friends, especially the ones who were younger
than she was. She did not take offense. In truth, she en-
joyed the experience herself. It nourished her strong egoism
to think that she could hold in security a robust man who
stood in such favor with those of younger years. He liked
all little girls, too little girls of six or eight. It was great
fun to put his strong arms round them, tell them that he
meant to kiss them, and then shake his luxuriant beard
across their faces until they squealed in merriment and mock
fear. And of course he liked bigger girls his own daugh-
ters, for instance, fitted out in bright-colored dresses and
broad brimmed be-ribboned straw hats. When they came
to him thus attired and volunteered to ride a mowing ma-
chine or a hay tedder, his romantic affection was at its
highest. When they passed this stage and were young
women, he still showered attentions upon them. He liked
to make them blush, if he could, by talking much about
moonlight and quiet lanes and the gallantries of love-making
when he was a young man.
Was he not entitled to have his family in the front row
of the choir? No preacher ever expressed a doubt; no
preacher wanted to endanger himself as the representative
of the King of kings in this well-fed parish. When Wes-
ley's wife was young, she sat there by a kind of social
eminent-domain. When Hazel, the eldest daughter, grew
into possession of a good soprano voice, she took her place
beside her mother. When Barbara revealed a voice as good
as Hazel's, she added herself to the row. Then came Myra,a strong contralto, and she took the last of the four front
chairs. Almost before they knew it, Beulah, the youngest
daughter and her father's favorite, was a good-sized girl. In
Wesley's eyes she remained "the baby." The other girls
were sure he was spoiling her, but he always brought them
to silence by saying: "Oh, don't get jealous, now. I mean
to treat you all alike in my will." He did, though, want her
in the choir. But the front row was already full. Who
would dare say which member of the family should be
humiliated by sitting in the second row?
Mrs. Lettinger solved the problem with one sweeping
motherly gesture. Yet it was not a wholly unselfish move.
For one dark night while Wesley was unhitching the team
behind the church and she stood waiting, she overheard
somebody in the blackness express the unrestricted opinion
that old Ma Lettinger's voice was as badly cracked as the
Liberty Bell. So she stepped out of the choir altogether.
The four girls ought to hold the fort. When Hazel mar-
ried a state senator who lived on a stock farm three or four
miles north, there was a general prediction that some other
family would have a chance in the front row. But on all
important occasions, Hazel came whizzing down the crushed-
stone pike behind a team of sleek dark bays, and took her
place with the other girls. After the minister's wife died,
even while the memory of her good works lingered, the
rumor got abroad that the preacher was going to Wesley
Lettinger's more frequently than the financial state of the
church necessitated, and there was another general predic-
tion that the front row would be broken. In the natural
course of events in Methodist procedure, the minister would
be going elsewhere, and he would take his new wife with
him.
Yet Giles saw before him the family of Lettinger daugh-
ters intact. They excited no harsh envy, for they did not
abuse their advantageous position. They sang well, theydressed like people of distinction, and their group conduct
was above reproach. People were rather proud of them.
They provided the choir with a good front.
But in their voices there was nothing of the Culbertson
choir's tremulous warning of perdition. They sang like
well-fed larks that had never tasted anything of life but
health and gladness. No matter what they sang, their ro-
bust spirits swept away all thought of criticism except from
the minds of the few who habitually looked for the ridicu-
lous. When, for instance, old Mason Selby's fourth wife died
and he had the Lettinger sisters sing at her funeral, and
they sang, "We Are Going Down the Valley One by One,"
Peter Search, who had stood up with Mason at his first
marriage, was the only person who spoke of any over-
appropriateness in the language of the song. Mason was
comforted, and the presence of these four beautiful women,
with never a suggestion of death in anything they did, made
him feel vaguely that despite his successive losses, it was
still good to be alive.
Giles sat ready to hear them. And when they began with
a song that had a joyful refrain running:
"Salvation is free, salvation is free;
A perfect salvation for you and for me!"
something of their electric healthiness swept through him
until he felt stirred from his feet to the top of his head.
They were alive. They proclaimed a happy mingling of
physical profusion and spiritual simplicity. As they surged
on through the assuring stanzas, their faces alight, their
bodies rhythmic, Venus seemed clearly ascendant over the
Virgin. But when the song was ended and they sat with
heads bowed while the minister prayed, the Virgin gained
momentary possession.
Giles remembered little about the sermon except that it
was well delivered and very dull. He was full of thoughtsabout the youngest singer in the front row. She might
almost be the beautiful girl-the composite image of all the
beautiful girls about whom his forbears had dreamed for
ten thousand years. Certainly he had seen no one so beau-
tiful since the days he had spent in the bedroom off the
kitchen reading Ivanhoe and Henry Esmond. The deep
rose of the velvet dress she wore was absolute in its ap-
propriateness. Did she know how she stood apart from
everyone else in the church in beauty?
He watched her after the service was over. No, she was
not going home with any boy. Giles heard the sleek-looking
young man who waited to take her older sister home say in
intimate family fashion, "Well, hook onto my other wing
here, Babe, and we'll be going."
Most of the people had walked. The roads-except the
pike-were in that state of spring lumpiness and softness
when walking is the least disagreeable means of locomo-
tion. Giles merged himself in the long file of people going
in his general direction, and tried to make out who was just
ahead. It was the sleek young man with the two Lettinger
girls. When he came to the foot of the hill that would even-
tually bring him out at St. Bernard's and the ridge road
home, he noticed that the three were a part of the crowd
that took the fork of the road to the left down the creek
valley.
Hadn't he had an adventure? All this country, and all
these people, so different from Wiggam's Glory! In a flush
of pleasantness he walked slowly along the gently rising
road. He must come back. All these nice people! He
almost felt like one of them himself. Of course he must
come back. Maybe he had found the beautiful girl. Of
course, of course, he must come back-soon, within two or
three days, or three or four.
Below, the creek road pulled over close against the base
of the hill, and his own road rose along the hillside. Di-rectly under him a half-dozen people were calling to each
other and laughing about matters peculiarly significant to
themselves. They had drawn a little apart so that they
could enjoy the sweetness of privacy and still be within easy
hearing.
"Yes, and if you pitch him over, Myra, he'll be done
for, now that the preacher has taken Barbara."
"Oh, I don't know! I've got the Babe here on my other
wing. I'll just wait till she grows up."
"She'll be gone!" the other masculine voice called back.
"I saw a fellow watching her to-night."
"Who was he?"
"Don't know. Some big tall lummox."
"He wasn't a lummix, either!" Beulah protested. "I
guess I saw him."
"Somebody said he was a young coal digger from over by
Wiggam's Glory. Can't you see Beulah washing diggers?"
There were many ha-ha's.
Giles felt his ears burning. He did not care to hear any-
thing further about himself, yet he did not want to stop
listening. He walked so that his feet would not rumple up
the bits of conversation that came to his ears. But the
voices were going round the bend in the road. He could
make nothing more of what was said.
His legs were now anxious to hurry. So he was a coal
digger, was he? Well, just since when? To be sure, his
father worked in the mine through the winter months. But
the rest of the time he was a potter. If these people thought
that fact made him himself into a coal digger, why, they
didn't know what they were talking about.
Was he now so certain that he wanted to go back? The
girl, to be sure, had defended him, in a way. But the meet-
ing itself was not exciting. The two well-dressed sinners
who went forward to the altar did not so much as raise onelusty wail or shout. Of course, there was the singing-it
was pretty good. And the youngest of the four
...
St. Bernard's towered above him in the darkness and
clutched his thought for a moment away from Beechwood
Chapel. His feet made a shuffling noise as he walked in
the rough, drying road. The white hovering angels on the
tombstones were close across the low iron fence there at his
right. And Hope-yes, Hope was over there, too, within a
hundred feet or so of where he was. He walked lightly, lest
he meet her.
Soon he was away from the cemetery, and his mind went
back to what he had heard about himself. His face re-
mained warm, and the sides of his head felt creepy as if he
were about to be at grips with some tremendous thing.
He passed No. 1 Company Row and Wiggam's Glory. He
came to his own house and tiptoed in. Upstairs he felt cau-
tiously for the lamp and lighted it. He placed it on the big
chest under the tiny window in the gable, and then stood
and looked at the cavernous room. How different its log-
and-chink walls, its deal board floor, its water-streaked oak-
shingle roof close above his head, from the church with its
dazzling chandeliers, its rich hangings, its heavy, polished
seats, its altar with the balustrade in front, its well-clothed
people especially the four in the front row of the choir.
He took off his Sunday suit, hung it on the big spike
driven into the top log of the wall in the corner, and went
humbly to bed.T
CHAPTER II
HE NEXT morning after he had grunted some non-
committal replies to his father and mother at the
breakfast table, he wandered out into the side yard,
his head throbbing anew with perplexity over what had been
so casually said about him the night before. A yearling
Plymouth Rock rooster walked proudly and circumspectly
through the open gate, only to be pounced upon by old
Rowdy and roughly rolled on the bare ground with loss of
few feathers but much personal pride. When at last he was
through cackling at the episode, he restored his sense of
adequacy by pecking the heads of his hens with unnecessary
virility and by crashing into one of his brethren who hap-
pened at the moment to have his back turned and his head
down. That was how Giles felt. He wanted to tear into
something. But what vast thing was there to tear into? He
would he would-well, maybe go down to school and do
that experiment in light before anybody else got started.
The air was positive with promise of spring. In another
week or two there would be flurries of March snow and
howlings of March winds. But this morning a subtle per-
sistence in the air affirmed that spring could not be held off
much longer, even by March. The frostiness retreating be-
fore the sun was pleasant to Giles's bare hands. They tin-
gled; they felt tight with vigor. Who wanted warm hands
when it was possible to have them glowing with cool fire?
In every direction sounds were distinct. Off to the east
straight ahead he heard much unusual pounding and ham-
mering. It sounded as if somebody were tearing down all
of the Company Row on the hill above Culbertson. Whenhe came in sight of the Row, however, he saw that it was all
there. He saw, in addition, much lumber thrown along the
road in front of the houses, and men at work. When he
came up he found the men unwontedly active in the build-
ing of a paling fence-digging post holes, setting posts,
spiking rails on, and nailing the palings. The palings were
neat and low and even, and promised to make livable yards
where there had been nothing but trodden ground before.
Surprised and interested, he stopped and looked.
"Company putting up fences?" he asked Pewee Dunkle.
Pewee laughed a crusader's cheerful laugh. "Ever hear
of the Company puttin' up anything it didn't have to? But
we've decided it's about time our wives had decent places to
live in, anyhow. They's to be a fence back between every
two houses, as well as out front here. And as soon as the
grass gits up over in that clean place there in the field, we
mean to carry over sod. And then if work ain't any steadier
than it has been, maybe we'll find some flat sandstones for
walks from the steps out to the front gates."
Women were working, too. Some were in their back yards
raking up sticks and weeds and cabbage stalks and old shoes
and straw. Some were trying to refurbish doors and walls
that had remained more or less untouched since the houses
were built.
Mrs. Pewee Dunkle stepped out at the front door. She
hesitated a little at the sight of Giles, then asked Pewee
proudly: "How does that look?"
Pewee turned with sympathetic scrutiny. "Purty scrump-
tious, I'd say."
She had bought dye, colored gunnysacking which had ac-
cumulated, and made hangings for the front windows. She
thought she had better explain to Giles.
"Seems too bad never to have anything but a torn blind
or paper at the windows; so I've made some some dra-
peries.""Yes," Giles answered in honest sympathy. He stood at
the edge of the road, a tall hulk of an overgrown boy, with
a small dinner bucket dangling at the end of one long arm
and a couple of books held easily under the other, and gazed
at Mrs. Dunkle's modest artistic results.
Pewee beamed. "When you git married, Giles, don't you
wait as long as I have to 'preciate what a good wife you've
got."
Mrs. Dunkle turned and hurried into the house, embar-
rassed by this overwhelming praise.
"Our women," Pewee went on, "would look just the same
as anybody else if they had a chance. We can't give 'em
much of a chance, but by-”
He caught himself just as lifelong habit led him to the
brink of profanity.
"Purty nigh slipped clean over Niagry Falls that time,
didn't I?" He laughed at his narrow escape, and then went
on with what he had in mind. In detail he outlined once
more everything that was to be done.
Giles looked admiringly about, visualizing all the proposed
transformation.
"You see, Giles," Pewee said in great earnestness, "this
is what happens when Jesus Christ comes to Company Row."
Pewee's throat filled before he was through with the sen-
tence. As he stood there a strange, acceptable new life
seemed his by miracle. He felt as if he had narrowly
escaped the deadliest fall of slate that had ever come down
in a mine. Enraptured, he stood silent in the bright morn-
ing sun. Then Twinkle Savage brought him back to earth
by asking him to fasten his end of the blue cord to the top
of the new paling.
Giles forgot Beechwood Chapel. He walked slowly along
the Row and reflected upon all these unexpected evidences
of a new spirit. That evening he walked slowly past again
on the way home. By the first warm Sunday in March,with idle days in the mine increasing rather than diminish-
ing, the transformation was beyond easy belief. A straight
fence stretched the full length of the Row, and dividing
fences separated the houses so that each family had a yard
of its own. The women had collected flintstones from the
fields and from along the road and built borders around
flower-beds that waited for seeds. Broad stepping-stones
led from a number of the houses to roadside front gates.
Men and women and children dressed in the best clothes
they could devise were coming out of the houses and mov-
ing churchward in the sunshine as if they were happy in
what they were doing.
Why were they happy to go? Was it possible that any-
body had once lived such a mighty life that some of its puis-
sance had filtered through every encrustation that enemies
and friends alike had built round it for centuries, and now
was able to fill these battered, frowsy, half-dead bodies with
zest when nothing anywhere immediately round about
seemed worth living for?
A tremendous suggestion pressed itself into Giles's mind
and filled him with warmth. He would look into this Jesus
of Nazareth. He had been more or less brought up on
Sunday School lessons, but about Jesus they had told him
little. The preachers at church, he thought, had told him
less. If there was a story of Jesus, he believed he would
read it.
He did not care to attract family notice by reading the
big Bible that reposed on the front-room table; this was a
private affair that he was undertaking. So he went into the
little bedroom off the kitchen and got down a copy of the
New Testament in large print from the shelves. It had be-
longed to his grandmother. He could read that unmolested.
But even that he read with a textbook in physics or civil gov-
ernment at hand, so that he might readily turn to "studying"
if anybody came in. As March howled its way into springhe read each of the four Gospels, and then picked out parts
that he liked and read them again.
Secretly he carried about with him a youthful, easy be-
lief: he was akin to this young man of the Gospels. Jesus
was a carpenter's son, for one thing. That, of course, was
not so bad as being a coal digger's son. Still, there was a
sure foundation for likeness. Jesus had confidence, too.
Sometimes Giles had, sometimes he didn't know whether
he had or not. But he wanted to have; and Jesus had it.
Jesus could tell people what was what, and feel sure. He
could say, "Get up and walk" with such utter absence of
doubt in his own mind that there was none left in the mind
of anybody else. The way he talked, too, to that Samaritan
woman at the well! She was a beautiful woman, Giles was
sure. The fact that she had five husbands argued somewhat
on that point. But anyhow, he knew she was beautiful.
Wouldn't that have been something to see-Jesus stand-
ing by the side of that low well talking with a dark-haired
woman about the age of that oldest one in the front row of
the choir, perhaps, and dressed in a blue purplish flowing
costume with something deep red and rich-looking tied back
from her forehead. She was accustomed to making men
listen to her, but when Jesus spoke, she was the one who
did the listening. He said, "Whosoever drinketh of the
water that I shall give him shall never thirst." That
stumped her completely.
Giles hunted out passages that spoke of the manner in
which Jesus talked with other women, and reread them
many times. He liked that story of the woman who
broke the precious ointment in honor of Jesus and then
used her flowing hair to complete the worshipful incident.
Think of having a beautiful woman do that! And Jesus
was not in the least disturbed, either by her or by those who
sniffed at her. And Martha and Mary! Sometimes Giles
thought he liked Mary better; then sometimes Martha.There were days when he was not interested in girls who
were too serene. Still, Mary was the one who was ready
to sit and visit. And Jesus liked her, too. No, it was not
easy to say which.
Yes, Giles thought, Jesus had the right kind of confidence.
He outfaced people. He outfaced the salesmen who invaded
the temple. He outfaced the devil. He just met the devil
on the mountain top, heard his proposition, and looked him
out of countenance. Aye, he even outfaced ridicule. That
was harder than outfacing the devil. A lot of nice people
that one had no way of getting at, on their way home from
church talking about a young lummox, for instance! Or a
lot of shouting men and women and Theda and all the other
girls and boys tugging at one and trying to lead him up to
Mr. Avery's specially constructed mourners' bench! Would
Jesus have allowed himself to be led up? Giles felt better
than he had felt for many a day after he asked himself that
question.
And Jesus had been much alone. The gang had been after
him, too. He hadn't had very strong support from anybody,
so far as one could see. That night in Gethsemane! Giles
liked to lie stretched across the bed in the little room and
close his eyes and see the rich darkness, and the disciples
who could not keep awake, and over there by a big rock
and some ancient and magnificent trees, the figure of this
young person of thirty-three with his face uplifted in the
midnight. Giles thought there was some kind of music, too,
and the faintest glints of light coming through places where
the clouds were thin.
And then they killed him!
Wasn't there enough poetry in that to color people's lives
for a long time? Giles in his young unbefogged way
thought he could understand how this great poetical source
had outlasted everything that tended to destroy it-the
militant unbelieving, the harsh-voiced shouters for it whowere without understanding, the fine, respectable people
who did not like it in its full essence, but wanted a little
flavoring of it in their respectability. It was for the sensi-
tive; and the sensitive persisted in happening along.
Giles moved about with renewed confidence in his capaci-
ties. Alertly he walked past Culbertson Company Row and
considered possible limits to the transformation there. Un-
afraid he listened to Mr. Avery while he preached desperate,
bolstering sermons against the awful sin of backsliding.
Unafraid, too, he went to Beechwood Chapel; and when the
youngest girl in the choir looked intently at him, half with
amusement as if he were a specimen of some sort, and half
with interest, he returned her look with such strength of
spirit that all trace of the amusement vanished from her
face and left her looking with interest alone.U
CHAPTER III
NDER the increasing sun the yellow mud in the
roads dried up as hard as adobe. Then April
showers flashed down and left green grass in the
fields. The men in the Culbertson Company Row, since the
mines were doing little, found conditions right for complet-
ing their many improvements.
Giles stopped one evening to chat with Pewee and his wife
while they worked at their front yard. Pewee had bought
lime and was whitewashing his fence. Twinkle Savage and
Zink Williamson had already whitewashed theirs out front
and were now at work on the ones that separated their
houses from Pewee's. In a few hours Pewee and his wife
would have their smudgy little house surrounded on three
sides by clean white. The men had brought the sod, too,
and had found the flat sandstones. The sod was green and
the stones were beginning to look as if they had been walked
on a little. Mrs. Dunkle, dazed with the happiness of seeing
things going for once in her favor, was wondering if she had
allowed for enough flower beds. She asked Giles, and he
thought having or not having enough seeds might be the de-
termining factor.
"Oh, we've got plenty, all right," Pewee answered for her.
"I told Cora that she was a-goin' to have enough of some-
thing for once in her life. These will be enough, won't they?
With work at the mine as slack as it is, I've got to charge
things anyhow. So I couldn't see how two or three dollars'
worth of flower seeds was a-goin' to make much difference."
Giles regarded the bepictured envelopes in the split-basket. There were seeds enough in them, he estimated, to
make the entire yard solid with flowers.
"Yes, plenty, I see."
He studied the shape of the yard, considered the direct-
ness with which the sun struck the front of the house, and
measured with his eye the height of the fences.
"Why, this is what I'd do. I'd make these two beds along
the sides of the yard narrow. Then I'd make these on each
side of the gate wider. You'd better make the ones up
against the side of the house wide, too. The sun will be hot
and may kill out anything up close to the weatherboarding.
About this wide for each one, I should say."
He paced off the dimensions and put some stones at the
corners for markers. Then he studied the whole yard again.
"Yes, that would be about right." He spoke as if he had
designed flower beds all his life.
"And that round bed there in the middle by the walk
ought to be a little larger, I believe. Let's see." And he
got down on the clean earth and quickly stretched the circle
of flintstones into a greater circumference.
"See? Now if you put a thick bunch of phlox there in
the middle and a band of these petunias round the edge to
fall out over the stones, you'll have a fine bed."
Pewee stood in wonder and admiration. "Do you learn
that in school-in jometry, or whatever they call it?"
Giles did not wish to destroy Pewee's respect for sys-
tematic education. "Yes," he admitted, "we do study
geometry."
"Maybe I don't wish I'd 'a' had a chance to learn some-
thin' myself," Pewee said with great regret. "Guess I'll
take a year off when I'm fifty and go to school. Slack as
work is, I could purt-nigh begin this spring. All of this
time off will be hard on people some of these days."
Giles thought he had better be going. He handed someenvelopes of seeds back to Mrs. Dunkle, and noticed that
her hands trembled with excitement.
Before he had gone far in the spring afternoon bright-
ness, he wished he had stayed a little longer. Maybe he
would stop again to-morrow. Yes, he believed he would.
It would be a great joke on somebody if all the women in
the Row should buy seeds and develop flower beds.
By the time he reached home he was in a pleasant frenzy.
But supper was ready, his father was studious about some-
thing, his mother was unusually quiet, and the frenzy abated.
After supper his father stayed out in the kitchen with his
mother and they talked long in low, deliberative voice.
When he ventured out through the kitchen they stopped
suddenly and seemed embarrassed, until he went on out into
the smoky spring twilight.
The next afternoon Twinkle Savage stopped him and as-
sured him that half the women in the Row were now going
to have flower beds and were going to come to him for ad-
vice. He had got himself into something, Twinkle thought.
The frenzy returned. What if there should be a Com-
pany Row with white fences and with morning-glories round
all the doors and windows, and roses in the corners of the
yards, and hollyhocks with fat lazy bees droning from flower
to flower? He would tell his mother what was happening.
He swept into the house in a gust of enthusiasm. But in
one flash of what his eyes saw, he lost it all. By the fire-
place, scarcely warm with a few spring-day embers, his
father and mother stood in mysterious consultation. His
father's shoulders were drooped and narrowed. His mother
leaned on the other end of the mantel and tried to conceal
tears that crept down over her cheeks from her red eyes.
They stood confused in the late afternoon quiet of the
house, as if they wished not to see him at least not yet.
He walked lightly to the kitchen to leave his little dinner
bucket. Before his hand had placed it on the end of thekitchen table, a dozen things, all impossible enough, had
flashed through his mind. Two or three nights before he
had heard them talking about divorces. But for what reason
could they be considering a divorce? Blankly he walked
out toward the orchard; blankly he walked on down the
ridge; blankly a half-hour later, while he sailed flat stones
the size of silver dollars into the edge of the woods, he
heard his mother's broken voice calling him to supper.
His father tried to eat some ham and potatoes, but after
a bite or two he put his knife and fork down and said it
was no use. His mother, always so serene and rosy, had
lost so much of herself that Giles saw what she was going
to look like when she became an old woman.
At last his father spoke: "I hate to tell you, son, but
something terrible has happened to you."
Giles tried to lift a bite of meat to his mouth slowly as
if he were attentive but not disturbed.
"We put that money we were saving for you into the Coal
Company so that we'd be sure to have the thousand dollars.
I don't know what went wrong, but we never received any
dividends. Yesterday I was notified that there would be a
meeting of all stockholders to-day to consider the affairs of
the Company, and this forenoon I went to Centerville to
attend. It was bad enough never to have received the divi-
dends; but when I got there I found that we were never
going to get back what we put in. The new Company sold
its own stock to us and got out from under. The engineer
says this 'squeeze' in the vein that makes it so thin and hard
to work goes clear on back as far as the Company owns.
He recommended that the tipples and other belongings be
sold for junk to pay debts, and we'll be lucky if they do."
He waited long for Giles to say something, but Giles was
overwhelmed, not only by the thought of the loss to him-
self, but by the solemnity of his father's manner."You understand that that means there's not a red cent
left for you?"
"Yes."
"It may not seem so terrible just now. But the time'll
come when it will. It's all right to say that money is not
everything; it's not. But there are some things-like
knowledge that you may not be able to get to unless you
have a little money to start with."
Giles went on through the motions of eating.
"I once meant to get out. I was just as sure of it as
you've been. I had the money-two or three times as much
as we put in the Company. But the woman I got tangled
up with and married used it all, and by the time me and
your mother got squared away a little, it was too late-too
late for ourselves. So we thought we'd begin early on you.
If you could get out, that would be as good as getting out
ourselves-maybe better. We'd have made it if it hadn't
been for this.
"As it's turned out, so far as you're concerned, we might
as well have spent the money for the kind of a house your
mother ought to have."
"Yes."
Giles was so suffocated as much for his father as for
himself that he was equal to no words beyond the one.
He wanted to put his hand on his father's shoulder and tell
him that he was grateful, no matter how things had come
out; and he wanted to throw his arms round his mother and
tell her how much he loved her. But youthful lack of
logical and emotional coördination left him sitting just where
he was without a movement of his body or a word from
his lips.
Tears were filling his mother's eyes, and she got up from
the table and went into the front room. His father soon
followed her. Giles thought he had better walk out into
the spring air. He wandered down the ridge-a favoriteplace when he wanted to solve problems and tried to think
it all out. Aimlessly he walked back to the house and out
to the wood-yard where he hacked absent-mindedly at the
chopping block and walked round and round like a clay-
grinder until full night had come. But he was unable to
make logic out of a sequence which brought calamity only
to those who did nothing to deserve it.
He walked into the house and dropped into a chair. His
father and mother were still numb from the news. Luke
sat with his elbows on his knees and his chin in his palms,
a shell only of the man he had been two or three days be-
fore. It had been bad enough when he lost his own money.
It had been worse when he made ware from the wrong
kind of clay and lost an important season's profits; it had
been still a little worse when a killing frost had snatched
another season's profits from him; and it had been worst
of all when Eli Raynor, sleek from Centerville, had declared
the arrival of a tin can age that would rob him of profits in
pottery indefinitely. Yet when bad enough had come to
worst, he still had a son to be sent out in his own place, and
mines into which to go and earn money-slowly enough to
be sure if he stood ready to endure the suffering. But
now that his son was almost ready, the money he had saved
was snatched away, and the industry in which he might
once again have earned slowly had faded into nothing. The
prospect filled him with a despair compared with which all
earlier despairs seemed bright with hope.
Giles sat for a time and tried to think of something to
say. But he could not, except to remark that he believed
he would go to bed.
The next morning he wondered if it were worth while to
go to school. But since he would finish his course in a few
weeks, he decided it would hurt nothing to go on.
He found the Culbertson Company Row vastly disturbed.
All the women were out in their front yards talking acrossfences. Some were in tears, some were profane with rage.
Had Giles seen the morning Centerville Gazette, they
wanted to know? The mines were to be closed down for
good. Already Pewee Dunkle, Twinkle Savage, and a
number of other men were on their way to Carbondale,
Bluefield, and Smoky Hollow to find work before the others
from the Wiggam's Glory Row should have the news.
The next morning teams were backed up in front of
houses, and furniture ready to be loaded was standing out
in the yards. Pewee Dunkle and a teamster were sliding
a cookstove along the floor of a wagon bed with much shud-
dering of the iron legs and much rattling of the lids.
"Maybe I wasn't in luck," Pewee said to Giles. "Just
two men ahead of me, and we all got work. When I come
back past the tipple from lookin' for a house, they was
twenty-eight men lined up, and the boss didn't have work
for a one of 'em. I got a purty good house, too. Not as
good as one of these. The weeds in the garden are comin'
strong, and the road don't leave much room for a yard. But
I guess we'll have to make out. I was mighty glad to find
any kind, for I wanted that job. They say the roof in that
mine is as safe as any in the state of Ohio."
Mrs. Dunkle came out to watch them load in her new
iron bed. Her face was regretful, but it carried a faded
smile of reminiscence as if she could always know that she
had once possessed a nice house and yard for a day.
"Ain't it just too bad," she asked Giles, "that we've got
to pull up and go somewheres else and leave all of our nice
things? I told Pewee last night that it was the first time
we had ever had things nice, and now-" She stopped
and surveyed the yard. "But I s'pose it must be for the
best. Pewee thinks in another year we may be able to have
a yard over there."
Giles's own hurt sharpened his interest in the swift dis-
integration of the Row. It did not require a year; it didnot require a month. Within a week boys had smashed
windows. Then to hurry matters, a tornado snorted past,
leveled one house, whipped shingles from a half-dozen
others, and spilled the bricks of unsteady chimneys down
across the roofs of two or three more. Rain poured in
everywhere and swept soot and accumulations of grime
down over patches of whitewash and on out into the middle
of bare floors.
The few tenants who remained took full advantage of the
quick desolation. They gathered the shingles up for kin-
dling. Then they looked for other easy fuel. Why not burn
the palings? Why not burn the steps and the stairways of
the houses? But a few lingering families could not burn
up the entire wooden leavings of the Row in their cook-
stoves. Somebody hit upon the idea of burning an entire
house. It made a huge fire, and attracted a little atten-
tion; but nobody cared, since nobody lived in the house
and nobody ever would. Somebody tried another, but the
wind was in the wrong direction, and he burned down half
the Row, including two houses where people lived, and
cleaned up much of the fencing that had not yet been
burned piece-meal.
After school had come to an end for the year, Giles
wanted to see what No. 5 Company Row was like, or No. 1
over beyond Wiggam's Glory. He walked across to No. 5
and found it in worse state than the Culbertson Row. If
one did not work in a mine, why should one live in No. 5
Company Row? Only four or five families remained.
Squat, begrimed houses stood in the baking sun with win-
dows smashed, doors wide open, and soot, whitewashing
and stray strips of wall paper littering the floors. The night
before, somebody fired the tipple, and nobody was concerned
about bringing the firebug to justice.
The sight increased Giles's sickness over his own family's
plight. For a time he sat on the steps of the abandonedCompany Store, too disheartened for movement. Then he
wandered homeward. On the hill above the Row he saw
three girls of his own age sitting below the path in the shade
of a clump of hickory bushes. They were intent upon some
enterprise of their own. He stopped in the path to watch
for a moment. They had pushed their dresses above their
knees and with long straws of broomsedge were measuring
the circumference of their legs. Somebody declared her-
self the winner, and they all shrieked in pumped-up glee.
Giles thought he recognized one of the voices, and when he
came up, he found that he had.
"Hello, Giles!" this voice called.
"Hello, Gustie!"
He knew the other girls, too. They were Cora and Lydia
-Cora Dever and Lydia Pendleton of the old school days.
"Been over to the Row?" Gustie asked, anxious to make
talk.
"Yes."
"Didn't find much of it left, did you?"
"Not much."
"Most everybody's gone; and we want to. But we don't
know where to go. Is there anything to do over at Cul-
bertson?"
"Just about the same as here."
"Oh, my God, think of that, girls."
Gustie righted her clothes a little more completely.
"Why don't you sit down, Giles?" she asked in her best
English, "and talk awhile. We've not seen you since I don't
know when. You don't look busy, and we're not. We were
just sitting here killing time."
The other girls laughed unrestrainedly, then sobered their
faces as Gustie succeeded in holding herself to a short smile.
Lydia was graceful. She always had been. But now half
reclining on the grass, she was more graceful than ever. Shelooked as if she had been through a little less than the
other two.
Giles told Gustie that he believed he had better not stop
to visit. He must get home in time for dinner.
"Why don't you ever come over to a dance in the old
Company Store-some Saturday night?" all the girls wanted
to know.
"Oh, I hadn't thought about it. Maybe I will."
"Better come. The place may burn down some night,
and then there won't be any more."
Yes, Giles thought he might.
He swung over the ridge and down past the corner of the
woods where he had seen the Italians at their enterprise one
day. Suppose the young Italian should come along now.
Well, who would be afraid of him if he did? He wouldn't
be young any longer, for one thing. But anyhow Giles
wasn't disturbed. He'd spank any Dago that got smart
with him. He followed the road up past the pot-shop, and
then took the obscure path on the left to Wiggam's Glory.
No. 1 Row was just like the others, except worse. Not a
family remained. The houses stood undamaged. But with-
out sign of life anywhere, they were more disheartening
than if they had been half wrecked or half burned. Below
the mine, in the hollow, the greatest of all the gob piles in
the region had been fired by somebody seeking escape from
ennui, and it was burning with smoldering flames and a
mountain of sulphury smoke. It was bad enough to have
a great black industry roaring away on every side, but this
was worse the excrement of industry.
The possibility of spending a lifetime in such a region!
That night in the seclusion of his gaunt room with its log-
and-chink wall low under the roof, where he was accus-
tomed to go to struggle with unyielding ideas, he grappled
with one that he had until recently never so much as con-
templated. Heretofore it had mattered little what the im-mediate world was like, so long as he knew that he was
certain to spend most of his life elsewhere. From that log
house he had seen a path broadening away and away until
it encompassed a vast and radiant horizon. Now, instead
of that path, he saw a barricade that kept him where he
was, just as his father and mother had been kept despite all,
just as a few million of their contemporaries in one place or
another had been kept.
He lay in the quiet and fearfully gave his imagination free
rein. He constructed his future as it might now be: He
would get work-if he could find any; he would some day
marry one of the girls in the region; he and his wife would
dress up every Sunday for a time, as every man and woman
did when they were newly married; then they would spend
their money on their children instead of themselves and send
them to Sunday School; then sooner or later work would be
slack and clothes hard to find, and everybody in the family
would stay at home to economize; and then when the chil-
dren were fifteen or eighteen they would start forth confi-
dently on a journey that eventually would end up in just the
same place. Or perhaps he would never marry! He might
just live on with his father and mother and help them, and
keep out enough money for his own clothes, and be locally
referred to as the best-posted man in Culbertson and the
best shot in the township. That would not be so bad-for
a time. Yet the years would creep along until a day should
arrive when his Sunday clothes would feel stiff on his arms
and legs when he dressed up to go to church, and his fingers
would be muscular and hard, and his shoulders would sag
as his father's had already done, and people would look at
him as he lingered with his hands behind him under the
locust trees outside the church door and say, "Queer thing
Giles Dabney never married. And now he's beginning to
look old-forty-eight or fifty, he must be." And he would
exchange senseless jokes with them about feeling as youngas ever except when he tried to jump a fence, and they
would laugh themselves along, Sunday by Sunday, until they
were sixty, seventy, and heaven only knows how far be-
yond.
Was that all there was ahead?CHAPTER IV
H
E SLEPT. But he was awake again in the after-
part of the night. The quietness roared in his
ears. A night bird calling softly only added to his
sense of isolation. There he was! His dream of joining a
staunch company somewhere else was only a malady of
youth, was it, to which human beings were more or less
subject before they surrendered to solid realities?
He wanted to leap from his bed and shout "No!" Was
he going to let a few bumps tame him into staying in what-
ever enclosure he chanced to find himself? He would get
the upper hand. He would not be like the others not like
his father and mother, even. He would do something that
had poetry in it, whether others had been able to do so or
not.
With trembling hands he pushed the covers back, got up,
dressed, and tried to slip from the house without having
anyone hear. His mother, though, caught the cautious tap-
tap of his hard shoes on the stairs and asked him where he
was going.
"Squirrel hunting," he answered. "Over north the other
side of St. Bernard's. It'll be light by the time I get there."
It did not seem a logical thing to do so soon after a great
calamity. Even the words sounded trivial. But his mother
offered no protest.
The moon was over in the west-just above the tree-tops
in Wiggam's Glory. On the ridges it still surrounded every-
thing with a white mist in which rabbits raced off ahead of
him with the long easy leaps they take in stories. Above
the hollows, blue smoke from the gob piles hung heavy andshadowed what was beneath. Beyond Wiggam's Glory the
edge of the layer of smoke pressed over almost to the hill.
There it was, right there, just out of reach. Beneath its
edge he could see the merest smudge of all the vacant houses
down toward the mine.
Before he reached St. Bernard's the moon was down, and
darkness hugged everything. Yet so imminent was dawn
that he felt no sense of night as he passed the church. He
kept to the right and took the road that would bring him
down across the creek valley and up past the high hill with
the woods on its shoulder. As he began the long descent, he
seemed to be dropping away from something. He swung his
rifle freely, and took long strides which brought his feet
down with a distinct "Clap-clap." In the narrow valley,
trees overhung the road and made it so dark that he had to
rely on the noise his shoes made to tell him whether he was
going in the right direction. He almost bumped into the
end of the old covered bridge.
The road rose round the base of the high hill like the arc
of a spiral. Enough light was coming to mark the contour
of distinctly shaped objects. He walked deliberately, his
pulses quickened by the climb, his humbled spirit cheered
by the coming visibility of new surroundings. Some night
animal leaped from the low rail fence above him on his left,
and slid and rattled down the steep bank into the relative
light at his feet. It was a raccoon carrying a large bird
which obscured vision on Giles's side.
As the road came to the top of a high ridge it straight-
ened out and then descended the other side of the hill with
steep directness. Giles climbed the fence above him, took
his bearings, and pushed along the edge of the open woods
toward the hilltop. He looked back. There was dark gray
close along the eastern horizon, and paler gray above. He
walked on a few paces and looked back again. The palegray was tinged with the thinnest pink. He stood watching.
In another moment it was a deeper pink.
He was going to the top of the hill. He thought he left
the house because he could not endure staying there, but
now it seemed that all the while the hilltop had been in his
mind. He hurried upward. He would go all the way to the
isolated tree in the middle of the field.
Somebody had lived there once. The tree was a black-
heart cherry. At its base were the faintest remnants of an
older tree beside which this one had sprung up.
Overwrought and impressionable, he looked toward the
east. The veil-like clouds above the horizon and the bands
of them close down gave the sun its greatest opportunity.
It was not yet in sight itself, but it was near. The deep
hollows still looked heavy and dark, but the tops of the
hills caught the preliminary glow.
There it was! No, it was only a brighter fleck of cloud.
He became aware of the rifle on his arm and turned to
lean it against the body of the cherry tree. When he turned
back and looked, he saw the thinnest edge of a circular
brightness; then a little more; then still a little more. The
upper edge disappeared behind the band of clouds and then
in a moment began to appear above them. The darkness
in the valleys gave way. White houses grew whiter; green
fields grew clear to the eye; and then as the sun climbed
higher and sent its rays over a boundless sweep of wrinkled
hills, a stretch of the river that was in full view caught the
light like a mirror.
Giles turned to look westward. The houses were ablaze
with sunshine. The fields glittered in dew and green and
tawny gold. And the hills were without limit! At home,
if one looked away for two or three miles, enough hills of
timber overlapped each other to constitute a solid circle.
The world seemed to end there. But here there were nolimits. He had known that for a long time, but now he
felt it.
A tingling inspiration, a surge, a command of will, swept
through him. He was not going to let himself be swallowed
up. He would not! He would not! What if a few mil-
lions of others had? What if his father and mother had?
He would do differently. What he would do, how he would
do it, seemed a trifle vague. But such things had not to be
decided at the moment.
His face, his entire body, became warm as if he stood
within touch of a candent fire. It seemed good to enjoy
the warmth engendered by resolve without looking or trying
to look beyond it. His only wish was to stand there a
great oversized seventeen-year-old on a high hilltop in the
flush of morning and know that life was like that.
The assuring warmth did not desert him; but slowly it
merged into a milder ecstasy in which feeling was less in-
sistent, but none the less clear. He was aware of a great
honorable something within him-an unrestricted mind, a
conscience, a composite accumulation of ancestral yearnings
-that told him he might be equal to life's cruelties. In
this assurance he stood lost in a long reverie, at ease with-
out effort to be so, like an Indian on a high mesa, like a
mystic in a semi-trance. He did not weary of standing. He
did not require a new position or a new view. In unrest
he had found an encompassing peace.
When he became aware of his immediate surroundings
once more, the sun was far above the horizon. Houses and
roads and fields of wheat and patches of woodland looked
much as they had always looked. Crows scolded in the
lower edge of the meadow, and red-tailed hawks, almost as
large as eagles, circled high over the abrupt valley to the
northward. What he saw about him seemed to partake of
the world in which he had always lived.
For the sake of appearance, and out of respect for hisreputation as a marksman, he thought he had better kill two
or three squirrels before he started homeward. He reached
for his rifle, and though he was startled by its lightness,
he looped his arm under it, and swung off down across the
meadow toward the woods.
He brought down a great fox squirrel that plumped hard
on the ground right at his feet and swished his magnificent
red and black tail once or twice before he lay utterly still.
After the feeble alarm sounded by the rifle had been for-
gotten, he brought down a gray squirrel-a young, sleek
one-and then another fox squirrel. They lay within a few
feet of one another, just where they had yielded up life.
The morning had filled him with a great kindliness. Why
did he kill them, anyhow? But then they shouldn't tempt
him by coming so near. Or did they? Well, these would
be enough, anyhow. He must have a good reason for com-
ing back soon. He gathered them up, slipped a sharp stick
through a hind leg of each, and started down through the
woods in a direction that seemed homeward.
The timber was large, the ground clean and open. The
great oaks with sharp steeples of dead limbs high in the
sunlight, and less resistant yellow poplars with blunter
steeples, filled him with something of the awe that used to
catch him up in the depths of Genevieve's big woods. But
the fear that was a part of his earlier experience had long
ago vanished. Now it was only the awe which one feels in
the presence of the magnificent, in the presence of colossal
handiwork that has been patiently done. On the steeples
of two oaks which stood so close together that their tops
intermingled, sat two lazy turkey buzzards, oblivious of
anyone spying upon them from the depths of the trees be-
neath. Giles had never been so close to one of the kind be-
fore. He could see their bare, gray-red heads, their long
beaks with the hooked point, their dull brown wing feath-
ers, their occasional lazy attitude of inquiry. He lifted hisrifle and tried catching one of the turkey-like heads over the
sights. But no, he guessed not. What would he do with
one of them if he did kill it?
He climbed the fence and jumped down into the woods-
pasture of sugar maples. Off to the left he caught a glimpse
of the covered bridge, and moved in that direction. He
came into the faintest track of a road in pasture grass that
had grown up and fallen over in its luxuriance. This trail
seemed to lead to the bridge. Noiselessly he walked along
in the soft grass.
"Hello!" somebody said, right at his side.
He jumped in surprise, then saw under the spreading
maple which reached out almost to the trail, a girl of fifteen
or sixteen standing beside a two-year-old bay colt that
gazed at him with friendly eyes.
"Hello-o-o!" Giles answered, dragging the last syllable
out inquisitively. He slowed his pace as if to stop.
"Been killing some of my squirrels, have you?" She
asked the question in fun, to make talk, but in her voice
and manner there was something which suggested that she
held the upper hand.
"Your squirrels?" Giles asked, taking up her banter.
"Are they your private property?"
"No, but this is my woods-pasture."
"Well, I killed these up beyond the fence in the big woods
-up toward the top of the hill."
"Yes, I know. Our woods-one corner of it-goes clear
up to the edge of the meadow.
"They're pretty." She looped her arm through the colt's
rein. "Let's see them."
"You've seen them before, haven't you?"
"Not these three-that I know of!"
So she was getting bright, was she?
"Oh, what a wonderful tail! Look at the red and black.
It's almost like a fox's tail.""Well, it is a fox squirrel!"
"Smarty!"
She lifted the soft brush to her cheek. "Oo-o-o-h! It's
like a fur coat."
The colt backed away with a mild snort. He did not care
for these foreign-looking bits of animal life so near.
"Dan! What's the matter with you?"
She pulled at the bridle until he moved up with his nose
on her shoulder.
"Isn't he a nice colt?"
"Yes, fine."
"He needs riding. And since he's not quite ready for the
road, I ride him through this woods-pasture."
Giles could see how this soft grass was better for young
hoofs than a gravelly road would be. But he did not feel
any great inevitability in the current of conversation, and
moved as if to go.
"You didn't take me seriously, did you?" she asked.
"No, not very."
"Hunt all you like of them. They eat things if you don't
kill them off."
From the first glance when she spoke to him, they had
known each other, but neither made open use of the fact
until he was on the point of going.
"Saw you at our church one night, didn't I?"
"It may be. I've been there. Don't you sing in the choir
-front row?"
"Why, yes, but how in the world did you ever know
that?"
They both laughed at the transparent mystery.
There was a moment of silence. Then Giles moved again
as if to go.
"Say, you must be in a hurry."
"No-o-o," he answered, suddenly feeling a little abler totalk to an isolated girl. "But I've got three or four miles
ahead, and I haven't had any breakfast yet."
"My, you must be hungry. You ought to bring your
breakfast with you, or have somebody get you some."
How was this? What was it she was suggesting? But
before Giles could phrase a reply, she restored the conver-
sation to mere facts. "I hear they've closed down all the
mines."
"Yes."
"You're not weeping about it, are you? I'd think you'd
be glad to be rid of them."
There were, Giles admitted, advantages in not having
Company Rows or gob piles-or there would be when they
were gone. For ten minutes they talked facts.
When Giles started she pulled at the colt's bridle and
walked along as far as the sagging farm gate that led out
of the field into the road by the bridge.
"He won't stand yet," she explained, "and it's hard to get
on him bare-back unless you're up on something. He's
pretty high, up front. In another year or two he'll do a
mile in about 2:10."
Giles squeezed through at one end of the gate where it
sagged away from the post, and turned to say good-by. She
stepped up on the edge of the lowest board and stood with
her elbows and forearms over the edge of the top board.
The whole of her was like everything else that Wesley Let-
tinger owned-luxuriant. And her face was interesting and
beautiful.
"Good-by!" he said.
"Good-by! Expect you'll be over this way again, maybe."
"Yes."
"Hunt all you like in my woods."
"All right."
"Good-by!"
"Good-by!"As he entered the covered bridge, he glanced back. The
two-year-old flashed like a streak through the maple grove,
the rein on his neck, Beulah riding as free as the wind, her
arms outstretched, her head thrown back, to catch the full
witchery of swift movement.
Giles took the hill road with impetuous strides. When
he was well up he stopped for breath, and turned to look.
Despite all that he had suffered, he felt himself living a page
of a story. He turned and hurried on.
There was St. Bernard's. He could build a church like
that. Of course he could. Didn't he see why they made
that roof so steep and so high; why they built those two
arched false windows; why the true windows were of
colored glass; why the cross on the steeple was so simply
designed? Didn't he see all these things?
He strode on, his physical weariness lost in his new sense
of adequacy. What could he not do if he always felt like
this? And in an effort to answer his own question, he
walked all the rest of the way home in enjoyment of his
strength.H
CHAPTER V
TE WALKED into the front yard where his mother
worked in hope at her beds of flowers, and startled
her by abrupt speech.
"Mother!"
She straightened up from where she worked at some tiny
fall-roses.
"You ought to have a new house!"
What now could be in the head of this restless creature to
whom she had given life?
"Why-why, yes, perhaps we ought. But it's no use to
think about that especially now."
Nevertheless, her voice betrayed interest.
"Well, why isn't now just the time? What else have I
got to do?"
"But it takes money, and we-"
"No, sir!" he denied in a bantering voice that she thought
had been lost in his maturing life. "It doesn't take money.
It just takes lumber and a builder. I know where I can
get the lumber, and I can do the building myself."
She was accustomed to occasional audacities close upon
the heels of his depressions, but she was scarcely prepared
for this one.
"But what do you know about building houses?"
"I know it's not very hard, or some of the people who
work at it would never get them up. Those empty houses
over in the Wiggam's Glory Row are so bony you can see
how the whole thing's done. What can you say to that?"
Before she could say anything he hurried on: "I'd get the
lumber over there, too. That office building and supplyhouse is built of good lumber. And it belongs to us just as
much as to anybody, doesn't it? Isn't some of our money
in it?"
She could not deny that. Nor could she deny that the
material in it was good-incomparably better than what had
gone into the dwelling-houses. The studding was straight;
the joists were stout; and the siding was clear. It was just
as well to have no knot-holes in the siding of a building
where powder was stored. And the roof was slate, too-
something that would not catch fire.
Val was overwhelmed by the proposition, yet went about
with a face that felt new and pink. Luke came up from
the pot-shop at noon and learned from her what Giles had
proposed. It seemed reasonable. But like most reasonable
things, it aroused skepticism. Anyhow, they couldn't take
the lumber unless they got permission. He was getting out
a kiln of fruit jars and had to hurry back. Still he would
think it all over.
It would be difficult to say why he worked at his stone-
ware so persistently. He did not sell much-although since
the Company stores had closed a few of the women left in
the region had returned to their earlier preference for fruit
jars over tin cans. On one sure basis of logic, however, he
could persist in pottery: there was nothing else to do. With
shoulders drawn, and with incessant pain covering the natu-
ral alertness of his face, he gave himself to his labors.
That evening when he came up he was in a brighter mood.
There might be something in Giles's idea. Of course, they
would have to get authorization to take the lumber. But he
believed he could get it. He would write.
The reply from the attorney in charge of the Company's
affairs said that everything was now about closed up; that
there could be no imaginable sale for the old buildings; and
that Luke as a stockholder might as well help himself. The
buildings would probably be burned down by somebodypretty soon, anyhow. It would, of course, be better to have
the transaction legal. So he was enclosing an agreement in
duplicate, and when he received from Luke one copy duly
signed-he had already signed both of them himself-and
the sum of twenty-five dollars, Luke would be authorized to
take such lumber as he needed.
Was there a joker here somewhere? Luke read the agree-
ment twenty times in order to make sure. He did not in-
tend to wake up some morning and find that he had mort-
gaged his life to still somebody else. Then he asked Giles
to read it. Had Giles learned anything down at school that
would here arouse suspicion? Giles read carefully, looked
on the back, read again, looked on the back once more, and
at last expressed the opinion that it was without trickery.
Yes, Luke had the twenty-five dollars. He had had al-
most another hundred ready to put into the Company when
the crash came. The crash saved that, anyhow. So he
signed the agreement and sent it and the money along.
Giles saw his mother's face losing most of its weariness;
his father's, a little of its pain.
A new house? They had not thought much about that.
They had been thinking about their son. It was difficult to
get their minds squared away toward something that would
be selfishly comfortable for themselves. But the fact that
their son was in it, that in a degree it was still their son
they were thinking about, enabled them to change their di-
rection somewhat. They were helped, too, by the fact that
they were now thinking about something which might come
to pass.
The location of the house, yes. It would have to be built
on a definite spot. In principle, the choice was easy; it
should be in front of the old one. On half the farms from
