the Ohio westward there was, behind the new white frame
house or the bright red brick one, a discarded house of logs
with a lean-to kitchen that now served as a place of storage,or stood vacant and slowly settled into the ground while the
energies of the family went into the upkeep of its more
comfortable successor. Yes, it would look well to have an-
other farm with the new house just in front.
But some twist in Giles's way of looking at things led
him to dissent. He favored a spot just outside one corner
of the inclusive yard. His father and mother listened won-
deringly, a little doubtfully. But the certainty with which
he envisaged everything eventually swept them along with
him.
"Why, if you put it here, it will reach almost from that
tree over to this one, and back toward those two. You will
have shade on three sides to begin with, and all the room
out front for flowers or whatever you want to put there.
The front fence would be right along here" and he hur-
ried to show them where. "And the side fences here."
Nervous with his dream he ran from spot to spot in an ef-
fort to reveal everything at once: "The walk-right down
here; the front door-about there; and flower beds all the
way down to the gate and over here and here."
He was in one of his moods when he was irresistible.
Why, yes, of course, that was where the house should be.
Where else could anybody think of putting it?
As the short season for the making of fruit jars came to
an end, Luke accompanied Giles to the Wiggam's Glory
mine to salvage materials. While they worked one morning
at taking slate from the roof of the supply house and letting
it down with a rope, Luke asked thoughtfully: "How do you
know you can get the angles and rafters sawed so they'll
fit?"
"Mr. Burrell," Giles answered. "He was a carpenter,
you know. He built furniture, too."
Yes, Luke knew. He once turned over to Mr. Burrell a
bit of cabinet-making which he had been trying one morn-
ing to do himself.The rafters and the joists were marvels-heavier than
necessary, but what did that matter? The weather-boarding
was perfectly clear of knots. And so was the grooved floor-
ing. It was a house of gold! They would not need to touch
one of the cheap, tottering houses in Company Row.
Luke was sure there was twice as much lumber as ever
could be used in one house. Giles was inclined to agree.
But there it was! Why not take it? They were entitled
to it.
When with great patience it had been hauled home, a few
pieces at a load on the slender wagon drawn by the blind
claygrinder, it overflowed all limits. The stable bulged with
window frames, sash, and doors. Every shed had siding
and flooring sticking from the ends of it. And on three sides
of the site itself, rough lumber which the weather would not
damage greatly was piled high.
Then the foundations had to be assembled. That was no
fun. The stones had to be found; they had to be hauled;
they had to be unloaded; and the ones for the cellar and the
low wall just above ground had to be dressed up a little.
Through long days they worked. Giles thought they would
never finish. And when at last they had enough on the
ground in early October, he was ready for a rest before he
attempted trenches or walls.
The truth was that voices were calling to him. The red
maples cried aloud; the yellow ones piped softly; and the
tall bronzing hickories whispered without stooping. One
morning an hour and a half before full day, he was up and
out of the house and on the road to the Lookout.
Up the spiral road he made his way, and on up across
the meadow. Then he sat in the crisp twilight on a block
of sandstone at the foot of the cherry tree and awaited the
sun.
There were no clouds-not even an Indian-summer haze.
The sun came up without poetic preliminaries and quicklyflooded the reds and purples and yellows and greens with
brightness. In the presence of such glory it was not possible
to sit. He had to stand-to stand and feel great tears of
faith welling up in his eyes and then lose himself alto-
gether as he contemplated the unending beauty that was not
without sadness. All the drudgery of foundation-gathering,
all the imperious lusts of flesh that was young and strong,
all awkwardnesses of every kind in the world to date were
swept away by the celestial flood.
When the sun was high and his ecstasy had as before
merged into great calm, he walked over to the north side of
the meadow to discover what was there. Below, the decliv-
ity was almost perpendicular; he stood above the tops of the
trees. For a time he studied the distant rolling hills, and
then turned and walked toward the shoulder of woods be-
tween him and the covered bridge in the deep narrow valley.
Why was he going down through the woods? He was in
no mood to kill. He did not care whether he saw any wild
animal or not, except as it made up a part of the general
scene. Did he think he wanted to meet Beulah? Well, now
on that point he was not altogether certain. He would see
first whether she was there.
He climbed the fence into the maple grove. He looked at
the trail and saw that a horse without shoes had been mak-
ing constant trips out and back. Evidently she was giving
Dan plenty of exercise. Or-he was not quite sure of her,
and put the thought aside. But what he had thought was,
"Maybe she has been coming to see if I ever returned."
A world that had long imposed doubt upon him filled him

to work at his house again the next morning.
Strangely enough, he was not now certain what he wanted
to build. He found that he had grown away from the
thought of a plain two-story front of four rooms and a
kitchen behind-the style then popular in the region. His
mother would be content-enraptured-at any kind of a
new house. "Not too much of one, though," she had cau-
tioned him, "or there'll be nothing to put in it." He would
bear her admonition in mind. But he had an overabundance
of lumber, and could try whatever kind he chose.
He thought he had seen houses, but now he began to see
them anew. And how ugly most of them were! They were
too squat, or they were too high and thin, or they were pon-
derous like a barn, or they were covered with sawed grape-
vine ornamentation that wound all over verandas and gables.
The good ones were brick or stone, he guessed. No, some
of the old white ones were just as good. Genevieve's was
half and half. But it was too magnificent. Finding the
beautiful house, he began to think, was going to be as diffi-
cult as finding the beautiful girl.
But he unexpectedly had abundant time for his search;
winter came early and froze the ground as hard as stone.
He would devote the winter to houses. Whenever he
discerned one far on some hilltop, white in the morning or
evening sun, he tramped as many miles as intervened in
order to inspect it. Usually when he came up close to it
he saw that it was better viewed from afar. But occasion-
ally he had the delight of coming face to face with a struc-
ture that had proportion and touches of grace.
Books had houses in them, too and magazines. He
leafed through all the illustrated books in the house; he
hunted up his old copies of the Atlantic Monthly and
Harper's Magazine and searched for houses in the adver-tisements. From somewhere he conceived a shape that sat-
isfied him. But would it look like that when it was built?
He had better find out. He would build a model-so many
feet reduced to so many inches, like the map in the geog-
raphy.
He cleared the smokehouse, built a roaring fire one morn-
ing in February, and set to work. It took longer than he
had thought. But eventually all important details were fin-
ished. It would do. It was fairly low and fairly wide.
There would be four rooms downstairs, and that many up-
stairs with dormer windows front and back. They could
use as much of the upstairs as they liked. Out front there
was a small portico, and out back a long porch to eat sup-
per on in hot weather.
In April he began building. But work went slowly.
Through some miracle his father was receiving orders for
stoneware. Not only could his father not help him much,
but he himself had to go down and help his father occasion-
ally with a kiln of ware that was ready to be set or drawn.
Still the house gradually came into being. He worked with
the swift thoughtfulness of a concentrated mind. When he
encountered a problem that he could not solve unaided, he
hurried away to Mr. Burrell, who tried desperately to
brush cobwebs from his mind and find memories of the
knowledge which Giles required. It had been a long time
since he had done more than make a wagon bed or design
a poplar coffin for some child or other whose parents were
cramped somewhat beyond the ordinary. It was difficult
to remember. But usually, when his faded glimmer of a
principle or method was added to Giles's quick readiness,
the result was worth having.
Increased returns from pottery guaranteed plaster and
paint. In late July, Giles was ready to put plastering lath
on. In August, after the plasterers had come and done their
work, he was fitting windows and putting on the paint.With the end of his labors not far ahead, he worked
sometimes with the rush of a young bull, sometimes with
quiet sensitiveness. He felt the least bit satisfied. Did not
everybody who happened along say to his mother, "Oh,
what a lovely house you are going to have! Just too lovely
for words!" And did not his mother blush a little in her
tremulous joy at the thought? These things he heard while
he quietly edged the paint along the sash of an upstairs win-
dow, or put the finishing touches on the banister that he had
devised for the head of the stairs. He could hear his mother
sweeping out shavings, too, and all sorts of oddments in
wood that might easily catch fire. She lived in mortal dread
that the house would burn down before she had a chance to
live in it.
One morning when the paint was dry, his father came
up early from burning off a kiln of ware. He was sweaty,
but the heat had given him a certain bodily and mental
radiance. He wondered why this was not the time to move
into the new house. He was as impatient as Val was, and
as fearful. So he and Giles began to carry things over.
There was no paper on the walls, and the carpets were in-
sufficient for the floors. But what did it matter? The floors
were tight, and the skim-coat of plaster was so clean that
carpet and wall paper seemed almost non-essentials. White
bedrooms for everybody! And a dining-room to eat in!
Val saw her own cup of blessedness overflowing.
The news of this house, a comely house built by a boy
who had just become eighteen, spread far. People who
passed along the road came in and said, "I just thought I'd
stop and see your new house, Mrs. Dabney." People who
lived farther off and did not believe the story, came to see
whether it was true or not. One day Pewee Dunkle and
his wife came all the way from Smoky Hollow. Pewee be-
lieved the reports; he thought Giles capable of anything.
But they just wanted to see with their own eyes. Work atSmoky Hollow was none too good, anyhow-no better than
it had been at Bluefield-and they took the day for the
visit.
In the afternoon Pewee inspected every corner of the
house as carefully as if it had been a coal mine with a
suspicious roof. His state of mind, though, was happier.
He was filled with wonder. All the rooms downstairs plas-
tered! All the rooms upstairs plastered! Windows with
neatly painted sashes everywhere-three or four to the
room! Pewee was so deeply impressed by the dignity of
it all that when he came downstairs he called Val "Mrs.
Dabney." He wondered if she knew what a fortunate
woman she was. His effort to tell her in elevated speech
carried him far above the earth.
He thought he had better ease back. "You know, Mrs.
Dabney," he said in an effort to change to subtle humor,
"there's only one thing I see to be afraid of. This big Giles
will be a-gettin' himself a wife some of these times, and then
he'll want the house himself."
He laughed noisily at the far-fetched idea, and Val smiled
in concurrence at the absurdity. Pewee was only trying to
be whimsical. Yet his remark left the tiniest pulse of fear
in her heart. She smothered it, she tried to believe, by say-
ing to Pewee: "Well, it's his house if he wants it. He built
it, didn't he?"
Still a little of the fear remained enough to make her
feel for the first time since she had fed this young giant
from her breast when he could not so much as hold his
head up, that for a certainty he would some day be married
and giving a large part of his affection to somebody else.
Giles told Pewee-and the other visitors that building
a house was nothing very difficult. None the less, he took
pride in his completed work. He knew that probably not
one of all those who came to see it would go to the trouble
necessary in studying houses as he had done, in figuring outangles, and in learning to saw with steady nerves along the
hair line made by the sharp point of a pocket knife. They
might talk about building; but he had built. There was a
difference between the one and the other. He walked to
Culbertson and went to church-when he felt like it-with
a little of "Well, maybe I'm not so much of a good-for-
nothing as you think" in his attitude-just a very little.
But he could not long be content with a completed dream.
The matter-of-factness that follows the finishing of a glori-
fied work began to press in upon him. He felt the need of
a new breath, a new dream.
He got up one morning at three o'clock and walked to
the Lookout. He saw the sun rise from behind the farthest
hills and transform a world. For a half-hour or longer he
stood there as silent, as completely lost in contemplation, as
if he had been the last Indian that had stood there in reli-
gious ecstasy. When finally he moved, he felt as if he had
been out of the world for a week or a month. Compared
with this great experience, houses and towns and churches,
and fussy little human beings who starved one another for
something they thought they wanted, seemed too pagan to
be admitted to one's thoughts.
What he should do must be more tremendous, after this,
than anything he had yet dreamed about. Suppose he were
to stand on this Lookout and see all these hills beautified
with white-fenced roads, and neatly trimmed orchards, and
well-proportioned houses built exactly where they ought to
be-wouldn't that be something?
He went down through the woods. That colt of Beulah's
was a three-year-old now. He was old enough to be shod,
and would therefore require no special exercising in a maple
grove. She probably had entered him in the county races
at Centerville. He would have to step along to do a mile
in 2:10-yes.
He walked beneath the maple trees, along the trail, andout to the sagging gate. He was not yet quite ready to face
Wiggam's Glory; the hill still held him. So he sat on the
gate and looked at the old covered bridge and the road up
the hill beneath an arch of trees, and dreamed dreams.
"So you're back, after all, are you?" somebody said just
behind him.
Sprawlingly he slid from the gate and faced about. Beu-
lah wore a riding habit. But for head-dress she had pulled
a red scarf back tightly from her forehead. She looked like
a well-bred gypsy.
Before he could find his tongue to any good purpose, she
said as if she were speaking to somebody ten years younger
than she was, instead of a year or two older, "So you've been
building a house, have you?"
"Where did you hear that?"
"Oh, everybody knows about it. They say it's good
looking. My dad was over that way last week and he says
so, too. And the way you built it for nothing! You must
have an eye for business."
He blushed to his ears at the praise. It wasn't anything
much to build a house.
Like any two country youngsters who are unaccustomed
to speech, yet who feel that for some reason speech must be
made, they talked on. She put her elbows and forearms on
the top edge of the gate in order to be at ease. Her fore-
arms were close-knit, and the curves extended right down
to her wrist like those of one of the statues in his Ancient
History. Her face was not only beautiful, but fascinating.
It looked at him and said: "I'd just love to be sincere, as
you seem to be. But you know there are so many things
one would like to have."
To wearied ears their conversation would have seemed
colorless enough. Yet when they parted, Beulah gave Dan
free rein and rode once more like a nymph under themaples; and Giles swung up the long hill in sturdy, rhyth-
mic strides, more certain than ever that he was going to do
a more momentous something or other in the world than
anybody had yet dreamed.CHAPTER VI

HE RAGE of youth was upon him, and found him
with nothing to do. Who was there who wanted to
discuss building with him when winter was at hand?
So he stalked restlessly about and looked off in directions
obscured in summer by the leaves, but open to view when
the leaves were gone. Or he sat in the warm, pleasant
kitchen while his mother got supper or cleared the dishes
away, and whistled sketches of tunes, drummed on the edge
of the stove hearth or the table, or re-read the Centerville
Gazette for the tenth time. When he wearied of this, he
got up, stretched languidly, and then sat by the dining-room
window.
Who was that girl going along the road, anyhow? He
would like to walk up and put his arm round her waist and
look into her face and see the girl of his dreams. She might
well be, from the looks of her. She moved along with pro-
found grace, as if a million of her forbears had engaged in
the endless experience of coming up out of nowhere into a
recognition of their capacity to be interesting. Yet if he
should overtake her aye, and slip his arm round her
waist as she walked she would almost certainly lift up a
face that was degraded, and inquire who the hell he thought
he was. And she might ask the question in a voice which
would make him understand that it little mattered who he
was, since she was not given to drawing fine distinctions
between one man and another. Well, he could talk with her,
whoever she was. He would look upon any girl. He still
hoped, though, to find one with gracious, honest ways-
like
...Now there was Theda. He had not seen much of her for
a year or two, but whenever he had seen her he could tell
easily enough that she had not been utterly brutalized by
life. She clerked in Randolph's store at Culbertson, and
wore long paper cuffs over her neat sleeves to keep them
from getting soiled, and carried her lead pencil sticking
through her thick roll of dark hair. Everybody called her
a nice girl. Giles thought she was, himself. She was almost
beautiful, too. She was beautiful that night at the church
when her face was radiant. If she were just interested in
something-in building houses, or in going off early in the
morning and watching the sun come with a shout from some-
where behind the horizon, or in absorbing some of the mys-
tery of a world not to be found behind a counter-if she
were only excited over doing some of these things, she would
be beautiful. But she had the best genteel job in Culbert-
son, and her heart was set definitely on keeping it. It was
easy to keep that job and at the same time remain a nice
girl. Giles tried to think of himself as loving her. At
times he could almost do it. Little beams of brightness
sometimes crept up into her face from her neck when he
talked with her. But when he tried to think of sitting down
opposite her three times a day for forty years-maybe fifty
-it seemed impossible. He wanted a wife whose mind
would go racing off to all sorts of places and back again.
He wanted somebody who would raise some big questions
such as that Samaritan woman had in the back of her head
when she talked with Jesus at the well. Perhaps that was
not just the kind either, but it was something different from
Theda.
Sometimes, too, he dared to dream of Beulah, his goddess-
gypsy. He saw her flashing beneath the trees on her thor-
oughbred. He saw her with her elbows and forearms on
the top edge of the gate. He saw her with nothing of that
upper hand manner in her movements, walking under themaples as if she were expecting some one. And when he
jumped down off the fence at the lower edge of the woods
and she saw him, she ran with arms outspread up the slope
to meet him, and he with arms outspread ran down the slope
to meet her, and they clasped each other in a swift embrace
and went whirling and dancing away under the trees in rap-
ture unending.
Sometimes he dreamed until he was disgusted with him-
self, and got up and went out and measured off the new
flower beds in the frozen front yard and tried to imagine
how the spaces would look when the lilacs were high and
the roses were in full maturity and in full bloom.
Late one wintry afternoon at the end of November when
he was outside the house trying to drive two or three stakes
in the hard ground, he chanced to look out along the road
and saw a girl walking westward. She lifted her arm in
friendly greeting.
"Come and go to the wake!" she called. It was Gustie
Malloy.
"Who is dead?" he called back, as if that had the least
bearing on her proposal.
"Old Pat Finnegan, up by the church; the humped-over
man."
Giles rushed the matter through his mind. He was in
need of excitement, yes.
"But I'm not a Catholic," he shouted back.
"Makes no difference. I am. I'll take you."
At the driveway she turned in.
"My, what a lovely house it is," she said admiringly. "It's
nicer than anybody has said. I knowed all along that you'd
do something grand some day."
Val came out, bundled up to gather eggs.
"I want to take him to the wake, Mrs. Dabney," she said
without shrinking. "I hate to walk all the way up there bymyself, and then come all the way back by myself in the
morning. And I promised Mrs. Finnegan I'd be there."
Val had heard not only whispers but plain speech about
Gustie. She wished Giles had not been out in the yard so
conspicuously. But 'twould only make matters worse to
bring up objections now to his going.
"Why, yes, if he thinks he'd care to go. I never went to
one myself."
Giles had reached his own decision, and went blusteringly
into the house to get ready.
"My, I don't wonder you're proud of him," Gustie said,
talking in the manner of a grown-up woman. "I always said
he was going to be a great man some day. I told him that
myself once before we quit the Wiggam's Glory school. I
wish I could say I'd ever done anything myself that
amounted to a row of pins."
Val thought that if Gustie were as base as testimony
tended to show, she must be even baser. For she put up a
hypocritical front walking right in like a good neighbor girl
and asking a boy to go to an Irish wake with her. But Val
was not certain. Maybe some of the stories were not true.
"Why don't you come in long enough to eat your supper
We eat early."
with us, and then go?
Well, yes, why not?
Gustie wanted to have a good look
at the entire interior of the house; anyhow.
When she and Val were inside, they experienced mutual
amazements-Gustie at the cozy taste of everything in the
house, and Val at Gustie's good-looking dress under her
good-looking coat. Val noticed, too, the ease with which
Gustie made herself agreeable at the table. Where had she
been able to learn so much?
When she and Giles started off together in the twilight,
Gustie said to Val cheerfully: "I'll see that he's delivered
back to you in the morning."
Was there something ironic in Gustie's voice? Val wasnot quite certain about that, either, but she decided at last
that perhaps there was not.
Giles and Gustie trudged along for a moment or two be-
fore either spoke. Then Giles asked: "So you're going to be
my guardian angel, are you?"
"Chtt!" Gustie responded, and a rush of worldly wisdom
swept her face. "Well, why shouldn't I? I'm just about
bad enough to make a good one."
Again they walked in silence-a great silence but not a
difficult one.
Gustie spoke first.
"When do you expect to get away?"
"Hard to answer that. Don't know whether I'll ever get
away. It takes money. And ours was in the Company."
"Wasn't that the awfullest thing anybody ever heard of?
I'd like to hang somebody for it."
"Yes," Giles answered promptly. "I shouldn't mind hang-
ing two or three myself."
They went into detail. But soon Gustie was back at the
main issue. "You mean, though, to find some other way of
going, don't you? It'd be awful to have to stay in this dump
all your life the way I s'pose I will."
"Yes, I'd like to get away, of course. But-"
"But what?"
Should he tell her? She always had been a sympathetic
hearer.
"But if I can't, I believe I'll start out building good-
looking houses for everybody around here."
Giles was startled to see how bare and skeleton-like, how
stripped of glory, his dream was when it was reduced to its
lowest terms in a dozen hard-edged words. He wished he
had not mentioned it at all.
"Yes," and Gustie hesitated. "Yes, that would be won-
derful, of course. But you must never stop with that.There's something more than that for you to do. And
you've got to do it."
"Say, you are looking out for me, aren't you?" Giles
was bantering, but a trifle self-conscious.
"I'll make you get away, that's what I'll do. I'll make
you do something worth while-and it's not to be found
around here."
It was still bright enough to see the outlines of the Wig-
gam's Glory Company Row.
"There! Look down there at that!" Gustie went on.
"How would you like to spend a lifetime in a place like
that?"
"Don't expect to."
"Well, sometimes you can do it whether you expect to or
not."
So they talked. It was not the speech of two youths who
look at each other as soon as they meet and say in their
looks and under their words, "Now I know I'm a young
woman and you're a young man," or conversely, "and isn't
that fact the most interesting thing in the world?" It was
friendly speech almost like that of two men-but not quite.
Gustie had established a point of view that was exclusively
hers. Out of her cuffings at No. 5 Company Row, out of
an over-early maturity forced upon her by circumstance, out
of her associations with men whose lusts were their only
concern of moment, she had developed an attitude of wari-
ness in behalf of those who had not traveled her way.
As they swung along in rhythmic stride their conversa-
tion moved easily into more philosophic paths. Gustie pos-
sessed little enough of what human beings call knowledge,
but she had troubled her head for a long time about the
whyness and the whenceness of things.
At last the bulk of St. Bernard's loomed in the blue dark-
ness. A field of fairylike gray told them where the church-
yard was."My, there's lots of people buried over there," said Gustie.
Giles admitted that there were many.
"And do you know, the first funeral I ever attended when
I was a kid was here? It was that little Fealty girl. I was
scared to death. Do you remember her?"
Yes, Giles remembered. Yes, he remembered.
At the wake there was plenty to eat, there was plenty to
drink, and there were plenty of people to do both. The en-
tire night might have passed without anything but joyous-
ness had not Padraic O'Toole decided on one more drink at
about two-thirty in the morning. This last drink, when
added to all the others, was just enough to bring his imag-
ination out of bondage into freedom. He began to tell ghost
stories, and he told them with so much authentic detail that
all the women put their fingers in their ears-or pretended
to-and begged him to desist. Such protestations only
proved that he was achieving success. So he gave his florid
fancy a clear road. He lived across on the hillside from the
Potter's Field, and at night whenever any unfortunate had
just been buried there, a pale gray light moved along the
road over toward the sacred churchyard. It would move
over in that direction, and seem to get lost, and then come
back. Once he slipped down by the road and watched it
pass. It was in the form of a human being-just as if a
real person had been made up of sunlight or moonlight
shining into a dark room through a crack. Yes, it was just
like that. And from time to time it sighed heavily, wearily.
" 'Oh-h-h-h!' like that it sighed. Then after it had gone
a few steps, so to speak, it would sigh again: 'Oh-h-h-h!'”
Padraic waited a moment to let the impression become
firm. In the interval, in the next room where the corpse
was, there was a ghastly sigh.
"By God, here he comes now!" Padraic exclaimed, his
eyes bulging, and he rushed for the kitchen and the highback porch, the nearest exit that did not lead directly
through the front room.
There was shrieking, and tumbling furniture, and a helter-
skelter rush for the kitchen door. Women and men went
over the edge of the high porch in a great heap in the orchard
below, quickly struggled to their feet, oblivious of raked
shins and wrenched ankles, and tried to run farther in the
morning moonlight, the men puffing in their fullness, the
women crossing themselves, and praying "Holy Mary,
Mother of God!"
Gustie did not believe in ghosts; so she did not run as
fast as the other women, although she said she felt creepy.
And she was the first to begin rounding up the girls and
women in the orchard, as a hen pheasant would round up
her family after a hawk had swooped down and scattered
them. It was nothing. The undertaker had not been quite
successful in keeping old Pat's crooked body straightened
out, that was all. But some of the women shivered in the
orchard until the low moon had gone all the way down and
left the orchard spookier-looking than the house, before
they would venture back.
The incident afforded Gustie the right kind of material
for a new train of philosophic reflection as she and Giles
made their way homeward in the full light of day. She
was much interested in knowing why things were as they
were, and especially in knowing why they were not some
other way.
Giles could offer few satisfactory explanations. Anyhow,
what he was trying to get hold of was not some abstraction
but a specific undertaking.
The winter offered him nothing to do. So he went wher-
ever he thought there might be entertaining activity-to
every wake, every revival meeting, every church social, every
dance, within walking distance. He found fun in being a
big young ruffian that people were beginning to like. The...
boys said, "We might as well ask old Giles to go, too." The
girls said, "The great big But I like him, don't you?"
At play-parties they were glad to be kissed by him because
he kissed them as if he meant it; at dances they were always
ready to give him a dance because he held them as if he
never intended to let them get away, and whirled them over
the floor like a tornado.
Energy! In the early evening when he started off from
the house, he felt glowing and competent and ready. Some-
times when he entered a room and saw all the girls concen-
trating attention upon him as he stood with his head loom-
ing above every other, he imagined that the other boys might
be the least bit envious. But they made no trouble. He
knew he could thrash the life out of any one of them and
not half try, and they knew it, too. Whatever was ahead,
he felt equal to it.
Spring came at last, despite a threat not to do so. In
recognition of the fact, there was to be a grand, final dance
in the old Company Store Hall at the deserted No. 5 Row.
Since nobody lived there, the dancers had to come from
somewhere else. That, of course, was precisely what was
desired. Nobody felt restrained; nobody felt watched. So
everybody came. On special occasions, brisk young men
and brisk young women of good families traveled all the
way from Centerville in order to enjoy this complete
freedom.
Yes, this last dance was to be the season's greatest. The
management said so. They guaranteed a good time for all;
and they were ready to say that all would be there.
Giles thought he had better not miss it. There might be
a little touch of poetry-an extra-good fight or two, maybe
a little play of six-shooters, and certainly some swift music
by the cheerful musicians. He was in need of poetry; he
had better go.
The management had spoken truthfully: it was the sea-son's most important social affair. Long before the first
dance was announced, more than the usual number of men
were ruddy with stout liquor. And when the dancing began,
there were so many couples on the floor that collisions were
numberless. The men were content to grumble at each
other. But the women and girls asked in full voice: "What
the hell you think you're a-tryin' to do, anyhow-tramp a
body's clothes off?"
Within an hour there had been two unimportant fights,
and a little flourishing of six-shooters. But the men con-
cerned were tactfully reminded that they might interfere
with the pleasure of others if they undertook to shoot each
other on a floor so crowded. So the dance went on. The
air grew heavy with liquor, over-abundant cheap perfume,
and the sweaty odor of human bodies. The musicians in an
effort to be followed with some faithfulness swayed and
scraped and pounded. The dancers shouted more raucously
still to one another and swept on and on in a wild confusing
whirl over the floor.
Giles thought he got more poetry out of the spectacle by
watching than by participating. He stood by a window and
studied all these fellow creatures trying so desperately to
free themselves from the tedium of their lives.
"Hello, Giles," some one said. There at his side were
Gustie and two or three other girls who had come over
from Culbertson, and Sockey McPherson. The other girls
had drafted Sockey. They did not feel in Gustie's class-
not yet and did not quite wish to come with her-or
rather have her come with them-unless there should be a
male person along to give a balanced respectability to their
mission.
"I'm surprised to see you here in such a place," Gustie
said. "Didn't you know what it'd be like?"
Giles was not certain whether she was serious or only
wanted to seem so."Oh, I thought I'd come over and see for myself."
"Been dancing?"
"Dancing? Do you suppose I'd try to dance in that?"
"No, hardly. Fact is, it's more interesting out of doors in
the moonlight. Just look out."
They looked out together from the wide upstairs window
where Giles had been standing, and were oblivious of all
the bedlam at their backs.
After a time Gustie asked: "Do you suppose anybody
lives on the moon?"
"More of them live on moonshine!" Giles answered, and
ducked his head down low and dared not look her in the
face after the bad pun.
"Smarty again!" she said, then once more was serious.
"But I want to know. I've thought about it a lot."
They leaned on the window sill and looked away into the
moonlight and touched upon many matters not related to
the dance: how people chanced to be on the earth in the
first place, what they were put here for, and what became
of them when they departed.
"It would take a lot of room for everybody that's ever
lived, wouldn't it?" Gustie asked as though Giles ought to
be an authority on that subject. "It would take a lot of
room even if everybody stood up all the time."
They talked, too, about schooldays, and what had hap-
pened since, and when he would be getting away maybe, and
what he would do in the far-off romantic world.
"Do you know what I'd like to do?" Gustie asked.
She was so frank, and she had been so much interested
in him, that Giles wondered if she were ready to say that
she'd like to go with him.
"Do you?" she insisted.
"No."
"Then guess."
"But I haven't the slightest idea.""That would make it all the more fun to guess."
"Well, let me see, then."
Giles pondered the matter.
"No, I give it up. What?"
"Will you laugh at me if I tell you?"
"No."
"And you won't tell anybody that I told you?"
"No."
"Cross your heart?"
"Yes."
"All right, then. But you're sure you won't laugh?"
"No, I won't laugh. Go ahead."
She hesitated a moment still, then said, "I'd like to be a
nun."
Giles did not laugh, nor did he speak. He cast a quick
glance at Gustie's profile there so close beside his face, and
with the full moonlight across it, it was a beautiful profile—
more beautiful than he had thought that morning when he
first became aware of it down at the forks of the road on
the way to the Wiggam's Glory School. Her face was alight
as if the brightness came from within.
"Well, why don't you?"
Gustie was as slow in answering his question as he had
been in asking it.
"Why don't you?"
"Well, the truth is, I don't know whether they'd want me.
Lot's of people don't."
Somebody gave Giles a resounding whack between the
shoulders. He straightened up and whirled round all in
one movement. A man not quite so tall as he was, but much
heavier, stood smiling an undisciplined smile at him.
"Saw that neat form in the window beside you, and since
you didn't seem to be making any use of it yourself, I
thought I'd take it over." He was just enough in liquor to
believe that he was irresistible."Oh, go ahead and tend to your own affairs," Giles sug-
gested, not in utter unpleasantness, but abruptly.
"What's that?" the man asked, his ruddy face becoming
purplish. "What's that you're a-tellin' me to do?"
He stepped up until his nose was within a foot of Giles's.
"Will you just say again, young fella, what you said be-
fore?"
"Why, you're to mind your own affairs, and let other
people mind theirs."
"Oh, little boy," he shouted, "where I come from I make
mincemeat of anybody who talks to me like that. I'll just
give you a sample."
He backed off, cast an eye about as if he wanted every-
body to see, and then flourishing his arms like a windmill,
shouted all the louder: "Watch out, here I come! I'll flat-
ten out that big nose of yours! I'll paste you right into
that wall!"
Just as he arrived, Giles darted to one side and out into
the open, and let him crash into the wall-knuckles, face,
and body.
He whirled round, enraged but expecting to see Giles
disappearing down the stairway. "You God-damned-
God-damned coward baby!"
But Giles stood facing him, too close to allow another
bull-like rush.
"Watch out! He's too old for you, Giles!" some friendly
voice shouted from the semi-circular crowd.
But a battle was on. The man's arms were flying again.
Giles thought he might not be so dangerous as he looked,
and stepped in and let him have a swinging sock right in the
middle of his thick face. He was too much of a gorilla to be
stopped, though, by a smash of that kind. He came right
on.
Giles saw that he must keep him away, and that he
must bring an end soon. At the first opening he drove full
into his meaty face again. This did not stop him either,but it straightened him up, and with the lightning of youth,
Giles was in close with a cut to the chin. The man went
backwards as if he had been suddenly chloroformed, and
his heavy head crashed against the sharp projecting corner
of the window sill. Then, crumpled and with his face up-
ward, he lay like a great piece of meat close to the wall.
For a moment there was a vast stillness in the hall. Then
some of the women spoke. "Oh, he's killed him! He's dead."
Giles stood in tense consternation at what he had done.
Why didn't somebody say something or do something?
Then there was a babel of voices, and two or three men who
liked to be prominent in all matters of combat walked over,
squatted down close to the figure, and pulled the head and
shoulders round so that the body could be straightened.
They left a pool of blood, and made a long red smear where
they swung the head round. Some of the women wanted
to be theatrical and swoon away, but their hardened sin-
cerity could not so easily be overcome.
"Well, I guess he's done for," one of the men said. He
twisted the head over. "Boy, look at that gash where he
went into that sharp corner."
"Mercy, what'll they do with Giles for it?" some thought-
ful woman wanted to know.
"They won't do anything with him," Gustie answered, in
strong everyday speech. "It was the old buzzard that
started the trouble. We had a right to talk if we wanted
to. And he just walked over and cracked Giles right be-
tween the shoulders."
"Won't need to worry, anyhow," one of the three men
said. "He's breathin'. He's a-goin' to come round."
Soon he trembled and shuddered, and then breathed more
normally; and then he tried to look about to see what was
happening.
"Better take him out, so's we can go on with the dance,"
somebody suggested; and four men lifted him up, and oneheld a bunch of crumpled paper from packages of popcorn
under the back of his head, and they carried him out. Some
member of the management gallantly brought some more of
the paper and smeared up the blood from the floor, and the
dance went on.
Sockey and three or four others came up to tell Giles
what a feat he had performed. "It'd 'a' done no harm even
if you had put him out for good. He thinks just because
he can lick everybody in Bluefield, he can do the same
everywheres else. I'll bet he'll stay at home where he be-
longs after this."
Giles suddenly felt weak and relaxed, and had little in-
terest in what they were saying. He was a bit more inter-
ested when Gustie ventured to offer congratulations and say
how much she regretted that she had been the cause of
trouble.
After this near-tragic purifying of minds, everybody felt
even-tempered and chivalrous, and the dance went on more
joyously. Sockey was moved to show the right spirit by
taking Gustie for one dance, or maybe two. While they
were lost in the crowd, Giles went out.
"We'll go with you a piece," one of a small group of men
down in front of the building said. "You can't tell, that
son-of-'er may have somebody with him who'll try to do you
some dirt."
They walked along slowly, and the men enlarged upon the
skill with which Giles had stepped in and caught him on the
point of the chin. "I'll bet he saw stars of forty colors-
and then the sun."
Giles assured them that they needn't go farther unless
they liked. But they walked on, proud to be with a young
man who gave such positive evidence of having a bright
future. They would go all the way, and then down the
road to Culbertson-which would not be so much farther,
anyhow, than down the railroad track from the Row.CHAPTER VII
NE DAY a week later while Giles was still feeling
in the background of the world after using up so
much of himself in combat, he walked homeward
along the road below Genevieve's big woods.
"Well, sir," said Dr. Seabright as he overtook him, and
drew in his new team, "I hear you've been indulging in
social life."
Giles smiled half shamefacedly, half triumphantly, and
colored round the neck and ears.
"Yes."
"Climb in. But be lively. These new roans are the pep-
periest ones I ever hitched to a double-tree."
The team was off with a lunge.
"Old times," Doc said, "picking you up like this."
"I was thinking of that myself."
Doc sat straight in the seat, the buggy whip aloft in his
right hand, the lines tight between the fingers of his left.
His face was as swarthy and as alert as ever, the dark cir-
cles under his eyes were but little wider, and the hair on the
side of his head in front of his ear seemed little grayer than
a dozen years before.
"Just down to see what's left of the Row," Giles explained
in order to account for his presence on the road. "S'pose
you heard that they burnt the old store and hall down-and
a lot of the houses."
"Yes, so I heard. Who did it?"
"Everybody, I guess. They got tired of dancing the other
night and thought they'd have a fire. Some of the houses
caught from the store."Dr. Seabright seemed but casually interested.
"You must have given that big bruiser quite a wallop."
"What makes you think that?"
"Why, they called me in to patch him up the next day.
The gash in the back of his head was two inches long and
better. I really ought to have taken some stitches, but he
already has three or four big scars in his scalp, so I guess
one more won't make much difference."
He scolded his horses a little for their impatience.
"How did you happen to be over there?"
"Oh, I just wanted to find a little excitement."
"Tasting a bit of life, eh?"
"I guess so."
"Unh-hunh-h-h!" Doc answered with great deliberate-
ness. "Well, it's all right-it's all right; provided, of course,
you don't try a whole meal of it all at once."
What was this that Doc was saying?
"But you have to have experience, don't you?"
"A certain amount of it, I guess, as a kind of a working
basis. That's what people say. But if you spend all your
time going round looking for experience you won't get much
else done, for there's a lot of it to look for. Imagination's
worth more than too much experience. If you have imagi-
nation, and will get some good stiff liquor and get drunk just
once by the right easy gradations so that you can savor
every step of it, and then sober up and think it all over,
you'll be in a position to imagine all the joys of heaven and
earth and the remorse of hell for the rest of your life with-
out trying anything else much. If you have imagination,
that is. Some people don't have much, and they don't use
what they've got; so they have to keep on tasting around."
Dr. Seabright had not talked to anybody except a patient
or two since early morning and he was ready for an audience.
"Just over to leave some pills with old Pud Magee. He's
about ready to hand in his checks. May last a couple ofdays yet. I doctored his first wife a long time back, but
when this new doctor hung out his shingle at Culbertson ten
years or so ago, old Pud decided to save money, and passed
me up. But when he got sick some time ago himself and
the doctor told him there was nothing to be done, old Pud
sent for me. I wasn't dying to go, for I had a hunch that
he was on his last legs. And I didn't go at first. Then he
began to send messengers to me. They got down on their
knees, so to speak, and begged me. I sat pretty for a time,
but when they brought the word that old Pud had said I
was the only man on earth who could save him, I told them
to tell him that if God Almighty hadn't got in too much of
His work by the time I arrived, I'd come and save him.
"Ha! Old Pud! He had a theory of life. He said:
'Make them knuckle every one of them. If you can make
everybody else knuckle, you're sure to have some of the
things you'd like yourself.' After his first wife died, I said,
'Pud, she was a fine woman.' 'Yes,' said he, 'she was. But
she was always just a leetle too light for my work.' Every
man who had a little money, every pretty girl that got in
his way, anything that got its head up a little into the fresh
air, old Pud tried to make knuckle under. He's about
through now, but he's still looking for somebody or some-
thing to bend and break. The spirit is still willing, but-
his old carcass is pretty much the worse for wear. About
two days, I'd say."
They passed old Genevieve on the road, with a new dog
at her heels.
"How do you do?" she said in clear, resonant voice as if
she were not averse to meeting them.
As soon as they had passed, Dr. Seabright said: "Old
Genevieve's a character, isn't she? Everybody thinks she's
just a wild rip-snorter of a woman and nothing else. But
you always give ear, don't you, when she's around any-
where? There's a little of the high manner in her. What-ever she tries she comes off with in good style. If she'd ever
decide to kill a man, there'd be nothing small about the way
she'd do it. She'd make it a show worth seeing. Yes, sir,
she's got the sublime vitality."
They passed the pot-shop. Luke was carrying in some
drying ware from the ware-yard. He nodded a salute as he
balanced a long board of fruit jars on his open palms and
steadied it through the door.
"You don't take to clay yourself, do you?" Dr. Seabright
asked Giles.
"No. There's not enough for dad to do, let alone me. I
never cared for it anyhow. I'd rather build."
Doc was busy turning Luke over in his mind. "Your
dad is another character-not like Genevieve, of course.
Any other man would have chucked the pot-shop after the
first year. But he just held on. I suppose he likes the feel
of clay the same as I like the smell of medicine. And who
can say that he's not just as well off-maybe better? He's
had a good deal bumped out of him, but he's got the sublime
vitality, too. Nothing ever quite knocks it all out of some
people.
"As a matter of fact, there's a good deal of it in your
family. Your mother's got it. Tog her out and send her
to the Court of St. James's and she'd do just as well as any-
body. Her father had lots of it-and her mother a little,
too. Old Nate raised coal digging to the level of a fine art.
And if he had been yanked out of the mine some Monday
morning and put in the Governor's chair up at Columbus,
he would have had everything all sized up before the end of
the week. Your other grandfather had it, too. He ought
to have been a general. He always got high enough above
things to see them.
"Yes, sir! Nine hundred and ninety-nine humans out of
every thousand are beginning to degenerate before they're
fifteen years old; and by the time they're thirty they'recoasting with a smooth track ahead for the bottom of the
hill. So when you judge by what people have to get along
with in the first place, and by what goes on inside their heads
all the time, your family have done pretty well.
"Look at those fields of your dad's. Everything about
here overgrown with sedge-grass and briars and locust
thickets right up to his fences. Yet see these. Fences up,
weeds down, trees trimmed, no matter what time of the year
you happen along. I ride by once in a while just to have
my faith restored after I've been talking with some lousy
buzzard like old Pud."
Up by the house Doc sat in the buggy for a time and
talked on. The roans pranced in impatience.
"Don't know but what I'd try one of these automobiles if
the roads around here were a little smoother. But I guess
these will have to do for the present."
He gave them an easy rein, and they were away. Giles
walked into the house. His mother was not there. He
walked into the front yard. She was not there either. But
he noticed the spring work she had been doing on her flower
beds. She was going to have plenty of flowers. But she
ought to have something higher there on each side of the
entrance something with flowers or vines falling over it to
give a moundlike impression, but with a little something
showing through.
With Dr. Seabright's recharging of his mind only a few
minutes away, and with a definite problem before him, he
was at once in a state of fervor. He spent the rest of the
day in visualizing the entire yard as it would look after
everything was growing-the color of the beds, the outline
of the lilacs and tree-tops, the walk from the gate up to the
house. Of course there ought to be some vases or jar-
dinières or something. Now that he thought of it, he seemed
to remember having seen some somewhere. At supper he
was in a fair rage. He could talk of nothing else. Andsince his father was interested in anything that had to do
with pottery, he listened amiably.
Ten days later his father asked him to come down and
help draw kiln. Giles liked anything else better. He cared
not at all for either the sweltering warmth of the inside of
the kiln or for the hurried trips to the ware-yard with both
hands full of warm ware. But he agreed to help.
They took the ware just as it came. When only a small
section remained standing, Luke lifted an inverted twenty-
gallon jar from its plate at the bottom of a stand and re-
vealed a jardinière mottled in deep olive and cream. He put
the inverted jar down, lifted the jardinière carefully, turned
it in his hands and felt the smooth glaze, set it on the jar,
and stood back and looked at it, not without pride.
Giles returned from a trip to the ware-yard.
"What do you think of it?" Luke asked without daring
to look up.
"Why-why-all right! Yes, there's nothing the matter
with that."
He moved round because the light on the smooth glazed
surface blurred the colors.
"You bet it's all right. Did you make a pair of them?"
"Yes, I made a pair, and an extra one. I thought some-
thing might happen to one of them in burning."
They drew the other two. The flames had got in next to
one of them and left little color in the glaze on one side.
But the other one was a match for the first.
When they were through with the kiln, they carried the
two pieces up to the house. After supper they had a full
family council to decide just where they ought to stand,
what kind of growth they ought to contain, and whether
Luke should make additional ones. Soon these meetings
developed a new freedom of speech. All three members of
the family talked as if there were nothing to conceal-
nothing that they wanted to conceal. They forgot that Gileshad suddenly acquired a wide reputation for having almost
killed a man in a fight over Gustie Malloy. They forgot
that their entire future was in very unstable expectancy. It
was good just to put themselves with all the heart they had
into a single enterprise that promised immediate returns.
It was good to go to bed at night and feel that they had
surrounded themselves with the tiniest barricade of some-
thing in color, in rhythm, in balance.
Giles began to make trips to the pot-shop to work with
his father. But down there they did not work well together.
Giles thought more swiftly, his imagination was less blunted
by habit, and his youthful nerves wanted to carry his quicker
decisions into immediate execution. Luke was thinking of
Giles, moreover, as belonging in a magnificent world far
away, and Giles was beginning to find everything about him
interesting enough to claim a lifetime of devotion.
One afternoon while they were engaged in the tedious
process of setting kiln and the silence had grown so long
that anything was apropos, Luke said without preliminaries:
"You must get away from here, though I don't see how.
And the sooner the better. If you get tangled up in any
way around here, you'll be here for life. I thought I could
stay here and develop something, but you see how much it
ever amounted to."
So strangely intimate did this bit of counsel seem for both
the son and the father that they remained warm and nervous
and speechless for a long time. When the last stand of
ware was finally in place, Giles unceremoniously hurried
away and left his father the entire task of mixing the mor-
tar and walling up the kiln door.
Spring merged into full summer. Giles dreamed on until
his accumulation of dreams had become enormous, and heard
receding echoes of his reputation as a young tough who
fought over girls who were not worth fighting over, and
wondered why somebody did not give him a chance to buildsomething. If only he could earn some money-enough for
some clothes, some car fare! If only-yes.
One forenoon he decided to plan a new drive from the
back yard round to the drive that led out to the road. He
was wondering how long it would take to pound up enough
flintstones to surface the entire stretch.
"I've been looking at your house," said a ringing voice
behind him. He whirled round. "And I wonder if I could
get you to build me a new buggy shed."
It was Miss Genevieve Crosby with her new dog at her
heels-the latest dog in a long succession.
A buggy shed! Well, that was not the kind of building
Giles had dreamed about, but here was Genevieve with a
definite proposal, and he was listening to all that she had
to say.
"And I've heard so much about the house you built that I
thought you'd be able to do what I want done."
Giles asked her about such details as came to mind.
"Well, I see you can build it all right," she said, break-
ing into the middle of one of his questions. "But you'll
have to come over and get the idea of it, and make out a
list of the things I'm to order. I want it done right away.
I don't want to have to wait till Thanksgiving Day to see it
roofed."
Giles was sure nothing he was under obligation to do
would prevent him from coming over the next morning to
make out the bill of needs.
"But why not this afternoon? I'm in the notion now, and
I don't want to wait a minute longer than I have to."
Yes, probably; yes, of course. If she were in a hurry, he
could go yet that afternoon.
She was relieved to know that everything was arranged
for, and began to cast glances over the smooth lawn and the
luxuriant flowers.
"Your mother look after all this?""No, I do part of it, I guess."
"You do? Why, this looks like some of the. Why, I
didn't suppose there was anybody left west of the moun-
tains that knew how to take care of grounds."
She stopped abruptly and looked.
"Where did you find nice crockery like that?"
"Some of my father's make."
"Down here?"
"Yes."
"Well, I'll be damned! I didn't know he made anything
but fruit jars and milk crocks."
She examined the jardinières with great care, as if she
could not quite believe what she saw.
"Do you suppose he'd make some for me if I'd pay him
enough?"
"I'm sure he would."
"I'll have to ask him when I go down past the shop."
And she was off without further words than, "Now, I'll
be ready for you at two o'clock."
Giles arrived ahead of time, but already the buggy shed
had more or less passed out of her mind.
"See here!" she said. "What do you suppose you could
make out of my big yard-anything better than as it is?"
What was this she was asking? Did good fortune like
this happen along so naturally except in carefully planned
story books? Could he make anything out of her big yard,
did she say?
He tried to conceal his amazement, his confusion, by walk-
ing over the grounds. But soon his interest in the project
drove his confusion entirely away so that he did not have
to conceal it. He studied the even but bare-looking lawn,
the two evergreen trees down by the gate, the definite slope,
the lane out front.
Yes, Giles thought he could make something of it. He
would need some high plants, like lilacs and roses andsyringa bushes. And some trees on the slope-yes; perhaps
some maples. "The two evergreens there by themselves
make it look like"
Giles thought of what Sockey had said when the two of
them came over to make their observations.
"Like what?"
Her face bore the trace of a smile, but it was intent also,
and Giles hesitated to complete the sentence.
"Like a graveyard," at last he said.
"Like a graveyard? Does it? Well, if it does, it'll have
to be changed, for I'll be damned if I want to occupy a
graveyard yet awhile."
Her confidence in Giles was at once great. "But there's
not much that could be done before planting time this fall,
is there? Or next spring?"
"No, not much."
"All right, then. You make out your list for the buggy
shed and when the lumber's here, come over and build it.
Maybe I'll have you build a better fence to take the place
of the old one there along the upper side of the deer park,
too. I'll pay you for the building when you're through.
Then when you finish up the yard next spring, I'll pay you
for that. Is that a bargain?"
And it was.A
CHAPTER VIII
T LAST to build again! It was not like building a
house, to be sure, but it was building. To close
one's eyes and see how a thing was going to look
when all put together; then to open one's eyes and enjoy
the feel of the saw and hammer and chisel in one's hands
as one made parts piece by piece; and then to watch them
slip into place until the magical relation was established and
made them into something different from the mere sum of
themselves! Where could anybody go and find experience
transcending that?
The sight of the compact, temple-like shed when it was
virtually completed filled Giles with regret. He was ready
to put it out of his thoughts, but he wanted other building
to do. A foreign and selfish idea flashed through his mind:
What if lightning should strike Genevieve's house or one of
her barns? Then he would have plenty of building to do!
Or would he? What did he know about the big timbers in
a barn? Oh, well, the lightning had not yet struck. And
he still had that big front yard to do when the time came.
How much money did builders earn? The morning he
was ready to turn the shed over to Genevieve, he asked his
father that question. His father could not answer at least
very definitely. He knew what stoneware sold for. Giles
pondered the question all the way down through the woods
and up the hill past Genevieve's deer park. By the time
he had worked for an hour reducing the track of the sliding
doors to a greater smoothness, and had oiled the stiffness
out of the latch on the narrow side door, he thought he had
arrived at a figure.When he reported to Genevieve that everything was now
all in readiness, she once more stood off from the shed and
looked at its well-proportioned exterior, and then walked
through it admiringly.
"Well, the next thing is to know whether you're going to
charge me more than I'll be able to pay." There was a ro-
bust twinkle in her dark eyes.
Giles hesitated, and then was suddenly overwhelmed with
the fear that his figure might in fact be too high. He ended
by saying, "Oh, I hardly know what would be right."
"Well, I never quarrel much over good work. And I'll
be damned if I'll have anybody around if his work is poor."
She reflected. "I suppose you've not worked a great deal
for other people, have you?"
It was a hurtful admission to make, but Giles had to make
it. "No, not a great deal."
"Well, then, how would this do?"
She slipped a roll of bills from the pocket of her gray-
black dress, counted off a few shavings from the roll, handed
these to him and requested him to count them.
Yes, Giles thought that amount would do very well, since
it was almost twice as much as he had thought of charging.
But his conscience caught him.
"You're sure you're not giving me too much, Miss
Crosby?"
"Oh, go 'long! No! I'm satisfied if you are. The shed's
got the right look. I didn't know there was anybody left
who could make one."
Giles thanked her and moved as if to go.
"And now you'll be thinking about the yard, will you?
Your father has agreed to make me some crockery as nice
as your own-maybe nicer, I told him, for he's going to get
good money for mine. I may want some other things, too.
Do you suppose he could make me a couple of big bluevases-two feet tall or more to put in my front hallway?
I like such things."
No, the buggy shed would not end his building for Gene-
vieve. That big front yard, by the time he got through
with it, would be the show place of the county. More than
ever, people would be puzzled about Genevieve. But that
mattered little; they were puzzled greatly as it was.
The dream took such firm hold of him that he believed he
could live till spring with nothing else to think about. But
as summer drifted along into late August, imperious youth
cried aloud for less abeyance and for days that did not re-
peat themselves. The life he lived became colorless. He
wanted it brightened up. He wanted to be lost in mystic
sublimity. He wanted to stand on the Lookout, maybe; he
wanted to see the towering white-oaks and poplars; he
wanted to come back through the maple grove; he wanted
to meet Beulah, maybe, but without feeling too much in
his place which evidently was a little below hers. Per-
haps not even the wisest of mortals could have told him just
how the constituents of his longing blended-or what they
were, even. But it was a longing that persisted in making
itself known.
Many visits to the Lookout had made it easy for him to
throw off the colorlessness of life when he climbed to the
summit in the dawn. He swiftly lost himself in contempla-
tion, and then when the sun was well up, but not too warm,
he lay on the steep hillside and felt himself glowing with all
sorts of patterns of things which as yet he could discern
only as highly colored feeling.
All through the late summer he made these pilgrimages-
and met no one. Once, after he had done most of the fall
planting on Genevieve's grounds, he went one morning and
found a sharp crispness in the air on the hilltop. In it there
was exhilaration, but also a threat, a mighty guarantee that
though a moving spectacle was here to-day, something morecruel would be here to-morrow. Birds lingered and made a
medley of calls, but their voices were subdued.
The winter was longer than he had feared. He took the
precaution of subscribing for a magazine that published pic-
tures of gardens, and he scrutinized every page of every
number at least a hundred times. But he wearied a little
even of this. So he made solitary trips to Culbertson and
said "Hello" to everybody he saw. He attended the re-
vival meetings and listened and watched undismayed. He
tramped to Beechwood Chapel when that community con-
ducted its well-mannered revival, and saw the Lettinger
family still holding the fort. But Beulah was a woman now.
Her dresses were as long as anybody else's, and she sang as
if there were nothing new about the experience. The
preacher must like that song, "Salvation is Free." He was
forever announcing it, Giles thought.
Yes, Beulah had grown up. Yet once, when Giles looked
in her direction, she looked back with the not unpleased ex-
pression of a girl who still has some great enrapturing ad-
venture ahead. Still she had grown up. When the service
was over she took the arm of a well-turned man of twenty-
five who wore a trim mustache and parted his hair very
smoothly from the middle, and they went out together as
though doing so were neither new nor romantic.
March came, and shrieked and wailed slowly along in a
succession of snow squalls and chilly but bright days. Then
warmer days came, and the buds were soon growing large
and bursting into fuzzy leaflets, and wild flowers bloomed
in every sunny thicket and woods-pasture. Did the sun do
it all itself unaided as it rose higher and higher in the south-
ern heavens, or was there some great power in the earth as
well that pushed life out everywhere into the sustaining
warmth?
Giles felt languid in the brighter sun. Would spring bring
back life to him-the glow within and without that madehim know that he was competent for any emergency? No
longer did much of the sacred aureole seem to surround the
house he had built, or the front yard, bleak in the spring,
or the family hills that stood clean among so many that
had been defaced and overgrown. These ugly, hideous hills!
They seemed to be pressing in closer all the time upon his
own, and upon him-closer, closer, closer!
One night he awoke in the middle of his frightening
dream about himself. There he was, a man of his father's
age! And instead of being less hemmed in than his father,
he was more so. He was standing outside the Culbertson
church on a bright Sunday morning vainly trying to look
alert and penetrating after a week, a month, a year, a decade
or two or three, of labor that laughed at alertness and
frowned upon penetration. The sublime vitality about
which Dr. Seabright liked to discourse, had been ground
slowly, slowly out of him until it was all gone except a glint
of brightness that came into his eyes when something un-
usual struggled through his mind.
He could see even farther than that. He could see as
far ahead as he was to go. And he saw only a straight,
rutty road to the bottom of the hill. Yes, there were some
excitements in prospect. After a week of silence in which
he struggled hard with hands that refused any longer to
straighten out after the work was done, and pondered much
over Gustie's problem of the whenceness and whyness of
things, he would go down on Sunday and listen to the un-
steady, overbalanced singing of the remnant of a church
choir. Every four years he would go down and hear some
cheap campaign orator-the kind committees assigned to
small towns-give insufficient reasons why the people should
support one presidential candidate rather than another.
Three or four times a year, after he had come in from work
and washed his face in the washpan on an upturned box on
the back porch and had wiped his face on a coarse toweland combed his hair before the kitchen mirror, he would
listen to some energetic peddler of patent-medicines, or of
the Life of the latest popular hero, discourse authoritatively,
while he ate supper, on the superiority of American political
practices. Once a year, after winter had long palled on him,
he would go down and watch the minister conduct Decision
Day, when it was growing harder all the while to frighten
sinners to repentance. Of course, there would be minor
experiences-thousands of inconsequences in the empty
grind of uneventful years. But these few would be the great
occasions, the high points in life to be talked about and to
be used in dating all lesser events.
Very quietly he crept from his bed, slipped into his clothes
except his shoes, and went noiselessly out of the house. He
sat on the edge of the portico in the bright moonlight and
put on his shoes. Then he tiptoed to the gate, closed it care-
fully so that it would not slam, hurried off to the road, and
turned westward. One of his aunts, not unwilling to have
him feel that his mother's family tree was a sturdier one
than his father's, had recently given him a dramatic ac-
count of how his father's mother had stolen away and
drowned herself. And as he sought to get away without
awaking anybody, he was thinking all the time of how
shrewdly she must have managed things in order to escape
notice. One can become shrewd when one is trying to get
the upper hand of something.
The moonlight was too brilliant: it revealed all the dry
bones of the abandoned Company Row in the hollow beyond
Wiggam's Glory. No, it was not too brilliant: it revealed
also the silent bulk of St. Bernard's, and the more silent
churchyard; the network of shadows over the road down
the long hill and over the roof of the covered bridge; then
more palely, the road winding up the hillside; then more
palely still, the meadow on top of the hill.
Was he going to suffer disappointment at last? Was thedawn now going to be impotent in saving him from the
boniness of his surroundings? No, it was not.
Two hours later he walked down through the woods and
the maple grove toward the bridge. He came upon Beulah
Lettinger gathering jack-in-the-pulpits. She was the first to
speak-she always was.
"Why, there's nothing to kill this time of year."
"No. I just happened over this way."
She thought it strange that he should be there, and cast
a swift inquiring glance into his face. But his face was
older than it once had been, and did not reveal its true in-
tent so readily. It was better not to put her inquiry into
definite words at least not just yet.
"See my flowers?"
She lifted a great bouquet of them for him to see. She
had a half-dozen kinds.
"But I can't find enough of these jack-in-the-pulpits."
"Why, there are dozens of them right there under those
beeches."
"Where?"
"Right up there."
"Show them to me."
They both flushed, for both knew that she could not fail
to find them herself, yet they hesitated not to go together
in the direction Giles indicated.
Beulah did not let a silence accumulate. "I'll make you
pick a few."
"All right. I know how."
"You do?"
They both smiled as if what they had said was very wise.
Giles found three or four. "Here," and he brought them
to her.
As she took them to crowd them in with the others, their
hands touched tremblingly. It was nothing. It had hap-
pened countless millions of times before in the history ofthe world. Yet they felt it sweep a great restraint from
their speech, and they saw it bring a sudden great bright-
ness to the earth round about them.
Stumbling in excitement, Giles found other flowers. When
she took them, and he felt her hand against his, he pressed
it ever so gently, and she pressed his in return. But they
did not look into each other's eyes.
She had as much of a handful as she could carry. Why
gather more? They were by the road, and climbed over
into it and walked down to the sagging gate. She stepped
back through into the field, but turned to talk. Disjointed
words had not left her quite content.
"You haven't told me why you come over this way."
"No."
"Well, then, why don't you?"
Her manner was inquisitive, but it was also intimate.
Giles hesitated to tell her, yet he liked to have her keep
on asking.
"Yes. Please."
"Oh, I don't know that I could."
"You must have a girl over here."
Giles thought she was trying to take the upper hand
again.
"No, not that I know of."
She gave a cheerful little fillip of a laugh. She was glad
-at least just now-to hear his denial.
"Then you oughtn't to be afraid to tell me what it is."
"Oh, I'm not afraid to tell you."
"Then why don't you?"
"I'm afraid you won't-won't-"
"Won't what?"
"Won't just understand."
"Well, now that's nice or else not. Do you think I'm
about two years old?"
Giles admitted that he had not said exactly what he hadin mind. Still he hesitated to speak further. He looked at
the ground not far from where he stood, and smiled doubt-
fully.
"Come on. Please."
She was sure he was fascinated by her. She would be
the experienced one.
"Come on."
She slipped her free hand along on the edge of the gate
until it touched one of his. But swiftly the experienced
one was aflame herself. She pressed his hand closely and
warmly because she liked the experience, and laughed little
laughs of intimacy, and gazed up into his face with the
intentness of a witch, and begged him to tell.
"You'll not think I'm crazy?"
"Of course not. Why should I?"
She was falling into a pleasantly concurring attitude.
"Why," he began, and then stopped.
"I'm waiting."
She laughed a happy, cozy, bubbling laugh. He smiled
quietly.
"Why, I just like to go up to the Lookout to enjoy the
sunrise."
In her present state, she saw an early morning pilgrim-
age for such a purpose as not only reasonable, but glorious.
For a moment the dreamer in her was dominant.
"It must be wonderful!"
"Yes."
"You can see over the top of everything, can't you?"
"Yes-the hills and river and fields and houses, all the
way to the sun."
Yes, it must be wonderful. Everything seemed wonderful
now.
"I'd like to see it myself."
"Why don't you? You live right here by it."
"I know. But-""But what?"
"But I'm not sure I like to get up quite so early-"
Another thought chased that one out of her mind. This
new one was not to be mentioned in full voice there in the
sunlight. It had to be whispered. And by the time she was
ready to whisper it, Giles already knew what it was going
to be. So when she said in the softest breath to be heard,
"You take me?" he promptly whispered back, "Yes."
His answer was scarcely audible to himself, yet they
heard each other as if their words had been shouted from
the clouds.
Neither dared look up.
"When?" she whispered as though the slightest detail of
such an undertaking required secrecy even in the contem-
plation of it.
"Any time. The sooner the better. Bright moonlight yet
in the morning now."
She reflected. Her face was swept clean of everything
but eagerness.
"To-morrow?" Giles asked.
"No, not to-morrow. Company's coming and will be
here till Sunday."
"Then how about Monday?"
"All right."
"Here?"
"Yes, here. Then we'll go up."
"Let's say four o'clock. That will give us enough time."
"All right."
"You'll be sure to come?"
"Of course that's if I can get out of the house."
"But you must."
"I'll try. I think I can."
She glanced round. She was startled to be reminded that
they were not immersed in any lovers' silvery darkness, but
in the full light of mid-morning."I'd better be getting back, then. They wonder enough,
as it is, what I do out here."
It did not occur to them that the present moment offered
all the possibilities of a rendezvous. Their minds were full
of the adventure that was to come.
As she moved to go, she let her pulsing hand touch his
again, but without any lingering hesitance rushed away.
Nor did she look back before Giles entered the echoing
covered bridge.
His legs walked of themselves. His eyes saw nothing be-
fore them. His ears roared with the pleasant pounding of
his pulses. How was this? He had wondered if life were
ever going to return to him again so that he could go on
with Genevieve's building. And now just where was he
headed for? At the top of the ridge he glanced about as
if he could tell by looking with his eyes. Of course, there
was the Lookout back there the Lookout that helped him
to see clearly. Yes, the Lookout-that's where they were
going to see the sunrise together. They were going. Then
his mind ceased to keep possession of itself, to make dis-
tinctions between things that were definite in outline and
things that were confused.
This was Friday. That meant that it would be a long
time till Sunday night, till Monday morning. There was
the weather, too; the weather had to be considered. If it
should be raining, why, of course she would not be there.
But if it should be just a little uncertain, just a little cloudy
with the moon covered up part of the time, would she know
that she should come then; that such broken clouds some-
times made the most splendorous sunrises of all? Why did
they not think of all those possibilities while they were to-
gether?
That afternoon, the next morning, the next afternoon, he
tried to make himself think about the planting to be done
for Genevieve the next week. But trees had lost their powerto attract him, lilacs their rich sweetness, roses their color.
On Sunday night he thought he would go to bed early.
He saw his father and mother moving about, and they
both tried to talk with him in the easy quiet after darkness.
But they had lost all reality for him. He even wondered if
it were not some lack of power in themselves that had kept
them in Wiggam's Glory. When they were young, they had
never had such an adventure as he was going to have now
in a few hours. He did not want their unreal selves in the
way this evening. Yes, he would go to bed. Time passed
swiftly when one slept it through.
But after he had coaxed himself into a restless doze, he
was startled into wakefulness by dreaming that he had over-
slept. What if he should oversleep? He had always been
able to awaken when he wanted to. But in previous in-
stances he had had only himself to consider. If he had gone
a little late, no one else would have experienced any incon-
venience.
After all, it might be better not to go to sleep. But how
would he put in the time? What could he think about?
That was just the trouble he could think about nothing in
particular. His mind leaped swiftly, lightly, from one sub-
ject to another without lingering, without steady flight. His
heart alone was unvarying. It pounded away there inside
himself with regularity, without respite, in warm throbs
which filled him with such a sense of life that he was sure
life would never cease. Other people might sicken and die,
but he didn't feel as if he ever would. He could meet any-
thing-and overcome it.
But just lying there with his mind flitting was not the
way to put in an entire night. It probably was not more
than one o'clock now, and he was not to start until three.
He would read. He would hold his mind firmly on some-
thing.
He lighted a lamp and found a book that he had beenreading that afternoon. When he tiptoed back to the bed
with it, he put the lamp on the big chest that he kept near
his bed as he did in the old house. His silverine watch
ticked away as steadily as his heart. What time was it? It
was ten minutes to eleven.
His mind would not stay on a page any better than on a
thought of his own making. Wasn't the story going to be-
come more enthralling? He would look ahead and see. It
did not promise to do so.
Finally he closed the book with a finger at the place, and
dropped back on the pillow in disgust at himself.
He must have gone to sleep. He felt as if he had. His
finger was no longer at the place in the book. In truth, he
no longer held the book in his hand. But the lamp was
burning still. Its flame, though, was yellow and smoky.
