He reached over to the chest and held his watch up in the
pale light. It was three-thirty.
In a breath or two he was out of bed and dressed. In a
few more he was out of the house. As soon as he was a
little out of hearing, he ran, kicking flintstones noisily in
the process.
How good it was to run, after so long a period of antici-
pation! What a relief to extend oneself, and take the neces-
sary full breaths of the cool night air!
But where was he running to? And why was he running?
No explanation seemed quite complete. He was not certain
whether he was directing the running, or whether something
was sweeping him along and he was just letting himself be
swept. Maybe the distinction was an immaterial one, after
all; it was a difficult one to make.
At quarter past four he came down the hill toward the
bridge. Had she come? Had she come and grown angry
because he was not there? Or had she come and gone
back?
An alien suggestion slipped into his mind. What if shehad decided deliberately, of her own free will, not to come
at all? Maybe that sleek fellow, who had looked as if he
owned a township, had come on Sunday night, and she had
decided to abandon the early morning adventure.
He tried not to rattle the cross-planks of the hollow
bridge as he hurried through. The moon shone brightly on
a gate that was empty.
His heart, warm enough from the exercise and now
clutched by disappointment, pounded as if it could beat
only a few times longer. All the while trying to equalize
his breaths, he walked close to the gate, as if he could not
already see well enough that no one was there.
A figure bounded from beneath a huge maple and came
up breathlessly.
"I was late," she said, in an impetuous undertone.
"So was I."
Some deterrent belonging in youth that grows up in iso-
lation kept them from any sudden passionate greeting. But
she slipped her arm snugly through his and they started up
the long hill.
"I'm afraid we'll not get up there quite in time," Giles
said when at last the turbulence of the preceding hour al-
lowed him regular breathing.
"Did you think I was never coming?" she asked in reply.
"Oh, I just walked through the bridge when I saw you.
I overslept."
"So did I."
"Then I ran all the way-pretty nearly."
"I did all the way from the barnyard down here."
They pressed arms in appreciation of this confessed
eagerness.
"Lee Turner wanted to come over last night, but I told
him I wasn't going to be at home."
There was another appreciative pressing of arms.The hill grew steeper, and the road grew brighter in the
coming dawn.
"I'm afraid we've missed part of it," Giles suggested, au-
thoritatively, as though missing a little of it were something
of tremendous importance.
"There'll be enough."
When they reached the lower edge of the meadow, there
were streaks of light in the gray east.
"We've missed part of it," Giles said again.
"But it isn't light yet-very."
They climbed the steep shoulder of the hill without look-
ing back. When they were at the cherry tree, he turned
and half whispered the one word, "Now."
She stood close at his side, her hand lightly through his
arm. She caught from his attitude the necessity of waiting,
and stood silent. There were clouds in the east, and that
meant color.
Soon there was a deep glow. Soon flashes of brightness
shot up into the sky behind and above the clouds. The
clouds were thin, and when the sun came slowly into view
the sky was ablaze with splendor, and every valley was
filled with a golden blue mist. Not even the presence of
Beulah quite kept Giles from being somewhat lost in the
great presence.
"And to think that this happens every day," said Beulah.
"It's just too wonderful for words."
She gave his arm a firm pull and thereby brought a new
element into the experience for Giles.
"How glorious! Oh, look! Let's dance-or run-or
shout. Come on."
With arms lifted she leaped into a dance. "Come on!"
And she swept him into her cadence.
They whirled and danced, with heads thrown back to af-
ford balancing resistance, until they were giddy and breath-
less. For a moment they panted, and then like a nymphand a satyr, they were off again. They danced and skipped
and whirled to every part of the hilltop.
They had to catch their breath again. As soon as Beulah
could speak, she said: "Oh, let's see what it's like over here
-way over." And they were off, arms round waists, to the
edge of the rocky declivity on the north. "And now over
there!" And they were off to the woodland on the south.
A little less impetuously they swung back to the magic
tree and sat on the block of sandstone and drank in the full
effect of the bright sun. Once more, as they looked in si-
lence, Giles felt the slightest approach toward his accus-
tomed mystical regeneration. But Beulah brought him
back.
"Goodness, I'd better be getting down home. They think
I'm crazy enough as it is."
They stood up, and as her hand brushed his, she gave
him a firm push. Neither spoke a word, but they kissed
each other rapturously, and then with arms encircling waists
walked over toward the woodland so that she might take
the nearest path home.
At the fence they sat on a seat made by the sagging of
two rails, and tried to prolong the exact rapture they had
enjoyed on the hilltop. But their kisses were hot.
"It was wonderful!" Beulah said, and pressed her face
closer against his shoulder. "Just wonderful."
"Yes."
It was Giles Dabney who said yes-Giles Dabney, son of
a coal miner, dreamer of sublime dreams, now sitting in a
secluded grove with this elegant person-old Wesley Let-
tinger's youngest daughter-close in his arms and enjoying
the experience, without a doubt.
Abruptly she sat up. Then she jumped to the ground.
"I thought I heard somebody whistle."
Giles stood beside her and helped her to listen. Seconds
passed, but they brought no sound save that of a crow'swings fanning the air as he swept out of the woods close
over their heads. They looked into each other's eyes, and
smiled in relief at hearing nothing. Then suddenly as if
caught up in a whirlwind, they threw themselves into each
other's arms and lost themselves in the most passionate of
kissings.
Somebody did whistle-just round the brow of the hill.
"Quick!" Beulah commanded in low tones. "It's Myra.
Here, jack-in-the-pulpits a lot of them."
Desperately they both gathered.
"Here!" she called again. "That's enough. Now get be-
hind that big poplar till I get ahead of her and bait her
down the other way."
In the moment of silence, they heard the whistle again,
but it was no nearer.
Quickly she gave him a parting kiss to dash away the
matter-of-factness that had for the moment been forced
upon them. "You must come to the house the next time.
But you must wait till they forget this. A month, at least.
Some Saturday night. Let me know."
Giles looked pained at the prospect of such a prolonged
interim. But she comforted him. "This won't be the end."
He watched past the edge of the big tree and saw her
come out into the maples below the strip of forest trees, and
then heard her calling to Myra and saying that here she was
down here; that the jack-in-the-pulpits were now just too
lovely for words.
The voices became indistinct as Beulah went farther to-
ward the valley road and farther round the hill. Then Giles
hustled away from his hiding and was soon out in the road
and going down the spiral toward the bridge. He heard a
rumble of wheels over the planks, and saw a team of roans
emerge. They swung off into the valley road that led up
past Wesley Lettinger's house. Dr. Seabright, moved by
some emissary of fate that whispered in his right ear, turnedhis head and saw Giles across the corner of the maple grove
by the farm gate. He lifted his buggy whip in salutation
as the roans sped on, and Giles responded with a lax lifting
of an arm.A
CHAPTER IX
T HOME everything seemed immersed in a strange
unreality, obscured in pathetic inconsequence.
What were ordinary hills and ordinary flower beds
and an ordinary house compared with his great experience?
When his mother said and she spoke sympathetically
enough-"I thought you were going to work at Genevieve's
to-day," her voice seemed thin and unresonant as if it came
through a fog from the other side of the world. And when
that afternoon he did go to Genevieve's, the pot-shop down
in the hollow never seemed so microscopic and dingy, the
road never so void of significance, the great oaks of the
woods never so dwarfed. What did all of these petty
things amount to?
When he approached Genevieve's hill, the unreality was
there also. Something had happened to this hill since
last Thursday. No magnetic energy was abroad to give or-
derliness to the activities about house and barn, or vitality
to whatever grew or roamed in the fields.
He went up to the side door and knocked. For a mo-
ment the unreality was increased. What was this they were
telling him? Genevieve would not be able to come out and
give him further directions? Something had happened to
Genevieve? The enveloping confusion that made the at-
mosphere low in visibility, and lower still in vitality, was
swept completely away.
Yes, something had happened to Genevieve. At nine
o'clock or after the night before, one of the hired men rode
swiftly away on horseback, came up at last to Dr. Sea-
bright's house in Cloverdale, and told him that Genevievewas in a critical state. Dr. Seabright did not permit the
man's great anxiety to hurry him. Men had been riding up
to his house for nearly a half-century and telling him that
he must hurry, that life was ebbing away. So what differ-
ence did one more anxious rider of this kind make? Still
he was a little surprised to learn that Genevieve could so
quickly fall into the class of the critically afflicted, and it
may be that he did harness his team with the least bit more
promptitude than was his wont.
Genevieve, in making her evening rounds to see that
everything was ready for the night, had stopped at the
east barn and noticed that her prize Holstein bull was loose
from his stanchion. She undertook to fasten him. He was
not ugly in temper, but spring was in his blood. While
she stood trying to fasten him, he heard some interesting
noise outside the barn and in his exuberance swung his
head to one side irresistibly. Genevieve caught the full
force of the swing, and crumpled down over a thigh that
was beyond use. A belated hired man passing from the
upper barn heard her piercing calls for help, dragged her
as best he could out of reach of the bull's head and feet, ran
down to the main road a quarter of a mile away and brought
another hired man who lived there, and together they car-
ried her to the house.
Dr. Seabright heard this compact preliminary report and
was impressed by it. But by the time he arrived at Gene-
vieve's house toward midnight, he had regained all his
calm. He threw blankets over his team and buckled them
so that the cool spring night would have no opportunity to
stiffen muscles, lifted out his medicine-case from its corner,
and tiredly walked up to the house.
In the hallway he set his medicine-case on a chair, took
off his hat and coat and hung them on a hall-tree, picked up
his case and strode toward the room pointed out. The hang-ing lamp and the bright spring fire in the fireplace revealed
Genevieve on a long couch by the south window.
Dr. Seabright was ready for a wild outburst. But it did
not come.
He walked over to the couch and still she did not speak.
"Well!" he said.
She was sobbing softly like a little child that is hurt
grievously.
"Oh, Doc," she begged; "I've broken my hip, I've broken
my hip, and I'll never walk again. My grandmother broke
hers, and she never walked another step with it as long as
she lived."
She wept pitifully.
"Oh, can't you save me, Doc? Can't you make me walk
again? You must do something! I know you can. Can't
you? Can't you?"
"We'll have to see; yes, we'll see," he said in a kindly
professional voice. "Suppose you-" and he directed one
of the men and the wife of the one that lived down by the
main road to move her very carefully-so that she would
be thus and so on the couch.
It was not possible to complete the examination without
causing her pain. She flinched, tightened her lips, and ut-
tered subdued "Oh, God's." But through it she begged
only that he make her able to walk again.
"I can go through anything if only you'll make me walk.
And I'll die honest to God, I'll die, Doc, in a month-if
I've got to be helpless the rest of my life with nothing to
do but think."
Dr. Seabright believed he was discerning the character
of the fracture. He turned to one of the hired men.
"I've
got some splints and some bandages in that big case down
in the rig. Suppose you bring them up. I've got to have
something stronger, too. Couldn't you find me some"
and he went into full detail."You might heat me some water, too," he said to the
woman and her husband; "and find me some big thick
towels."
"Oh, I know it's bad!" Genevieve began as soon as they
had gone. "But you must save me, Doc. Why, what could
I do if I couldn't walk over the fields and watch things
grow? Oh, I can't think of being shut in. I wouldn't be
shut in for-"
She stopped without completing the sentence. "Doc, you
can make me walk if you come often enough. You must
come every day. Why, if you come every day and look
after me, you can make the hip work whether it wants to
or not."
Dr. Seabright labored on, quietly, intently. He wanted
to be doubly sure.
"I'll pay you," she implored him. "I've got plenty of
money. You needn't worry about that. I'll not give you
any measly twenty-five or fifty dollars. I'll give you a
hundred, two hundred, or whatever it's worth."
Dr. Seabright was not accustomed to stupendous sums
for reducing fractures. He thought she must be growing
wild with the pain.
"Listen, Doc! I've got to walk. You must not let me
become a helpless old woman. Oh, my God in heaven!
Think of that! Somebody to help me up from my bed in
the morning and to the rocking chair, and somebody to help
me back again at night. Or maybe not be able to get up
at all. Listen, Doc! You're not listening to a word I say!"
"Yes, I am. I'm listening. But I'm trying also to figure
out just how I'm going to do this."
"Well, listen to this, then. You'll come every day till
I'm able to walk, won't you?"
"Oh, that would hardly be necessary."
"But I want you to. I want you to be here every after-
noon to tell me how I'm getting along. Every afternoon!""But somebody else might have a broken leg some after-
noon."
"Well, afternoon or morning, or any time, just so you
come. You're not thinking of the pay, are you? I'll pay
you for it all. What does a little money mean to me? I've
made some, haven't I? I'll pay you I'll pay you five hun-
dred, if you think that would be enough, and if you have
to come longer, I'll pay you another five hundred. But you
must come every day."
Dr. Seabright now felt sure that the fracture was not in
the hip at all, but at least half way down between the hip
and knee, just where the bull's heavy head had struck her.
"If that's not enough, if you think you ought to have a
thousand to begin with-"
"Oh, God Almighty!" Doc exclaimed, at last in readiness
to talk. "I don't want all your money; I wouldn't know
what to do with it. And I shan't have to come over here
every day, either."
Now that Doc had broken into speech, she felt better able
to deal with him.
"But I want you to come. You must. You must do
something special in my case. And I want to pay you extra
for it. You wouldn't leave a lone woman to die, would you,
just for want of attention?"
Doc saw the need of calming her. "All right, then, I'll
agree to come every day till you're able to be up and about
again."
"And I'll pay you the five hundred dollars. You'll prob-
ably have to have more, though. I expect my bones won't
knit very fast-anybody who's past fifty-five."
"They'll probably knit faster than the average," Doc
said casually, trying all the while to visualize the placing
of the splints-"and then you'll be wasting your money."
At last he added: "The fracture is down where I can get
at it. I think you'll be able to walk again all right-justabout as well as ever. But let me tell you something:
Within the next day or two you'll think that leg is burning
off in a slow fire. So don't blame the doctor who's looking
out for you if you get to wishing some night that you didn't
have it."
Genevieve smiled through all her pain. "I told you,
didn't I, that I could stand anything, if I just knew I was
going to walk again."
"All right," he said; "you'll walk that is, if you do what
I tell you."
Dr. Seabright spent the rest of the night there. The
next morning before he started across the country to look in
on a new mother and a new babe, he ate a good breakfast
that the hired man's wife prepared for him and drank two
cups of her stout coffee. Genevieve was haggard, and her
streaked hair was tousled, but she looked cheerful none the
less.
"You'll come early to-morrow morning, won't you?" she
asked. "You won't neglect me, will you? You must see
that everything is all right."
"Yes, I'll be along-sooner or later. You'll probably
get so tired of seeing me that you'll pay me off and let
me go."
That was just the way she wanted him to feel about it.
But she knew she would never weary of his coming. She
wanted his precious words of assurance that she was going
to walk.
Giles did not venture into the house to see her. He got
all the different versions of the story, anyhow; and he did
not quite know what he could say if he should go in. But
a day or two later when she learned that he was at work
as usual, she sent for him. Now that there was nothing in
the fracture to offer special discouragement, she lost some
of her childlike humility.
"The damned bull broke my leg," she explained, as thoughit were necessary, when Giles stood by the bed-couch, his
cap in hand. "I'll not be out for four or five or maybe six
weeks. I refuse to stay in longer than six. That's long
enough. I may want you to do something once in a while
besides work on the yard. I think you'd better rebuild an-
other stretch of that fence above the deer park-out by
the two big oaks. You'll see where it's leaning over too
much. And there may be other things. But go right on
with the yard. Don't you slight that. I want the best look-
ing yard in the county."
After the first painful days, Genevieve rather enjoyed
being so much waited upon, so much talked to. Soon she
was having Giles come in every morning to discuss develop-
ments in the yard. Soon she was keeping Dr. Seabright
longer than the purely professional part of his visit re-
quired.
One day after Dr. Seabright had assured her for the tenth
time that her leg was well on the way to a firm union, she
chided him pleasantly for not hurrying things up.
He was in a pleasant mood himself, and turned on her.
"See here! If you don't approve of my methods, why
did you call me in in the first place? I could have had a
good night of sleep instead of driving clear across here in
the raw spring night. Why didn't you send for this doctor
over at Culbertson?"
She would yield no point to him now. She felt sure, her-
self, that her leg was on the way.
"Well, the truth is, he is a mighty good doctor-has a
diploma and all but I couldn't bear the thought of having
some young fellow of thirty-five pawing me over."
"So that's it, is it? You didn't think I could pull you
through any better than anybody else, but your maidenly
modesty led you to send for me because I'm an old man
who'd be perfectly safe. That's what I call a hell of a
compliment."She refused to be outdone. "I didn't say that. I don't
know as any man is safe as long as he's on top of the sod.
I just said maybe seventy-two or seventy-three or whatever
you are, is a little safer, a little more dignified, than thirty-
five."
"Now you're trying to patch things up. But I don't know
as I'd admit even that now. All that I know is that you've
offered to pay me so much money that I've decided I'm
damned exceptional in some way."
They both laughed. Couldn't they carry on, even if In-
dian summer had overtaken them? Couldn't they? They
looked at each other, the reminiscence of flush in their
faces, and laughed again.
"How do you want that money paid you?" she asked
eventually. "If you want it in cash, I'll have to send to
the bank at Centerville and get some."
"Yes, when the time comes, I'll want cash." He spoke
as if he had thought the matter over and settled it long ago.
"Yes, I'll want cash."
When April had showered itself out of existence and May
had bloomed itself almost to an end, Genevieve sat on her
front porch and looked at her new yard, her deer park, her
towering white-oaks, and breathed in the warm, sweet odor
of the first creamy white locust blossoms. How like a lotus
garden it all was! And she would soon be walking about
again. She would go out and see the little white pigs, happy
and clean yet, as they crept under fences and roamed every-
where. She would see the little calves, and the cows, and
her prize bull, damn him! She would go down and look at
the deer at closer range and see how the fawns were getting
on. She would tramp through the woods and look worship-
fully at her yellow poplars. She would walk about in the
evening-every evening-when all the men had gone, and
enjoy her peonies, and then her petunias-the mammoth
ones for which she had paid so much money-and then thewaxlike gladioluses, and the multi-colored phlox, and finally
the sturdy fall-roses and chrysanthemums. She would walk
about thus and enjoy her summers. And she would not let
any nervous bull break another leg!
It was not late yet this morning, and if Dr. Seabright
came as he promised, she would pay him his money and tell
him now to go, if he didn't want to come and see her any
longer.
Dr. Seabright came. He told her that if she didn't fall
down the stairs and break her neck, she was now good for
at least thirty more years.
Very well, then, here was his money. Here it was, just
as he wanted it, in cash.
Yes, the money.
"I'll take only half of it," he announced and startled her
to the brink of profanity.
"Only half of it?

Yes, you will; every damned cent of

"I didn't say anything about keeping it in your house.
I just said I didn't mean to take it myself. But I'm ready
to tell you what to do with it."
"Then what's that?"
"You're to give it to somebody."
"Who?"
"This young Giles who has been working for you."
"Young Dabney?"
"Yes, young Dabney. Where's the money?"
She handed a long envelope to him. "I've been sitting
here with it in my hand ever since they helped me out here."
Dr. Seabright slipped the money out and looked at it-
five one-hundred-dollar bills. "More than I've seen in one
lump for quite a spell."
"Then why don't you keep it?""Because I've no need for it just now. I'd give it all
to him, but my wife could use some of it, I guess. I've had
plenty of patients this spring, but they've not been af-
flicted with any epidemic of paying their doctor."
He fussed nervously with the five bills. "Here," he said,
at last, "I'll take just two hundred, since there are no fifties
to split up with, and give him the three hundred. It may
save his soul or whatever it is inside of him that's worth
saving."
Genevieve professed to be dumbfounded. "Why, I never
heard of such a thing."
"Well, I don't know as I've heard of it so often myself.
But an old man gave me a couple of hundred once just at
the time when I might have gone straight to hell without it,
and I guess I'll try repeating the experiment."
"But you're not saying that young Dabney is going
straight to hell. Why, he's the nicest boy I ever saw, no
matter what they say about him."
"He may not be going straight yet, but he's lingering
along the roadside."
"You mean to say that he drinks?"
"No, nothing of the kind. I don't even know whether
he ever tasted anything or not."
"Then don't tell me that he's after women."
"I'm afraid he is."
"Why, the nice-looking young devil!"
She could not recover from the shock. "Good God! You
don't mean to tell me that a boy like that is traipsing around
after any of these ninnies I see when I go off the hill."
"Oh, no! You needn't worry about that, either."
"Well, what then?"
"Well," Doc said, catching up her own word, "I'll just
tell you what I saw. The morning I left here after I had set
your leg, I took the road down the long hill and up the val-
ley past old Wes Lettinger's place, to see how that Beechamwoman and her youngster were coming along. When I had
gone through the covered bridge and turned on to the creek
road, I happened to look part way over my right shoulder
and saw Giles coming down the hill road from the north.
Before I had gone two hundred yards I saw old Wes's
youngest girl coming down out of the maple grove a little
farther up with a big bunch of flowers in her hand. Now
the rest of it oughtn't to be very hard to work out, ought
it?"
"But she's a nice girl. They say the whole family of them
are pretty loving, but-. And she's pretty, too-just as
pretty as a picture. And old Wes will do something hand-
some for her. He's able, goodness knows."
"Yes, that's the trouble everything you've said. She's
the kind of a girl he'll decide to marry. Any man would.
It would be hard not to. And Giles will become a well-off
man and a local celebrity, and maybe be elected president
of the county fair or a member of the county commis-
sioners.
"That would be tragic. For he's a genius. But he doesn't
know it. It's hard to find such things out around here. But
if he gets away and sees how much easier it is for him to do
big things than little ones, he'll stir everybody up, and he'll
have a lot of fun doing it. He can put that three hundred
with whatever you pay him and he'll have almost enough
to get started somewhere on. Not quite enough-but that
won't hurt anything. If a young rooster has to jump up a
little every time he gets a grain of corn it doesn't hurt-
develops muscles, and good carriage. He'll help himself
along if ever he gets in sight of something. He's got it in
him. But if he's kept around here much longer, his active
mind will be attaching itself to something. You know how
it is; you can't quite hold youth up by the heels forever."
Genevieve had heard understandingly. "My bill will
come to quite a little, too, I suppose. You know he workedawhile last fall and that was to go in with what he's been
doing this spring. Did you see the nice fence he built-
two stretches of it?"
"Of course! He wouldn't build any other kind. He's of
the imaginative type-artist at whatever he does. Look at
his mother: quiet, thoughtful, but with her eyes open and
her head working. Ever see her yard? Or the inside of
her house? The boy's dad has some of the sublime vitality,
too. Only his dad got more bumps and he's the kind that
shows the hurts. But he has it, just the same. You know
when some people get into a tight place, they do a lot of
yelling and get credit for being martyrs. Others say noth-
ing; they just hold on. That's Luke. Look at that pot-
shop. It's the one thing in this world outside his wife and
boy that he ever thinks about. But he never had much of
a chance with it."
Genevieve had become reflective. "There are other peo-
ple ailing in the same way, I guess."
Doc cast a quick glance at her. Might she be induced to
become pleasantly reminiscent? "Yes, that's true that's
true. When I began the practice of medicine I didn't quite
mean to stay in Cloverdale all my life if you care for a
confession.”
"Well, we'll have to save him," she said, disregarding
his lead and going straight back to the original question.
"Then it had better be done right off. This is Saturday.
I suppose he won't be back till Monday."
"He won't be back till I send for him to pay him. He
left word yesterday, when I couldn't see him, that he was
through."
"All right, then, send for him. When he comes, tell him
that you were asked to hand this to him; then give him the
envelope and make him promise to keep it. That will make
us square all round. And if you fall down and break that
leg over before it's good and well, I'll make you get awooden leg-and I'll see that you pay at least a thousand
dollars for it."
They both laughed at this extravagant trial flight of Doc's
imagination. Then he said that he must go, and that if
some poor suffering mortal that craved his attention should
bring him over this way in a week or two, he might stop in
to find out how she was getting along, and whether she en-
countered difficulty in disposing of her long envelope.G
CHAPTER X
ILES walked along the horseshoe of a road that
evening past Wiggam's Glory. He glanced at the
white figure of Joan of Arc almost hidden by briars
in the neglected little park, and on across the deep, wooded
hollow to his own house. South of the house somebody was
going up along the ridge. It looked like the short man who
worked for Genevieve, but at the distance of half a mile it
was difficult to say. Anyhow, Giles was occupied with a
great matter. To the sickly sophisticated who are past
making distinctions, it would have seemed a matter of no
consequence. To the world at large, looking through astig-
matic or deadened eyes, it would have been only a question
of whether a tall young man should walk across the country
on this given Saturday night. But to Giles, it was one of the
significant matters which in their totality constitute fate.
In the course of a dozen years or more, the matter had
been coming up for consideration, more and more frequently,
more and more persistently, until it could not now be put
out of mind. All the lines of Giles's life crossed at this hour
just ahead of him and said: "You are to choose."
The landscape to which he was ordinarily so sensitive was
now for him non-existent. He did not see any of the black
remnant of the Company Row in the hollow. He did not
feel the warm evening sun bright in his eyes. He was
deciding.
If he should go off somewhere. He tried to visualize
the world he would encounter, but it remained as vague and
remote and mysterious as most things do when one has not
seen them. On the other hand
...Yes, on the other hand! Let the testimony be heard.
There was, for instance, the authoritative evidence of-
fered by the great successful man who had been brought to
Centerville only a few nights before to give counsel to sev-
eral dozen boys and girls who were graduating from the city
high school. Giles had gone over, since he himself was in
need of counsel from the outside. Impatiently he had lis-
tened to the preliminaries the invocation by a modern-
looking young priest who prayed "in the name of the Father,
and of the Son, and of the Holy Ghost," and the songs by the
white array of girls in the glee club. He wanted to hear
the great man who had accumulated so many millions that
he was declared by the presiding officer to be a national
figure. When at last he did stand up to speak, he spent
most of his time corroborating the chairman's words. Be-
fore he sat down he said that what he had come there to tell
them was that they should make it a rule of their lives to do
the next thing-the thing just ahead of their noses. Many
people, he said, went far away to find riches when the riches
were right at home. He advised the class to follow this
rule which had resulted without doubt in his own success
about which the chairman had been kind enough to speak.
Despite the strong trace of self-advertising in the pro-
nouncement, Giles was in a state of mind to consider it.
That night while he sat in the caboose of the one o'clock
freight with a sleepy rear brakeman, as the train strained
up one grade and bumped and rattled down another, he tried
to apply the wisdom to his own case. And now as he walked
in easy strides along the road and marshaled arguments
"on the other hand," the successful man's words bore down
their full weight.
If he should stay where he was and build good-looking
houses and design good-looking yards, he might well make
the region famous. Everybody who lived there would build
well, like Genevieve, and everybody who lived somewhereelse would come and see what one man's dream could do.
He would just go about, making the crooked straight, the
ugly beautiful, the literal poetic. There was a project that
was definite. He could see himself doing that. And the
picture was inviting.
There was another argument in favor of this picture, too.
Beulah was in it. Beulah was beautiful-almost as beauti-
ful as the girl of his dreams. When she ran to him that
morning in the moonlight, she was a witch-woman; when
she had clung to his arm as they climbed the hill, she was
an angel; and when they danced over the hilltop at dawn,
he knew he had his arms round a goddess.
To be the husband of old Wesley's favorite daughter!
To have this man who had bought coal lands and sold them
again, and added farm after farm to the ones he already
owned, invite him, Giles Dabney, aside and say, "Some
people think I'm taking a long chance when I entrust her
to you, but I have faith in you-and I'll see that you have a
good start. I've got fine sons-in-law, and I'll be glad to
have another of the same kind."
Think of being comfortable, of forgetting that Wiggam's
Glory ever existed! All the old fears and doubts gone!
Just one year of happiness with Beulah after another!
Children, too! Of course there would be children. Most
of them would be girls. He felt as old Wesley did on that
point. And his children would grow up, and the girls would
put their arms affectionately round his neck when he sat
in the big chair after supper with the light in his face from
the pleasant fire, and they would say, "Dear old dad, haven't
you got a new fur coat in your pocketbook for me?" and
he would take out his pocketbook and peel off a layer or
two of bills from the thick roll of them-one hundred, two
hundred, three hundred, or whatever a fur coat cost; and
they would hug him close and tell him that he was a sweet
old thing. Hum! He would be on top of the world then.With swinging, loose strides he started down the long,
steep hill to the covered bridge. His feet slipped and
crunched on the gravelly clay at every step. "Step-a,
crunch-a; step-a, crunch-a; crunch-a, crunch-a, crunch-a;
going, going, going, going-" He thought he heard old
Dave Malone, the auctioneer.
Where was he going? Why, that was simple: he was going
to keep his tryst with Beulah. He was getting out.
Deep within him he felt the faintest restlessness. "Are
you going," a tiny, intimate voice whispered, "or are you
being taken?"
It was the same voice he had heard that night down at
the church after Mr. Avery and all the personal workers and
all the high school had sought to bring him to the mourners'
bench. Then the voice had whispered, "Wait." Now it
was asking a question. It seemed to deal with fundamen-
tals-with such matters as life and death, or whether a man
should be himself or somebody else.
He turned into the creek road. The restlessness within
grew. As he walked, the voice whispered, sometimes shyly,
sometimes defiantly, "Going-taken? Going-taken?"
Well, there he was, anyhow, in sight of Wesley Letting-
er's big cream-colored house. He wondered who built that
house. It had plenty of everything on it.
There was a desperate restlessness within him. The voice
did not get above a whisper, but it was insistent. "Say,
what I want to know, once and for all, is whether you are
going, or are being taken?"
Wesley sat on the expansive front porch enjoying the
cool of the evening, the abundance of everything round
about, the guarantee in the west that a needed rain was at
hand. No petty troubles annoyed him. As Giles entered
the front gate and had a close view of Wesley sitting there
so floridly in the midst of his great abundance, he thought
of the rich man in the Bible."Good evening, sir!" Wesley called to him in robust cor-
diality. Wesley enjoyed addressing every young man as
"sir." He could put a trace of the patronizing in his voice
and make the young man feel at once just where he be-
longed.
Giles mounted the steps.
"Is your daughter Beulah at home?" He knew she was,
but he wished to start off with direct appropriateness.
"No, she is not."
Wesley saw the sharp twitch of disappointment in Giles's
face, and he was amused. These young men who came to
pay court to his daughters!
"You are young Dabney, aren't you?"
Giles had to admit that he was.
"I thought so. I believe I've seen you somewhereat
church, I expect. No, the Babe went off with Lee Turner
in his new automobile. I heard her say something about
you, but I don't know as I remember what it was. You
know these girls will be tempted to take a nice ride! Won't
you sit down? She may be back after a while."
Giles's tongue and throat were as dry as shavings. He
did manage, however, to say that he believed he had better
not sit down. He would be on his way. There was a rain
in the west.
Down at the cream-colored front gate he possessed enough
of a sense of disguise to walk on up the road as if he had
merely stopped at the house in passing. He walked straight
ahead. What else was there for him to do?
Lee Turner-he had heard that name before. His knees
trembled with a humiliating weakness. He felt insignificant
and worthless a stranger in a land where no one spoke his
language. He must hurry. He did not wish to suffer the
further humiliation of meeting Beulah and Lee Turner be-
fore he came to the road that would bring him back to Wig-
gam's Glory. How they would smile at him as he tried notto show his embarrassment! Then he grew sulky. He had
been caressed by a girl into coming to see her, and then she
arranged to be away when he arrived. Then he was angry
-red hot. Did she think he was a nobody that she could
wrap round her finger? Not much. When he next met her
on his way to the Lookout-or was he ever going back?-
he would just walk straight ahead and let her talk to the
gate post or the covered bridge. Then he grew lofty. Who
cared what had happened? Did it make any difference in
his great plans?
He grew ruddy at the thought of getting the upper hand
of the idea. He was swept by a surge of gratitude that
things had turned out just as they had. He had been de-
luded into a pursuit of respectability. He had been hauled
along when he thought he was directing his own steps. The
voice on the road had raised a question. He had ignored
the voice. And then the caprice of a woman had enabled
him to hear it again. And now-now it was not only a
voice, but a light. In his gratitude he shouted across the
tree-tops below him on the ridge. Now he could be alone.
Now he could see.
He sang in a loud voice. He shouted in a louder voice
still. Great was his freedom! And how great the envel-
oping brightness! He saw just what he might have been—
the respected son-in-law of Wesley Lettinger, with neigh-
bors coming to him for advice about barns and silos. The
nearest thing? The nearest thing was not the thing to do.
The successful man was a liar! A liar! Maybe his great
rule worked if you were just interested in grabbing without
caring for what you got. But if you cared! What if you
cared? Oh, it was a lie, a lie!
His father and mother knew better. Even if they had
been banged, they had not surrendered!-had not surren-
dered!-had not surrendered! Who could make them in-
dulge in cheap absurdities just to flatter the ego? He weptas he shouted and sang, because he now saw the depths of
their suffering, the greatness of the sacrifices they had made
for him.
Now he would get out! He shouted it to the trees, to
the winds that preceded the storm. He would not stay
there. He was strong. He had nurtured power when people
thought he was doing nothing. Would he encounter ob-
stacles? Well, what did he care what he encountered? He
was looking for something to encounter. He wanted to ex-
tend himself to the limit, then take a breath, and then-
heave it out of the way, whatever it was!
The storm was upon him. But who cared for that? He
was unafraid. He sang on. It was a baby's weakness to
fear the lightning. A bolt came down close behind him, and
the instant crash of thunder made his spine creep; but he
only lifted his voice higher and sang on. There was a flash
at his elbow that took his breath in the middle of a syllable,
and sent the bark from a locust tree flying in shreds into
the road in front of him. It was Genevieve's big woods over
again. But now he was as fearless as Genevieve had been.
He was not defiant; but he was joyous. Soon a bolt came
down ahead of him. In the deluge of sweeping rain he saw
the outlines of St. Bernard's. He sang on. Another flash,
and beside him was the churchyard itself-a shimmer of
glistening white and then the darkness once more. So many
forgotten dead over there! But he was alive. He was
going on an adventure. If he should meet Hope to-night, he
would tell her.
He would tell Gustie, anyhow. He liked Gustie, now
that he thought about it. She had so few nun-like qualities,
yet she wanted to be a nun! Gustie had seen the monster
in the sky over Wiggam's Glory. Gustie had felt it closing
down close over her, body and soul-close over everybody
who stayed there. That was why she wanted him to get
away. She had seen what happened to Pewee when theleavings of a poetic spirit moved him to build a decent fence.
He was yanked away before he knew it and made to hunt
for bread in a place where there were no fences. Hurrah
for Pewee! Hadn't he dreamed dreams, too? Hadn't he
imagined places where the ways of life were not so deadly
to men's bodies or to what was inside their bodies?
The lightning was incessant. It darted to the earth in
white fluid-looking bolts; it leaped across the sky; it quiv-
ered in every cloud. The thunder bumped and rolled echo-
ing to every part of the heavens. The blinding waves of
rain drove drenchings and drenchings of the cool water
through to his skin. The water ran down his shoulders and
back and legs into his shoes. Yet he remained ruddy with
warmth.
He came at last within intermittent sight of his house.
The windows were dark. His father and mother must be in
bed. He came up to the house. In the cover of the storm,
he slipped inside and up to his room. In the darkness, dis-
persed completely now and then by an unusually brilliant
flash, he undressed and went to bed.
He did not sleep. He was not interested in sleep. Why
should he lose himself in mental numbness for six or eight
hours when there were so many magnificent plans to be
worked out? Gradually the rolling thunder became indis-
tinct in the east, the flashes of lightning brightened up his
room less and less, and the wind was not to be heard at all.
In undisturbed quiet, he lay warm and very still and
sought to envisage his plans.G
CHAPTER XI
ENEVIEVE sent for you," his mother said when
he came downstairs the next morning in his second-
best suit. "The hired man said she was troubled
about something."
Genevieve? Didn't she like her fence? He had thought
it was an especially good job. Or didn't she care for the
finishing touches he had put on her yard? Perhaps she
didn't like the maples. Still, that could hardly be, for she
had watched him from the front porch while he worked at
them. Well, whatever it was, he would see her to-morrow.
His head was occupied with great matters just now.
He must walk. He would walk to church at Culbertson.
The people outside the church eyed him as if he might have
come for some evil purpose. But they did not reduce his
rapture in the least. He went inside, sat near an open win-
dow and breathed the heavy bitter-sweet odor of locust blos-
soms while he listened to one of Mr. Avery's young succes-
sors preach a strange gospel; namely, that Jesus Christ
was not well received by the respectable people of His day;
that He saw things too clearly to suit them. That was
right! That must be right! In a greater glow than ever,
as if he might not have missed a wink of sleep in a lifetime,
he walked back home along the quiet, warm road.
He was not ready to eat anything. But his mother had
everything waiting, and he sat down, happily, airily.
"You must have been out in that storm," she said, a trifle
anxiously yet cheerfully, after they had begun eating their
smoked ham and last year's potatoes-"by the looks of that
suit.""Yes, I came right through it. Never saw it rain harder
in my life. Didn't you hear me singing?"
He knew they did not.
"It began when I was way up by St. Bernard's. Do you
suppose I can press my clothes so they'll look all right?
I'll need that suit-in what I'm planning to do."
He did not often give his father a good opening; he shrank
from intimate family speech. But to-day-to-day was a
different matter.
"What are you planning to do?" his father asked with as
much interest as he thought it safe to reveal.
Giles crushed a potato with his fork. "Why, I'm plan-
ning to get away from here and learn something."
The silence was long, but not cumbersome. His father
cut away unnecessarily at the piece of ham on his plate.
Then, as self-consciously as an adolescent boy who is about
to say the most endearing thing he ever thought up, he said,
without lifting his eyes, "Well, I hope you can make it."
"Oh, I can make it, all right," Giles answered promptly.
"I've decided."
"Oh, youth! Oh, youth!" Luke thought. "What confi-
dence! What certainty! And to think that I felt like that
once myself!"
He ate with his face close down, and in the space before
his eyes he saw all that he had hoped to be and could
not. Val sipped tea in her manner and looked off across
the hills of blossoming locust trees.
In a state of sublime peace, Giles went upstairs and
threw himself across the bed for some sleep. Luke and Val
still sat at the table. They looked at each other profoundly
and smiled; then they looked away and smiled. Words
were sacrilege.
How was that? Giles thought he heard Genevieve's man
talking downstairs. "She wants to see him right away.
She's all kicked up and oneasy over somethin', and wasafraid he'd be gone again same as yesterday. She said if
he was here, that I was to wait and come with him."
Giles went with him. The man spoke with such easy
volubility about crops and work and horses and deer and
bulls that the distance was covered almost before they
knew it.
"Here he is, Miss Crosby!" the man called from the lane,
and then went on his way to spend the rest of the Sunday
afternoon at home with his family.
Genevieve sat on her front porch. "You're a hard young
devil to catch," she said in hearty pleasantness. "Why
didn't you come yesterday when I sent?"
Giles found it difficult to fall into her mood at once and
stammered seriously at the reason already known to her.
She was happy to see him confused.
"Oh, I know all about that. But what I don't like is
having to keep other people's valuables in my charge."
Just what did this mean? Before Giles could figure it
out, she went on: "I like my yard-and my fence. I was
used to such things once. But I didn't know there was
anybody left around here who was interested in them. I
wanted you to come over and get your pay, for one thing.
You didn't tell me how much it was to be; so I've just
made out a check for what I've decided to pay. If you
don't think it's enough, why, you'll have to work for some-
body else. And I don't suppose I'll see much of you any-
how-going away as you are to get your education. But it's
the thing to do. Then you can do whatever you want. It's
a hell of a life if you never get to do any of the things you
dreamed you'd like to."
Giles wondered if he were going to have the honor of
hearing the great confession for which all ears had strained
ever since Genevieve had established herself on the hill.
But no, she quickly came to the rest of the matter in hand.
"Can you keep secrets?""Sometimes."
"Can you do what you're told?"
"Sometimes."
"But I mean this time. It's the one I'm talking about."
"I don't know. What is it?"
"Well, if you're not getting to be a perfect young devil
for sure! Why didn't you say yes?"
She laughed a ringing, honest laugh. Then she gathered
herself together again. "Why, this is what it is: I was
asked to hand this to you and see that you accepted it.
Now do you promise?"
Giles hesitated.
"Well, it's not so big but what you can carry it," she de-
clared, more amused, more perplexed than ever.
"All right, then, I promise."
She handed him the two envelopes together-the one
which contained his check, and this anonymous one.
"Now take them along. And if I see you stop to peep at
them while you're on my land, I'll be damned if I don't run
after you on my new leg and wring your young neck."
He took them along. He would stop at the big maple on
the ridge above the pot-shop and see what he had. It did
not take long to come up to the tree, for he walked on air.
He sat down on the high bank of the path.
"Check for yard, etc.," one envelope read. He would
begin with that one.
"Centerville National Bank." Yes, of course. "To the
order of Giles Dabney." To be sure. "Three hundred dol-
lars." How's that?
He thought his pulses would leap out of his neck. His
eyes felt fluttery. He blinked them to make sure that they
were seeing. "Three hundred dollars." No, it could not be.
He had hoped she might give him one hundred and fifty.
And yet he could make nothing else out of the writing. It
was the same as the signature, "Genevieve Crosby," too.Had he better open the other envelope? It seemed sus-
picious. It was thicker. He turned it over. He bent it.
He scrutinized it in the hope of finding some mark of identi-
fication. At last he tore open the end and slipped out the
contents. "One hundred dollars," a great yellow bank note
read. He peeped under it. "One hundred dollars," another
read. And then beneath that, he saw another still.
He had lost his senses. He was crazy, that's what he was.
He felt crazy the night before in the rain. He wasn't then;
but now he was. Or he was dreaming. Trembling, he stood
up to see if he were awake. He seemed to be. But hadn't
he gone to sleep upstairs across the bed after dinner? Το
be sure, he had. And he was still asleep. That's where he
was. All the rest of it was a dream. He had seen Gene-
vieve before in dreams.
He stooped down and touched the earth. It felt like
earth. But Doc had always said that he was of the imag-
inative type.
He walked up to the house. His father and mother were
sitting out front in the cool shade. At least it seemed to
be the house, and it seemed to be his father and mother.
His mother was wearing her Sunday afternoon pink-and-
gray dress. He had seen it before. Of course he had seen
it-when he was awake. They looked toward him when
he came up, but their faces were bright with an unaccus-
tomed radiance. Yes, of course he was dreaming.
"Say," he said, and he was surprised to hear his own
voice, "look at this check for me, will you? Either I'm
crazy, or Genevieve has given me three hundred dollars."
They looked at him in silent amazement while he drew
the check from the envelope. No, his father and mother
were not real They were in a dream.
"Three hundred?" at last he heard his father say. "Let's
see." His father's voice was just as unnatural as his looks.
Yet he slowly stretched toward the check a familiar handthat trembled like the paw of a dog that has been much
whipped. He took the check, glanced at it, slipped it into
the edge of the book he had been reading, drew his glasses
from their case, slipped them on, took the check from the
book, and then studied it intently.
"Yes, that's what it reads-'three hundred dollars."
"Then I'm not crazy."
"Well, it reads three hundred, anyway."
They all laughed. It was time to have something to
laugh at.
"All right, then," Giles went on, "what do your eyes make
out of this? Is this a hundred-dollar bill?"
His father was speechless, as if there might in truth be
some question about his son's sanity.
"Is it, Mother?"
His mother declared that so far as her experience enabled
her to judge, it was a genuine bill of that denomination.
"All right, then, is this another?"
They stood amazed.
"And is this still another?"
News, for Luke, had always meant that something was
taken away. Now that news brought something, and then
made quick additions to what it brought in the first place,
he found it as difficult as Giles to believe his own eyes.
Still, one element of reality disengaged itself from so
much that was unbelievable. Genevieve was known, despite
her positiveness of speech, as a considerate, even generous
employer. She had plenty of money. She rejoiced that she
was not to be a cripple for life. These facts might account
for the size of the check.
Might not these facts account for the money, too? Yet
if she had meant the money as pay, why hadn't she put the
entire amount in one check?
Luke reflected. He smiled the smile of one who pursuesto some avail. "I'll bet old Doc Seabright left that money
for you. He likes you pretty well."
"But," Val protested, "he was never known to have that
much money ahead, was he?"
It all went through Giles's mind in a flash. "Of course!
And I'll make him take it back, too. I've got three hundred
without it, and he's old and might need it."
He was filled with great generosity. He would go to
Cloverdale. He would go that afternoon yet. Now that
the world had come fully to life, he was in the mood to com-
plete all transactions with dispatch.
He had good fortune. He met Dr. Seabright on the ridge
road. Dr. Seabright saw him coming and was full of anx-
iety. He thought Giles might be at that very moment on
the way to Wesley Lettinger's.
"Well," he said as he drew up, "what princess are you
off to see now?"
"I'm not off to see any princess. I'm just off to see you."
"Then you won't need to go much farther."
Dr. Seabright thought he detected a positive agreeable-
ness in Giles's voice, but his lifelong habit of associating
messengers with sick-beds led him to ask: "Anybody
ailing?"
"No, nobody ailing, but I want to see you just the same."
He rested the foot of one of his long legs on the horizontal
spoke of the buggy wheel so that he could get a little closer.
"Ever see this envelope before?"
"Well, now," Dr. Seabright replied, not at all caught un-
awares, "that would be hard to say. It's a very plain,
ordinary-looking envelope."
"Well, then," Giles said, falling into Dr. Seabright's style
of preliminary, "did you ever see what's inside of it?" And
he spread the bills over his fingers.
Dr. Seabright's face shone with amusement. "Well, I'd
have to say about them just what I've said about the en-velope. It would be hard to go on the witness stand and
take oath that I had ever seen exactly these bills before.
I could, though, take oath that I never had many such in
my possession at one time."
"You asked Genevieve to give them to me, didn't you?"
"Say, look here, boy. Were you going to walk all the
way to Cloverdale just to ask me some foolish question like
that?"
"It's not a foolish question. I was just walking over to
say that I earn good money when I work, and that I couldn't
accept a big gift like this from you."
"From me? What's the matter with me?"
"Why-why-nothing. I mean that I couldn't accept it
from anybody. Genevieve has paid me two or three times
as much as I expected, and that will start me off."
"And this will allow you a little extra! What's the mat-
ter with that? You may need it."
"Yes, and you may need it too."
"Now see here," Doc answered, "don't you cast any re-
flections on my financial competency."
He gave a pleasant bark of a laugh. "No, I'll never need
it. As long as I practice medicine, I'll have a patient once
in a while who'll pay his bills. And when I quit-well, I
don't expect to quit until I'm past the need of money. If I
have to, and I'm in danger of running short, why-" and
he gave his little bark of a laugh again-"I'll have to take
some of my own medicine and hurry the end of things!"
He saw that Giles was not deeply interested in profes-
sional pleasantries. "No, boy, you must keep the money.
Why, you can live the better part of a year on that if you
know how. Think of a whole year with your way paid, and
everything to be discovered. And if Genevieve has paid
you as much as you say, why, there's another year, maybe.
No, sir, boy, you don't know when you're well off. You
just keep the money-every cent of it."A man gave me two hundred dollars once. I know how
you feel. You don't want to be an object of charity. Well,
if you feel that way about it, that's the sure proof that the
money won't hurt you. If your conscience is too cantanker-
ous, why, we'll call it a long-time loan or something. Then
if you ever have more money than you can drag around,
and I'm about to be packed off to the poorhouse, you can
pay me back. How's that?"
He swung the team over to one side. "Here, climb in.
I'm going down the ridge your way a distance, and you can
save yourself a mile. And we can discuss some other sub-
ject."
When Giles said good-by to Dr. Seabright, who never
mentioned money again, or gave him any opportunity to
do so, he strode homeward in the deep afterglow of a sun
just out of sight, and felt that the gods who walked abroad
in the cool of this particular evening were more beneficent
than those ordinarily to be encountered in the region of
Wiggam's Glory.G
CHAPTER XII
ILES sat in the sweltering day-coach of the through
train and tried to enjoy the experience of getting
out. That morning when his father dressed up in
his Sunday clothes before breakfast as if he were about to
do something worshipful, and hitched the blind old clay-
grinder to the spring wagon and brought him and his papier-
mâché suit-case down to Culbertson in the dewy freshness
of six o'clock, it had been easy enough to say good-by to his
mother at home and to his father at the station. The world
must forever celebrate the day's happy importance. On
the early Centerville Accommodation, while he sat in the
creaking little coach and watched familiar hills glide by,
the same nervous enthusiasm sustained him. But when
the long ponderous limited train for the East came to a stop
on the sharply curved track at the Centerville Union Sta-
tion, the monster of an engine throbbing, the starchy negro
porters rubbing the dirt from the brass handrails of a half-
dozen or more sumptuous Pullmans, and even the day-
coaches at the rear shining in affluence that the dust of sum-
mer could not hide, he felt a trifle overawed. And now as
he sat alone in a high-backed green seat with his entire body
of accouterments in the suit-case beside him, the noisy mis-
cellaneousness of the people who filled the long coach
pressed in profanely upon his dreams and confused his view
of what he had thought he saw clearly.
Where were they all going, these people? Nothing in
their manner suggested that they were headed for any goals
of consequence. They lounged; they slept; they sat up
sleepily; they engaged in hot family quarrels without beingembarrassed by their audience; they ate luncheons of hard-
boiled eggs and overripe bananas; they tried impatiently
to comfort travel-weary children, and ended by boxing their
ears; they talked about how they thought their aunt's prop-
erty ought to be divided, now that she had at last died.
When he looked straight ahead along the aisle, some stout
man facing him in one of two seats turned together sat with
his coat off, his collar thrown aside, his shirt rolled in at the
neck, and fanned himself and looked defiantly as if to say,
"You think you're a hell of a young somebody, don't you?"
When he looked across the aisle, a petulant boy of five made
faces at him in his discontent with the world at large. When
he turned occasionally to see who was behind him, a restless
girl two or three seats beyond a red-faced Irish priest who
looked hot in his Roman collar, stared at him with a smile
of amusement which she might have been willing to trade
for one of pleasant banter if he had ventured to have a
word with her.
Couldn't any of these people see that he was away on a
mission of unending importance? Couldn't they? Well,
none of them acted as if they could. So he leaned his face
out over the sill of the open window and watched the high
drive-wheels of the engine making graceful revolutions as
the train crept along a steep horseshoe curve. With eyes
half closed to prevent them from catching cinders too read-
ily, he continued to gaze ahead outside the train until he
restored the integrity of his dreams.
He was getting away. The engine disappeared round a
curve that swept off in the other direction. Then as his
side of the train took the inner side of the curve again, the
drive-wheels came back into view. They were going a little
faster, a little faster, a little faster. They were hurrying
him away to the world's magnificence where he would work
at something expansive until he was middle-aged, until he
was old, until he was old and white-headed.Still he was not wholly away. Whenever he tried to peer
into the future, to visualize what he would be like twenty
or thirty years from now, always what he tried to see was
confused by the interposition of hilltops and faces just left
behind. Wiggam's Glory stood there ahead of him more
sharply than anything he could see in the future-the new
house with the old one back of it and off to one side, the
ridge and the maple tree down toward the pot-shop, the en-
circling hills of locust trees in full bloom, heavy with an
odor of sweetness and greenness. A rush of sympathy for
everyone back there filled his body with a resistless trem-
bling, and his eyes with easy tears which unprotested he
let make their way down over his wide cheeks to the sill
of the window.
How heroically, how futilely, so many people back there
were fighting! They did not know how to fight. They did
not always know what they were fighting for. But they
were sure they were worse off than they ought to be; worse
off than people in more fortunate parts of the world. Yes,
any of them back there; Pewee, for instance. Pewee was
full of fear, and his efforts at realization had been abortive.
Yet Pewee had dreamed. Giles wished for him something
better than life in a Company Row, a few transient weeks
of stumbling over lessons in the difficult New Testament,
a few days with a clean paling fence in front of the
house. Mrs. Dunkle would die wishing for a house of her
own with carpets on the floors, and draperies at the win-
dows, and luxuriant arches of pink and purple morning-
glories over every doorway. Eagerly the Dunkles had com-
mitted themselves to a round trip of eight miles in order to
see the new house which he had built. They had made the
same trip as soon as they had heard that he was going. Mrs.
Dunkle thought it would be wonderful, and Pewee, tearful
in his rejoicing, had counseled, "Keep a-goin' up, Giles; God
damn it, keep a-goin' up!"Sockey was over at Smoky Hollow, too, already a hard-
ened miner. He was grateful to Sockey. He would never
forget how to execute one of Sockey's upper-cuts to the chin.
And Bugs and Sib and Cross-eye, wherever they were!
And Cora and Lydia and Gustie! They would soon be
through with their transient joy of the flesh. Soon they
would be sour-tempered housewives in tumbledown houses,
quarreling profanely with disheartened husbands and yelling
in harsh, impatient voices at ragged, dirty-nosed children.
That would be their lot if they were lucky. If they were
not lucky they would spend a year or two in the Red Lan-
tern section of Centerville, and then scrub saloon entrances
for another two or three, and be through once and for all,
and glad of it. Giles wished he could run back and
greet them, and bring an echo of color to Lydia's blonde
cheeks by telling her that she had been the prettiest girl in
the Wiggam's Glory school, and send a flash of brightness
all over Gustie's face by assuring her that she had been
more important than she knew in helping him to get away.
If she wanted to be a nun-well, he wished her better suc-
cess than she would probably have.
He remembered all the rest-Mr. Burrell's brother, chief
figure in every scene he happened to grace; and genteel Mr.
Leacock standing at the corner of the piano singing in a
cracked whisky-tenor, "Here Am I, O Lord, Send Me,"
while a beautiful girl in white played the accompaniment;
and Mr. Avery-even Mr. Avery-with his saber scar across
his cheek, praying for an unending army of impressionable
sinners whom he could battle into repentance. All these he
remembered without jest.
Somebody inside the coach shrieked clearly, and Giles
thought he heard old Genevieve. There she went through
the storm, her defiant face uplifted to the lightning. He
saw the young Italian in overalls, too, but now he looked
remote and harmless, and was only so much background.And Doc came through the woods and stood with his buggy
whip in his hand and told the young gang what he would
do to them. Doc always had an eye out for him. Three
hundred dollars!
The train gathered speed as it wound through the lower
hills. He was getting farther away. But this only made
him feel how firm the bonds between himself and Wiggam's
Glory were, now that they were being stretched to tight-
ness. He saw his mother busy over the hot kitchen stove,
busy with her flower garden, busy with a hundred things in
order to make the next year pass. She leaned over a bed
of mammoth peonies, and her face was more beautiful than
the flowers.
"Mother!"
He cried out before he could restrain himself. The man
and woman in the seat in front of him thought they heard
him say something, and turned to see. But when they saw
him with his head out of the window, they decided that the
roar of the train had duped their drowsy ears.
He wanted to cry out to his father, too. He wanted to
tell him that he must know from experience how hard it is
for a son to speak intimately to a father, and how easy it
is to leave a father to infer his son's affection for him. At
this moment, his father was down there in the pot-shop, in-
tent over a ball of clay that was speedily becoming a fruit
jar or a jug or a milk pitcher or a jardinière, and thinking
of the beautiful things he could have made if he had been
able to get away and learn about slips and glazes and the
mixing of clays before the tin can age encompassed him.
"Hurry! Hurry! Hurry!" the train was murmuring.
Yes, hurry! He must not be away too long. He must
drink in the magnificence and master a tiny installment of
knowledge, and then speed back and report, "This is only
a little of the much I shall bring. When I have been away
longer I shall come with everything you have missed-everything except the one essential thing which nobody can
ever restore to you." How tenaciously they had clung to
the genuine, the honest! Would he ever be able to make
as much of his advantage?
He sat up. The roar of the wheels and the mournful
warnings of the whistle did not now obscure the voices of
those who traveled with him. They traveled with him, yes.
They were there whenever he looked. He began to wonder
if they were not interesting. But very evidently they were
not concerned with what was in his mind. So he leaned on
the window sill again. He felt less alone with the stiff rush
of the wind in his face, and the sting of occasional cinders.
Yes, he would hurry back. The aching hurt was as-
suaged somewhat by this thought that his return was not to
be delayed; by the other thought that he would carry the
news of everything discoverable. As he went through his
vows, he began to notice the landscape; he began to see that
it was interesting. Here before his eyes already was some-
thing to report back to the hills.
The speeding engine took the curves with a firmer swift-
ness. The singing wheels flying over the joints of the rails
made quick-time accompaniment to an Irish melody that
persisted in singing itself in his ears. The voice of the
whistle spread hoarse music over the rolling countryside.
What would the next town be, now? And after that, the
next? And then, the next? All that he had left behind was
yielding to what might be ahead.
He was away.
